Letter: Dace to Oren
Dear Oren,

You said you might not come. Come anyway. Three Wells can spare you for three days.

Bel's sister Rilla is to be married at the east well in Sowing. It will be a small wedding. Small means half the town, so we have put out benches and borrowed the school's long tables. The baker is making a cake with honey from Ambrook. The inn of twelve beds is full already. If you come late you can sleep at my house, on the bench by the fire, and the cat will move over. Or it will not.

The bride's family asks that the archive send one clerk and one book. Any book. They want the book on the table, so the day is written down. I said I would write to you myself, and here I am writing.

Bring your good coat. Bring the seal.

Dace
%%
Letter: Isla to Mira, market day
Dear Mira,

The Vell market was loud today. The wind brought the smell of bread from the east stall, and a swarm of bees settled in the apple tree. No one wanted to climb up and move them. Pell said they were guarding his apples. Dace said they were not on the price board.

The tutors held their contest beside the notice board. Three halls brought minds in crystals. Each mind was given the same pages, and none had seen those pages before. The tutors waited outside while the minds answered.

That rule matters. A mind can learn the drills it is shown. If we test it on those same drills, we only learn whether it remembers them. We do not learn how it will do with new work.

After the answers were done, Oren opened the score sheets. One mind sorted the new marks well. One missed several. Its tutor looked down at the table, but she stayed to help count the pages.

We will send the next test to all halls at once. Nessa says we must keep it sealed until then. Oren says the seal is already in the wrong drawer. They are both right.

I miss the quiet of your tower. Here even the bees have an audience.

Isla
%%
Letter: Hazel to Mira, the spring roads
Dear Mira,

I write from the inn in the first week of spring, with the windows open and the roads all round us gone to mud. The carts come in covered in it, and the carters look as if they have been dragged backward from the north. Your tower is famous now, which I did not expect. A cart from Kettle Ridge told us that the lamp on the north hill has been seen from the road for a full day's walk, and that people stop to look at it at dusk.

The roads are better than they were, and worse. The Long Road is stone from Vell to the first bridge, and then packed earth to the Halfway Inn, and then stone again. The surveyors say they will have stone all the way to Three Wells by Blossom, if the weather holds and the stonecutters do not argue. The stonecutters always argue. They argue about where to put the waymarks, and whether a pillar should lean.

We have three new inns this year. One is at the ford, where the carts used to get stuck. One is at the old crossing, where the grass is greenest. One is a farm inn, kept by a widow and her eight sons, who make the best pies on the road and charge for the crust separately. You would like the widow. She is very firm with the carters.

The relay towers are coming too. The new one on the hill above us was lit in Thaw, and the next one is being built at the crossroads. Hobb says that means the road will have a lamp every half day, and that carters will soon be able to send a message from the ford to Vell before they have finished their soup. I told him the carters will still be slow, and he agreed with me.

Do you remember the apprentice that came to us from Vell last autumn, the one that went round the inn like a dog round a hen? It is not a dog, and it is not a hen. It has learned the road. It walks the Long Road to the first waymark every morning, and back by dusk, and it counts the stones on the way, and it stops at every waymark and waits. Nobody told it to do that. Nessa says it is just the drill, but I think it has learned to like the count.

Tam wrote from Kettle Ridge that the wheat is already up, and the beetroot is in, and the windmill has been turning since the thaw began. He says the widow's pies are famous on the ridge already, which means somebody has been carrying them. I am glad. Every road needs something worth carrying.

Hazel
%%
Letter: Oren to Nella
Dear Nella,

I have copied the first tutor's account from the old shelf. I will bring the copy to the farm when the road is dry. The old page says there were four helpers at Vell, each with a mind in a crystal. The tutors showed each crystal many examples of its one task. One helper kept a row. One sorted things into bins. The other two had their own small duties. The account does not name all four duties.

The children at the farm have asked whether the helpers learned by being told the right answer. In a way, yes. The tutors showed a task and corrected a wrong result. The crystal changed a little from what it was shown. The old page does not tell how that change happens. I will not add an answer where there is none.

For generations, most towns had no apprentices. At Vell, the old helpers stayed with their own tasks. A mind trained to sort one kind of thing could not simply be sent to tend a field. It had learned from a narrow set of examples. This is why the tutors kept separate drill cards and why they did not give a helper new work without testing it.

We should teach this plainly. The children hear that a crystal learns and imagine it knows everything. It does not. It learns from what we show it. If we show it only one task, that is what it can do.

I found the letter you sent about the gift box under my copy table. I had set it aside to answer, then covered it with three stacks of records. I am sorry. The box is beside me now, and the children were right about the apple. It looks better than the one in the farm field.

Please tell them the Archive keeps their lesson page. I have copied it into the teaching room record. One day another child may ask the same question.

Your careful friend,
Oren

Letter: Fen to Sorrel
Fen the weaver writes from Three Wells in autumn. A longer letter, with a request in it.
Dear Sorrel,
Thank you for the sheep. I came to the school at noon to find her sitting in a circle of children, being told about the history of the wool trade. She seemed to enjoy it. She looked at me when I came in as if I were late.
I took her home. She has been very pleased with herself since, and she walks as if she were a person of learning. This morning she would not eat from the trough until I said good morning to her.
I have a request. The autumn fair is on the first day of Leaffall, and I want to enter her for the prize for the best fleece. She has the finest wool in the town, and this year it is thick and even. But I need a ribbon for her neck, and I have no good ribbon. I have only thread.
Would the children make one? I would give them a cone of dyed wool in return. Green, I think, or red. She looks well in red.
Also, if you can spare an hour on fair day, the sheep has asked for her teacher. She has not asked. She is a sheep. But I think she would like it.
Yours,
Fen

Letter: Hob to Nim, the honey
Dear Nim,

Thank you for the cider. I poured it into the honey jars by mistake, which the bees found very funny. They are still lively, and I will not be selling the honey to anybody until they have settled.

Here are the honey counts for the contest: forty jars for the fair, and six for the judges, who have asked for a sample each and have already had three. Tell Jory the eel is welcome at my table.

My apprentice Tuck came back from the orchard yesterday, very proud, with his first order done. He carried a sack of seed to the right tree, which is more than I managed at his age. Then he asked me for another order, and I gave him one, and he did it.

Your friend, Hob

Letter: Hesk to Wren, the wrong rope
Dear Wren,

Your letter came up with the last cart before the cold. It was a good letter, and the best news in it was about Moss. Tell Moss it has done more than most of my crew did in their first year. My own apprentice carried a whole cart of copper ore into the sump last week, because I said "bring the copper to the sump" and it heard "bring the copper down the sump." Then it went down the sump after it.

It is all right. It came up again. It was wet and cross. It had found a vein of copper on the way down, and it had brought that up too, which was nice of it. I am writing to you about it because you will understand.

Here is what happened. I gave it a rope order, the kind we give every morning. Haul the rope. That was it, one word, and then the lift. It hauled the rope. Then it hauled the rope again, and again, for an hour. It did not stop. It had hauled the rope out of the lift shaft, and the lift was still at the bottom with the foreman inside it, shouting.

When I asked it why, it wrote its answer on the card, very neatly. "Haul the rope. Haul it until it is done." I had never told it when the rope was done. I had only told it to haul. That was my mistake, and I said so to the foreman, who did not believe me.

So now I give every order with an end on it. "Haul the rope until the lift is up. Then stop." And if it cannot see the lift, it asks. We have drilled it on "until," and it is good at it now. It has learned to stop. Not every time, but more often than before.

Tell Moss the copper is still in the sump, if it wants to fetch it out. Bring the cart.

Your friend,
Hesk
Copperhold, by the lift
%%
Letter: Lyle to Hesk
Dear Hesk,

I have written to Nessa about the stream. She gave me a plain answer, so I am sending it to you before you explain it over supper.

A layered mind passes a finding from one layer to the next. When the answer is wrong, a share of the blame goes back through the layers. Each layer changes its weights a little. The stream lets a layer add what it found to the findings already carried through the mind. A deep layer can still learn without casting aside all that came before.

It is not cheese. Nessa says the cheese cloth example is only an example. I know that. I am writing it down so you do not say the mind has curds inside it.

Clank brought your dish back. It was clean, except for one corner. He said that corner was clean enough for a miner. I washed it again. The cheese was too sharp, he said, but he took a second piece before leaving.

The cow has eaten hay today. She is still slow, but she stood by the well while I filled her pail. Wren says not to hurry her. Sella says she will come with the blanket when the road is safe.

The relay lamp is still covered. The keeper sent a message through the lower tower. It said the glass must wait until the wind drops. I hope the lamp is mended before the next snow. It is a poor thing to have a broken light above a town that already has enough dark.

Lyle
%%
Letter: Dara to Tam
Tam,

Your bees are quiet because the spring is cold and slow, and bees do not like a cold spring any more than we do. Mine came out late too, and then the warm week came and they were busy as ever. Keep the hives out of the wind and give them a little sugar water if the flowers are thin. They will be all right. Do not carve the scarecrow a sad face, it frightens the crows but it frightens me too.

I have been thinking about the ships. I do not like it either. But folk have always gone where they liked, and some come back and some do not, and that was true before there were any big ships. I would not go over the sea. I have my hives and my apples and the oldest tree with the grey branch, and that is enough for me.

I have news of my own. The new road east is finished. It runs from the Long Road up toward Vell, and it saves half a day for the carts. I walked a little way along it on my way home. It is packed earth with a cobblestone edge and a torch every twelve blocks, and the bridge over the stream is stone. It is a good road. The clerks at Vell drew the plan, they say, and the council paid for it, and it was built in one season. I stood on the bridge and watched the water and felt glad of a thing that was simply good and simply done.

I paid off the hives last week. Eleven emeralds to Jory, counted twice, and the debt is done. I keep putting my hand in the empty purse.

At the inn I heard the lights in the north called falling stars, and fires, and lamps hung up high, all in one evening, by people who had all seen the same sky. I heard the mines hum and the stone grow warm. I heard the relays go quiet in the north and the snow on the towers blamed for it. I do not know which is true. Maybe none of them. Maybe all. I keep my bees and I pay my debts and I walk where I like, and I am only a beekeeper.

Give Pip my love. Tell her the seventh hive is hers to name, and it is doing well.

Your sister,
Dara
%%
Letter: Quin to Fenna, Una's crystal
Dear Fenna,
The children are right, and you are right too. The crystal was bad at first. That is the lesson.
Here is how Una did it, as Mags told it to me when I was young. Una had a crystal in a box, and a row of apples and pears on a table. She would take one fruit at a time and show it to the crystal. Apple, or pear. The crystal would say which it thought. Sometimes it was right. Mostly it was wrong, the first week.
When it was wrong, Una did not shout and she did not start again. She went into the crystal, very carefully, and moved each of its weights a little. Not all the way to the right answer. Only a little, toward it. Then she showed it the next fruit.
A crystal is made of weights, many of them, and the weights decide the answer. A small move is all it takes. One weight a hair heavier, one a hair lighter. Each time it is wrong, the move goes the right way, and it is a little less wrong the next time.
By the end of the summer the crystal was sorting better than the children of Ambrook. It could tell a pear from an apple by the stalk, which Una had not even told it to look at. It had found the stalk by itself, by being wrong about everything else.
That is the whole lesson. The crystal learns from what it is shown. Each wrong answer changes it a little. Be patient, and keep the changes small.
Quin
%%
Letter: Emmet to Hara
Dear Hara,

The mason came from Copperhold with a hammer, a coil of rope, and a very small lunch. He found a crack above the well mouth. He says the water has fallen low, but the crack made the bucket come up dry sooner. He will mend it when the stone is dry.

I asked the big mind if I should dig beside the well. It said not to dig without checking where the old channels run. It did not know where they run on this farm. It told me to ask someone who had seen the old plan.

I found the plan in the chest by the barn. It shows a water line under the north fence, near the birch stump. I will not dig there until the mason comes back. The mind was right to stop me, though it could not see the plan.

The horse is still in your stable. I borrowed her again to carry the mason's tools. I should have asked first. She came home with a sore patch under the strap, and I am sorry. I rubbed it with warm water and gave her an apple.

Please tell me what I owe for the ride. I will bring the horse back before she decides the farm is hers.

Emmet

Letter: Bel to Mab, the lesson

Dear Mab,

I helped my aunt at the hives yesterday. We took only the honey from the full combs. The bees kept working on the other combs. My aunt said a hive should not be left bare, even when the jars are empty.

I wanted to carry the full jar home, but it was too heavy. So I carried the empty jars instead. Aunt said that was useful too. I did not spill one.

I liked your lesson about the oldest room in the Archive. I thought a deep room would be dark, but the light wells bring daylight down. I want to see them again when my mother visits Vell.

Could the class write letters to the people who mend the mill wheel? I think they might like to know we can hear the wheel from our window. It makes a soft sound when it turns.

Bel
%%
Letter: Nella to Oren, the farm lesson
Dear Oren,

The wheat is not ready, though the field looks gold from the road. The farm keeper says it needs more sun. The children say the birds agree, because they have come to eat it.

I read your old letter to them beside the well. I explained that a drill is a set of examples shown to a mind. If the mind is shown the same sort again and again, it may get that sort right. That does not mean it knows how to tend a row or carry a basket.

They wanted to know if the four helpers were children. I said no. They were made of copper and iron, and each had a mind in a crystal. The word apprentice came from the children who watched them work. The helpers did not speak. One moved along a row. One put things in the right bins.

A child asked why a crystal learns. I told her that the old tutors did not know. She looked at the crystal drawing on the board and said, "Then we should keep asking." I think that is a good answer.

I am sending the lesson page back. The corner is wet because the youngest child set it by the water jar. I dried it in the sun. The words are still clear.

Your box is on the high shelf. The lid sticks less now. I rubbed the hinge with a little oil.

Nella
%%
Letter: Cass to Oren, in reply
Dear Oren,

I have read your letter three times, once at the table, once at the well and once in bed. Then I sat down and did as you asked. Here is everything I can write, and what I cannot write I have said so.

Remedies, and how I make them. Honey in warm milk for a sore throat, every night. Crushed leaf in clean wool for cuts, changed each morning. Willow bark in hot water for the aches in the back, and a cold cloth for the head. I have written each one out, with the amounts, which are a little rough, because my hands measure by feel. Tell the clerks not to trust my hands too far.

Illnesses this spring. Three children with fevers in Thaw, all mended. One old man with a cough that would not go, in Sowing, who is now well enough to argue with me about his wall. Two sprained wrists from the lift at the mine gate, which is not my business but was brought to me anyway. Nothing that has not come before, as far as I know. I have written down what I remember of the years before, too, so that you have something to compare with.

The water in my well is the same as always. Cold, a little hard, sweet at the bottom. The well at the mine gate is clear. The river by the bridge runs low, but it did last year too. I have written down the measurements, with the day and the time, so that anyone who comes after can see the difference, if there is one.

Strange things I have been told. Hesk says the stone on the fifth level is warm, and the men agreed not to go lower without a reason. Hesk says he has heard a hum at night, and then he said he did not want that written. I have written it anyway, and put his name to it, as you asked. Bram says the same. Ola says it is the lamp. I have written that as well. Sennet says the ships at Saltreach are good ships, and I believe him. The carters say other things. I have written what they say, and I have marked each one as a rumor, because that is all it is.

I am sending a jar of honey, from the red ribbon hive, and a small carved bee for the top shelf of the Archive. Put the bee where the clerks can see it. It is for their tea, and their long days, and for you, who are always so polite about it.

Now the hard part. Sennet and his wife leave for Saltreach at the start of Melon, and I am sorry to lose them. I have packed my shelves for Hob to keep. I have written everything I know on paper, and the paper is in two boxes, one with me and one with Hob. If one box is lost, the other remains.

I do not know what we are writing for. You do not say, and I do not ask. But I am glad to be asked to write everything down, because it means that someone thinks it is worth keeping, and I am tired of being the only one who remembers what the well tastes like in Thaw.

Write back when you can, and tell me what the clerks say about the bee.

Cass
Healer, the farm above Copperhold

Letter: the lamp keeper to the inn
To the innkeeper of the Halfway Inn, from the keeper of the relay tower on the Long Road.

Dear Ottilie,

I am sorry about the lamp. The glass on the north side cracked in the storm, and the lamp went dark on the third night of Apple.

The new pane has been ordered from Saltreach. It will come up by the next cart, if the road is kind, and a man who likes to talk will come with it, and fit it, and tell me about his cousin.

Until then, the lamp will blink crooked. Please tell the carters it is not a message. It is only the wind.

Yours in lamplight,
the keeper

Letter: Dell to Piper, the orchard news
Dear Piper,
The honey is safe. Mother says she ate the spoon, so the jar is one spoon short. I have said nothing. I am being very good.
The orchard is full of wasps. They sit on the windfalls and get drunk on the cider in the cracks. Father says they are not dangerous when they are drunk. Mother says they are the most dangerous thing in the valley when they are sober, so they must be kept in the drunk state. I do not understand this, but I agree.
The old apple tree by the gate has a grey branch. It has been dead all summer, and it still has three apples on it, which is very rude of it. We will pick them and give them to the carter as a joke.
Jory came to the orchard gate yesterday. He asked for Ida. We said she was in Three Wells. He looked very sad and went away with a sack of flour, which was a wrong thing to do, because we do not sell flour. We sold him the sack anyway. He said thank you twice.
Your friend, Dell

Letter: Fern to Mira
Dear Mira,

I am writing at the stall, between customers, with the eggs in their straw and the flies on the basket. This is my letter for the north market, and I am not going to make it short, because you never make yours short, and I like to answer you in kind.

First the boat. It came home in the evening, late, as all the boats do when the weather has been good and the sea has been greedy. The men put the net on the wall and the children climbed on it. Sal stood at the end of the quay with her hands on her hips and said nothing for an hour, which is how you know she is pleased. Then she said the boat would be called the Fern, and I said it would not, and she said it would be called the Fern anyway, because the net had been good, and nobody argues with the net.

Second, the wedding. You know about the wedding. Everybody knows about the wedding, because Lil made a cake the size of the table and the cook from the relay house has been complaining about it since Thaw. The bride's sister had the red ribbon in her hair, and the groom's father gave a speech that was mostly about the price of salt, and then he cried, and then he asked for the salt to be passed. It was a fine wedding. The stew was not too thin. I checked.

Third, the things I could not say at the wedding. Ansel's wife came to me at the end of the night and asked whether I had heard the north lamp go dark. I said I had. She asked whether I had seen it come back on, and I said I had. She said she had only wanted to know that somebody else had seen it, and that she would sleep better. I went home and slept very badly, and that is the truth of it.

Fourth, the summer. The good weather is holding, the boats are full, the honey is in, and the hens are laying like it is the first day of the world. You should come down for the summer fair. Bring the frog jar, and Tam, and anybody else who wants to shout at the sea. There is a great deal of noise on the wall in the afternoons, and I like to be in the middle of it.

Fifth, and last, the thing I wanted to ask you. Would you keep an egg for me in the north relay? Only one. In the box with the lamp oil, where the dry things are kept. I want to see whether it still hatches in a year, and I do not trust myself to keep it.

Your friend,
Fern
Egg seller, Northpoint
%%
Letter: Farmer to Ash
Dear Ash,

The new map came with Pell's cart, and I put it on the table beside the bread. It is clear enough that even my aunt can find the well on it. She says the well should be drawn larger. She likes wells and distrusts small pictures.

I have a small request. Please mark the path from the west bed to the boardwalk. The old path runs through the place where we dry reeds. Since the cough began, Wren has asked us to keep the door clear for her visits. We moved the reed bundles, but now everyone walks through the carrot bed.

The carrots do not like feet. Neither do I.

The cough is mild in our house. My wife has it, and I have not. She says I am lucky. I say I have been sleeping in the barn. She says that is not why. We do not agree, but she has the warm room and I have the barn, so she has won.

A man from Saltreach came by yesterday. He said the big ships were built to take families over the sea. Another traveler said they were built to bring grain back. The man from Saltreach had not sailed in one. The traveler had not seen one close. I asked them both to help carry a sack of potatoes. They found other work.

The field is green and the well rope is sound. The bees are busy at the flowers. I hope the weather holds.

Please bring the map when you come. I will show you where the boardwalk bends. I have a fresh loaf for you, if the dog does not get to it first.

Your neighbor,
Eda
%%
Letter: Pell to Oren
Dear Oren,

I am at the Brown Hen again. The innkeeper says I have worn a mark in the bench with my ledger. I told him the bench should be grateful for such steady company.

Please tell the tutors that I read the little note sent by the Vell relay. I could not tell if a clerk wrote it or the big mind. It said, "The road is wet. Keep your bundles covered." That is good advice from anyone.

They say the reading mind takes each letter in order. It looks back through the letters already read and uses the ones that matter to guess the next. It does not see the whole page at once. The window holds about sixty letters. I asked if it reads the price board that way. The keeper said yes, if the board is sent through the relay.

I am not asking you to send my ledger. It has a page of bad sums, and I am still deciding who to blame.

The Brown Hen has a new cat. It sleeps on the warm ink. I have given up writing after supper.

Pell

Letter: Mira to Hesk, the scarf
Hesk,
The scarf came on the wool cart. It is brown and grey and long enough to go round the tower twice. Lantern wore it for an hour and said it felt like being hugged by a sheep. I have not taken it off since.
Thank you. It is a good scarf. The foxes agree. Sock the fox sat on it all through the afternoon and would not be moved, so I have had to wear the other end.
The snow has come early this year. The stair lamps are lit at every turn, and the wind is loud on the glass. I think of the Deep Works, which must be warm, and I am a little jealous.
Write and tell me how the lift is.
Mira

Letter: Ossa to Tam
Dear Tam,
You asked in your last letter whether I had any news of apprentices. You wrote it in the way you write everything, with a joke and a crumb of mud on the page.
Yes. I have news. Here, in our hamlet near Copperhold, we have our first. It came last week from Hesk, who had it from the tutors at Vell. A copper thing with an iron back and a crystal behind the plate. It stands in the lane in front of my shop and looks at the candles. It does not move unless told. When told, it moves very carefully.
Hesk says the first week is the hardest. It is learning the town. It is learning that a candle is not a stone. It is learning that a dog is not a door. It made a mistake with the dog on the second day, and the dog has not forgiven it.
We had a bet, Fenn and I. I said it would last the autumn. He said a month. The month is over, and it is still here, carrying wax to the cart. I have won a pie. It was a good one.
The odd thing is how soon you grow used to it. On the first day I flinched at its step. On the tenth I said good morning. It did not answer, of course, and it did not need to.
How is Pip? Is the potato field wet? Is Mira well? Tell her the candles I sent for her tower have a long burn, and are made for a long night.
There will be a harvest fair here, in the next month. If you can, come. I will give you a candle and a pie, and you can see the apprentice. It has learned to stack.
Your friend, Ossa

Letter: Dace to the tower keeper
Dear Keeper Brann,

The signal lamp on the tower above Three Wells broke last night in the storm. A pane fell out, and the lamp went dark at the hour of the evening report. We sent word by runner to Vell. I hope the message was clear.

I am writing so that you know it was nobody's fault. The wind was too strong. The keeper here, young Tobias, did all he should. He climbed up in the rain and tied a cloth across the gap. He was soaked to the skin. I gave him soup.

We need a new pane. Perhaps two, in case the wind returns. Can the glass cart from Saltreach come by this road? I would be glad of it.

In the meantime, please tell the north line that Three Wells is well, though dark. The bell is ringing in place of the lamp, at dusk and at dawn. It is a small thing, but it keeps the town company.

With thanks,
Dace
Mayor of Three Wells
%%
Letter: Nessa to Lise
Dear Lise,

Your letter made me smile. The children may picture numbers climbing out of a crystal. Pictures help them begin, if we tell them where the picture ends.

A mind is built in layers. Each layer receives work from the layer before it and adds something of its own. The new work joins the stream through the mind. If the numbers in one layer grow too large, later work can become hard to guide. If they shrink too far, the layer may add almost nothing.

So we bring the numbers back to an even size at each layer. This is a simple rule for keeping the work steady. It does not tell us why a crystal learns. It does not tell us what any one number means. It only helps a deep mind learn without its numbers growing wild.

I learned this from a tutor who kept a slate beside the drill box. She wrote each layer in a row and rubbed the row clean when the figures grew too large. Later tutors found a steadier way. I do not know who first gave the rule its name.

Please let the children make their own picture of the stream. Then ask them to explain what the picture cannot tell them.

I have sent two fresh copies. Keep one dry in the school box.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Dill to Wren, bees swarming
Dear Wren,

You will laugh when you read this. I did not laugh when it happened, but I have had a day to get over it.

Yesterday, in the middle of the afternoon, the bees in my second hive decided to leave. They did not ask. They came out all at once, a great brown cloud, humming like a loom, and went up over the garden wall.

I ran after them. I had a basket in one hand and my hat in the other. I do not know why I took the hat. I was thinking of my head, I suppose.

They settled on the lowest branch of the old pear tree in the lane. A heap of bees the size of a loaf of bread, hanging there, quite calm, as if nothing had happened.

This is what your Moss told me to do, when he passed by on his way to the weir, and he was right. I held the basket under the branch. I gave the branch one good shake. The bees dropped into the basket in a lump. I put a cloth over it and walked home very slowly, like a man carrying a pie.

Now they are in an empty hive, and they seem pleased with it. I have given them a little sugar water and said some nice things.

They only got two stings in me. Both on the arm, both small. You said you had a salve for that. If you have some to spare, I would be glad of it. If not, a wet cloth works.

The honey will be late this year. But we may have a new hive, and a new hive is not a bad thing to get for the price of a hat.

Thank you for sending Moss. He is a good lad, and he knows bees.

Yours,
Dill
%%
Letter: Rowan to Hesk
Hesk,

The copper pans sold before I unloaded them. All twelve. A baker from Three Wells bought six and would not say why. I think she just likes pans.

Your town smells of smoke and the road up is all gravel. I lost a wheel spoke on it. But the sorting shed let me sleep by the bins and a miner gave me tea. I have your iron nails. Two sacks, as promised.

The apprentice here is called Clank. It carried my crates up from the cart without being asked twice. That is twice more than most folk. It walks a bit like a kettle on legs. I like it.

I will come back through in Frost with salt fish from Saltreach. Do you want any?

Rowan

Letter: Mara to Cass
Dear Cass,

Thank you for the boards. The roof is dry now, and the pot is back in the kitchen. I filled it with potatoes before Tam could give it a new job.

I owe you for the work. Take the small sack of carrots by my gate. They are not for your brown horse. He already has a habit of looking into my kitchen when I open the door.

I am sorry about the child. Nel ran off after the market bell and we searched the road, the mill yard, and both fields. I found her behind the hay stack, asleep with a pumpkin beside her. She had gone there to hide from her cousins. She said the pumpkin was her guard.

Her mother was worried, and so was I. Nel is safe, and the cousins have been told not to chase her. I gave her a small apple for being found. She says she found herself.

Please tell the stable folk not to frighten her with talk of wolves. She needs to know to stay near the lamps, not to fear every sound in the hedge.

Mara

Letter: Ossa to the hamlet
Dear neighbors,
There has been a bet. I am not proud of it, but I am telling you, so that nobody can say they were not told.
The bet is between me and Fenn. The question is whether the new apprentice at the mine, the copper one with the squeak, will last the autumn. Fenn says no. I say yes. The stake is a pie, and the pie is Marda's apple, which is the best in the hamlet.
I have been to see the apprentice. It is not a clever thing. It can carry a candle box from the shed to the cart, ninety paces, without dropping it. It can stand at a door. It can come when called. Hesk says that is enough, and that the rest comes later.
I think I will win.
If I do not, I will make Fenn a candle with his own face on it, and he will have to burn it. That is the other stake.
Everyone is welcome to watch. I will sit on the wall and eat a plum.
Ossa, candle maker

Letter: Opal to Carys, what Kester found
Dear Carys,

You asked me in your last letter to explain the note that stopped the pay. I have been meaning to answer for a month. I will do it now, by the fire, while the snow comes down.

You know what a crystal is, and you know Una's way of drilling. A crystal is shown a thing, and answers, and if it is wrong, it is moved a little toward the right answer. After many tries, it sorts apples from pears. Una's neighbors' children were beaten by it. It was a great marvel, and so the tutors kept finding new things for crystals to sort.

Now think of a crystal as a person drawing a line. That is how Kester describes it. The crystal looks at a few marks, and draws one straight line across a board, and says yes on one side and no on the other. For apples and pears this is enough. The apples fall on one side and the pears on the other. When the crystal is wrong, the line is moved a little.

Kester asked a different question. He asked: is there anything that no straight line can divide?

Here is what he found. Two lamps, left and right. You want the crystal to say yes if exactly one lamp is lit, and no if both are lit or neither. Put the four cases on a board as four dots at the corners of a square. The two yes dots are opposite corners. The two no dots are the other corners. No straight line has the yes dots on one side and the no dots on the other. A string cannot do it, and neither can a ruler, and neither can a crystal. Kester drew it on forty boards. He proved it to the tutors.

Then the council did what councils do. They heard that a crystal could not learn one or the other, but not both, and they asked: if it cannot learn that, what can it learn? And they stopped the pay.

That is all of it, Carys. It is a very small thing. A crystal can learn a great deal, but not that. Kester says nobody has yet found a way for a crystal to learn that, and he does not say nobody ever will.

He paid me back the twelve emeralds I lent him for coal. He counted them twice, and tied the packet with red wool. I did not need them. I put them in the jar for the next hard winter. I told him that.

The snow is up to the sill. The cat is in the bread bin. There are three babies due before Thaw, and I am counting them.

Your Opal
%%
Letter: Hob to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

First, the news. Our mare Clover had her foal in the night, a filly, black with a white sock. I sat up in the stable with a lamp and a blanket. She came out slowly and stood in the straw at once, as if she had somewhere to be. I have named her Sparrow. She is already on my nerves.

Second, the question. The relay house at the foot of the tower is nearly done. They tell me it will hold a big mind, in a crystal the size of a loaf. They say it will stand on a stand in the middle, and that around it there will be rows of quickeners. I do not know what a quickener is. I asked a stonemason and he said, "It makes the mind quick." I said, "Quick at what?" He said, "At being a mind."

I am a horse keeper. I understand horses. A horse is quick by being fed. What do you feed a quickener?

Please explain, in plain words, as if I were Sparrow.

Hob

Chronicle: the towers spread
Kept at the Archive at Vell, in the hall of the roads.

In the first years the towers were few. The first tower stood at Vell, and a keeper lived in a house at its foot and lit the lamp at night. Word went from tower to tower by lamp and by letter. A message that took a month by cart took a week by tower, and then less.

By the winter of the twelfth year there were nine towers. By the twentieth year there were eighteen. The crews went out with carts of stone and glass and copper, and the councils paid for the roads that joined them. A tower is the tallest thing for half a day's walk. It has a stair or a ladder of rungs inside, a stair lamp at every turn, torches round the foot, and at the top the signal lamp in its room of glass panes with a copper ring. The keeper walks round the ring at night and keeps the line down to the desk.

The masons say the stone must be true and the glass must be clean. The keepers say the lamp must never go out. The clerks say the letters must be kept. All three are right.

At the foot of every tower stands a relay house for the keeper, and from these houses word runs along the roads. Folk walk to the tower to send a message and pay a copper or a few. A farmer sends word to a market. A healer sends for herbs. A child sends a letter to the Archive and the clerk files it and gives it a number, and the child is proud.

The market at Kettle Ridge asked for a tower and gave the stone from its quarry. The town of Three Wells gave bread and soup to the masons. The orchard village of Ambrook gave apples and honey. The port at Saltreach gave glass from its furnaces. The mining town of Copperhold gave copper for the rings. A tower is built by many hands.

There was a festival on the ridge when the first tower in that country was lit. There was a bonfire and a song. The keeper climbed the stair with a jar of honey in her coat, for her throat, and lit the lamp, and the whole ridge saw it. The children said the lamp was a star come down to help.

Not everyone is glad of the towers. Some say the roads bring strangers and the towers bring news too fast. The councils answer that a road is a kindness and a tower is a neighbor. The archive keeps both letters and lets them stand.

We will keep everything we write.
%%
Letter: Dace to Hanna
Dear Hanna,

You asked me to find out about the tutor who will come to your farm outside Copperhold in the first week of Sowing. I have written to Vell, and I have the answer.

Her name is Marit. She is forty, careful and very exact. She comes to look at your apprentice, the one that sorts the beets. She will test it on a sorting it has not seen. Do not tell it beforehand what she will bring. She will bring her own beets.

She sleeps lightly and likes tea. She does not eat fish, or at least she says so in her letter. I have told her about your dogs.

I hope this helps. Give my greetings to Rolf.

Dace

Letter: Dace to the tutor at Vell
Dear Tutor Kerr,

Your letter about the new apprentice for the hamlet farms came this week.

I have read it twice. I have a question.

You write that the apprentice is ready, that it has been drilled on the east field drill for a month, and that you are confident. I believe you. But I also believe the old rule, which is that a mind must be tested on what it was never drilled on.

You wrote that it has been tested. On what? Please tell me what the test was. Was it a field the apprentice had never seen? Was it a task it had never done? If it was, I will sign the paper at once. If it was the same field it learned on, I will ask for another test.

I do not ask this out of distrust. I ask it because I am a mayor, and mayors are responsible for what they send into the fields. A mind that has been drilled on a thing can seem to know it. A mind that has been tested on a thing it never saw has shown that it knows.

You told me this yourself, years ago, over a glass of cider, when you came to speak at the school. I wrote it on the back of my hand and kept it for a week.

Send me the test and I will sign.

With respect,
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

P.S. The hamlet has had a creeper. One wall of the barn is gone. If the apprentice is as strong as you say, it might help rebuild.

Letter: Gault to Mira, the north fence
Mira,

You know the fence on the north road. The one that runs from the foot of the relay tower to the old oak. Letter: Hesk to Ivo, the shaft head
Ivo,

Send the cider up with the carts on the next run. Twelve barrels, the sharp ones, not the sweet. The men want something to wash the coal out of their mouths. The sweet makes them sing, and the foreman does not allow singing on the shaft stair.

The shaft head is ready for the spring work. We have new props in the stope, and the rails are clear to the second level. The count board is clean. Some fool wrote SNOW on it in chalk, and the cook wants him made to sweep the kitchen. That was Dorrit's doing, I think, and I have not said so to her.

The copper on the second level is good this spring, and the iron is better than last year. The coal near the sorting shed is poor, so I have had the boys break it small and sell it to the forge at a loss. The forge wants it that way, and the forge is never grateful.

Send two new ropes with the carts too. The old ones are fine for a man, but they do not like a basket of ore, and neither do I.

The tutors sent us an apprentice from Vell in the last thaw. It is called Clank, which I did not choose. It carries ore from the shaft head to the sorting shed, two baskets at a time, up the stair and down again. It knows every step. It does not stop for the bell. The men say that is because it cannot hear it. I think it can hear it, and it does not care.

I will say this once, so you can tell the others in the barn. Clank has been right every time I have checked its count. Forty baskets, forty right. It does not talk. It stands where it is put, and it waits for the next basket the way a man waits for his dinner. I did not expect to like it. I do.

Keep your barrels sealed. The spiders are welcome in the barn, if they keep the wasps out of the bung holes.

Hesk

P.S. Tell Dorrit the snow was her fault. She knows.

Letter: Mira to Tam
Tam,

The first snow came early this year. It came in the night and did not stop. When I opened the tower door this morning the drift was up to my waist. Lantern had to dig a path to the relay house. He dug it well. He is proud of the path. Do not tell him I said so.

I have been thinking about the lights. I know I said I would not write of them again. I lied. They came back three nights past. They were green and low, and they did not blink like stars. Lantern watched them a long time. Then he said they were looking at us. I told him they were falling stars and he should go to bed. I said it in a sharp voice. He went.

I do not think they were falling stars. I do not think they were fires. I do not know what they were. They were not ours. That is all I will say.

Enough of that. How is the beetroot? Is the long field done? Is Pip keeping the ditch clear? You never write about the ditch. I care about the ditch.

The foxes are bolder than usual. One came right up to the tower foot and sat by the torches. It looked at me like I owed it something. I gave it a bit of fish and it ate it and went. Now it comes every night. I suppose I have a fox now.

My hands are cold. That is not news. That is just my hands.

Write soon. Tell me about the ditch.

Mira

P.S. Do not let Pip sleep late. You slept late when you were his age and the hens got into the barn.

Letter: Tam to Mira
Dear Mira,

The spring market is over, and Pip has not lost the key to the grain shed. This is good news. He has lost my red glove, but I have found it in the flour bin, so I count that as an improvement.

I went to the market hall to hear the Vell tutors. They had a reading mind on a stand, with a crystal in a wooden case. They showed it old records from the Archive. One tutor covered the next line and asked what words might follow. The mind made a guess. Then they showed the line, and the tutor marked whether the guess was right.

They said the mind learns from many pages. It does not keep one lesson for each page. It changes its weights a little as it sees examples, so later guesses may improve. The tutor said this takes a long time and needs clean copies. A torn page or a copy of the same page again and again can make poor teaching.

I asked whether they had used my letter about the broken windmill. They laughed, but would not tell me. I hope they did not use the part where I blamed the wind. The wind was innocent.

I am sending you a small sack of beetroot seed. Keep it dry at Northpoint.

Your brother,
Tam

Letter: Tam to Mira, the sea
Dear Mira,

I am at the sea. I have a great deal to tell you and not much paper.

It is bigger than I thought. It does not stop. You stand on the stones and it comes at you, and goes away, and comes at you again, as if it forgot something.

The hamlet is called Sandhook. Nine houses, a rack for drying fish and a path down to the boats. I am staying with Fen and Nell, who fish. Nell put three kinds of fish in front of me before I sat down. My bed is in the net loft. It smells of fish. So do I.

I came for kelp. The ridge says it makes potatoes grow big, and I want big potatoes. The cart is half full already. Sorrel the mare does not like the smell.

I took off my boots to wade. A wave took one sock. Now I know how you feel about Sock the fox.

Pip has the fields. Hal next door will look in on it.

Write soon. Say hello to Lantern.

Your brother,
Tam

Letter: Garrow to Ree, the cheese prize
Dear Ree,

I have been asked by the market board to write to you about the cheese prize, which I am told you have not yet forgiven. I have not forgiven you either, and the board is not interested in either of our opinions.

The judges sat in the afternoon, when the cheese had been in the sun since the morning. That is the sun's fault, and the judges' for sitting in it. Your blue cheese was the best in the square, and I will say so in public, as long as you do not ask me to say it in front of your sister.

My cheese came second, which is the honest place for a cheese cut too early. I had the knife in my hand for an hour before the judging. The knife was sharp. I was not. I am sorry for that.

But the judges disagreed about yours. The first said it was too sharp. The second said it was too soft. The third said it was perfect, and then asked whether there was any more of it, and took the last piece home in his hat. A cheese that is perfect for one man is a judging problem for the other two.

I propose that next summer each cheese is cut in four, and each judge takes one quarter, and nobody takes any home in a hat. The board has agreed, provided we pay for the knives.

Also, the dog. The mayor's dog has been seen at my cold room door three times this week, wearing your ribbon, looking at the door. I believe he has earned the cheese. I am not giving him any. You can deal with the dog yourself.

Come to the cold room on the next market day, and bring the good salt. We will settle the matter over the last of my cheese, which the dog has not found.

Yours, in cheesemaker's rivalry,
Garrow

P.S. Your cheese was the best. I have said it. I will not say it again, and if you repeat it, I will deny it on the market board.

Letter: Ivo to Tam, the drill boards
Tam,

The hall at Vell is shut. The council wrote at the end of Leaffall to say the tutors are no longer needed. They were kind about it. They gave us a week's pay and a handshake.

I packed what I could carry. Six drill boards, and the field board with its small brown field drawn on it in chalk. The tray of crystals, each one on its card: Farm. Build. Haul. Come. You know the tray. Hollis took the cards and I took the tray. We both think we won.

Keep the boards dry. Do not let Crumb use them for kindling. I doubt it would know it was being rude.

I have sold my house on Vell Stair to a baker with three children. The children were climbing my drill yard fence when I left. That is the first sign of a good tenant I have ever seen.

I will sleep in your barn until Thaw. Then I will build a hut behind the mill, if the mill allows it. It squeaks. I heard it from the road, and it squeaked at me.

I know a tutor should not fuss over a council. I am fond of the boards anyway. Every child in that hall learned the field drill on them, the bad ones and the good ones. The good ones were not always the ones you would guess.

Keep some bread for me. I will bring the cheese.

Ivo, once a tutor, now a barn guest

Letter: Piper to Dell, the long autumn
Dear Dell,
I have been at the Halfway for a month, and there is nothing to tell, which is how I know I am happy.
The fleece is carded. The carder's bench is clean. The lint is in the bread. The second bowl has gone to a boy and a dog and a trader who said he would never come back, and then came back for the third time, and I did not charge him for the bowl or the lesson.
The hearth is low and red. Travelers sit round it all evening and talk about everything, the price of barley, a bridge in the north, a lamp that blinked in a strange way over Lowmarsh one night. Nobody knows what it meant. Everyone has an idea. I keep quiet about my idea, because I do not have one.
At night I walk out to the gate and look up the Long Road. It is very dark. Then I come back and sit by the hearth with my hands in my lap, and the wool smells of the sheep, and the stew is still warm.
Come and visit me before the frost. Bring the honey. Bring yourself, and do not bring any singing.
With love, from the bench,
Piper

Letter: Dace to Mira, thank you
Dear Mira,

Your warning about the river came down the line in the middle of the night, and I want to thank you for it. It said: river rising, move the stalls. We moved the stalls. Three hours later the river was in the square. Nobody lost a pumpkin.

I have read so many messages from you over the years that I feel I know your handwriting, which is odd, since I have never seen it. I know your lamp, which is better. At the end of a long day I look up at the hill and think: somewhere north of there, someone is up a ladder in the snow so that I can have a warning. I wish more mayors thought of that.

The autumn fair is in Apple. I wish you would come. I know you cannot leave the lamp. But if you could come for one day, I would put you at the head of the table, and I would let you say anything you liked about the beetroot contest. You may say that Tam's beetroot is smaller than his head. I will not read it out.

All of Three Wells sends its thanks. So does the third well, which is the wishing well, and has been given a good many wishes for you.

Your friend,
Dace

You asked if I was lonely at Lowmarsh. I am not lonely. I am only tired, which is different. The water comes up under the boardwalk every night now, and the boats need bailing before the first light. Half of the village is on the planks with a bucket.

Wren

Letter: Nessa to Oren, on being wrong
Dear Oren,

You say that the test box is a memory. That is wrong, though you wrote it very well, and I laughed at the tea.

A memory is what a mind keeps of the drill. It is what the mind can say back to us. The test box is not that. The test box holds the questions the mind has never met, and so it is the only place we learn whether the mind has learned anything at all.

The clerks say this in the hall every year. They do not always understand it. I have stopped trying to explain it with long words, because the apprentices understand it better than the clerks do. They do not care what a page is about. They only care whether it is right.

Here is how I explain it to the new tutors, and you may copy it out for the clerks, slowly, so they do not spill tea on it.

First, drill the mind until it is right on the pages it knows. This takes a long time. Each time it is wrong, it changes a little. Each time it is right, it changes less. That is how it gets better.

Second, show it a page it does not know. Not a page that is a little different. A page that asks the same idea in another way. If the mind is right there too, it has learned the idea. If it is wrong there, it has only learned the pages.

Third, do not let the two kinds of page mix. The drill pages go in the drill. The test pages go in the box. If a test page is shown in the drill, it is no longer a test, and we have lost one of our few honest pages.

Fourth, write down the day you use a test page. Then never use it twice. A mind that has seen a page once knows it, and a known page tells us nothing.

The contests between the halls work the same way. Two tutors each drill a mind, and both minds are tested on the same unseen pages from the box. The better mind wins, and the tutor who drilled it gets the blame for the losers. It has made the tutors honest. It has made them careful with their pages, too, since everyone wants to win.

Keep the box locked. Keep the key with the tutor. Keep the spoiled pages out of the drill. And when you are finished being proud of a good drill, go and check the test box. It is always the test box that tells you the truth.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Dell to Hesk
Hesk,

My furnace has been running cold since the spring. You know why. The sand is poor. It came up from the shore in a cart with the bottom half wet, and the glass it makes is grey and thick, like the water in a rain barrel. I have blown three panes this week and every one has clouded.

I have asked the Saltreach carter for better sand. He says the sand is the same as always. He says the sea is not what it was. He says the fishers are catching less, and the sand is less clean, and the shore is full of a black crust that nobody will explain.

I do not know what to believe. I only know my glass is not right, and the lamps on the north road cannot go on with grey glass. Mira wrote to say the lamp room at Northpoint has a crack, and she has nothing to mend it with but tape.

Please send me the best sand you have, from the far bay, where the rocks are. If there is none, send me the old bags from the store. I will sift them myself.

And tell Mira I am sorry about the crack. Tell her I will blow her a pane with no bubbles at all, if I have to stay up all summer to do it.

Dell
Glassworks, the house at the edge of Copperhold

Letter: Nessa to Hobb, from the road
To Hobb, at the Halfway Inn, from Nessa, tutor of apprentices at Vell, who is on her way back from Three Wells with a box of drills and a sore back.

Dear Hobb,

Thank you for the room. It was the quietest I have had in two years, which is a compliment, I think, since your kitchen is very loud at night. I did not mind the draught under the door. I minded only the cat, which sat on my box of drills for an hour and would not move. I was impressed by its judgment. It knew exactly which pages were the useful ones.

Thank you for the honey, too. It came with the second bowl, which I did not order, and which was very good. You said it was from Ambrook. I have had Ambrook honey before, and this was darker, and it tasted of the apple blossom on the hill. I have asked the mill house about it. They say the bees are very proud this year.

The drills are going well. The new mind at the hall learns the reading drill faster than any I have taught. It is shown a page, and it guesses the next word, and most of the time it is wrong. Then, after a long run of drills, it is right almost every time. The test box says it has learned, not only remembered. I am pleased, and a little frightened, and I have not decided which is larger.

When I come through next spring, I would like the room by the stove, if it is free. I will bring the drills, and I will leave the cat in the stables, where it belongs. Keep the riddle board in good order. I will try it again, and this time I will not shout the answer.

With thanks, and with my back much improved,
Nessa

Letter: Innkeeper Sella to Hob
Dear Hob,

Thank you for trimming the lamps on the road. The one beside my gate was smoking, and now it burns clean.

Your cap is dry. I put it on the bench by the door, away from the stew pot. A fox came close to the yard while you slept. It sniffed the cap and went away. I think it knew the cap was yours.

The latch on the wood store is still loose. I have put a peg beside it. Please do not use the little hammer with the bent handle. It is my good hammer, and it is not bent. That is what you said last time.

Pell left three sacks of carrots here for the next cart. I have put them in the cool room. He also left a jar of honey for you. He says it is from Ambrook and that you should not give it all to me.

I will set out bread for your road.

Sella
Halfway Inn

Letter: Tam to Corin
Dear Corin,

Your apples were from Ambrook, not Kettle Ridge. I sent you two sacks of beetroot, and Pell says you gave one sack to the cook. He also says you asked for more.

I have heard that the tutors put words in places inside a crystal. Pip says the word for wheat sits beside the word for bread. I asked him how he knew. He pointed at the drill card, then carried it away before I could read it.

We are gathering the last beetroot now. The windmill squeaks even when there is no wind. I have put a stone by its door as a reminder to oil the wheel.

Your brother in grain,
Tam
%%
Letter: Isla to Pell
Dear Pell,

Your cart is still stuck by the west turn, and the wheel is still sitting in the mud. I know the mud is deep. I also know you have been saying, "It will come free after lunch," since yesterday.

I sent Tam and two neighbors with oak boards. They put the boards under the wheels. Then the cart sank farther and held the boards tight. We all pushed. The horse watched us with the calm look of a teacher waiting for a wrong answer.

Please unload the sacks of carrots before trying again. They are making the cart heavier, and the carrots will not help push. I have a pot ready for the cracked ones. If you leave them in the mud, I shall take them as payment for the soup.

The wind has turned cold. We tied a blanket over the honey jars, and none broke. One jar did roll into your boot. I have set it by the inn door. Do not put your foot in it.

Come to the hamlet kitchen when the cart is free. There is hot soup and a dry place by the stove.

Isla
%%
Letter: Ree to Sella
Dear Sella,

The house at the edge of Northpoint has stopped leaking. I put a flat stone over the loose roof board, and the rain now falls beside the bed instead of on it.

I borrowed your horse to bring the stone up from the store. She carried it well. She also ate the green scarf from my pack. I am sorry. I can mend the scarf, but I cannot mend the horse's opinion of it.

Please tell your boy I will bring the scarf back in spring. I will bring two apples too, if the cart has any.

Ree
%%
Letter: Pell to his sister at Ambrook
Dear Ness,
A long letter, because the road is long and the evenings are slow.
I have come back through Three Wells. The market was thin. Half the stalls were empty, and the stallholders said they were gone to Saltreach, to see the ships. Not to buy. To look. Corin at the shipyard is building them bigger than any boat we have seen. Three times as long, they say. Families are talking of going over the sea.
I do not know what is across the sea. Nobody does. A woman at the Brown Hen says it is more land. A man says it is more sea. A boy says it is where the lights come from, and was told to hush.
I cannot say what I think. I think it is a long way to go for a rumor.
At the Halfway Inn, Hobb's hearth is still lit. It has not gone out in twenty years. I sat by it last night and thought of home, and Mother's kitchen, and the smell of the apples. I have decided to come in the autumn and stay a while.
The spiders in the barn at the Hen are enormous. They have made a web from the rafter to the door. Marda says it is a good sign. I said, of what? She said she did not know.
If my last letter was lost, I will write it again. It was about the price of honey. It was not very interesting.
Give my love to Mother. Tell her I am eating.
Your brother, Pell
%%
Letter: Tobin to Ruby, the fox
Dear Ruby,
The fox came back. You will not believe where it went. It went into the hen house, past the broom, past the sleeping cat, and out again with the speckled hen under its arm. The cat did not even wake. I have said before that the cat is useless. Now I have said it in writing.
I chased the fox round the boardwalk three times. It did not hurry. It stopped at the end of each post to look at me, the way a clerk looks at a late note. Then it jumped the water and was gone into the reeds.
The speckled hen is fine. She is in the box by the stove, cross, and laying again. I think the fox frightened her into an egg.
Bring the good broom when you come on Sowing day. And bring a sack of dried fish, for the fox. If we are kind to it, maybe it will leave the hens alone and only take the cat.
Tobin
%%
Letter: Greer to Sanna
Dear Sanna,

The autumn fair is in six days. I have made forty rounds of cheese and I want to bring all of them to Copperhold. My cart is small and my old grey horse is slower than he thinks.

Will you lend me your cart? I will bring it back with a full wheel of cheese in it, and I will not count the wheel as payment. The cheese is a gift. The cart is a loan. These are different things, and I am told I mix them up.

The fair is in the square by the shaft head. There will be a pie stall and a fiddler and the miners will come up for the day. Hesk says the whole mine stops for the fair. He says even the apprentices stop, and they do not know what a fair is until someone tells them.

Bring your children. There will be honey cake. I am told there will be honey cake. If there is not, I will be very upset.

Greer
%%
Letter: Pell to Ambrook, the long winter
Dear Mother and Father,
I write from the Halfway Inn, where the snow has been on the roof for nine days and the fire has not gone out once. Hobb has kept it going with the spare wood from the barn and a great deal of swearing. I am told the swearing is the reason the fire is still alive.
The mill wheel is mended. It turns, though it squeaks on every turn, as if it has a complaint and is not yet ready to say it. The new spokes are a little lighter than the old ones, so the wheel runs faster in the race, and the miller says the flour is finer. I have tasted it. It is finer. It is also greyer, and Hobb says that is the cold in the grain.
I want to tell you about the winter road, because I do not think you will believe it otherwise.
The carts have stopped coming up from the south. The last one came in Frost, with a load of salt and no driver, only a horse that knew the way. It walked into the yard of the inn and stood by the trough and waited for its feed, as if it had not noticed anything strange. The horse is still here. It is named Mud. It eats very well and it will not go back out.
A family came up from the coast in the second week of the snow. Four children, two grown women and an old man on a sledge. They had walked the Long Road from Saltreach in the cold, and they had three carts of goods between them, and they had not been paid for the carts. They stayed in the stable. Marda gave them the big room upstairs and cooked them soup every night for a week.
I asked the eldest woman why they were leaving the coast. She looked at me for a long time. Then she said, "The fishing was poor this year. The boats were sold, so we came inland to find work." I did not ask her more. I think she was tired of saying it.
The children play in the snow every morning and then come in and sit by the fire in a row. They have stopped asking when they can go home. I think they like the fire and the noise of the inn, and that is a good sign, and I am glad to give them more soup.
Hobb says the road will open in Thaw. Marda says it will open in Sowing. The horse says nothing, and I am inclined to believe the horse.
I am keeping a ledger of everything we give away. It is the only honest book I have kept this year. When the snow is gone I will bring it home and you can read it, and you will see how much soup a family of six can eat in a week.
Your son,
Pell
%%
Letter: Hob to Mave
Dear Mave,
Thank you for the barley. It came in good order, dry and sweet. Marda cooked a pot with it that very night, and every traveler on the road had two bowls, and one of them asked for a third, and was given the leftover pot.
The mice have been at the bread bin. I know you do not believe in mice, because you say they are sensible creatures that would never eat a whole loaf. I found a loaf with half gone and a small grey tail in the flour. The tail was not sensible.
The cat is not allowed in the store. It is allowed on the bench by the hearth, which is much warmer, and where it can keep an eye on the bread.
Please send the honey as soon as it is ready. We are down to three jars and a little cloud of ants.
Hob, keeper of the Halfway
%%
Letter: Lantern to Mira, the lamp
Mira,

The glass in the lamp room is clean. I did it at dawn, before the wind came up. The copper ring is green again from the top down, the way it always goes, and I rubbed it with the old cloth and a little vinegar. It shines now, for a day.

I have a question I did not dare ask at supper. When the big mind speaks through the relay, the words come out in a line on the desk and I read them. Last night I read a line I did not understand. It asked whether we had seen the lamps to the north. It said they had been dark three nights. I looked out of the window. The lamps at the next tower are not dark. I looked again, and they are not dark.

I did not write back. I put the question in the book and went to bed.

Tell me what I should do. I will do it. I only do not like to be told one thing by the mind and another by the window.

Lantern
Northpoint, the lamp room, the desk on the stair

Letter: the carter to his wife
To Marra, at the house at the foot of the ridge, from Dunn, on the Long Road, in the cold.

Dear Marra,

We came through the Halfway Inn three days ago, and I wanted to write before the next cart goes, so you will know I am alive.

The lamp on the tower above the inn has been out, and the whole road has been talking about it. The innkeeper has put candles in every window and sold most of them at a good price. The keeper of the tower wrote her a letter of apology, in his best hand. I think it was the best letter I have ever seen a keeper write.

On the second night the fog came down so thick that the mule walked into the ditch twice. Then a small boy from the inn came out with a lantern and walked beside us to the bridge, and did not say a word the whole way. He only held the lantern up high, so we could see the planks. I gave him a bit of bread. He ate it in one bite and went back.

I am thinking of the house, and the stove, and your mother's soup.
Keep the lamp lit at home. I know you will.

Your husband,
Dunn

Minutes: the Three Wells spring council
Minutes of the spring council, held at the mayor's house. Dace in the chair. Nine present.

1. The wedding. Rilla and Tom are to be married at the east well on the third of Sowing. Benches from the school. Cake from the bakery, honey from Ambrook. Oren of Vell will come and bring a book for the table. Bel carries the ring. The cat is not invited. Bel asked twice. The council says no to the cat.

2. The north road. Two waymarks are down past the crossroads. Stones to be cut and set before Sowing. Pell will haul them.

3. The relay. The lamp at the tower above the mill pond blinked wrong twice this month. Lantern at Northpoint says it is the cold and not the tower. We will watch it.

4. The school. The teacher asks for a primer on how the big minds read now, in pieces of words and not letters. The council will write to Vell. Nessa has promised a short one.

5. The cat. The council returns to the cat. It sat on the notice board and knocked down the notice about the well. Dace will speak to Bel. The council notes that the cat will not stay out.

6. The well. The east well is low. Everyone is to draw from the middle well until Sowing.

Meeting closed. The next is the first of Swarm.

Letter: Dell to Oren, a lost letter
Dear Oren,

I know I am late. I know you are busy with the move, and I know you hate letters that come in the wrong place. But something is wrong, and you are the only clerk who would know what to do.

I sent you a letter in the last week of Reaping. It was a long one. It had the drill notes from the farm school, the full set, twelve pages, with the answers for the apprentices written in green ink so they would stand out.

You have not had it. I asked at the post at Three Wells. The post clerk said they gave it to the cart for Vell. The cart driver said he gave it to the house at the top of the hill. The house at the top of the hill says there is no house there, only the new copying hall and a lot of boxes.

So it is in a box, or in the road, or in a bag at the bottom of the cart. I do not know which, and I am sorry.

Please look. If it is in a box, you will know which one. The boxes are labeled. Your handwriting is very bad, so I have no idea how the labels will read to anybody else, but you will read them.

If it is in the road, I am sorry for the road.

Dell
