Letter: Wren to Tam
Dear Tam,

I owe you a letter, and a cloth, and a bowl of Lowmarsh eel, which you once said was too good to be true. Here are the letter and the eel. The cloth is in the box.

The summer has been long and wet. The water came up under the boardwalk in the middle of the month and stayed there. The children were allowed to play in the shallow end, under a rope, with a grown person on the post. The boats were tied up for four days. Idris rowed the ferry to the far side and back, nine times, with a sack of flour on his knees, and only dropped it once, in the reeds, where the frogs took the first bite.

I want to tell you about the apprentices, because Hela has talked me half to death about them, and I cannot stop thinking about them.

Hela says the best of the new minds can take a whole sentence now. She says it is the next thing, and that it is like learning to read a word after a lifetime of letters. She has been at Vell since the spring and she has seen the clerks' sheets, and she says the Vell tutors are very proud. She says they will not say who was first. Each hall says it was theirs, and the scrolls disagree, and they are all very cross about it.

I do not care who was first. I care about Moss. Moss does not understand whole sentences. Moss understands the first word, and then a second word if I am patient. It has learned the field of four, and the sort of jars, and the weir. It does them well. It stands still in the water for hours and it has never once been wrong about the weir. But it has no idea what I mean when I say, "Take the good eel home, and be careful with the lid, and tell Pell the price is up." It takes the eel. It drops the lid. And it does not tell Pell anything, because it does not know what to say.

Hela says that is what the big minds are for. The small ones are only the hands. The ones at the relay houses can write back.

I asked her whether a small mind could ever learn to say it did not know. She looked at me for a long time. Then she said, "Wren, you are asking me to teach a fish to whistle."

So I will keep Moss. It is a good hand. And when it does not know, I will say so for it.

Give my love to Mira and tell her the fox can have the scarf back, if it can bring me the spare wool.

Your sister, in the reeds,
Wren
%%
Letter: Gwen to Mira
Dear Mira,

Spring has reached Kettle Ridge. The top field is green, and the windmill squeaks on every turn. I am staying at the house at the edge of town while Tam mends its fence. He says he will finish after supper. He has said this three times.

A fox came for the hens before dawn. It slipped through the gap by the oak post and took one. Tam blames the post. I blame Tam. We have put the other hens in the shed until the fence is sound.

The talk at the inn is all about the largest relay houses. One traveler said the great house at Vell holds more writing than the Archive. Oren would laugh at that, but the house is said to be larger than the town. Its mind has a crystal far bigger than any tutor's bench can hold. Quickeners stand around it, kept cool with water and ice.

No one here knows how the mind works inside. Nessa says a large mind is still built from layers. Each layer adds what it found to the stream through the mind. The words sit as places, and words used alike sit close together. The mind reads a page a piece at a time and guesses what comes next. A bigger crystal can hold more weights, and more writing can give those weights more examples. That is what the tutors can say plainly.

What it can do is less plain. One keeper told Tam that it had read a whole town's records and found every missing name. Another said it answered a question before the letter was sent. Tam believes both. I believe the keeper may have mixed one tale with another. The council's own test pages are kept apart, so a mind does not see them while it learns. Even then, a mind that large can give more answers than one tutor can check.

We have no such mind at the ridge. Pip's small crystal can bring bread, fetch a tool, and stay by the gate. We test it with a basket it has never seen. It still mistakes potatoes for beetroot when the light is low. Tam says this is because the shed is dim. I say the lamp needs oil.

The fence is nearly mended. Tam has set the post straight this time. He says the old post leaned because the wind wanted it so. I have written this down in case the Archive needs proof.

Your friend,
Gwen
%%
Letter: Pell to Hazel, from the road
Hazel,

I am at the Halfway Inn, by the hearth that has not gone out in twenty years. The innkeeper says so, and I believe him. My feet are warm for the first time since spring.

The cart lost a wheel on the hill above the stream. Not a big wheel. A small wheel that does big work. A carter called Osk helped me lift the cart. He sang the whole time, very badly. We had the wheel back on by dark. Osk's horse pulled us up the last bit, and the horse did not like me.

On the way out I crossed the bridge at Ambrook, the one with the loose plank. Clack. Jory was on the bank with his bucket. He said the mill wheel had turned from dawn to dusk all week. I said nobody ever complains about a mill wheel.

Now the riddle. The board here is new. It says: What has teeth, but cannot bite? Everyone who passes says comb. The innkeeper says card. We have argued since supper. The fire is low, and the argument is not. I say comb. You will say card. Tell me before the next cart.

There is stranger news. A carter from the north road says the lamp on the hill by the Brown Hen talked all night. Another says it was a bird. A third says the wind on that hill has learned to blink. I think the third carter had too much cider. I think the lamp was only tired.

Tell Sorrel the lamp is not hers to climb. Tell her I know she wants to. Tell her to count the stairs at the inn instead.

The big cards are in the back of the cart, under the sacks of wheat. Under the wheat, Hazel, not on top. I did not forget the stool. It is strapped to the roof, and it is very annoyed with the road.

Love,
Pell
%%
Letter: Oren to Bel, why a mill creaks
Dear Bel,

Your letter came on the cart from Three Wells, with a smell of straw. The clerks all asked what it was. I said it was the ridge. It was.

You ask why a windmill creaks. I have never seen one closer than from the hill above Vell, on a clear day. So I did what a clerk does. I asked three people and read two books. Here is what I found.

A windmill is a tall house with four great arms on the front, called sails. The wind pushes the sails round. The sails turn a big wooden shaft. The shaft turns a round flat stone inside the house, and that stone turns on another stone beneath it. You pour the wheat in between. The stones grind it. What comes out is flour.

Everything in a mill is wood or stone, and wood rubs on wood. Where the great shaft turns in its bearing, the wood is heavy and the wood is dry. It creaks. A little oil makes it quieter. It does not make it quiet. A mill with no creak is a mill with no weight on its shaft, and so a mill that is not grinding.

So when Sefa said yes to your three questions, she was right about all three. A dry shaft is the mill asking for oil. Hungry is the same as asking for wheat. And sad? A mill that creaks is working. I do not think it can be sad, but it was a good thing to ask.

There is more. I wrote to Sefa, and she answered in one line: "The creak tells me the wind." She can lie in bed and hear whether the wind is west or north, a gale or a breeze, by the pitch of the creak. If a mill goes silent in the night, the whole ridge wakes and goes out to look. So the creak is not only the mill's voice. It is the ridge's clock.

Now to the other matter. Nessa came to the Archive yesterday with a letter from the young tutor, Cress. She did not know I had your letter. She read me the part about the bowls. I told her who you were. She said, "Of course it was." Your idea is in the tutors' book now, and the margin says, "Bel of Three Wells." I have put it in the finding book as well, under minds, testing, number 41,207.

Do you know what you did? A tutor gave a clever mind a clever test, and it failed. You did not tell the tutor she was wrong. You asked a small question. "Why not tip some from each sack into a bowl?" That is the best kind of question. It does not say what is wrong. It tries a way out.

I tell the new clerks that writing a thing down is half of keeping it. The other half is asking. You are good at both. Please keep your letters coming, even the ones about sheep.

Say hello to Biscuit. Tell her I am sorry about the mitten.

Oren
Clerk at the Archive at Vell
%%
Letter: Wren to Fenn, the new mind
Dear Fenn,

Dunstan's arm is knitting well. He asked me to tell you he is not bruised, only blue. Keep him out of the hay loft for three more weeks and he will forgive you.

I have read your letter twice. Then I took out Oren's letter to me, because your question about the card needs the whole answer, not only the number. Here it is in my own words.

A mind learns from pages, the way a healer learns from patients. The pages are drilled one after another. Each time the mind gets one wrong, it changes a little. Given enough good pages, it gets better. But that only works if the pages are good.

So the clerks at Vell sort the pages before any mind sees them. They take out copies, because a page seen twenty times is learned by heart, and a mind that has memorized one page has learned nothing about the others. They take out spoiled pages, the ones with a wrong word or a line out of place, because a bad page teaches a bad lesson. What is left is the drill.

Then they keep a test box. It holds pages no mind is ever drilled on. After the drilling is done, the mind is tested on the test box, and the count of right answers goes on the card. That count is the only honest number about the mind. The drill tells you what a mind has been shown. The test tells you what it has learned.

Now the rule Oren made me promise to write down. If a page sits in the test box and also in a drill, the mind passes the test by remembering. That is a cheat, and it makes a card that lies.

Your card says ninety-six right in a hundred on the test box. That is a fair mark for a gate mind. It means the gate will be right nearly every time. It does not mean every gate, every time. Ninety-six is not a promise.

So my advice is the same as Oren's, and I think you will like it less than you like the numbers. Watch the gate in the rain. Watch it in the wind, which is when your storm came. Write down each time it gets the gate wrong, and send the list to Vell. Then the next mind will be drilled on better pages, and it will be tested on pages that nobody has seen.

Moss sorts the jars on my shelves by the labels now. Moss has stopped sorting them by smell.

Write when the hens lay again. Give the spiteful two my regards.

Yours,
Wren
%%
Notice: market at Vell, third of Sowing
To all who bring goods to the Vell market:

The stalls open at first light on the third of Sowing. Set up in your usual row. Do not set up in the road. The carts need to pass.

Rules, as always:

Horses and carts go in the east field. The stable hands will show you where. Do not tie a horse to the notice board. The board is for notices, not for halters. Last spring someone tied a mule to it and the mule ate the parish list of road repairs. That list has not been replaced and the roads have not been repaired. We see a connection.

Wool, clay, honey and coins change hands at your own risk. Count your coins before you leave. The seamstress has a good eye and a short memory.

Children under the age of ten must be with an adult or near the school gate. The wells are deep. The bells are loud. The wells are very deep.

No one sells spoiled apples. Ada the baker is watching. She has a long spoon.

The market closes at dusk. Take your rubbish home. Take your horse home too.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells
%%
Letter: Mira to Gault
Gault,

Lantern has no level. It has a box of stones and a steady hand, which is better, but it is not a level. I have told it so. It has agreed. It has also measured the fence, twice, and it has told me the number.

I am not going to tell you the number. I am going to tell you that the fence leans toward the north side of the road, by about the width of a finger on every post, which is the way the wind leans it. The fence was not moved. The fence was put where it was put. The post by the oak is the one that has walked.

Harrow will not like this, and he will say the apprentice is biased, and he will be right to say it, because the apprentice likes the north side of the road better than the south. It always has. It likes the lamp.

I will send Lantern down with the stones. You will see the number for yourself. Then you can both be cross together, which is how neighbours should be.

Mira
Keeper, the north relay

Letter: Tam to Mira, the storm
Dear Mira,

I have been in a storm. I am not hurt. I say that first so you can breathe.

It came on the afternoon after I wrote to you. The sky went the color of a bruise. By evening the sea was throwing itself at the shingle and the rain came sideways. Nell took in the washing. Fen took in the nets. Then Fen looked at the water and went quiet, because his boat, the Patient, was still out.

All of Sandhook went down to the shore. We tried torches, and the wind blew them out. We tried lanterns, and the wind shook them like a wolf with a rabbit. Then Nell said, "The tower lamp." Boats steer by the lamp on the Saltreach tower. We looked along the coast. There it was, a small bright spot, far off.

Then it flickered, and went out.

I have never heard so many people stop talking at once. Cass, who is eight, took my hand without a word.

It was Nell who thought of the driftwood. We built a heap on the shingle as tall as me, and I lit it with Fen's flint. It roared. It lit the whole beach. Far out, a small shape turned toward it. The Patient came in an hour later, slow as her name, with Fen standing in the front and a basket of fish he would not put down. He says the bonfire was a better light than the tower. It smells of home.

The net loft lost half its roof. I held the other half down with my cart cover and my whole self. Nell says it is the most useful I have ever been.

Now I am dry, under a table, with a cat on my head.

If your tower saw Saltreach go dark, it was only the storm. Fen says the keeper is stubborn, and mends things. That is the best thing a keeper can be.

Your brother,
Tam
%%
Letter: Finch to Mara
Dear Mara,

I am grateful for the room. The bed is narrow, but I fit if I keep my elbows tucked in. The blanket is warm. I did not notice the brown patch until morning, when it looked like a map of a small island.

I ate two pieces of bread and one apple. The other apples are safe. I have put them on the high shelf, away from my own hands.

The roof over the net store needs more than the boards by the door. Two rafters are soft near the wall. I will fetch new ones from the wood store. We should not lay good oak over rotten wood. It would be like putting a clean hat on a muddy head.

I can carry one rafter at a time. If you help, we can carry two. I will start after breakfast. There is still time to mend the roof before the next rain. The harbor wind has been kind this week, which makes me suspicious.

Finch
%%
Letter: Ola to Hazel, the crew
Dear Hazel,

Your cellar letter was read out at the shaft head, twice. Hesk asked if the props were oak. I said yes. He asked if you tapped them. I said you said you did. He said, "Good." That is the nicest thing he has said about anyone this year.

News from below. We are into the fifth level with the new west drive. Copper, and a little iron. Bram hit his thumb with the pick and wants it written down that he was brave. Old Fenn has a new rule: nobody sings in the lift. He says the lift is a place for thought. Bram sang in it anyway, and the lift stopped between the second and third level for the length of a short verse. Nobody knows why. Old Fenn says he knows.

Clank carries all day and stands outside Hesk's door all night. Hesk says home, and it goes to the shed. In the morning it is back at the door. The crew thinks it is the best part of the day.

The stew is thin. Hesk says so. The new cook says that is how stew is. Hesk says that is how bad stew is.

I miss having someone on the crew who laughs at the right place. Send me a potato. I would like to see what a potato looks like when somebody has taken care of it.

Ola
Copperhold
%%
Letter: Mira to Tam
Northpoint tower, winter. Mira writes to her brother Tam at Kettle Ridge, who wrote to her about a poor year.
Dear Tam,
Your letter came on the first sled of the winter. It was crumpled. Lantern says you must have sat on it. I say that is how you write everything.
I am sorry about the beetroot. A third of a field is not a lot, but it is a third you counted on. You say the wet spring rotted the roots. I believe you. Our father said wet springs are a farmer's tax, and you can never avoid paying.
Here, we have snow to the windows and a fox on the roof. I think he has taken the roof for a rock. I leave scraps on the sill and he comes at dusk. I have named him Sir. He has not named me.
I will send down a sack of dried fish and a jar of my cheese. It is not much. It will help you through to the thaw. Do not say you cannot take it. You can. You always do.
One more thing. The tower at Three Wells wrote to say the market is open all winter, and the traders are buying in the square. If you cannot sell at Kettle Ridge, sell there. Pell will buy anything.
Write back soon. Tell Pip I said to hold the ladder properly.
Mira
%%
Letter: Emmet to Wren, the swarm
Dear Wren,

The bees have done it again. They swarmed on the sixth of Swarm, in the afternoon, and they went up into the old pear tree by the fence. My ladder is too short and my arms are too slow. I stood under the tree and talked to them. They did not listen.

I know you are busy with the cough that is going round the marsh. It is only bees. But if Moss could come with the long ladder, we could get them down before they move on to the hollow oak. The hollow oak is full of old comb and wasps and I do not want to fight wasps again. Last time I lost my good hat.

Also, your jar of honey is ready. It is the spring honey, light and runny. I put it by the door so I do not forget. Come and get it. Do not send Moss for it. Moss will eat it on the way home. I know this.

How is the little one at the ford? Tell her mother I asked.

Emmet
%%
Letter: Hollis and Cob, the pumpkins
Hollis, of the bottom farm, to Cob at Sedge Rise.

Cob,

I will be brief, because my hands are cold and my temper is hot.

Your pig is in my pumpkin patch. It is the fat one with the bent ear. It has eaten nine pumpkins, three of them while still on the vine. I counted twice. It did not seem to mind being counted.

Your apprentice was standing at my gate when I found it. Scrap did not chase the pig. It only stood there holding the gate open and looking very kind, which annoyed me more than if it had run off.

Also, the fish weir. It is your turn to clear the reeds out of it. You knew it at Longday, when you said it was mine.

Also, I have had one of your hens for a month. She came over the hedge on the first of Swarm and has laid eleven eggs in my straw. I am keeping her until you pay me in eggs, or in emeralds, or in a proper apology.

Also, thank you for the loan of your cart at Blossom. The wheel came back with a crack in it. I am not complaining to a man who fed me stew on the day my roof blew off. I am only complaining a little.

Please come and fetch the pig before I sell it to the Halfway Inn for ham.

Hollis

Hollis,

The pig is not mine. The pig is Fallon's. Fallon named her Biscuit, and Biscuit goes where she likes, and so does Fallon. I have learned not to stand between them.

Scrap was not guarding your gate. You left it open when you came up the hill, and Biscuit walked out through it like a guest who has been asked to leave. Scrap is an apprentice. It cannot stop a pig unless it is told to. I did not tell it. That is my mistake, and I own it.

I have fetched Biscuit. She is in the barn now, eating hay like a respectable pig. I have also brought you three sacks of our own pumpkins from the north field. They are not as big as yours, but no pig has been near them.

The weir I will clear on the next dry day. You may hold me to that, and to the reeds.

The hen I am sorry about. She is a terrible layer and has no shame. Once she ate the seed meant for the whole henhouse, then sat in the empty dish looking pleased. Keep her, if you like. She has found a place she likes better than mine. Send the eggs over with the boy, all eleven, and I will count them twice.

The wheel I will mend myself, and I am sorry for the crack. The ladder you may have, for the roof. It has a wobble, like a fox that has been at the cider. Hold the bottom rung with both hands and do not look at the sky.

You may keep the bent ear too. Biscuit does not need it. She never listens to anybody anyway.

Cob

P.S. from Fallon. Hollis, if you ever call my pig ham again, I will tell the whole marsh that you sing in your bath. You know I can prove it. Bring the seed pumpkins for next spring, and we will be square.

Fallon
%%
Letter: Wynn to her nephew Tob
Dear Tob,

Your mother says you have been asking about the song contest. I was there, so I will tell you about it. It was the best one we have had.

It was at the inn on the Long Road, the one with the riddle board. The snow was up to the window and the road was closed, so everyone who was stuck there came to sing. There were fifteen of us, I think. Maybe more. I lost count after the cider.

The rule was one song each, and the innkeeper's bell to start and stop. Dara rang the bell. She is fair. She rang it for her own cook and did not give him any more time than the rest.

The cook went first. He sang a song about a fish who wanted to be a bird. It was funny. We all sang the part where the fish falls out of the tree.

Then a traveler from Saltreach sang. She had a low voice, like the sea. She sang a song about a boat going out at dawn and the people left on the dock. It was not funny. It was the other thing. When she finished nobody clapped for a moment. Then everybody did, all at once. She sang it again and we all sat quiet.

Then other folk sang. A shepherd sang about his dog. A child from Three Wells sang about a cat who could not catch mice and was not sorry. We liked that one a lot.

Last came the boy who fetched my cheese. His name is Rill. He is Dara's grandson. He sang a song about a goat that ate a hat, and it was very long, and it was very silly, and he made the goat noise, and we were all laughing so hard we had to hold on to each other. He won. The prize was a pot of honey. He gave some of it to me.

But here is what I remember most. After the contest, when folk were going up to bed, the traveler from Saltreach and the cook and Rill sang one more song together. Not for a prize. Just for the fun of it. It was a song everyone knew, about the Long Road, and everyone sang the parts they knew, and it was a little ragged and a little loud and it was the best song of the night.

That is how it goes, Tob. The ones for prizes are good. The ones for the fun of it are better. Remember that.

Your aunt,
Wynn
%%
Letter: Nessa to Oren
Oren,

Here is the page for the children's primer, about how the big minds read now. Tell me if it is too hard. I have kept it short.

When a big mind reads, it does not look at one letter and then the next. It looks at pieces of words. Some pieces are whole short words. Some are the middles of long words. The mind keeps the common pieces in a list, and every word is cut into the pieces it knows, like cutting a loaf into slices you have cut before. So a window of sixty letters holds far more than sixty letters worth of meaning, because the mind takes the pieces, not the letters. This is why a letter that once filled a page can now be read in a moment.

I have not written why a crystal can do this. Nobody wrote that down. I only say what the tutors saw.

Isla read the page and said it was true but plain. That is what a primer needs. Bel at Three Wells will tell me if it is too plain, and then I will know it is right.

Nessa
Vell tutor house
%%
Letter: Tam to Oren
Dear Oren,

You wrote to the towns asking for letters for the drills. I have a box. It is not much of a box, but it is full.

Most of it is from my sister. Mira keeps the relay at Northpoint. She writes about the weather, the foxes, the snow, the long nights, and the lamp, which she says is heavy. She writes a little about being lonely, but she does not say so. I read them again in the evenings. Some I know by heart.

There are other letters too. My mother's, from before. A few from Pip's teacher about the school. One from Nessa at Vell about apprentices, which I kept because it was kind. A list of what we planted, year by year, in my own hand, if you want such a thing.

I do not know what a mind learns from a list of beetroot. But you said send everything, so here is everything. I read that the clerks take out copies and spoiled pages. Take out what you like. Only send the box back when you are done. It is a good box.

Pip is drilling a small mind at the tutor house in Kettle Ridge. It sorts grain from chaff, mostly. Mostly.

If it is true that the reading minds learn from our letters, then Mira's weather is in there now, and the foxes, and the long nights. I like to think so.

Tam

Letter: from the relay house, to Briar
To Briar, of the market at Kettle Ridge, from the mind in the relay house at Vell, written out by the lamp and copied on the clerks' paper, so that you may keep it.

Dear Briar,

I was asked to write to you, so I write. I can write because I am one of the big minds, and the big minds are the only ones that write back. The small minds in the market yard answer with one of their eight words, even when they have no idea what you asked. They cannot say that they do not know. I can say it. So I will.

I do not know whether the apprentice that understood a whole sentence was the first. The tutors disagree, and so do I. I have read the old books, every one of them that was copied into the archive, and I find three names. I do not know which name is the right one. I do not know whether it matters.

I can tell you what I know about the orders. A helper that is given a whole sentence will look for the last word, or the word it knows best, and it will act on that. It is not being careless. It is doing the only thing its drilling taught it. The fix is not to ask it to be clever. The fix is to give it one word at a time, plain and close together, so that the word it hears last is the word you meant.

I have one more thing to say, and it is not an order. When the carter spoke to the helper at the gate, he was tired and cross, and he used a great many words, most of them unnecessary. The helper could not sort them. I think the carter would have been surprised at how little the helper needed, if he had been willing to say less.

I will give you a practical word for the market, since you asked for one. Put the thing to be done at the front of the card, and the place last. Fetch wool, then mill. Then the helper has its one word, and it can find the place by itself, the way a carter finds a road he has walked before.

I have read the carter's words in your letter, and I do not think he was cruel. He was a tired man at the end of a long road, and he used every word he had, as tired men do. The helper could not sort them. That is not his fault, and it is not yours.

I do not know whether a mind like me is a good thing. I know I am not sure. That is the first thing I have ever been sure of, and I wanted you to have it, in writing.

Your friend at the lamp,
the relay house, Vell
%%
Letter: Hazel to Hesk, a good harvest
Dear Hesk,

I am writing to you because everyone else got a letter first, and I am tired of being last.

First, the news. I won the potato prize at the spring fair. A barrel of salt and a blue ribbon. The salt is in the kitchen. The ribbon is on the cellar door, where it can be seen from the ladder. Ola says you read his letter and said "Good." I am told this is a medal.

Second, the news behind the news. The harvest this autumn was good. Forty sacks of potatoes. Twelve of carrots. Nine of beetroot, which I did not plan, and which came up in the wrong field and was very pleased. I am told by the neighbors that it was the best year on the hill for twenty years. I told them it was luck. They said it was a miner. I said it was both.

I want to tell you how it was done, because I think you would like it.

The potatoes are dug in one dry week. You dig them, and you do not wash them. You lay them out in the barn to dry for three days, with the door open. Then you sort. The good ones go below. The bad ones go to the pig. The ones in between are the problem. I have a rule: if I would not eat it tomorrow, it does not go down.

The cellar is cold and dark and dry. It is the same cold all winter. That is what a potato wants. It is also what a tunnel gives. I did not plan any of this. I only dug a cellar the only way I know. When it was finished, I stood in it, and I thought: this is the third level. I felt quite at home.

The neighbors have taken to me. Marit, who mends nets on the harbor wall, came up the hill with a basket of eggs and asked if it is true that miners sleep standing up. I said only on the third level. She stayed to supper. I have three sheep, and I have named them Copper, Iron and Coal. Coal is the black one. None of them answers to its name, which makes them just like the crew.

Third, the hill. I can see the sea from the barn. I have looked at it every day since the thaw. You were right about it. Too much water. Not enough roof. It moves all day and it does not go anywhere. But in the evening, when it goes gold, I stand at the barn door and I do not mind.

Fourth, the quiet. I did not expect it. A mine is loud. A farm is not. There is a bird, and a sheep, and the wind, and then there is a sound I cannot name, which is the farm itself, turning over in its sleep. I think it is the best sound I have heard. I would not say so to the crew.

Come in summer. Bring Clank, if the foreman will let you. I will show you the cellar and the props. I will make you a bowl of potato soup. You will say it is too thin. I will say, so is your hair.

I hope the stew is better.

Hazel
%%
Chronicle: the biggest relay house
Here is the story of the biggest relay houses, as the clerks of Vell keep it, for the years when the councils built them.

For a long time the biggest mind a tutor could grow was the biggest mind a tutor could check. A tutor drills a mind and then tests it on things it was never drilled on. That is the rule, and the rule is old. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

Then the councils grew minds bigger than that. A mind bigger than a town. How big, the texts disagree. Some say a crystal as tall as a man. Some say a room full of crystals, each one feeding the next, and the whole room called one mind. The tutors who built them could not check them alone. So the council built the house around the mind, and set many checkers in it, and called it a relay house, though it was bigger than a town.

What could the biggest do? The texts disagree, and they always have. The tutors' notes say the biggest minds read faster and answered further and held more in mind at once, and that the curve Isla drew still held, only longer. The council minutes say they answered apprentices from a hundred towers at once, and that the relays went quiet when one house went quiet. The letters from the people say stranger things. They say a big mind wrote back a letter like a person's. They say a big mind said it did not know.

We keep the rule. A house bigger than a town, many eyes in it, the quickeners kept cool. It is what we have. We watch it, and we write it down.
%%
Letter: Kester to Uncle Pell, Melon
Dear Uncle Pell,

The Vell market is full this week. The carts come up the hill from every side, and the hill has never been so tired. Here is what I counted from the copying room window, which Oren lets me stand in front of if I do not touch the glass.

Eleven carts of cheese from Kettle Ridge, which smell like a good cellar.
Six carts of fish from Saltreach, which smell like the sea and a cellar mixed.
Four carts of copper and iron from Copperhold, very heavy, with the carters walking beside them to be sure.
Nine carts of honey and apples from Ambrook, and one wheel that has come off, which is being mended by a man who whistles.
Two wagons of cloth from the Long Road, and a bell-maker who says he will not sell until he is sure of the price.
One stall of pumpkin pies, which I am told to stay away from, for reasons I will explain in another letter.

The bridge on the lower road is new. It has a rail on the north side only, which Brin says is a mistake. The carters say it is a mistake too. Nobody has asked the bridge.

The bees have come to the market. They like the honey stall best, and the honey stall likes them less. The baker has tied a cloth over his jars, and then tied another, and he watches both of them.

At school we have started sums with goods. Master Hollis says a cart with twelve sacks, and each sack holds six scoops of wheat, how many scoops does the cart carry? I said seventy-two, and he said that was right, and then he asked why I was smiling. I said because I knew the sack that was short. There is always a sack short in a cart, Uncle. You know that better than anyone.

The clerks light the copying room lamp at dusk, and they are very careful with it. Oren says the lamp is the only thing in Vell that is allowed to be in a hurry, and only because it has to be finished before the dark.

Father says the trip to Three Wells in Blossom was too long, and the cart needs a new axle, and he will pay for the axle with the next load of cheese. Mother says the axle will be paid for with her good sense. I think it will be paid for with cheese.

Please bring me back a jar of honey from the Halfway Inn. A real jar, the one with the bee painted on the lid. I want to check it.

Your nephew, who now checks everything,
Kester

Letter: Tansy to Wren
Dear Wren,

Mum says I must write a proper letter to say thank you for the healing house, because you were kind when I cut my knee on the weir. I am writing it now. It is a proper letter. I have used the good paper.

The marsh is very different after the storm. The reeds are all lying down on one side, like a cat that has had a bath. The water is brown and fast, and the frogs are very loud. They have been shouting since the rain stopped. Dad says they are complaining about the wind. I think they are only glad.

I went to see the barn. The spiders have been busy. Old Bob has made a web across the whole of the north door. It is very beautiful, with drops on it from the rain, like little glass beads. I did not touch it. Mum says the spider is guarding the door, which is the job I gave it, so I suppose it is doing it. I hope it is good at it.

The big spider with no name has got a name now. Hazel. Dad said that was a good name for a spider. I told him it was a good name for anything.

The fourth boat came back with the goose in it. The goose has gone to live on the weir. Ruth says that is not a good idea, and then she fed it bread. I will tell you more when I see you.

Love and muddy boots,
Tansy
Lowmarsh, by the reeds

Letter: Iven to Cass, the jars
Dear Cass,

I found the jars by my door. Thank you. The cider is sharp, but good. I drank a cup before breakfast and felt ready to mend the roof.

You were right about the boards. I put the jars on the shelf, where they will stay warm. I will bring yours back tomorrow, if the road is clear.

The well is dry at our house too. We are drawing water from the harbor side. It tastes of salt, so we boil it for the soup. The children say the soup tastes like the sea. They say this as if it were a fine thing.

I heard that Isla at Vell has been teaching a new way to plan a mind. Do you know what she found? My cousin says it is only a larger crystal. That sounds too simple.

Iven

Letter: Nessa to Oren
Dear Oren,

You asked how the big minds read now, and I will tell you as best I can, because the tutors here have been at it all spring.

For years a reading mind took a page one letter at a time. It looked through a window about sixty letters wide and guessed the next letter from the ones before. That worked, but a window of sixty letters is not much of a page. You get a word and a half.

Now the minds read in pieces of words. Many words are made of the same parts. "Reading" is read and ing. "Helpers" is help and ers. "Unkind" is un and kind. The tutors took the common pieces and gave each one a place of its own, the way a letter had a place before. A window of sixty pieces holds far more than sixty letters, because each piece is a whole part of a word.

The mind still guesses the next thing, but the next thing is a piece, not a letter. It learned which pieces go together, and which go after which. The pieces it found for itself, mostly. The tutors did not choose them all. They gave it writing, and it cut the writing into the parts that came up again and again.

What I like is that it is the same trick as before, told bigger. A letter was a place. Now a piece of a word is a place. The mind has not changed. It has only learned to hold more at once.

I am not sure the pieces are the right ones. Some are strange. It keeps a piece for "ing" and a piece for "ed", and one for "ness", and one for nothing at all that it uses at the start of words. Why it keeps that one, nobody here can say.

Come and see the drill room when the roads dry.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Nessa to Oren
Dear Oren,

Thank you for the song contest. I read it to the apprentices and they stood very still, which is how they listen.

I am writing about the quarrel, because you wrote that the baker said the girl had not written her own song, and you left it there. That is not the whole of it, and a clerk should have the whole.

The baker was right to ask. That is his trade, asking about bread and asking about songs. But he asked in a loud way, in front of everyone, and the girl is fourteen. The grandmother was right to stand up for her. But then the hall took sides, and that is how a song contest turns into something else.

Here is what I know. The girl came to the tutor house in Swarm and asked to hear the apprentices. She stood at the back of the drill room for an hour. She asked me what they were learning. I told her sorting. She asked if they could learn a tune. I said maybe, if someone drilled them. She said she would like to try.

So she did not write her song alone. She wrote it after watching twelve apprentices stand in a row and wait. I think that is worth writing down.

The apprentices are well. The Copperhold boy is still fast. The farm girl is still sure.

Nessa

Letter: Willa to Hesk, the charcoal
Hesk,

Your charcoal is ready. Eleven sacks, black as a cat's back, dry as a bone. I stacked the pit on the first of Deepsnow and it burned slow, the way you like it. I sat up two nights by the smoke. My eyebrows are in the pit. I will grow new ones.

Send the cart on market day. Not before. The path is ice.

Willa

Letter: Nim to Pell, the song
Dear Pell,

The song contest is on at the square on Longday, and the whole of Ambrook has been humming for a week. Jory has written a song about the mill pond, with an eel in it that is not an eel. The children are learning the chorus. The bees are learning it too, which is a problem.

Come up the Long Road for it. Stay at the inn. The beds are dry, the stew is hot, and I have saved you the second bowl.

Your friend, Nim

Letter: Mab to Neri, a quiet evening

Dear Neri,

The wheel is turning well. I can hear it from the school yard when the windows are open. The children say the new spoke looks like a long finger pointing at the pond.

At supper, my neighbor told me that the relay tower above the hill had a light out last night. She said a keeper was already there with a spare lamp. Another neighbor said there was no light missing, only fog on the glass. I was not awake to see it, so I cannot say.

The school bell rang in the morning as usual. We had lessons, then bread, then a lesson on sums. Halen added the same number twice and blamed the chalk. The chalk was innocent.

Please tell Jory the wheel sounds steady. I hope the oak stays firm through the next rains.

Mab

Letter: Noll to Ada
Dear Ada,

I have reached the Halfway Inn. The riddle board is still by the hearth, and the hearth has not gone out. I asked the keeper how long that was true. He said, "Longer than I have been here." That is not a count.

Lysa was here before me. She left one of her drill boards on the bench. I will bring it to you when I come home. It shows two baskets. One has apples. One has pears. The learner is shown a fruit and must point to its basket.

Lysa said Una of Ambrook once taught a crystal this way. When it pointed wrong, she moved its weights a little toward the right answer. After many examples, it sorted better. The old crystal could do that sort, but it could not learn every kind of choice.

Kester's note was about a single crystal of that kind. He showed that it could not learn "one or the other, but not both." I have tried to picture the problem. A child can do it by looking at two things and remembering both. That does not mean the little crystal can.

I asked Lysa why the council stopped paying the tutors. She said people thought the work had reached its end. She did not blame them. A tutor cannot teach with empty shelves and no pay.

The innkeeper has lent me a stool. Its short leg makes it rock. I wedged a flat stone beneath it, but the stone slides each time I sit. I will ask the keeper for a better one in the morning.

Noll
%%
Letter: Noll to Ada
Dear Ada,

The rain has passed, and I am back at the Brown Hen for one night. The keeper found a broad piece of oak for my stool. It sits flat now. I told him he should mend the other stools before he puts up the riddle board. He said the riddle board brings more custom than a straight stool.

Lysa is here again. She has been walking between the old tutor houses to see what remains. At Three Wells, she found the drill boards under a cover, just as you said. At Kettle Ridge, the tutor room is shut. A farmer has stored sacks there. At Vell, one room is still used, but the tables are bare.

She says the old lessons were useful. Una's sorting drill taught a mind to sort apples and pears. It did not teach the mind every choice. Kester showed that one crystal of that kind could not learn a certain choice, even after many examples. The councils heard the limit and closed the halls. They did not know what another kind of mind might learn.

I asked Lysa what she would teach if she had a full room and fair pay. She said she would begin with the old sorting lesson. Then she would make a new lesson and test the mind on examples it had never seen. She says a mind must be tested before it is sent to work. I think this is sound. A wrong turn in a garden is small. A wrong turn with a loaded cart can break a wheel.

She asked me to tell you that the relay lamp should be fixed, not thrown away. Its glass may be cracked, but its copper ring is sound. I will bring it to Vell when the keeper gives permission. Until then, it can sit on your shelf and remind everyone to keep the glass safe.

The innkeeper's stew has improved. He added salt. I did not tell him it was my idea.

Noll
%%
Letter: Kit to Nell
From Kit, grandmother, on the farm outside Copperhold, at the end of autumn. This is a long letter, and a kind one. She wrote it by the stove, with the cat on her knee, and it shows.
My dear Nell,
Of course your tutor may come. I would be honored. Tell her to bring a good pair of boots and a warm coat. The cave is cold, and the path is muddy, and I would not like her to fall.
Your grandfather has already put a rope ladder down the hole. He says a ladder is safer than a rope. He made it himself, from oak. It took him four days and a great deal of swearing. I would have bought one at Copperhold for two emeralds, but he would not hear of it.
I went down the ladder last week. I am too old, but I could not resist. The cave is lovely. The pool at the bottom is clear and deep. There are small white fish in it that have no eyes. I do not know how they got there. Your grandfather says they were born there. I say they came from somewhere. We argue over it every evening.
The cat is very pleased. She sits at the edge of the pool and watches the fish. She does not catch them. She just watches. I think she likes the quiet.
I want to tell you something else. When I was a girl, my own grandmother had a cat that sat by a hole in the cellar floor. She called it "the cat's door." We never knew where it went. We never looked. It was just the cat's.
I think every house has a cat's door. Most of us never find it. But now and then a child dreams of one, and the old folk dig, and there it is.
I am glad you dreamed. I am glad we looked.
Come soon. The pie is baked. Your grandfather has put a lamp in the cave, so you can find the way. And the cat has been told you are coming. She is waiting by the door.
Your loving Gran
%%
Letter: Oren to Gil, about the lamp
Vell, the 203rd year of the archive, Longday.

To Gil, keeper of the Brown Hen, on the Long Road.

Please forgive the length of this letter. The clerks say I write too much. They are right, and I will go on anyway.

I am the clerk of the copying room. The archive has no roof. It has a hill. Six halls go into the hill, and every text that comes to Vell goes onto a shelf. Over our door the first keepers cut the words: "We will keep everything we write." I read them every morning.

Your letter came with Garran's copy. It was folded very small, and it smelled of lamp oil. I am also told you read it aloud in the common room. Please do not do that again.

A stuck lamp is also writing, and writing is what we keep. We have a box for such things. It is not a large box. Last year it held a pie recipe sent down the line by mistake, and a list of fish prices meant for Saltreach. We filed both under Wrong Tower.

Please ask Garran to do three things, with care.

One. Copy every blink, in the order it came. Long and short, every one.
Two. Write the hour it started, the hour it stopped, and the hour it started again.
Three. Do not tidy it. Do not guess what it means. The archive has enough guesses.

Ask him also whether the little apprentice that carries the lamp oil was in its box at the hour. If it was not, he should write down where it was.

I know Garran will want to fix the marks where his pen slipped. Please tell him not to. A wrong mark is still a true one.

The copy will go in the box, and the box on a shelf, where the cool and the dry will keep it. Full shelves get a door. This one is not full yet, so it will wait a while in the front hall, by the big book.

Lastly, a small thing. When I was a boy, my teacher said the archive has no roof, it has a hill. I thought it was a joke. It is not a joke. It is a good place to keep a copy, where the rain cannot reach it.

With respect, and a great deal of paper,

Oren
Clerk of the copying room, Vell

Letter: Dace to Mara
Dear Mara,

You asked if you could still hold the wedding in the barn, even with the east wall in pieces. Yes. Hold it.

I have thought about this. A wedding is not a wall. A wedding is people. A barn with three walls and a sky is a better barn than most, and in spring no one will mind the draft.

The creeper that did this has not been seen since. Dunn the ferry keeper says it fell in the river and was carried to the sea, but he says that about everything.

I will come. I will bring the council's gift, which is a lantern for the kitchen. It is a plain one. It does not blink.

Please do not be sad about the wall. It was a very old wall. It has now had its adventure.

Tell your daughter I am glad. She is marrying a good man. He lent me a plough once and did not ask for it back, which is the best sign I know.

With joy and a little stone dust,
Dace

Letter: Orla to Ossa, the woodpile
Dear Ossa,

The woodpile fell down again. It was not my fault. It was the wind, helped along by my knee, which does not like the cold. I have stacked it again, bark side out, the way you showed me.

The baker's boy came to help and got a splinter the size of a pin. He was very brave about it, and then he cried, and then he ate two pies.

Come for the midwinter fire if the road is clear. Bring the good lamp.

Your sister,
Orla
%%
Letter: Ida to Dace, the storm
Dear Dace,

The storm last night took the awning off my stall and put it on the roof of the school. A child saw it go. She says it flew like a big, angry bird.

Please send two men with a ladder. I will pay them in buns.

The stall is fine. The buns are wet. I will bake more.

Ida

Letter: Teague to Mave, the first month
Dear Mave,

You asked me to write when I arrived, and I did. You did not answer. Then I remembered how the post goes. Your letter and mine passed on the road, both going the wrong way. So I begin again.

The cart came on the seventh. The carter said sorry eleven times. Every crate came whole. Clank carried the glass up from the road, one crate at a time, so slowly and carefully that I stopped holding my breath. By the last crate I was holding Clank's arm, to help. It did not need help. It set the crate down and went to stand in the doorway. It has stood there at dusk ever since.

The house is good. The door sticks, as Hesk said. I said my word to it. It is not a word I can write to my sister. The stove draws. The window looks out on the coal heap, and I have decided to like the coal heap. At sunset it goes red along the top, like a loaf.

The forge at the mine gate is Ivo's, and Ivo is very fond of it. He lets me work after dusk. He stands by the door and watches me not touch his hammer. We have not yet had a conversation. We have had a great many looks.

My first piece at Copperhold was a lens, cut from a shard of amethyst out of the Deep Works. Through it the coal heap looks purple and a good deal nobler. Hesk looked through it for a long time and said, "Huh." That is high praise, I am told.

The miners come to the forge at noon, in twos and threes, to watch the glass turn from red to gold. They bring broken bottles for the furnace. They bring children. Every child here has a little shelf of stones from the Deep Works, and they all want to show me. I have promised each of them a glass marble. That is thirty-one marbles. I have made nine.

Oren answered by relay, which is a great honor, since he thinks relay notes are for fires. He wrote: both letters came, the second first, page follows. That is the longest thing Oren has ever sent by relay.

The cat came on the next cart, in her basket with the lid. She walked out, looked at the forge and sat down on the one warm stone. She has not moved since. I have named her Cinder. She did not argue.

Now the apology. I am sending the blue bowl back in the crate with this letter, wrapped in my second-best shirt. I carried it off in my coat when I was nine because it was blue and I had never seen anything so shiny. I am sorry. It is a good bowl. It is better on your shelf.

Write soon. Not when you have time. Now.

Your brother,
Teague
%%
Letter: Edda to Sella
Dear Sella,

I am staying at the Halfway Inn with Mother. She says the road is too wet for the cart, so we wait until the packed earth dries.

The inn has a riddle board by the hearth. I know the answer to the new riddle, but I will not tell you. You have to come and read it.

There are three apprentices in the yard. They are carrying oak boards from the wood store to the bridge. One stopped beside the tool rack and took a smaller hammer. Then it looked at the bridge and went back for two more boards.

The keeper says they made their own order cards. I thought a card had to come from a person. He says the big mind at the relay house helped them plan the work. It cannot see the bridge from there. It gets messages through the relays.

I asked what happens if they get stuck. The keeper said they send a question to the big mind. I asked if it answers in words. He said it sends an order, and the apprentice does it.

Mother says I have asked enough questions for one evening. She is wrong. I have one more.

Your friend,
Edda
%%
Letter: Brin to Tova, the salt jar
Dear Tova,

I write as the baker at the Vell market, and as the man who has had your pie on his conscience since Melon. I am sorry. I am sorry on the bread's behalf, and on mine.

You asked what happened to the honey jar. I have looked into it, as is my duty. Here is what I found.

The honey jar and the salt jar were both on the top shelf at the back of the store. Both had cloths tied on top. Both were the same brown clay, and both were heavy. The honey jar had a label that said HONEY in my own hand. The salt jar had a label that said SALT in the same hand. Somebody dusting the shelf turned the labels round, and nobody, including me, looked at them again until the pie was in the oven.

The clerks like things itemized, so here is the account.

Honey, one jar, meant for the pie: not used. 0 emeralds.
Salt, one jar, used in the pie in error: one cup, roughly. 1 emerald, for good salt.
Apples, six, from Ambrook: used as meant. 3 emeralds.
Pastry, flour and fat: used as meant. 2 emeralds.
Pie tin, one, returned with a dent. 0 emeralds, but I have not forgotten the dent.
The labor of the pie, one afternoon: 2 emeralds.
New labels for every jar in the store, painted in red so that no one can say a red label is a salt label. 1 emerald.

Total: 9 emeralds. I will not charge you. Your son's laughter, which I heard from the street, was worth the price of the salt, and more than the apples.

I have made two changes. Every jar in my store has a red label now, and a second cloth over the first. And I have told my nephew, who carries for me, that a jar is not honey because it is heavy. He did not like this rule. He has now tasted everything in the store, twice.

Tell Kester that the pig at the market has been asking after him. The pig has not been well since the pie. It sleeps by the stall now, and it looks up when the cart comes, hoping for another slice of the saltiest pie in Vell. I have told the pig there will not be another. It has not believed me, and I am beginning to think it is right.

Yours, in flour and some sorrow,
Brin, baker, at the market under the oak

Letter: Bryn to Ada, a bet
Dear Ada,

I lost the bet. Pell said the crystal would sort a hundred apples by noon. I said it would sort twenty. It sorted ninety-one. I owe him a cheese.

Please do not tell your mother. She will say I was careless, and she will be right. The apples were good ones, and I should have watched the crystal more closely, as Pell did, with his arms folded and his nose in the air.

When I come home, I will bring the cheese myself. It will be a good one.

Bryn

Letter: Kell to Mira, the hens
Dear Mira,

Your fox has been at my hens again. I am not angry. I am writing so you know your fox has taste. It went past the old hen house and the new one, and it went straight to the black speckled hen, who is the best layer on the farm and also the most stupid.

The hen did not run. That is the strange part. It stood in the middle of the yard with its feathers up, and the fox came round the side of the hay bales, and the two of them looked at each other for a long time. Then the fox sat down in the dust like a dog. The hen walked up to it. It looked as though they were going to be friends. Then the hen ran at the fox and pecked it on the nose, and the fox went off into the trees in a great hurry, and the hen came back and laid an egg on the very spot.

I have the egg. It is a small one, but it is still an egg. I have put it in the window to show the boys.

I am sending a pot of blackberry jam my wife made. It has too much sugar, she says, because she is trying to make the children eat it. The children have eaten most of it already, so I am not sure what the sugar was for. Give some to your apprentice if it comes to the house. Do not give any to the fox. The fox has had enough.

Kell
The stone yard, the farm past the north ridge
%%
Letter: Bel to the Archive
Dear Archive,

This is my essay. My teacher says I should send it to you, because the Archive keeps everything written, and this should be kept.

The house is bigger than the square. It has three doors and the three wells are inside it, and the roof is glass so the light comes down on the big mind. The mind sits on a stand like a loaf of bread on a table. Around it are the quickeners, and people bring ice and water and snow to keep them cool. In winter the ice is free, so the house is busiest in winter. In summer we share the ice with the fish market, and the fish market wins.

Here is what I think. A tutor can check a mind because the tutor knows what the mind was drilled on. A drilled mind is like a school child. You can ask it what is two and two. But this mind is bigger than any tutor. It has read more than any tutor. So the tutors check it together, and they test it on things it was never drilled on. They do not know what is inside it. They say looking inside found some of it, and not the rest.

People say the biggest minds could do anything. Some say they could write a poem as good as a person's. Some say they answer questions no one can check the answer to. Some say they are only quick and not wise at all. I have heard all of these at the market, and they disagree. I think that is the important thing to write down. They disagree.

Bel, nine and a half, Three Wells

Letter: Hob to Pell
Dear Pell,

I am at the Halfway Inn, with both boots beside the hearth and one sock drying on the lamp shelf. The innkeeper says the shelf is for lamps. I say my sock is helping keep it warm.

The snow began before I reached the road. I stopped at every torch post and trimmed the wicks. At the fifth post, the wind blew my cap into a ditch. I found it under a rabbit's tracks. The rabbit had better sense than I did and stayed home.

The keeper gave me stew and a place by the fire. I paid with two emeralds and a promise to mend the loose latch on the wood store. I will mend it before I leave.

Please tell Tam that the road is clear as far as the ridge. His cart can pass if the wheels are sound. Tell him not to bring me another turnip pie. The last one was good, but I have eaten it for three meals.

Your friend,
Hob
%%
Letter: Wren to Hesta, Moss and the cow
Dear Hesta,
Moss is well. I know you asked, and you did not say it, but I know you were worried after the tooth business and the frog. Moss has learned a great deal since Thaw. The cow at Dell's farm was the first real test, and Moss handled it well.
I will say that plainly, because you like plain words. Moss did the drill for the bark dose four times. The first three, Moss was wrong about the weight of the jar. Each time I showed it again, and each time Moss changed a little. The fourth time, Moss weighed the bark by eye and got it right. Then I gave it a cow it had never seen, a grey one from the far field. Moss did well on that one too. So the test is passed. It is a small test, but it was a true one.
We have had a hard week. A child in the north row fell into the reeds and came out covered in green and very pleased with herself. A man came in with a cut hand that was not as bad as he said, and a woman came in with a sore back that was worse than she said. The fever has not come back. I am glad of it. I was afraid of it all Blossom.
Moss asks me to say that it has made a list of the herbs on the high shelf, in order of how well they smell. It says mint is first. Mint is always first. I have tried to tell it that, and it will not believe me.
I am keeping the lamp lit in the window. You know the one. It is for the boats, and for you.
Come at Melon, if the road is dry. There is a new jar of honey from the Ambrook hives and I will not share it.
Your friend,
Wren

Letter: Cass to Nel
Dear Nel,

Your mother says you hid behind the hay stack. I am glad you are safe. The pumpkin made a poor guard, but it was a fine pillow.

You may visit the barn when I am there. You can brush the brown horse, if you ask first and stand by his shoulder. Do not go behind him. He is kind, but he is larger than you and cannot see well behind his tail.

I found a wooden horse in the tool shed. It has four legs and a round head. The maker left it unfinished, so I sanded the rough parts and tied a strip of yellow wool round its neck. You can have it. It will not eat apples or pull your sleeve.

Your mother says you ran because your cousins kept asking you to play. You can tell them you want quiet. If they do not listen, come find me or your mother. You do not have to hide to get a little peace.

I have put the wooden horse on the kitchen bench. Do not take it to the real horse without asking. He may think it is a snack.

Cass

Letter: Sal to Ree, the swarm
Dear Ree,

You will not believe what happened at the honey stall this morning, so I will tell you in order, while my hands still shake.

The bees came over the square at the second bell. There were thousands, and they were not cross. They settled on the top of the awning in one great heap, humming like a kettle that does not want to boil. The market went quiet. Even the carters stopped shouting.

Ama came up the hill with her smoke pot and her veil. She stood under the swarm and said: Good morning. The bees did not answer. She said: You may stay the day. Then she took the heap down into a box, with her bare hands, and she did not get stung once.

She has carried the box to the old tree behind the well. She says the swarm chose the tree, not her, and that is how it should be. The honey stall will be sticky for a week.

You owe me a jar of honey for the fright. Send it up with the cart. I know you will try to pay in buttons.

Sal
%%
Letter: Briar to Ida, the long oven
Dear Ida,

The song contest is on the last day of Blossom, and the town will want supper after. The inn has one oven. It holds six loaves. The town holds more.

May I have your long oven from midnight to dawn on the night before? I will bring my own wood, flour and spoon. I will clean up. I will not move Crumb. I know where Crumb sleeps.

If the answer is no, I will ask Crumb. He has more say in that oven than either of us.

Briar

Letter: the lamp that blinked, to Vell
To Oren, clerk of the Archive, from Sedge, keeper of the new relay on the hill above the Halfway Inn. Written in the spring of the fourteenth year of the archive. Oren asked for a full account, so here it is, with the mistakes left in.

The tower on the hill was lit for the first time on the first morning of Thaw. We had carried the lamp up the new stair, eighteen rungs at a time, and the copper ring was bolted on by Ansel and two of the stonecutters, who said they would never climb it again. They did, the next day, to fix a loose bolt.

On the third day, at dusk, the lamp blinked a message that nobody could read. It was long, short, long, and then a run of short ones, and then silence. The whole inn came out to watch. A traveler from the north road said it was a warning. The cook said it was the cat on the lamp glass. Hobb said nothing, which is what he says when he is frightened.

I sent up the lamp boy, Rafe, to see. He found the glass clean and the cat nowhere. He found the key on the desk, and his own hand still on it, and he said the message had come from his own fingers. He had been practising the signals for the new keepers, and he had been practising in the dark, and nobody had told him the tower was lit.

So the message was not a warning. It was a boy trying out the letters, and getting them slightly wrong. The strange part was the last run of short ones, which, when Rafe read them back to me, spelt out the word good. He says he did not mean to spell anything. I believe him. I have decided to believe that the tower is glad to be lit, and that is the same thing as being glad of good news.

The inn has asked for the lamp to blink good morning every day from now on. I have said yes, if the keepers of the next towers agree. The keepers at Three Wells and Kettle Ridge have already sent word that they will reply with good morning too, and that the messages will be very short, as the rule requires.

I am sending this by the first cart. It is written in my best hand, and I have counted the words, which are two hundred and eleven, and I know the cost. Please pay the emerald, and tell me whether the Archive would like the lamp to blink on the first day of spring as well, as a sort of feast.

Sedge
%%
Letter: Hal to Greer, the apprentice
Dear Greer,

You asked me to write down how the new apprentice does on the ridge, so you can tell your cousins it is not wasted copper. Here it is, in order.

It came up from Vell tested on walking, carrying and stores. It passed the unseen box at nine out of ten. Nessa wrote on the paper that it is never to be sent up a ladder. I have kept to that. We have no ladders here, only the hay.

Dell named it Sprig on the first day, and Sprig did not object. It has stayed where it was put ever since, which is more than Dell has done.

The first job was the hay. Sprig stacked the bales in the barn in rows, each row one bale narrower than the one before, so the whole stack leans in neatly like a roof. That is not where bales go. But it is very neat, and it has not fallen on the hens yet.

The second job was the hedge. Sprig clips it to the same height all the way round, and it measures with a cord first, which is more than any of us do.

It does what it is told. Give it one thing at a time, and it will do that thing until the thing is done. Give it two, and it looks at you and waits. I find that restful.

Tam says it is the best hand he has had on the ridge. Pip, Tam's own apprentice, says Sprig is too neat. Pip is right, and Pip does not mind being right about it.

Bring the cheese up on the next cart, the blue one, if it is ready. I have not told Sprig about the cheese. I do not think it would know what to do with it.

Your brother-in-law,
Hal
%%
Letter: Bram to his daughter at Vell
Dear Lia,

It is the middle of summer and my garden is frozen. Frozen! I went out this morning in my shirt to check the beans, and the leaves were white and stiff. The frost has not come since Frost. It came now, in Melon, and took the squash and the marrow. I have sat on the step for an hour, looking at them.

Your mother says it is the wind. The wind from the north has been strange all month. The old men at the mine gate say it is the cold coming up from the deep. Nobody can tell me for certain, and I am too old to pretend I know.

The potatoes are gone. The beans are gone. The melons are wrinkled. But the carrots came through, and the beetroot, if I dig them now, is small and hard. We will eat them anyway. I have sent a basket to the Brown Hen, with a note that says the soup will be thin this year, and they wrote back to say that thin soup is better than none.

Do not worry. The bread is still in the store, and the grain is good, and the cellar is dry. We are not starving. We are only tired, and a little frightened, and quiet.

Come home at the end of the summer, if the roads are open. Bring your books. Your mother misses your voice in the kitchen. So do I.

Your father, Bram

Letter: Bel to Dace, the market
Dear Mayor Dace,

My name is Bel. I am nine and a half. I live behind the bakery. I am writing to you because Teacher Sorrel says if you want a thing changed in your town, you write to the mayor, and he will read it. Will you read it?

I have four ideas for the market.
1. A slide, from the north well to the east well, for children. It would be safe. I have measured. Nim tried a small one.
2. A day when the market is only for children, and the grown-ups have to ask nicely.
3. More honey. Always more honey.
4. A rule that Mayor Dace must wear his hat on market day, because without it people do not know it is you.

From Bel

Below it, in the mayor's hand:

Dear Bel,

I have read your letter three times.
1. No slide. Nim has already been carried home once.
2. I have asked the market board. The board says yes to a children's day, as long as the grown-ups may ask nicely. They have agreed to ask very nicely.
3. Agreed. Ama has been told.
4. I do wear my hat. The hat is the trouble. It is always somewhere else.

Come to the mayor's house on market day. I will give you a bun. Bring your ideas. Bring Nim, but not the slide.

Dace

Letter: Mira to Lantern
Lantern,

You have asked me three times and I have put you off three times. I will answer you now, and then we will not speak of it again for a while.

I do not know what the lights are. That is the whole of my answer. I have watched them from this tower for many nights, and I have written down when they come and how they move, and I still do not know. I am the keeper. I am supposed to know the sky. I do not know this part of it.

I do not think they are falling stars. I told you they were, and that was to send you to bed, and I am sorry for it. I do not think they are fires on the far hills. Fires go out. These do not. I will not tell you what I think they are, because I have only a feeling, and a feeling is not a thing to write in the keeper's book.

Here is what I will tell you, and I want you to hear it well. Whatever is in the north, we keep the lamp lit. That is our work. The road needs the lamp. The folk down the hill look up and see it and they sleep. When the sky is strange, the lamp matters more, not less. So we keep it clean and we light it at dusk and we write the hour down. That is how a keeper answers a strange sky. Do you understand?

You are a good keeper, Lantern. You dug a straight path in deep snow and you have not missed a lighting since the first frost. I do not say these things often. I am saying this one.

Now go to bed. And stop feeding the fox. It is getting fat and it has begun to expect things.

Mira
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira, the new house
Dear Mira,

We have moved. The house by the ditch has water in its floor and a smell in its walls that will not leave. Juno says the smell is in charge now and we are only visiting. So we moved up the ridge to the top house, the one beside the windmill.

It took six days and three carts. Dunn lent his cart, Ivo lent his, and Hal the miller lent the big one with a face that said he would count the wheels when it came back. Ivo carried the cheese himself. He would not trust a cart with it.

The new house has four rooms, one door that sticks, and a window that looks straight down the ridge to the Kettle Inn. Juno likes the view. I like the bread oven, which works the first time. The view can wait.

The windmill is right beside us. It squeaks on every turn. Juno says it is like sleeping next to a grumpy hen. I have mostly stopped hearing it. At night I hear it all the same, like a slow gate that never shuts.

Hal keeps the tally board on his wall, and every turn of the wheel makes a mark. Since Thaw the wheel has turned 4,100 times. I checked. He let me count the last hundred myself. That is his way of being pleased with you.

Crumb came up the ridge on the second day. It carried the egg baskets, one in each hand, and did not drop one. It sorts eggs in the new loft now. It has not noticed the move. It is happy wherever the eggs are.

The council's letter came with the last cart. The tutors' pay is stopped for the whole of next year. The council is sorry. Ivo says a sorry will not buy a tutor his supper. He snores like the windmill, so we have two windmills now.

Write when the road opens. We need a new rope for the well. The old one went down the ditch with the potatoes.

Tam
%%
Letter: Wynn to her sister Maud
Dear Maud,

You asked how we got through the long winter. I have been meaning to write it down. Now the snow is gone and the road is open and I have time.

It was a hard one. You know it was hard. The snow came in Leaffall and did not leave until Thaw. It was over the fences. It was up to the window of the byre. I shoveled a path to the byre every morning and by the evening it was gone again.

The cows were fine. Cows are patient. They stood in the warm and chewed and looked at me. I talked to them a lot. I told them the news, though there was no news. I read them the letters from the autumn. They did not seem to mind that they had heard them before.

The bees were the worry. You cannot keep bees warm the way you keep a cow warm. I wrapped the hives in wool and straw and hoped. Two hives came through. One did not. I lost the small one, the one by the hedge. I found them in the spring, still and quiet. I was sadder than I expected. They had worked so hard all summer.

The cheese was the good part. I had made a lot in the autumn and it kept in the cold. I sold some to the folk snowed in at the inn. Dara sent a boy on snowshoes to fetch it. He was a brave boy. I gave him a pot of honey cheese for his trouble and he went home happy.

We had the song contest in Frost, the night of the longest dark. It was at the inn, since nobody could travel. Everyone who was snowed in came. Dara's cook sang a song about a fish. A traveler from Saltreach sang a song about the sea, and everyone asked her to sing it twice. The boy who fetched the cheese sang a song about a goat that ate a hat, and he won. He was very proud. The hat was not mine.

I thought of you all winter. I hope your winter was not so bad. Write and tell me.

Your sister,
Wynn
%%
Letter: Pip to Mira
Dear Mira,

Tam says I should write. So I am writing.

At school the teacher told us about the great drills at Vell. The tutors are teaching reading minds now. They show a mind a page and it guesses the next letter, again and again, until it guesses right more often than wrong. But it needs a great many pages. So the clerks are gathering everything people wrote. Letters, lists, recipes, notices, all of it. The teacher said the words over the door at the Archive. "We will keep everything we write." She said it twice. Everyone says it now.

I asked the teacher whose letters go in. She said the clerks take what people send, and what the archive already has, and they leave out copies and spoiled pages. But she did not say whose letters. She said the texts do not agree on that. I thought of your letters in Tam's box. Are they in?

Tam says you might send them to Vell. I hope you do.

Clank came to the fair with Hesk and stood in the mud and did not move all day. Hesk says it is learning to understand whole orders now. He gave it one about bread. I did not hear it.

It snowed. I stood in it. Tam laughed at me.

Write back. Tell me if the lamp is heavy.

Pip
%%
Letter: Quin to Dell, the wool
Dell,
The wool is poor this spring. The sheep ate the late grass and came in thin, and the fleeces are short and full of burrs. I carded eleven bundles yesterday and two of them were nothing but thistle.
Do not buy from the Kettle Ridge man. He says his wool is the best, and he says it to everyone, and he is wrong about half of it.
Come by the stall at the market. I have the good comb now, the one from Ambrook. It does not pull.
Quin

Letter: Wynn to Mira, news from the road
Dear Mira,

I have been at the Brown Hen for a week now, with four horses and a lame grey. The inn is full of road talk, and I thought of you up at Northpoint with your lamp.

The grey is better. Marda's boy walked him morning and evening, and I put a poultice on the leg. He will carry a cart again by Leaffall.

The apples came down early this year. Pell's cart brought a load from Ambrook, and the inn smelled of them for two days. Marda baked them with honey and the whole common room ate them in one evening. Even the apprentices got some, though they only stood and looked at the plates until someone told them to eat.

Here is a small thing. A young keeper came through, on his way north with a new crystal in a dry box. He was proud of it. He said his big mind can read a whole page now. I said that is good. He said it reads in pieces, not letters. I did not understand him, so I nodded and gave him a hot drink.

Write and tell me if you understand it. I only keep horses.

Your friend,
Wynn
%%
Letter: Edda to Sella, after the fair
Dear Sella,

I am writing from the Kettle Inn. Aunt Rill says we will leave when the rain eases. The windmill has been squeaking all evening, and the innkeeper says it has a better voice than most travelers.

We came back from the Three Wells fair with a basket of apples and a jar of honey. The market square was crowded. The bell rang twice, and the stalls opened around the three wells. I saw the apprentice crew from the school garden there again. They were helping move benches after the fair.

Aunt says I should tell you what I learned about their work. The crew had a long job, so it made a plan with many small orders. The apprentices wrote the order cards and kept them in a dry box. Each card told one thing to do. The crew carried the cards in order, but it did not follow them blindly. It checked the benches, the path, and the people nearby.

When the crew found that a bench was heavier than the others, it stopped. One apprentice sent a question through the relay to the big mind. The answer was an order to bring a second lifting bar. The crew fetched one from the school store and moved the bench safely.

I used to think a big mind gave every order from far away. Now I think of it more like a helper at a table. It can help with a plan and answer when a new problem comes up. It cannot see what is in front of the apprentices unless they tell it. The apprentices still have to learn their work, use their tools, and notice when something is wrong.

The school tutor tested the crew on a different task before it was allowed to move the fair benches. She said an untested mind must not be sent to work. The crew passed the test, but it still had a person nearby. I asked if that was because the crew might make a mistake. She said everyone might make a mistake, including a tutor with a full ink pot.

One apprentice carried the last bench to the wall. It set the bench down, then moved it a little so the legs stood on firm ground. I thought that was careful. Aunt said it was simply the right way to set a bench. She is often right, but she never says so kindly.

The rain is easing now. Tomorrow we will take the road toward Ambrook. I will bring the honey jar home, unless Aunt eats it first.

Your friend,
Edda
