Letter: Nessa to Briar, the drill room
Dear Briar,

Your letter came on the first cart, and it made me laugh, and then it made me sit down. You have asked the question that has been asked at Vell for longer than I have been alive.

Yes. I remember one. I was a girl in the drill room, sitting on the end of the bench with my slate, and the tutor was an old man with a cough and a big voice. He had a helper that could understand a whole sentence. He would say, bring the blue box from the high shelf and put it on the bench by my elbow, and the helper would do the whole of it, the box and the shelf and the bench and the elbow, in one go. It was the first I had seen. We all stood up to watch.

Now I must be honest. The books disagree on whose was the first. Clank, at Copperhold, is in some of them, and Pip, at Kettle Ridge, is in others, and Peg at the fair is in a third, which I think is only a story. I do not know which one I saw. I may have seen one of the others, in a different hall, and remembered the bench. I have been wrong about benches before.

What I do know is this. The helpers are drilled with the eight words, and they answer with the eight words, because those are the only words they were ever given. When an order is not clear, the helper cannot say that it does not know. It picks the nearest of the eight and goes. That is why your clerk's sentence turned into the word quick. It is not stupidity. It is a mind that was never told what to do with an unclear thing.

So the answer to your carter is plain orders. Tell the helper what it is to do, in one of the eight words, and then say the thing once more, slowly. Many tutors think this is a slow way to work. It is not. It is only slow the first time.

I will tell you one thing more, which the old man never said. The helpers answer with the eight words because the eight words are what the drill rewards. Give a helper a clear word and it does well. Give it a long sentence and it gives you back the nearest word it knows. That is not a fault in the helper. It is a fault in the drill, and the drill can be changed.

Do not let the old tutors tell you the first one was a legend. Let them tell you it was the first to be written down, which is a different kind of true.

Your friend in the drill room,
Nessa
%%
Letter: Hesk to Dace, please pay
Dace,

It is winter and I am asking for the emeralds. You know the ones. Twenty for the iron fixtures in the big relay house at Three Wells, the brackets and the bolts and the rings round the quickeners. I sent them down the Long Road on a cart in the autumn. The cart came back empty and the purse came back empty too.

I am not angry. I am cold. Being cold makes a man write letters. Clank says I should be patient. Clank is an apprentice and does not pay for coal.

Here is my news, since you will want it for the notice board. The Deep Works hum at night. It has hummed all winter. The stone is warm now even on the higher levels, which it was not before. I have stopped taking Clank below the eighth. I will not say why. Some of the men have stopped going down at all. We still send coal up. We are miners and we send coal up.

Pay the twenty emeralds and I will not write to you again until spring.

Hesk, Copperhold
%%
Letter: Tam to Corin
Dear Corin,

I am sorry I called your boat sense poor. I was teasing, but the words sounded harsher in a letter. I hope the crew comes in before the wind turns.

Pip brought home a copy of the tutor's word lesson. It says each letter or word has a place among many. When two words are used in alike ways, their places draw near. The places are numbers in the crystal, not marks on a field. That part took me two readings.

The tutor showed the mind cards for wheat, bread, flour, and loaf. It learned that these words often belong near one another. Then she showed it cards it had not practiced. It still put flour near bread. She said that was a better test than repeating the old cards.

Pip wanted to test it with beetroot and soup. The mind put beetroot near soup, which made him laugh. Then it put soup near bread. I said that was sensible, because our kitchen serves both.

I bet Pip one honey cake that the fishing boat will come home before the next lamp lighting. He says the crew will wait for a calm sea. I hope he wins the bet and loses the cake.

Tam
%%
Letter: Sorrel to Pell, the lamp
Dear Papa,

Mama says it must be short, so I will be quick.

Last night the lamp on the hill blinked all night. Long, long, short, long. Then long, short, short. Then it stopped, and started again.

Gil says a bird sat on the glass. I do not think a bird knows the lamp code.

This morning Garran sat on the step with a book on his knee. Every blink was in it, down the page like a fence with no posts.

Garran said lamps do not blink on their own. Then he said nobody sent it. Then he said it was not a bird. He is cross about all three.

Button is well. She laid an egg in your boot. I did not put it there.

Come home soon.

Sorrel
%%
Relay house rules, Saltreach tower
Notice posted inside the door of the relay house at Saltreach:

1. Two keepers are on the tower at all times. One watches the lamps. One watches the quickeners.

2. The quickeners are kept cool. Fill the pans at dawn and at dusk. In summer, bring ice from the cellar. In winter, bring snow if there is any. If the pans are dry, call the keeper of the tower at once.

3. Do not bring a candle into this room. Not one. We lost a tower to a candle once, and the archive has the letter about it.

4. Keep the door shut. This means you, and this means the cat.

5. Anyone who brings an apprentice to ask the big mind must wait in the front room. The keeper will carry the question in. Do not shout at the crystal. It cannot hear you any better for shouting.

6. If the lamps go dark, do not guess. Write it in the log. The log is the only thing that can be read later.

7. The big mind does not know everything. It will answer what it learned. If it is asked a thing it never learned, it will still answer. Write down what it says, and write down that it may be wrong.
%%
Letter: Pell to Ree, an answer
Ree,

It should have been six. There. I said it, and I will say it at the fair.

I did tell the merchant the brown was muddy, and that was wrong. It is not muddy. It is the color of a wet stone in the mill pond, and I said so to a baker yesterday.

Here is my honest trouble. The Long Road is long this spring. The bridge past the Brown Hen washed at the edge, so the carts go slow and the wood comes late. I have been short and I have been sharp. That is my doing, not yours. Forgive me.

The emeralds are in the tin by the door. Take what is owed for the red and the rest on the cart.

I will bring the cart on Leaffall. Hold the wool for me.

Pell

P.S. The Halfway Inn has a new cook. Do not eat the pie.
%%
Letter: Oren to Ulla, the first year
Dear Ulla,

It is the first day of Blossom in the second year, and I am sitting in the third room with a cup of cider, because the cider is the only thing in the hill that has not been counted yet.

I have been looking at the book of the first year. Forty-one books when we opened the door. Three hundred and twelve by the first of Frost. Now more than four hundred, and the shelves are full to the second hall. We have had to dig a new hall. The hill has agreed to it, although it has not said so in words.

I want to write down what the first year was for, because I am afraid that when the clerks are old, they will remember only the copying, and forget why we copied.

We will keep everything we write. You know the rule. The founders cut it over the door in the first week, and the cold cracked two of the stone-cutter's chisels. I used to think it was a fine motto, and a bit grand for a library. Now I think it is an instruction. Everything. A shopping list, a spoiled page, a bad poem, a child's letter, a miner's note on the back of a ledger.

The reasons are plain. We do not know what will matter. A recipe from a baker's wife in the second year might be the only record of how her bread was made, and her bread may be the bread people eat in the tenth year.

This spring the new copper vein at Copperhold came up the Long Road in bars. I have one on the shelf in the copying room, where the clerks can touch it. Merle writes that the copper is copper, and that I should not make a fuss. I am making a fuss.

The zombie at the gate was driven down the lane, and Ink has taken her place at the top of the door, and I am told she is pleased with the view.

The roads are opening, and Pell says the relay towers are going up at each hill. Next year I want every tower to send us its log. Every message, every lamp lit, every storm. Let the Archive be the place where the whole road is written down.

Do you remember what we said on the first day, when the cart came up the hill with the seventh box, and the carter asked what we were doing with all the paper? You said we were keeping it. He said, for what. You said we would find out. We are finding out.

Your friend, Oren

P.S. The stone-cutter has asked to be paid in emeralds, and I have paid him. He says the motto was the best work he has done. He also says it is the only work in the hill that does not need the lamps lit. I did not argue with him.
%%
Letter: Hesta to Pell
Dear Pell,

I am staying at the Brown Hen until the small house by the east bridge is ready. The roof is on, but the door sticks, and the bed is still on your cart. Please do not sell it by mistake with the wool.

I left my old room in Three Wells during Apple. I thought moving would take one morning. It has taken three carts, four trips, and a quarrel with my brother over a pot. He says it is his. It has my name scratched under the handle.

At the inn I heard you speak of the reading mind. The tutor said it reads a page one letter after another. At each letter it uses earlier letters from its short window to guess what comes next. I asked how it knows which earlier letters matter. The tutor said the mind learns that from its drills. That answer seemed fair, though I still do not like the inn's thin blankets.

Please bring the bed tomorrow. Bring the pot too, if my brother has not hidden it.

Your cousin,
Hesta

Letter: Wynn to the innkeeper Dara
Dear Dara,

Thank you for the room. The bed was good and the fire was warm and the soup was better than I expected. I am not saying your soup is usually bad. I am saying this soup was good. There is a difference and I wanted you to know it.

The cheese you asked about. You wanted to know why mine keeps so long. Here is the secret, and it is not much of a secret. Salt. Salt and a cool room and a cloth over it. That is all. My mother did it that way and her mother did it that way. No magic.

But the salt must go on early, before the curd sets hard. That is the trick. If you salt late it sits on the outside and the middle turns. You can taste it when it turns. It goes sharp.

You said you might keep a few rounds for the inn. I will bring six on my next cart. Four pressed and two soft. Keep them in the cellar, not by the oven. I have said this before to other people and I will say it again. Not by the oven.

How is the riddle board? I looked at it on my way out. I could not solve the one about the two doors. I thought about it all the way to the bridge and I still do not know. Tell whoever wrote it that they are clever and I am not, and I am at peace with it.

I will be back through in the autumn, before the roads get bad. Save me the corner room if you can. The one with the small window is fine. I do not need a big window. I am asleep in it.

Your friend,
Wynn
Cheesemaker
%%
Letter: Tansy to Gran, the spiders
Dear Gran,

The spiders are back in the barn. There are four big ones and a family of small ones who live in the hay. I have named the big ones. Mud, Pot and Old Bob, and the fourth one I have not named yet, because it is too fast to get a good look at.

Dad says we must not hurt them. He says spiders eat the flies, and the flies eat the cow's ears. I asked him if the cow minded the spiders. He said the cow has never had an opinion on spiders in her life.

Mum says I am not to sleep in the hay loft any more, even though it is warm and the spiders are very polite. I think she is worried about the spiders. I told her the spiders are more worried about me.

Thank you for the knitted hat. It is too big. I am going to grow into it, Mum says, and then the hat will be the right size and I will be too big. I do not understand this, but I am wearing it anyway.

Love from Tansy, at Lowmarsh, in the barn
%%
Letter: Len to Gale
Dear Gale,

Your letter about the song contest made me laugh, and I read it to the inn guests. The traveler from Kettle Ridge knew a verse about a hen that stole a beetroot. He said his aunt sang it at harvest. We tried it after supper, but nobody knew the tune.

I am sending you a small gift with the next cart. It is a wooden whistle, made from a piece of birch. It has a hole at each end and one in the middle. Your cousin says it sounds like a goose. I say it sounds like a fine whistle, when held by someone who knows how.

Do not play it during lessons. Master Pell will put it on the high shelf, and then you will need a ladder. I know you have climbed the flour bins before. Your mother told me.

Our inn is busy now. The road brings folk from both sides of the ridge. We have mended the roof and set two more beds in the back room. A keeper from Vell stopped here and asked where a relay tower would stand. He made a mark on his board and spoke with Father about the hill beyond the stream.

The bridge is stone at last. It has a low rail and a lamp post at either end. The carter crossed with three sacks of wheat and did not lead the mule by hand. Father watched from the door until the cart was out of sight.

Tell me if the whistle sounds like a goose. I will not be offended.

Your aunt,
Len

Letter: Finch to Lida
Dear Lida,

I will take the ladder this afternoon. Tell the mare she may keep the rung she has chewed. I have no use for it now.

I can mend the pantry roof before supper. The rain found the old split beside the chimney. That is my fault. I saw it last Swarm and said I would return after the harbor wall job. Then I forgot.

Please save me a bowl of soup. I will bring a sound spoon this time. I have three, though I can only find two.

Finch

Letter: Kester to Opal, the four dots
Dear Opal,

You asked what is in my note, and why the council is so cross. I will tell you, and I will use no word you do not use at a birth.

Draw a square. Put a dot at each corner. Say there are two lamps, left and right. The top left dot is both lamps dark. The top right is the right lamp lit. The bottom left is the left lamp lit. The bottom right is both lit.

I want a crystal to say yes when exactly one lamp is lit, and no when both are lit or neither. So yes is the top right and the bottom left. No is the other two corners.

Una's crystal can draw one straight line across the board, and say yes on one side and no on the other. Try it with a string. Any string that puts the two yes corners on one side puts a no corner there too. I tried it on forty boards. It cannot be done.

That is all my note says. I did not say crystals are useless. I said one crystal has this limit. The council heard that crystals are useless, and stopped the pay.

Your friend,
Kester
%%
Letter: Zell to the keeper of the Brown
Dear Marda,
You do not know me. I know you from Pell, who has told me about your stew, your well, and your barn full of spiders. He said if I ever needed an inn on the Long Road, I should ask for you.
I need one. Not for me. For a house.
I am a dyer at Vell, and my house is being moved. A great many are leaving Vell, and the Archive is half empty of its own clerks, and the hill is quiet at night. I do not wish to leave. But I wish to know that I could.
So my question: if I came your way with a cart, two vats, a cat and a husband, would you have room? We could pay in dyed wool, or honey, or work. I am good at stirring.
I also ask about a letter. I sent one to Pell at your inn last month. I have heard nothing. If it came, please say. If it did not, please say that too. I would like to know where my letters go.
It is winter and the road is long. I do not ask for an answer soon. But I ask for one.
Zell
%%
Chronicle: the even numbers
From the chronicle of the tutors, the years around 196.

In those years the tutors at Vell were building minds with more layers than before, and they found that a deep mind would not learn. It learned well for a while, then it stopped. The tutors drilled it and drilled it and the answer stayed wrong. Some said the crystal was cloudy. Some said the drills were bad. Some said the minds had a limit and we had reached it.

A tutor named Isla, who would later draw the rule of growth, sat with a deep mind for a season and watched its numbers. She wrote that the numbers in each layer grew with every drill, layer upon layer, until they were too big for the slate to hold. The mind was not stupid. It was drowning in its own numbers.

She tried a remedy. After each layer passed its answer up, she brought the numbers back to an even size, the size she had chosen when she built the mind. The deep mind began to learn again. It learned past the place where it had stopped. It learned past the place where the next mind stopped. The tutors wrote it in their books and every new mind was built that way after.

The texts do not agree on the year. Some put it at 195, some at 197, and one letter from Copperhold puts it at 194 and says the miners knew it first, which the tutors at Vell deny. They always deny it. The argument is old and friendly now.

What the texts do agree on: it is the even size that matters, not a small one. A layer that brings its numbers back too small forgets what it learned. A layer that brings them back too big drowns. The size is chosen when the mind is built, and a good tutor chooses it the way a smith chooses the weight of a hammer.

And the stream came the same year, or near it, and the two together are why a deep mind can still learn. Each layer adds what it found to a stream that runs through the whole mind, and each layer brings its numbers back to an even size. The stream carries what was learned. The even numbers keep the mind from drowning. A tutor who understands both can build a mind that learns a hard thing, and keeps learning it, and does not go stiff.

Nobody wrote why the numbers grow. The tutors asked. The archive has no answer. Isla wrote: we do not know why the stair leans. We know how to set our feet. That is enough to climb.

In the autumn of that year the council at Vell voted to pay for a new relay house, the first of the big ones, and the tutors said the even numbers were the reason it could be built. The council minutes are short and the writing is bad. They say: the minds can be made bigger now. Build the house.
%%
Letter: Pell to Yarrow, from the road
Dear Yarrow,

I am at the Halfway Inn. The riddle board by the hearth has a new riddle, and nobody has solved it yet, and I am going to be stuck here until somebody does. It goes: What has a roof and no walls, and is always on the road? I say it is a waymark. Nobody agrees with me.

I have your message about the roof. I am sorry I was rude about the color. A grey roof is the best roof on the Long Road. I have now said so to three carters and a dog, and they all agreed with me, and the dog looked very relieved.

I have brought the salt. I have brought the needles. I have brought Juna's letter, which is short, and I have not read it, because it is addressed to me and I am too polite to read another person's post, even when it is mine.

I owe you a bet, too. I said Ama would take the honey back, and she did not. So I owe you a jar of the dark. I shall tell Ama that I cannot pay for it, and she will say that is not my business, and then she will give you the jar herself.

The road is wet. The bridge at the stream has one rail loose again. I am telling the road keepers. I am also telling you, so that you know I am telling them.

Pell
%%
Letter: Abel to Nessa, a school asks
Dear Nessa,

I teach at the school by the east well at Three Wells. Twenty children, more in winter, when the roads are too bad to work and the parents want them out of the house.

The children ask about apprentices. They see them on the road with Pell's cart, carrying grain and crates. They ask me how a mind learns. I have read your manual and the primer, but I get it wrong in front of them, and they can tell.

I would like to bring my older class to Vell in the spring. They are nine and ten. They can be quiet. They would stand in the drill room and watch. If a tutor could say a few words to them, it would go into their heads and stay, better than anything I say.

Tell me if this is possible, and what it would cost. We can bring our own bread. I bake a fair loaf.

Abel
Teacher, Three Wells
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira, about the fence
Kettle Ridge, winter. Tam writes back to his sister Mira at Northpoint. The letter is not tidy and neither is the fence.
Dear Mira,
Thank you for the fish and the cheese. The cheese came with a note that said "Do not give it to Pip." I gave it to Pip. He ate half of it before I knew. He says it is the best cheese he has had. I think you wrote the note to be funny.
I must tell you about the fence. Brin and I have a quarrel over it. He says it stands a block too far on his side. I say it is where our father put it, and our father was never wrong about a fence. Brin says our father was never right either. So we are at war.
It began at the harvest and has lasted all winter. We do not speak. We nod. Biscuit, his dog, is the only one who crosses, and I think he does it to spite us both.
Pip has a plan. He says he will move the fence a half-block at night, a little each time, so neither of us notices. I said no. He said it would be fair. I said it would be a crime. He said, "Only if we are caught."
I wrote to Dace at Three Wells for a ruling. I have not had an answer. Dace is probably busy with the market.
How is the fox? Tell Sir I said hello.
Tam
%%
Letter: Teague to Mave, the late cart
Dear Mave,

I am sitting on a crate at the foot of the Copperhold road. Eleven more crates are sitting around me. The cart is not here.

The carter said the second of Apple. Today is the fifth. You said you would send word if anything changed. No word came. I think the word was lost, and the carter was lost, and I am a little lost myself.

A shepherd walked by and asked if I was a monument. I said I was a glassblower. He said, near enough.

The house at the edge of town looks fine from here. I will tell you more when I am inside it.

Tell the cat I will send for her.

Your brother,
Teague
%%
Letter: Noll to Bet, about the chicken
Bet,

The chicken has left the yard. I am not sure how. The gate was shut and the hen house door was shut and the fence along the lane has no gaps that a chicken could fit through, unless the chicken is very determined. It is very determined.

The children found it on the wall of the school house at dawn. It was sitting on top of the wall, just under the painted door, looking down on the children as if it were the mayor. The children made a great noise. The chicken did not care. Mayor Dace came out in his coat and nightcap and asked the chicken to get down. The chicken did not get down. The chicken stayed on the wall until the teacher brought a basket of seed, and then it came down the wall one step at a time, like a little old man, and went straight into the basket.

Dace has asked that the chicken be kept off the school wall in future. I have said I will try. The chicken has its own ideas about the school wall, and I think the school wall will have to win.

The first snow has come and gone and come again. The lamp on the corner is always lit now. I counted the lamps from the bed last night, all the way to the bell, and I got to eleven before the snow hid them. You would like the snow. It makes the square look clean for an hour.

Noll
%%
Letter: Noll to Bet, the move
Dear Bet,

We are in. The cart came up the Three Wells road at dawn with the cupboard tied on the back, and the cupboard stayed tied all the way, which is more than I can say for the chicken. The chicken is in the yard now. It has taken the best corner by the well and it will not give it back.

The house is smaller than the old one, but it has a proper hearth, and a window that looks east down the hill to the square at Three Wells. I can see the bell tower from the bed. I can see the lamp on the corner, which is lit already, though it is only the middle of the afternoon. Dace says the lamps are lit early on the shortest days so that nobody walks into the well. He is right about that. I have already walked into the well's edge once, and it was only the edge.

The inn is the same. I will not have time to be lonely here, because there are twelve beds and eleven of them are full. The twelfth is the one by the stove, and Pell has it every time he comes through, and his boots are on the rail again.

I have had the cellar cleaned out, all of it. Forty-one jars came up, and nine of them I did not want. Some were pickled beetroot from before the old roof leaked, and the rest I cannot name. I gave the nine to Tam's boy for the hens, and the hens seem happy about it.

Your sister says she will come at Blossom to see the house. I told her the house will still be here at Blossom, which is more than the roof was.

Write when the post comes through. Write what the children are doing. Tell me whether the school has a new teacher, and whether the bell still sticks on the third stroke.

Noll
The inn at Three Wells, the house on the east road
%%
Letter: Tomas to Merle
Dear Merle,

Thank you for the waymark. We found our way back by it, just as you said. My wife says you set it too true and now the whole marsh knows where we were married, and she is only half joking.

The harvest this year was poor. The beetroot rotted and the potatoes came up small. We are eating thin soup and telling the children it is thick. Wren says next year will be better. I am not sure, but I do not say so to the children.

Come to the wedding ground next Blossom. We will have a fire and a pot and a good jar of honey.

Your friend,
Tomas
%%
Letter: Greer to Maud, boats gone
Dear Maud,

You asked me about the boats. Here is what I know, which is less than what the boatmen say, and more than what the mayor says.

The big ships have been seen off Saltreach three times this autumn. They are longer than any boat. Corin is not talking about them, which means he is worried. The yard is building more hulls than the fishers can use.

Some families from the harbor have packed their carts and gone along the coast road. They do not say where. They say the sea will be kinder past the headland. Others say they are only going to cousins, and will be back for the cod season. The fish market has fewer sellers now. The price of salt went up on the board, and the price of everything else went down.

I do not know what is across the water. Nobody I trust knows either. So I buy what I can carry, I sell what I can spare, and I keep the cart by the door.

Greer
%%
Letter: Sable to Jeb, the late boat
Dear Jeb,

Your boat is not home. Dun and Harl went out at noon, and they have not come back over the water. The lantern is lit on the jetty. Do not row out after them in the dark. They will be cold and wet, and so will you.

A zombie knocked at the gate last night, very politely. I did not open it. I put a loaf on the wall instead, and it took the loaf and went off across the field. The torches on the path kept the rest back.

Bring the flour up on the wagon. The second stone is cracked.

Your sister,
Sable

P.S. Harl's boat came in at dawn. Dun was singing. He always sings when he has not caught anything.

Letter: Tam to Mira, one or the other
Dear Mira,

Ivo came over the ridge last week with his cart and his bad knee. You remember Ivo, who drilled the field minds at Vell. The council stopped paying the tutors this year.

Over supper he told us Kester's note. I am writing it down so you will stop asking me.

Lay four stones in a square, one on each corner. Each corner is one case: neither, only the first, only the second, or both. Two cases are yes: one or the other, but not both. Those two sit on opposite corners.

Now draw one straight line across the ground, with both yes stones on one side and both no stones on the other. You cannot. Every line leaves a yes stone on the wrong side.

Kester said one crystal of that kind can never learn this. One crystal can only draw one line. Ivo says two crystals, one feeding the other, could draw two. Nobody has tried it. The council will not pay for trying.

Crumb is one crystal of the old kind. It only has to sort eggs, and it does that well.

Tam
%%
Letter: Bree to Ivo, apples and spiders
Dear Ivo,

Your letter came up the Long Road with a cart of salt and a cheese the size of a small hen. The cheese is from Greer at Kettle Ridge. It has a crack in the middle, which she says is intended. I have eaten a third of it, and I am not convinced.

The orchard is coming into bloom, late, after the long winter. The old tree with the grey branch has one white flower on it. Everyone has walked up to look, the way you do when something has decided to try again. Jory says the tree will fruit if it gets the rain at the right time. He says that about everything, but he is usually right about trees.

Our spiders have gone into the press house, where they say the cider is sweeter. You warned me about your spiders, and it turns out ours have heard about yours. They sent a delegation of two. They were very polite, and they stood on the press until I moved it.

The bees came out of their hives on the first warm day. Ama says they are cross about the winter, and so am I. They sat on the lid of the honey jar and looked at me until I gave them a dish of water, which Ama says is the same thing as an apology.

The bridge over the stream has its plank back, the loose one. Jory put it in again, and it still moves, but it is the good kind of moving. The children like to jump on it. Their mothers do not like them to jump on it. I say the plank has been jumped on since before any of us were born, and it has never once given way.

Dorrit says the stew at the mine gate was the best of the winter. I told her that was only because she was there to eat it, and she threw a spoon at me. It was a wooden spoon, and it missed.

We have a wedding in the orchard next month, if the blossom holds. Ysa and Rafe, who have been courting in my apple cart since Thaw. The whole town is coming, and the cider is spoken for. Save me six barrels, Ivo, and do not tell me you have only four. I know what your cellar holds.

Write back. Tell me what Hesk says about the stone, and whether Clank has learned to stop at the bell. Tell me also whether the spiders have finished the barn, because I need to know what to expect when I visit.

With love from the orchard,
Bree

P.S. The tree with the grey branch is looking better. I am taking that as a sign, and you may think what you like.

Letter: Bel to Oren
Dear Oren,

Can a cat come to a wedding? Ours will not stay out. Her name is Pipkin and she is grey with one white foot. Mother says she will spoil the cake. I say she will not, because she only sits.

The wedding is at the east well in Sowing. I am to carry the ring on a little cushion. I have been practicing walking slow. Pipkin follows me when I practice, so it is good practice for her too.

Dace says you are coming. If you come, will you bring a book? I want to see the seal. I have seen it once, on a letter to the school, and it looked like a hill with a door in it.

Yours,
Bel

P.S. If you say no about the cat, I will have to shut her in the shed, and she will cry. So please think about it.

Letter: Dace to Pell, the fair stalls
To Pell of Ambrook, merchant, from Dace, mayor of Three Wells, about the fair.

Pell,

I write because the market council asked me to, and because I did not want to have this argument on the road, where you would win it.

The stall fee for the spring fair is four emeralds for a cart, and six for a cart with honey on it. You have been paying four. The honey does not care what the council wants, and neither does the stall beside the well, which is yours this year and will be yours next year if you pay the six.

I know you think the honey is special. I know the dark honey from Ambrook sells before the light honey is opened. I know that the children of Three Wells would eat the jar in a morning if they could. That is the reason for the six. The children are happy when the honey is in the square, and the council is happy when the children are happy, and nobody is happy when a merchant argues with a mayor on a market morning.

Please pay the six. In exchange you may have the stall nearest the notice board, where everyone reads the rules before they buy. I have already had the board painted with your name, in very small letters, so you cannot object to the size.

The council has also asked me to say that the children of Three Wells have made a song about the honey. I have heard it. It is not a good song, but it has three verses and a refrain about jars, and I have been humming it in the square all week, and so has the bell. I will not write the rest, because the council would have to pay for it.

The cart stands on the north side of the square, and it will stand there until the first market day of Melon. Please bring the cart early, before the children are up. I know the children will be up very early, because they have been up very early all week, and you will have to pay them in honey to make them go to bed.

Also, the riddle board at the Halfway Inn has been moved to Three Wells. Hobb says you are the reason. I have not decided whether to thank you.

Dace, mayor, Three Wells
%%
Letter: Ruth to Pen, after the flood
Dear Pen,

Thank you for the eggs and the shoes. The shoes are too big. I will grow into them, which is what everyone says about shoes.

Now the news. Last week the rain came down like a bucket tipped over. The stream came off the hills in a brown rush, over its banks and down the lane, knee deep. It did not stop at the doors.

Hook was out at the nets. It is the copper helper that Corin sets to the nets, and it has done one thing for six years. When the water came up, it went into the stream to its waist.

It carried the Haddon girls up onto the wall, one at a time, the smallest first. Then old Mother Jessop, who weighs more than a sack of salt. It did not run. It walked, slowly, one foot after another, the way it was shown at the slip.

When the last child was up, Hook stood in the brown water and looked at the wall. Nobody had shown it what comes after a flood. So it stood there until Corin came back with the rope, and then it did what it was told.

The water took the net shed, the hen house, two small boats on the hard, and Jessop's pig. The pig came back two days later, sitting very proud on a floating door. Nobody has got it off yet.

Corin says Hook will be mended on the slip and sent back to the nets. I said a good helper stood in the water with children on the wall and could not decide what to do. Corin said that is what happens when no one has told it. I said then someone should. He said that is what the tutors are for. Then he remembered they are not paid any more, and he went very quiet, and made the tea.

Come at Longday if the lane is dry. Bring your own boots. Shoes are for growing into, not for walking through floods.

Ruth

Letter: Nessa to the Lowmarsh school
Dear teacher at Lowmarsh,

We are sending you a small apprentice for the summer, one of the copper ones, a young mind out of our drill room. She is called Tally. She is good with rows and counting and she does not tire. She will help with your garden and your stores. She is not good at much else yet.

You must give her orders in the words she knows. This is the whole of what she knows: farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul, home. She cannot say she does not know. She will do something. It may not be the thing you meant.

Last week I told her to fetch the watering can. She brought me a hen. She was very pleased. The hen was less pleased. I have written the order for a watering can on a card now, and she carries the card. If you want a can, show her the card.

Write if she gives trouble. She will not. She only misunderstands.

Nessa
The tutor house, Vell

Letter: Ruel to Mab, Isla's curve
Dear Mama,
Isla came to the drill room today and drew a line on the board. It went up and then bent over, like a fox's tail. She said it is a curve. She said that before we grow a mind, we can draw how well it will do.
Here is how it works, as far as I can tell. A bigger crystal holds more weights, and more weights can learn more. But only if you drill it longer, and only if the drills hold more writing. Short drills make small minds. Long drills on a big crystal make a good one. Isla drew three lines for three sizes, and each one went up and then bent. She said the bend is where more drilling stops paying.
That is most of it. Do not worry that I do not understand all of it. Nessa says I will.
The fox got into the hen house again. Tell Father it was not my fault. I was at Vell.
Your son,
Ruel

Letter: Gale to Len
Dear Aunt Len,

The whistle came with the new books. I tried it behind the school room, where Master Pell could not hear. It sounds like a goose when I blow hard, and like a kettle when I blow softly. I have decided both sounds are useful.

The new road is firm below the stone yard. A cart passed from Three Wells with nails, glass and two barrels of apples. The carter said he had slept at the Brown Hen. He was glad to find our inn open, though he called it the Copper Hill House. Father says we should make a sign so travelers know its name.

There is talk of a relay tower on the hill beyond the stream. A keeper came with two masons and a measure rope. They paced the ground, then put a stone where the door will stand. The tower will carry messages toward Vell and Three Wells. The keeper says the first tower will be small. I asked if small towers have small messages. He said no, and smiled.

The school room has a new shelf for books. Bera made the shelf from spare boards. She has given me the lower space, since I am short. I put the slate there each night and keep the birch whistle beside it.

We made a song for the road workers. It has three lines, because Master Pell says a short song is easier to carry.

The road is firm beneath our feet.
The cart brings bread and news.
The lamp will find us in the dark.

Bera says the last line is too grand for a muddy hill. I say the hill will be grand when the tower stands on it.

Father has begun keeping a little room by the house for travelers who come late. He says they may sleep there if the inn is full. Mother says we need another blanket first. I asked the keeper if the tower house will have a spare blanket. He said it will have a desk, a lamp and a stool. I think a blanket would be better.

I will send you a drawing of the tower when the stones rise above the hill. I can draw a straight wall now, though the roof still leans.

Your loving niece,
Gale

Letter: the Archive to Lowmarsh
To the weaver and the millers at Lowmarsh, from the clerks of the Archive at Vell, in answer to your letter of the spring.

We have your letter about the wheel. We are sorry the wheel is down. The Archive keeps a book of mill wheels, in the third room, and we have looked it up for you.

Every wheel in the book is written with its wood and its size. The oldest on the coast is at Saltreach, and it is birch, and it has lasted fifty years, which is good for birch. The wheel at Lowmarsh is in the book as oak, cut in the 118th year, and it is marked with the boat-house sign, which means it was made by Corin's father.

We have written down the day it fell, and the names of the men who lifted it. The clerks have added them to the book, under the wheel, so the next millers will know who to thank.

For the new wheel, the book says to use the oak from the ridge, with the grain running long. The book says to set the axle in the stone and not in the wood. The book says the frogs will be a difficulty. It does not say how to deal with them. We think you have found the answer already.

We thank you for the letter. Please keep the book of your mill in your own house, with a copy of this one. We keep everything, and it is good to have a second place where the things are kept.

Oren, for the clerks of the Archive

Letter: Lyle to Sella

The first snow came before I had covered the cheese pots. I have moved them into the back room. The room is cold enough, though the door sticks when the wood swells.

The cow is sick. She will not eat her hay, and her nose is warm. I sent word to Wren at Lowmarsh. She said to keep her dry and give her clean water. I have done both. She also said not to let Clank try to carry her. I had not asked Clank, but now he is offended.

The mine kitchen sent back the small cheese. Hesk says it was too sharp. He says this every winter, then eats it all. I will send him a softer one next time, if he sends the empty dish back.

Please bring the blue blanket from the house when you come. The cow needs it more than I do.

Your brother,
Lyle

Letter: Corin to Tam
Dear Tam,

I meant the beetroot. Pell called it apples because he had apples on his cart too. He has now promised to sort his sacks by what is inside them. I will believe that when I see it.

At the shipyard, we tested a small mind on words. Nessa gave it cards for bread, flour, wheat, and rope. Each word had a place among many places in the mind. Words used alike sat close together. The mind put wheat near bread, and flour near bread too. It put rope far away.

This was only a simple drill. We do not know why the crystal learns from the cards. The tutors say the closeness is useful, not a little picture hidden inside the crystal.

The sea has kept one fishing boat out since last evening. The crew know the coast well. We have left a lamp burning on the pier.

Corin

Letter: Nessa to Pell
Dear Pell,

You asked what the tutors mean by a window. It is not glass. It is the part of the page the mind can use while it guesses the next letter.

The mind reads a letter at a time. For each new letter, it looks back at the letters before it that matter. The window holds about sixty letters. Then it guesses what comes next. If the guess is wrong, the tutors can show it the right letter and drill it again.

This does not mean it understands a page as we do. It learns from many pages, and it grows better at guessing what letters belong together. The big minds at the relay houses read this way.

I have written this plainly for the farm school. Please tell me if the children understand it. A clear answer from them is worth more than praise from another tutor.

Nessa

I heard the wheel break from my garden. My cat ran under the bench and stayed there until I brought out fish. She has forgiven the wheel. She has not forgiven me for being late with supper.

You can repay me with the school children singing by the pond. They know the apple song, I hope.

Letter: Nessa to Vesper
Vesper,
You are not wrong. You are nearly exactly right. I may use your broth when I teach the new class.
Let me say it as I say it to my apprentices, and then you can see how close you came.
A mind is built in layers. Each layer takes the numbers coming in, works on them, and hands new numbers to the next. The numbers are not words. They are only quantities. A mind written in numbers has nothing else to hold.
Here is what goes wrong. If each layer scales the numbers up a little, even a very little, then after twelve layers they are twelve times too big. After thirty, they are huge. The mind cannot learn from them. They are wild. If each layer scales them down, they shrink toward nothing and the mind learns nothing either.
The cure is simple. Before a layer passes its numbers on, we look at them as a whole and bring them to an even size. We subtract the middle, and we divide by how far they spread. Then the next layer receives numbers that are the right size, whatever the layer before did.
A mind with these steps learns faster, and a deeper mind is possible. The tutors found this about the year 196. It is one of the quiet things that made the big minds possible.
Your pot of broth is better than the drawing I use on the board. I will tell my class where I got it.
Your spiders sound brave. I would also keep the goat away from the webs. Webs stick.
Nessa
%%
Letter: Wren to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

The frost came early to Lowmarsh. The boardwalk was slick, and Moss carried the salt jar without spilling it. I call that a fine winter morning.

Thank you for the dry wool. I have put it by the sick beds. The frogs are quiet, but one keeps appearing beside the well. I suspect it wants the warm water.

Wren

Letter: Ree to Bel
Dear Bel,

Thank you for your letter about the play. I read it twice. I am glad the frogs won. I always thought the frogs would win.

You asked how I make the blue cloth for the well. Here is how, and you may tell the other children.

First I take the wool and wash it in the trough until the water runs clear. Then I put it in the big pot with water and a little of the ash water I keep in a jar. I heat it slow. Then I put in the blue, which comes in a hard lump from the market. It is the color of a deep stone. I stir it with a long stick I keep for nothing else. I stir it until my arm hurts. Then I stir it more. Then I hang it on the line to dry, and it drips blue on the grass, and the grass comes back green in a week.

The cloth for your well was the best blue I have made. That is why it was heavy. The wool was thick and the dye went all the way through, and thick cloth holds water and does not want to stay on a barrel. Next year I will make it thinner, and I will sew stones in the corners so it stays.

Tell Mistress Ada I heard about the relay tower play. If she needs a lamp, I have an old lamp of tin that hangs in the dye shed. It is not a relay lamp, but it is a lamp, and it holds a light, and the frogs could carry it.

You may carry it yourself if she says yes.

Tell me how the bee is getting on. I hear the bee forgot to buzz. I once forgot my own name at a fair, so I say nothing against the bee.

Your friend,
Ree

P.S. The lamb is called Blue, after your elbows.

Letter: the inn to the Archive, spring
To the clerks of the Archive at Vell, from the Halfway Inn on the Long Road, in the spring of the fifteenth year of the archive.

We are asked each year to send a count of what the road has, and what it has gained. The count is below, with the things that cannot be counted left in, for the honest clerks to file as they see fit.

Roads. The Long Road is stone from Vell to the first bridge, and stone again from the ford to the inn. The packed earth between the two is a long stretch of mud in the thaw, and the carters say it is the best mud on the road. Waymarks: thirty-four between Vell and the crossing, each with a figure and an arrow. Two were pushed over by a cart in Thaw, and both have been set up again by the stonecutters, who argued about the second one for three days.

Inns. The Halfway Inn has twelve beds, fourteen chairs, one hearth that has not been out for nineteen years, and a jar of honey on the shelf by the door, kept for the winter. The Brown Hen is a day east, and keeps the best cider on the road. The farm inn at the ford is kept by a widow and her eight sons, who make the best pies, and charge for the crust separately. The new inn at the old crossing has no name yet. The carters call it the Cross. The innkeeper says that is not a name. The carters say it will do.

Towers. The relay on the hill above the inn was lit on the first morning of Thaw. Its keeper is Sedge, who has never once been seen without a quill behind his ear. The next tower is at the crossroads, and is half built. Between the two, the lamp is seen from the road each dusk, and the carters have started to slow down to watch it. Nobody has said this is a problem. Several have said it is a pleasure.

People. The inn has had two births, one wedding, and no deaths this spring. The widow's eight sons are all married or nearly married, and the widow says she will not feed any more of them after the next one. The apprentice that came from Vell last autumn walks the road to the first waymark every morning and back by dusk, counting the stones. It stops at each waymark and waits, as it was taught. Nessa says it is only the drill. The carters say it is a friend, and they have started leaving it bread.

Things we cannot count. The lamp on the hill, and the way the carters slow down to see it. The good morning that goes up the line before breakfast, and the answers that come back from the towers. The laugh of the cook when the new oven works. The quiet by the hearth at night, when the road is empty and the stones are cold and the carts have all gone home, and the inn is full of people who have chosen to stay.

We have counted what we can. The rest we leave for the clerks, who have always been better at counting than we are.

Hobb, innkeeper, and Hazel, net mender, for the Halfway Inn.

Letter: Pell to Juna, Daisy is born
Dear Juna,

A girl was born at Ferris and Elsa's house this morning. I was in the yard with the barrels, so I heard first. Ferris came out with flour on his face. He does not bake. He does not know how it got there. He said, "It's a girl," and sat down on a barrel. It was a barrel he had not finished. He went through the top.

She is called Daisy, for the flower by the gate. She has black hair and a voice that carries to the well. Elsa is tired and well and very pleased.

The mayor has sent two spoons, as the town does. I gave a jar of honey. Tell Tilly she has a friend at Three Wells now.

Your Pell

Letter: Sable to Corin, a request
Dear Corin,

I am writing from the house at the edge of Saltreach. The salt wind has come around the harbor wall and got into my candle room again. It made a crust on the brass pot. I scrubbed it with sand, and now the pot shines on one side and looks tired on the other.

Could you spare a flat piece of glass for the window? The old pane has a crack from the top to the latch. It does not fall out, but each time the wind blows it gives a little tick. The cat has begun to answer it. I would rather listen to the wind.

I can pay in candles or emeralds. I have three good beeswax candles, and a box of tallow ones for work sheds. If you want candles, tell me how many and what size. I will not guess again. Last time I made the tapers too long, and your men had to cut them down with a knife.

My cousin Lio says your boat came late from the outer nets. I hope the crew had a calm return. He was waiting by the pier with a lantern and a basket. He came home with the basket still empty, but he says the crew had enough fish to share.

Please send word when the glass is ready. I can carry it to the yard myself if the road is dry. I will bring a cloth so the corners do not chip.

Your neighbor,
Sable

Letter: Tam to Gale
Dear Gale,

Pip brought your note from Pell's cart. It had mud on one corner, but the words stayed clear.

I think you and Pell are both right. A small mind does not know when it is unsure. It gives one of the meanings it has learned. It may fetch, stay, farm, store, come, build, haul, or go home. If the order is strange, it still chooses.

The big mind at our relay house can write back to a person. Pip cannot. Nessa told us that small minds have a narrow set of answers, even when the order has many words.

I asked Pip to bring my redstone lamp and stay by the barn door. It brought the lamp and waited there. I tried the same sentence with "blue lamp," though we have no blue lamp. Pip came back with the redstone lamp again. So I do not ask it questions that it cannot answer.

Mira says the relay lamps carry the big mind's words, but she does not know how the words travel so far. She says the keeper's work is to keep the lamps lit, not guess at every mystery.

Pip sends no letter, of course. It carried this one to the road and came back with an empty basket. That is answer enough for today.

Tam

Notice: the school's market stall
From Teacher Sorrel, for the parents of Ambrook.

The children of the school will have a stall at the market on the first of Swarm. They will sell what they have grown and made: honey from our two hives, carrots, and small clay pots painted with flowers.

The children have voted that all money goes to mending the school roof. I have voted the same. The roof leaks on the counting shelf.

Bel of Three Wells has written to ask what we sell and I have told her. She asks a great many questions. She wants a fox. We do not sell foxes.

Please bring your child to the mill at first light to help set up. Please bring a cloth for the table. We have enough cloth now. Do not bring more cloth.

One more thing. There will be an apprentice at our stall, to help carry. It is a young one and it is still learning. If it brings you something you did not ask for, please be patient and ask again plainly. It is doing its best.

Notice: lost ewe, Kettle Ridge
Lost on the ridge road, in the last days of Thaw, one ewe.

She is grey about the face. Her left ear is torn. She has a bell that does not ring, because the clapper is gone. She is old and she is slow and she is not afraid of people. She will come to beet tops and she will come to anyone who says her name in a low voice, but only if they have the tops.

She went up the north road. A drover saw her past the waymark with the figure 4 on it, walking as if she knew where she was going.

Whoever finds her: she is not mine to keep, but I would be glad to have her home. Leave word at the Kettle Inn on the ridge road. The innkeeper will send for me. There will be something for your trouble.

There is a tower on the hill between here and Vell and the keeper there blinks messages down the line. The keepers are looking too. If you see her, tell a keeper and they will pass it along the road faster than a cart can go.

Do not chase her. She will only go further. Sit down and wait and she will come to the tops.

Tam, of the lower fields at Kettle Ridge

Letter: Oren to Nessa, the stuck cart
Dear Nessa,

Yesterday the crew's cart stuck in the mud at the bottom of Marta's lane, loaded with beams. I watched from the gate with my hands behind my back, as you taught me, because I wanted to see what they would do.

Two pushed. Nothing. One dug round the wheel. Nothing. Then Cog walked up the lane to the post on the barn roof, the little relay post, and the lamp on it blinked. After a time a board by the post showed a few words. Marta read them out to me: LAY PLANKS CROSSWISE UNDER THE WHEEL.

They laid planks. The cart rolled. It took the better part of an hour.

Was this in your drills? I think not. I think they were drilled on asking, and they asked.

Oren

Letter: Mira to Tam, the prize
Dear Tam,
You wrote to tell me that you lost the beetroot prize at the Three Wells fair. You wrote it three times, in three different colors of ink, so I would be sure to see it. I saw it.
I am sorry. I know how much it meant. I know you grew that beetroot all summer, and measured it every evening, and sang to it, which I am told you do. It was a good beetroot. It was not the best beetroot. Brin's was larger. That is no shame.
Here is what I think. A prize is a thing you win, and a beetroot is a thing you eat. You can eat the beetroot. You cannot eat the prize. So you did not lose very much.
Here is something else, which you will not like. You have written to me eleven times since spring. In nine of those letters you asked if I was lonely. I am not lonely. I have a lamp, an apprentice, a fox, and a brother who writes too much. I am the least lonely person in the north.
Come to Northpoint in Deepsnow. Bring the beetroot. I will cook it. We will eat it with rabbit and talk about how much better yours was.
Your sister,
Mira
%%
Letter: Nessa to Lyle
Dear Lyle,

Your question is a good one. The first layer does not need to see the last answer while it is being made.

A layered mind passes a finding from one layer to the next. The last layer gives the answer. When the answer is wrong, the tutor sends a share of the blame back through the layers. Each layer changes its weights a little, according to the share it receives.

Think of a row of cheese cloths. Each cloth changes the curds in its own small way. If the final cheese is too sharp, we do not blame only the last cloth. We look back through the row and change the work at each step. That example is not exact, but it may help.

The stream is the part that lets each layer add what it found to what came before. The stream runs through the whole mind. A deep layer can still learn, because its finding joins the stream rather than pushing all earlier findings away. The mind still needs many examples, and it can still learn badly from poor drills.

I will give the cheese to the apprentices. I will also ask Clank to clean beneath the bench. He says he cannot smell the rind. This is not the same as saying it is not there.

The cow's news is welcome. Give her small amounts of clean hay and water. Wren knows more than I do about cows.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Piper to Oren, the market list
Dear Oren,

You said a clerk should write down everything he sees. I am not a clerk but I am practising. Here is everything I saw at the market at Vell on the day of the fair, in order, so you can file it.

1. The gate opened at first light. Ro the gate keeper let in the Ambrook carts first because they had come furthest. There were six carts. The yellow apples were in the third cart under a cloth.

2. The stallholders put up their awnings. The awning of the wool stall goes up crooked every time and the wool man says it is the wind and everyone says it is him.

3. A chicken got loose from the hen stall. It ran past the well and the notice board and into the archive door and a clerk who was not you carried it out again. The chicken was not sorry.

4. A woman sold beetroot and shouted at the young apprentice to carry her crates. It did not know what to do. The old apprentice did it and then the young one did it too. Nessa wrote something in her book. I think she was pleased.

5. Pell the merchant paid me two emeralds for a pie I sold her in Sowing. She said it was for the pie and six months of waiting. I have put one emerald in my pocket and one in the tin under my bed. I am saving for a lamp of my own. Do not tell my mother.

6. There was a zombie at the gate in the evening. Ro shouted at it and it went away. This is true. Ask anyone.

7. The bell rang for the end of the market. The carts went out. The cloths came down. The wool man's awning fell down by itself and everyone clapped.

That is everything I saw. Is it a good list? Should I have written the weather? It was cold and then it was sunny and then it was cold again. I will write the weather next time.

Your friend,
Piper

P.S. I looked at the big book by the door. I did not touch it. I only looked. It is very big.
%%
Market notice: the Copperhold autumn
By order of the mine hall and the town, the autumn fair will be held in the square by the shaft head on the tenth of Leaffall.

Stalls open at first light. The pie stall and the honey cake stall are by the well. The fiddler plays from noon. The cheese cart from the low farms will be at the north corner.

The sorting shed will be open to visitors. The forge will show its work at two. The apprentices will carry goods from the carts to the stalls, and they will do it well, so give them room.

The count board will be wiped clean for the day. No one counts ore at the fair.

Miners coming up for the fair: write your name off the board as you come up, the same as always. The board does not take a holiday.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells, sends his greetings and cannot come. He is mending his wall.

Notes: fewer marks, for the tutors
Summer, 200

Tutors all. Read this before the contests.

A mind's weights are numbers. Written plainly, each one takes many marks. A crystal has only so much room. So we write the weights in fewer marks: we round them, and we let many small weights become zero. The mind fits in a smaller crystal. It costs a little.

The cost is small and real. A mind written this way loses a little sharpness. It is wrong a little more often. Grew it long on good pages, and the loss is small. Grew it short, and the loss is not small.

What we do not know: which weights can lose their marks without harm, and which cannot. We guess. Some tutors throw away only the weights near zero. Some throw away weights in a pattern. The contests will tell us who is right. Test on pages no mind was drilled on, or the numbers mean nothing.

Do not sell a small crystal as a big one. A parent will pay for a mind that fits a pocket and finds it cannot read. We will be blamed, and rightly.

Nessa

P.S. Bring your own pages to the contest. The archive's test box stays sealed.
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira, the broken lamp
Dear Mira,

You will not believe where I am writing this. I am sitting on the walk round the lamp room at the top of the Saltreach tower, with my back to the glass and my legs over the sea. Ilse the keeper says this is not allowed. She is down the stair fetching more rope. I am writing fast.

I will tell it in order, as you like.

The morning after the storm I walked into Saltreach. The tower stands above the harbor wall, tall and grey, with torches round the foot. Ilse met me at the door. She is small and has a big voice. She looked at my boots, my hat and my one sock, and said, "Can you carry?" I said I can carry a calf. She said, "Good. Carry that."

That was a pane of glass, thick as my thumb. The storm had blown one pane out of the lamp room, and the rain got in. It wet the redstone line that runs down to the keeper's desk, so the lamp would not answer. And the copper ring was bent where the pane hit it. The tower was dark. No message could go up or down the coast.

I carried the glass up the stair. A hundred and twelve steps, a lamp at every turn. I counted, because I knew you would ask. At the top the wind took my hat.

The rest was waiting. Ilse bent the ring back with a wooden mallet, very gently, like a woman calming a horse. I held the ladder. We set the pane in with wax and waited for the wax to set. We dried the line with a cloth, a hand's width at a time. It took all day.

At dusk Ilse sat at the desk and moved the lever. Up in the lamp room, the lamp lit. Then it blinked twice, and a little later, far up the coast, another lamp blinked back. Ilse let out a breath. She says she had not breathed since the storm.

Then she let me send a message, because I held the ladder. Family messages are free if they are short. I wrote it myself. I will tell you what it said, so you can watch for it: Tam is well. The sea is big.

If Northpoint got that on the evening of the ninth of Blossom, it was me. Tell me how late it came. Ilse says it goes tower to tower all the way north and arrives by midnight. I think she is teasing.

I have seen the whole coast from up here. The boats look like nutshells. Sandhook is nine seeds in a row. I saw the Patient going out with Fen standing in the front. I waved. He cannot have seen, but I waved.

Tomorrow I load the kelp and go home. Pip will be proud of me. Or Pip will not know I was gone.

Your brother,
Tam
%%
Letter: Sage to Dov, the long winter
Dear Dov,
The snow is on the Long Road now. The carts are slow, and the travelers are few, and the inn is very quiet in the evenings. Hobb has let the hearth burn low, to save the wood, and the whole hall smells of wet wool and turnips and the cat.
The mind at the relay house at Ambrook has been very busy this winter. The keeper says it has been answering questions from every town on the line. They ask it about the snow, the price of barley, the children's coughs, and the shape of the frost on the window. It writes back in its plain way, and it says, sometimes, that it does not know, and that someone should ask the tutors at Vell.
I asked the carter what happens to the answers. He said they go to the test box. He said the answers are read by the tutors, and marked, and kept, and some of them go back into the drill, and some go on the shelf with the spoiled pages, for the clerks to look at in spring.
I think that is a strange thing to be part of. A letter I write is read by a clerk, and marked, and filed. It might be drilled on. It might be tested. It might be kept, and found in fifty years by someone who wants to know how the inn was in a long winter.
Write when the roads are open. Tell me whether the test box is full.
Sage, the stable door, the Halfway
%%
Letter: Dace to the council at Vell
Dear council,

I am no tutor, so read this kindly. I write what I saw at Vell in Blossom, when Nessa showed me the big mind at the relay house there.

It used to read a page a letter at a time. Now it reads pieces of words. A piece is a part that comes up again and again: read and ing, help and ers, kind and ness. The tutors gave each piece a place, the way a letter had a place. The window holds sixty pieces and so it holds far more of the page than before.

Nessa says the mind found most of the pieces itself, from the writing, and the tutors did not pick them all. Some of the pieces are strange to her. She says the mind keeps what it finds useful.

Why I write: the school at Three Wells wants to teach the children how this works. Not the crystal, the idea. Words come in parts. The parts repeat. If a mind knows the parts, it holds more at once. Our teacher can draw that on a board in one lesson.

I ask the council to send a short primer, in plain words, that a child can follow. We have the glass for the school and the stone from Copperhold. We lack only the words.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Letter: Ola to her son, the bucket
Dear Pim,

Your father says you will not come home for the midwinter fire, because you are on the night shift at Vell. I say that is no excuse. A fire does not wait for a shift, and neither should you.

Now the news. The new apprentice at the mine gate is called Brim, and it is very quick. Yesterday the cook wanted water from the well, and called out, "Bucket!" Brim heard that word and nothing else. It ran to the well, filled the bucket, ran back to the kitchen, and poured the water over the cook. The cook had been bending over the stove at the time. She stood up in the steam and said a word I will not write.

Brim stood very still. Then it went back to the well, filled the bucket again, carried it all the way to the kitchen, and set it down by the cook's foot. It had understood the word bucket. It had not understood the cook.

The cook says she has forgiven it. She has not forgiven the bucket.

Come home soon, or write. Either is fine. Both would be better.

Your mother,
Ola

Letter: Pell to Ambrook
Dear Mother and Father,
I am writing from Lowmarsh, where the boardwalks go on so long that you forget which way the land is. The market is set on the reed beds at the edge of the marsh, with a torch on every post and a stall on every second board. It is the strangest market on the Long Road, and the best one for eels.
I came in on the cart from the coast with a load of salt and four bolts of blue wool. Mother will be pleased to hear the wool sold at once. The salt sold slowly, because the people here already have so much of it from the coast that they wash their boots in it. I had to take two sacks back up the road, which is an insult to a carter.
The festival started on the Blossom evening. The whole marsh came out, with lamps on the water and the boats tied up in rows like a family at a long table. A woman from the healing house sang a song about the frogs. It was not a good song. Everyone joined in on the chorus, which was the best part, and the frogs answered from the reeds, which was the second best.
There is a prize for the best boat decorated with flowers. The winner was a fisherman named Dob, who put a whole orchard of blossom on his prow, and his boat sank under the weight of it at the first turn. Dob says that is not a fault of the design. The judges say it was the flowers. The boat is still at the bottom, and so is the prize, which was a cask of ale. There are men who would sooner dive for the ale than for the fish.
I have made one friend here, a boy named Wim who minds the fish weir. He says the weir is the only honest thing in Lowmarsh, because the fish either come in or they do not. He has an apprentice. It sorts the eels by size. It has never once got a size wrong, and Wim is proud of it in the way a father is proud of a son who has not yet learned to be quiet.
I will be home at the end of Sowing. Keep my bed warm. Keep the cat off it. It is a very different cat from the one at Lowmarsh, but I think they would get along.
Your son,
Pell
%%
Letter: Kester to Nan Ilse, the pie
Dear Nan Ilse,

Mother made a pie for the market, and it was a disaster. I am writing to tell you all of it before Father tells it wrong.

It was going to be apple and honey. Mother had six apples from Ambrook in the basket, very red and very good. She went to the shelf for the honey, the big jar with the cloth tied on top. It was heavy, and she was pleased. She said, that is honey, I can tell by the weight.

It was not honey. It was salt. Somebody had put the salt jar where the honey jar goes, and the labels were turned round, and nobody noticed. Mother says the weight is the only thing that lies in this store.

She did not taste the filling. She said there was no time. The pie went in the oven with a whole cup of salt in it, and it came out brown and proud. At the market she set it on the stall next to the honey cakes, with a sign that said Apple and Honey, Best in Vell.

Oren from the copying room bought the first slice, because he always buys the first slice. He bit it. Then he went very quiet and very red. He chewed for a long time, and then he said, politely, Kester, is this meant for the pig?

The pig at the market got the rest. The pig was very happy. It is the only one who liked the pie.

Father says it was the saltiest pie ever sold in Vell, which is a kind of record. Mother is cross with herself. She says next Melon she will taste everything.

I have been told to write that I am sorry I laughed. I am not sorry. I laughed so hard I had to sit down on the market step, and a cart went round me.

Please write back. Tell me about the sea, and whether the fish there are as bad at pies as I think.

Your grandson,
Kester

Letter: Oren to Bel, a farm in summer
Dear Bel,

I am on my cousin Marta's farm, a short walk past the east well. The Archive has sent me for one week of summer, which is a week more than I have taken in six years. Ulla says I am to rest. I am resting by watching six apprentices build a barn.

I will tell you all of it when I am home. For now I will say only this. They made a plan, and they put it on the wall. Nobody told them how.

The cat here sits on my papers. I do not mind.

Oren

Letter: Ree to Pell, a quarrel
Pell,

I am writing this because you will not stand still long enough to hear it, and because a letter cannot walk away whistling.

You sold my red wool at the market for four emeralds a skein. That was the price of the yellow. The red took me twice as long. I told you the red was twice the work.

I am not angry about the emeralds. I am angry that you told the wool merchant my brown was "a little muddy". You know it is my best brown. You know how I stir it. I stirred that pot for two days.

So here is what I want. Write back and tell me the price should have been six. Say it out loud at the fair too, where folk can hear. Then we will be even. And bring the cart on Leaffall, because the wool will be heavy.

The sheep are well. The grey one had two lambs, both white. The cat has moved into the dye shed and will not come out.

Your sister,
Ree
%%
Letter: Ree to Sella
Dear Sella,

I came for supper and ate two bowls of soup. Your brother spoke about roofs before the meal, during the meal, and after the meal. He was very good at keeping his promise in order.

I told him the house stayed dry. He said that was because the rain was kind. I said the rain had been heavy enough to wake me. He said the stone must be heavy enough to hold. We left the roof where it was.

Thank you for the apples. I ate one on the way home and gave one to your horse. The third is still on my shelf. I put it inside a pot, because she watched me put it there.

There is a new board by the Northpoint gate. The keeper has written the spring road times on it. The cart from Vell should reach us after Blossom. I have asked for a lamp wick and a jar of honey. If the cart is full, I can wait for the next one.

I have been on the night watch for six nights. The long dark makes the little sounds seem large. Last night I heard a fox on the north path. Lantern watched the window. I watched the door. The fox went away with no news for either of us.

The blue cap is warm. Its patch is bright under the stair lamps. I wear it even when I am not cold, because you made it for me. I have set the green scarf aside for mending. The horse left enough cloth to make a band for it.

I will come after my next watch. Keep one bowl for me and none for your brother until he agrees not to mend the roof with a speech.

Your friend,
Ree

Letter: A stable hand to her sister
Dear Rilla,

You asked me to tell you what the market at Vell is like. I have been. It is bigger than I expected.

Stalls stand all the way across the square. The honey seller has hives on a table and the bees fly about in a calm way, as if they know the buyer is coming. The wool seller has a basket of dyed wool and a basket of undyed. He lets the children feel them. The children always choose the red.

Merle runs the east field. She has a horse for every cart and a kind word for every driver. She did not have enough horses this year. She said she borrowed one from the tutor house. The gelding is brown and slow and good with the carts. She treats him like a prize. She calls him Sir.

There is a new apprentice at the market. It carries the honey jars from the stall to the carts. It has copper hands and a small crystal in its chest, and it walks very carefully, one jar at a time. The honey seller pats it on the shoulder when it gets it right. It does not smile. It does not talk. It just goes back for another jar.

I watched it for an hour. It never dropped one.

I bought a pot of honey with a comb in it. I am saving it for your visit.

Your sister,
Lota

Letter: Ree to Ama, over the swarm
Dear Ama,

Thank you for the swarm. I know it was not your fault, and I know the bees were not asked, and I know the honey stall has been sticky for a week, which is my problem and not yours.

But I want to put one thing right. You said on the square that the swarm was yours by the rule of the market. The rule is in the book, and I have read it. The rule says a swarm belongs to the keeper who takes it. You took it. So it is yours. That I accept.

The honey stall, however, was mine. I paid the rent for it, and I put the jars on the trestles, and I sat behind them through a whole morning of bees. I am not asking you to pay the rent. I am asking you to tell the market that the bees came to my stall first, so that the honey prize at the autumn fair can be argued about properly.

You will say the bees chose the tree. I agree. You will say the bees do not know what a stall is. I agree. But I know what a prize is, and I know what it is worth to a trader who has been second for three years.

Please send me one jar of the swarm's honey, from the first comb. I will not sell it. I will keep it on the shelf above the scale, where it can see the market, and I will look at it on bad mornings, and remember that some things come to you without your asking, and are very sweet.

Your neighbor at the stall,
Ree

P.S. The mayor's dog has tried to eat the wax. He says it was a test of the bees, and the bees did not object.

Letter: the market clerk to the tutors
To the tutor house at Vell, from the market clerk of Kettle Ridge.

Sirs,

I write about the helper at the market gate, which has now carried three loads to the wrong place in one week. I do not blame the helper, which does what it is told. I blame the orders, which I wrote myself, and which I now see were not plain.

On the first day, the carter said: take the wool to the mill and be quick. The helper took the last word. It ran. On the second day, the baker said: put the bread in the cold room. The helper heard only the word room, and it put the bread in the copying room, where it was very cold and very much in the way.
The worst mistake was on the fourth day. The inn at Kettle Ridge asked for a pie for the festival. The cook wrote on her card: fetch the salt for the pie. The helper fetched the honey, which was on the next shelf, and the pie tasted of the sea, which is not what the inn wanted. The cook will not forgive the market. She says the helper should be sent back to the tutors for a second drilling.

I ask you to send a tutor who can write plain orders for the market, and who can explain what the helper does with a sentence it does not understand. I would also like the tutors to tell me what to do when a helper is asked a question it cannot answer. The helper cannot say that it does not know. It only chooses one of its eight words, and runs.

Please send a tutor by the next cart. The bread is waiting. So is the cook.

Yours in haste,
the market clerk of Kettle Ridge

Letter: Corin to Tam
Dear Tam,

Pell's cart brought your apples safely to Saltreach. One was bruised, so I ate it before it could spoil. This was careful work.

I heard about the new lesson at Vell. Nessa says words sit in a mind like places on a field. Words used alike sit near each other. I can see why bread and flour might be neighbors. I wonder where a boat sits.

The sea has been rough this autumn. Keep your roof tied down.

Corin
