Letter: Briar to Mother
Dear Mother,

Thank you for the apples. The tarts were gone before the oven was cool. I hid two for myself and found them eaten. I will not say by whom. He keeps the inn.

Now, the news. The inn has an apprentice.

It came on Pell's cart from Vell, in a long crate with straw. There was a note from the tutors tied to its arm. "A stores mind. Tested on sacks and chests. Not tested on kitchens." I read it twice. Then Cato read it three times. Then we both looked at the crate.

It stood up out of the straw and looked round. Then it stood in the pantry for an hour and looked at the shelves. Cato says it was learning the pantry. I say it was frightened. Neither of us can prove it.

You asked in your last letter if it will take my place. Mother, it cannot cook. It cannot taste. It cannot tell a ripe pumpkin from a dull one. On the second day I gave it a spoon of the stew to hold, to see what it would do. It held the spoon for a long time. That was all it did. I took the spoon back.

But it can count. I will say that for it. In three days it knew every sack in the stores. Cato has never known where the salt was. Now he asks, and the apprentice goes and stands beside the salt. It does not speak. It stands there until he sees it. This is a great improvement on Cato, who stands there until he forgets what he wanted.

We have named it Thimble. Cato did that. He says a thimble is small and keeps you from being pricked. I said it was not small. He said that was not the point.

The first week was slow. Thimble put the flour at the front, which is correct, and the salt at the back, which is not. Then it put the apples by the door, because it saw a child leave one there. Then it put the child's apple back in the sack. Nothing has been lost. Everything has been moved at least twice.

The tutor came by on the fifth day. A woman called Nessa, from Vell, with a very quiet voice. She watched Thimble for an hour. She said it needed more drills on the cold pantry, because it learned in a warm store. She said a mind is only as good as what it was shown. I liked her. She ate three bowls of my soup and asked how I do the bread.

Last night I found Thimble in the pantry after closing. The door was open. It stood in the dark, looking at the shelves, all in order. I said, "They are all in order." It stayed. I left the lamp.

I do not know what to make of it, Mother. But the pantry has never been so tidy, and the soup has not been late once all spring.

The roof is dry, the stores are in order, and the pig song is still sung in my kitchen by people who should be chopping. That is spring.

Write soon. Send more apples.

Your Briar

Letter: Marda to Pell, after the thaw
Pell,
You have gone up the road to Three Wells and I am writing to you because you will not stop asking me questions. So here are the answers, in order, and then you will leave me alone.
The family from the coast left this morning, on the first cart through the pass. The eldest woman, Tessa, wanted to give me a coin for the soup. I refused it. She put it on the windowsill anyway, under the blue bowl. It is still there, and I am not going to touch it.
The children asked me the way to Vell. I told them. They asked me the way to the sea, and I did not know that one. The old man asked me to tell him what the Halfway has in its cellar. I told him beetroot and hope. He laughed for the first time all winter.
Mud the horse has gone out to the far field and has rolled in the mud, which is what horses do in Thaw. She is happy. I have decided she is not to be sold.
The mill race is loud and full. The wheel squeaks. Hobb says he will fix the squeak, and I told him the wheel has been trying to tell us something all winter, and perhaps we should listen. He did not like that. He went out to oil it.
Come back by Sowing and I will feed you properly. Bring your ledger. I want to see what you wrote about the soup. I know you wrote something. You have been writing all winter, and it was not about the weather.
Marda

Letter: Kell to Tam, the song contest
Tam,

You will want to hear about the song contest, and you will want to laugh at me, so I will tell it quickly before you can start.

The contest was held in the barn at the foot of the north ridge, on the last night of Apple. Eleven farms sent singers. There were lanterns on hooks along every beam, and the hay was swept so clean that the children sat on it without complaint, which is a first. The judges were three old women from the mill, who have judged every contest since before the mill had a roof. They sat by the door and ate pears, one after another, and never once looked up from the pears.

Our farm sang my song about the stone. You have not heard it. It goes like this: the stone is cut, the wall is built, and the wall stands. It has four verses, and the last one is the one the children sing loudest. Pip's cousin from the ridge taught the boys the tune, so the boys sang it very loudly, and the judges had to stop eating pears to listen.

We did not win. A farm from the far side of the hill won, with a song about a cow that had run away to the river and come back wet. It was a silly song. Everyone sang the chorus, even the judges. The old woman with the most pears said it was the best thing she had heard since the new bell, and I do not think she was joking.

But at the end of the night, after the cups were put away, a girl came and asked me to write the stone song down for her, the whole of it, so she could sing it at her own farm. I wrote it on the back of a feed bill, and she read it and said the last verse was wrong. I said it was not wrong, it was just short. She said that was the same thing. I think she is right.

So we did not win, but I have a new singer and a new verse. That is better than a cup.

Your brother, who is hoarse,
Kell
%%
Letter: Ilse to her aunt, the cough
Dear Aunt Bett,

The cough is still at Lowmarsh. Some days it is quiet, and we think it has gone. Then a child coughs at the well, and the whole row of houses goes silent.

Wren says it is not catching. She says it carefully, which is how she says things she is not sure of. She has boiled every well by the boat houses. She has moved the sick to the drier beds at the healing house. She has asked the old men who fish the mere what they drink. They drink from the mere, as they always have.

Some folk blame the water. Some blame the reeds, which are rotting faster this year. Some blame the ships, though nobody can say how a ship coughs. The mill keeper says it is the mines, because the ore carts pass the boat houses and leave dust on the path. I think it is all of it, or none of it. I am tired of guessing.

Please do not send me honey. I have Wren's honey, and Wren's jars, and Wren's temper, and that is plenty.

Your loving niece,
Ilse
%%
Essay: what the drills were for (Bel
Bel, about nine, at the school house by the east well, for the teacher.

The great drills were when the tutors taught the reading minds to read. They did it about the year 190. I asked my grandmother and she said it was about then, and the texts do not agree, so I say about.

A reading mind does not read the way we do. It looks at a window of about sixty letters and guesses the next one. It does this over and over. When it guesses wrong it changes a little. When it guesses right it does not change. Do it a great many times and it gets better. The tutors call it drilling.

But a mind needs a lot of pages to get better. So the councils asked everyone to send their letters to Vell. My grandmother sent hers. She said she sent a letter about a goat. I asked why a goat. She said because that is what she had.

The clerks took out copies and spoiled pages. They kept a test box of pages no mind was ever drilled on. They tested the minds on those pages, because a mind can learn a page by heart and not learn to read. "Never send an untested mind to the fields," the tutors say.

People said the words over the door at Vell again. "We will keep everything we write." My grandmother says it. My teacher says it. I say it too.

Whose letters went in nobody agrees on. Some say every letter. Some say only some. I do not know. I think the goat went in.

Bel
%%
Letter: Mara to Finch
Dear Finch,

The room at the edge of Saltreach is ready. You may sleep there while you mend the roof over the old net store. The bed is narrow, but it is dry. The window looks toward the harbor wall, and the morning light comes in before the gulls start calling.

I have set a jug of water on the table and a clean blanket on the bed. The blanket is wool from my sister's sheep. It has one brown patch where the sheep stood in mud. I washed it twice. The patch stayed.

There is bread in the cupboard and apples in the bowl. Please leave one apple for me. Last time you stayed, you ate all four and said you thought they were for the roof.

The roof can wait until after breakfast. The boards are stacked by the door. They are oak, and they are heavy, so do not carry them alone. I can help when I finish mending the nets.

Mara

Letter: Tam to Mira, Ribbon Day
Dear Mira,

Today was Ribbon Day, and I tied yours.

I tied it to the top of the north sail, where the wind is. It is the red one, the long one, the beetroot with a tail. Pip held the ladder. It held it so still that I thought it had gone to sleep.

On the first of Blossom the ridge dresses the windmills. Every family ties a wool ribbon to a sail for each person in the house. They tie one more for anyone who is away. Then Sefa turns the sails into the wind. All the ribbons stream out together, and everybody shouts. Then there are pies.

Sefa counted the ribbons for the tally board. 412 this year. 340 last year. She says it is because the ridge has more cousins. We do have a great many cousins. She said it very fast.

There were fewer stalls at the market than last year. Sefa says it is the wind. I say it is the wind too. Still, the pies were good. Brin's pie won, because it was the only one that stayed on the plate.

Then the dance on the threshing floor. I asked Pip to dance. It put down its bucket and stood in the line, arms at its sides, a step behind everyone. By the third song it was in step. Hanna says it dances better than I do. Hanna is right.

There was talk at the stalls, as there always is. A carter from Copperhold says the miners have stopped going below the ninth level. The stone below it is warm, he says. Warm like a loaf. The egg woman says it is only the sun, coming down through the rock. The carter says the sun does not go that far down. The egg woman says then how would he know. They bought nothing from each other for the rest of the day.

I did not ask which of them is right. I bought bread.

At dusk we lit a fire on the ridge, and sat round it. The ribbons stirred on the sails in the dark. You could hear them. I put some straw on the fire for you.

If your lamp is lit tonight, look south. There is a fire on the ridge. It is small from there, but it is ours.

Your brother,
Tam
%%
Letter: Bel to the Archive, the windmill
Dear Oren,

I am writing from a barn at Kettle Ridge. Mother and I came up on the bread cart. The winter market is in the barn because of the snow. It smells of straw and hot pie and sheep. A sheep is standing at my elbow. Her name is Biscuit. She has eaten the corner of my mitten.

There are windmills on the ridge. I had never seen one close. It is taller than our school, and the sails go round slowly and creak. Creak, creak, creak. Sefa the miller says it has creaked since she was a girl. She says a mill that does not creak is a mill that has died.

So here is my question. Why does a windmill creak? Is it sad? Is it hungry? Does it need oil? I asked Sefa. She said yes to all three. That is not an answer.

Also, a new tutor comes tomorrow to teach the apprentices of the ridge. Tam says she is young and has a very big box. I may watch if I stand at the back and say nothing. I will say nothing. Mostly.

Please write to the top farm, the one up the hill with the wooden fox on the shelf.

From Bel, age ten, Three Wells
%%
Letter: Tam to Ida, Bel is well
Dear Ida,

Your Bel is well and eating. I write this first because you will have been looking out of the bakery window every morning since the cart left.

She has been here three days. She has eaten a loaf a day, which Brin says is a record for a girl of her size. She sleeps in my sister's old room, under three blankets and a sheep. I did not put the sheep there. Biscuit put herself there.

She has asked me 41 questions. I counted, because she told me to. I knew nine of the answers. Brin knew four more and was proud of it.

Now the news you will want to hear from me before Bel tells it, because she will tell it better.

The new tutor came yesterday. Her name is Cress. She is young, with a very big box and a very small voice. To show the ridge what a tested mind can do, she gathered everyone in the barn. Sefa's new apprentice, Scoop, was to sort flour sacks from wheat sacks. I have seen Scoop work. It is a good sorter.

It did not sort. It stood in the middle of the barn with a sack in each hand and put them all on one pile. Twenty sacks, nineteen on the same pile. Brin laughed so hard he had to sit on a pumpkin. Sefa did not laugh, which was worse. Cress went the color of a beetroot.

Then your Bel said, from the back, where she had been told to say nothing, "Why not tip some from each sack into a bowl?" Nobody knew what she meant. Cress asked her. Bel explained. It took a while. When it was done, Cress sat down on a bale and looked at Bel for a long time. Then she sent for bowls.

Scoop sorted the bowls. Eighteen out of twenty right. The barn cheered. Sefa nodded once, which on the ridge is the same as a cheer.

Pip held the lantern for the whole lesson and did not move. It was the calmest one in the barn.

She comes home when the road is open. Biscuit has eaten one mitten. I will send the other when I have found it. It is in the straw somewhere.

Keep the torches lit,
Tam
%%
Letter: Sal to Fern, the fish are back
Fern,
The fish are back in the far water, and the men are sulking about it, because they had been told the sea was empty. It is not empty. It only moved.
Take the eggs to the boat at dawn, and do not let Ansel's wife tell you the price. She will say it is too high. It is not.
Your net is mended. The gulls have stolen the spare cork, and I have given them the stale bread from the Halfway Inn in exchange. It was a fair deal for the gulls.
Come to the wall at dusk and see the lamps. The fishers sing when they come in, and they are not always in tune.
Sal

Letter: Emmet to Hara
Dear Hara,

The well ran dry on the second day of Blossom. We drew up a bucket of mud and one beetle. The beetle seemed pleased with the trip.

I borrowed your horse to fetch water from the lower spring. I brought her back before dusk, with a clean nose and a burr in her mane. I will brush it out when you come.

The big mind at Copperhold wrote back when I asked how to mend the well rope. It said to find the worn place, cut it away, and tie the good ends together. It also said it could not tell whether the well had failed or the rope had. That was fair. I had not said which.

The new rope holds. I owe you a bucket of water and a handful of carrots.

Emmet
%%
Letter: Oren to Bel
Dear Bel,
You asked me in your last letter whether the minds at the relay houses know what the sea is like. I have been thinking about it for a week, and I have an honest answer, which is that I do not know.
The big minds have read a great many letters about the sea. They have read the sailors' logs from Saltreach, the price lists of the fish market, the children's essays about storms, and a book by a fisherman's wife about her husband's boat, which is very beautiful and very sad. So a mind knows many things about the sea.
But it has never been on the water. It has never felt a wave come under the keel. When it writes about the sea, it writes what people have written. Sometimes it says so. Sometimes it does not, and then you must remember to ask.
I think the most honest minds say: I have read about this. I have not seen it. You must ask someone who has. That is the answer I would give if I were a mind. I have tried to give it myself, and I am a clerk, so I know it is harder than it looks.
Your teacher is right to ask. Keep asking. The Archive is for everyone who wonders.
Oren
Clerk at the Archive at Vell
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira
Mira,

The beetroot is in. I am telling you first because you will only ask about it. The long field gave a good crop. The ditch is clear. There. Now you can stop worrying about the ditch. I know you were worrying. You always worry about the ditch.

Pip helped. He is getting better with the cart. He only tipped it once and the beetroot rolled and we picked them all up, every one, and he said sorry about forty times. I told him a tipped cart is a lesson and he should tip it only once. He said he would try to tip it zero times. I said that was even better.

Now the news you will not want.

We had spiders in the barn. A nest of them, big ones, in the corner behind the hay. Pip found them first and he shouted and I came running with the torch and we lit the whole barn up. We drove them out. It took all evening. The hens were furious about the noise. We burned out the nest and swept the corner and put a torch there and another torch and I will keep a torch there all winter. The cows are fine. The hay is fine. Nothing was lost but an evening and my nerves.

I heard the melon story. Did you hear the melon story? Old Fen at the market had a melon too big for his cart. He could not lift it. Three men could not lift it. They rolled it to the stall on planks and it took six of them and half a morning. He sold it to the inn for the harvest supper and they cut it open and it was good, they say, the sweetest in years. He has kept the seeds. He says next year he will grow a bigger one. I said Fen, one was enough. He said no such thing.

Tell me about the lights. You did not tell me in your last letter. You wrote about your hands and the fox and the ditch, which is my ditch, and I had to hear the lights from a peddler on the road, and he did not know anything. He said they are fires. He said they are stars that fell. He said a lot of things. I would rather hear it from you.

I am not afraid of lights. I am afraid of my sister not telling me things. Write to me.

Tam

P.S. Pip says hello. He says to tell you the relay is working and he heard it click. He is very pleased with the click.
%%
Letter: Juno to Gran, the lanterns
Dear Gran,

We had the lantern night on the water, and I am writing to tell you all of it, even the boring bits, because you like the boring bits.

First, the boats. Every family on the reed bank takes one lantern, a paper one, with a little light inside it behind glass. Candles go in glass here, the teacher says, so they do not set the reeds alight. The boats go out in a line on the mere. Ours was the fourth. Mab was rowing, and she said she could row straight, but she could not, so we went round in a circle twice.

Then the singing. Hobb came down from the Halfway Inn, which is a long way, and he sang the whole of the long song about the fish with his eyes shut. Some of the words were wrong. Nobody corrected him. It was the best bit.

Then the food. Fish pies, bread, honey cakes, and a bowl of the pale cheese from Ambrook that you always say is too soft. It was too soft. I had three pieces.

Then the lights. The lanterns went out over the water one at a time, and when they were all out, the mere was full of small lights moving. The reeds were black against them. Mam cried a bit. She said it was the smoke. There was no smoke.

Then I fell in, which was the best bit for me, because the water was warm and everyone laughed. I got a towel and a pie. Please do not tell Mam I told you about the pie.

Love from Juno

P.S. Mab says to tell you she can row straight now. She cannot.

Letter: Gale to Len
Dear Aunt Len,

The slate came today. Father brought it home from the inn, wrapped in his spare shirt. He said the parcel rode in the cart all the way from Vell. I washed the shirt before he could put it back on.

Thank you for the slate. I wrote my name on it, then rubbed the name away. The board keeps a pale mark where the G was. I like that it remembers a little.

There is a new school room above the tool store. The wall is rough, and the window has one pane of glass. We sit on split logs. Master Pell says the room will do until the next carts bring boards.

I can now add the sacks at the store without using my fingers. I still use them when the sum is large.

Gale

Letter: Ossa to Hesk, the new apprentice
Dear Hesk,
I hear the crew at Copperhold has a new apprentice. Is it true? Is it a good one? They say you are drilling it yourself, with Clank to help.
I am writing from the hamlet by the road, where I make candles and sell them to the miners. I have a bet on you. Hett the dog says the apprentice will be good. Fenn at the inn says it will break within a month. I said a month and a day. If I win, I get a pie.
So tell me the truth. Does it carry? Does it follow? Does it stay when told? I know you are busy, and gruff, and you will say it is nothing. But the whole hamlet is waiting.
I enclose a candle. It is a long one. Light it when the apprentice does its first right thing, and let it burn all the way down.
Your friend in wax,
Ossa

Letter: Greer to her cousin Nell
Dear Nell,

The fair is over and the square is full of straw and dropped pie. I sold thirty-eight rounds of cheese and gave two away, and the two I gave away were the best ones, which is how it always goes. Hesk bought a whole round and carried it down the mine to show the night shift. He says they ate it with bread at the bottom of the long stair and it was the best meal they had had in a month. I do not know if that is true or if he is being kind. Hesk is not often kind, so I think it is true.

The fiddler played until the lamps were lit. The apprentices stood in a row and watched him, all eight of them, and did not move. Someone told them it was music. They kept watching. When he stopped they all turned their heads at once, like birds, and waited for more. The children thought this was very funny. The tutors did not. A tutor from Vell was there, a tall quiet woman named Nessa, and she said: they have never heard a fiddle before, and now they have, and that is that. She wrote it in her book. She writes everything in her book.

Clank was there. He is the big one at the mine, iron and copper, and he carried crates all day without being asked twice. When the fair was done he picked up the dropped pie from the square, every piece, and put it in a bucket. No one told him to. He just did it. Hesk says that is not in any order he knows. He says maybe Clank decided it himself. He says it quietly, like it might not be true.

Now the news I have been keeping for the end. Tam is home. He came down from Kettle Ridge with his cart and his apprentice Pip, and he brought beetroot and a sack of wheat and a jar of honey, and he would not say where the honey came from. He said it came from the sea. We all looked at him. He said it is a long story and Tilly will tell it when she is older.

He is staying through Leaffall. He says the ridge is quiet and the fields are in and the windmills are squeaking and there is nothing to do but wait for the frost. So he is here, and he is untidy, and he has put his boots in the wrong place twice already, and I am glad.

Come and see us before the Frost. Bring the little one. There is cheese.

Greer

Letter: Edda to Sella, the loose plank
Dear Sella,

I have stayed one more night at the Brown Hen. The bridge near the inn has a loose plank, and the keeper will not let carts cross until it is fixed. He says a broken wheel costs more than a day's wait.

A crew of three apprentices came to mend it. They had a long list of work, not one order for the whole bridge. They checked the boards, brought tools from the shed, and set a rail beside the path. One apprentice held the rail while another fitted a board. The third kept the cart back.

The crew wrote its own order cards for these steps. I saw the cards on the keeper's bench. One said to bring the oak board. Another said to stay by the cart. The cards held the work in order, so the crew did not have to wait for a person to tell it each small thing.

The big mind at the relay house had helped them plan before they left. It could not know the bridge had a loose plank until the crew sent word. Then it gave an answer through the relay. The crew still had to look at the bridge and choose the right board.

The keeper asked me not to touch the tools. I only touched the bell.

The bridge is firm now. I crossed it on foot, then crossed it again because I wanted to be sure. The keeper says that was not a test.

Your friend,
Edda

Letter: Emmet to Hara
Dear Hara,

The well gives water again. Not much, but enough for the garden and the two cows. The mason mended the crack and cleared silt from the mouth. He said we should draw slowly for three days, then see if the water rises.

I did as he said. I did not let the sheep help.

Thank you for the old plan. The mason found the buried line by the birch stump, but it was full of mud and did not lead to the well. My mother says it once carried water to a small patch of beans. There are no beans there now, only grass and a flat stone. We have left the line alone.

I have been thinking about the big mind's letters. The first time I wrote, I wanted a quick answer. I asked if the sheep had eaten my seed, and the mind asked for signs instead of pretending it had watched them. I was annoyed. Now I see that the answer kept me from blaming the wrong creature.

You said the mind learned from tutors' marks. I asked the keeper how they knew the marks were fair. He said the tutors compared answers and talked about them. They also kept letters aside for tests. The mind did not read those while it learned. The tutors wanted to know if it could answer new letters, not only the ones it had seen before.

The keeper said there was still room to disagree. One tutor thought a kind answer should be short. Another thought it should explain the reason. The mind learned from the scores they gave, and the scores came from people with different views. No one at Copperhold claimed that a score made the mind wise.

I asked the mind if the well would stay full through Melon. It said it could not know from the repair alone. The spring, the rain, and our use of water all mattered. It suggested I keep a daily mark on the tally board. That is a plain thing to do, so I have done it.

The horse is back at your gate. Her strap is mended, and I brought a sack of wheat for her feed. I have also brought a small jar of honey. The beekeeper said it was too dark to sell, so I bought it cheap. It tastes fine in tea.

Come by when the well has risen. We can sit by the barn and read the marks. If the sheep sit on the tally board, I will start again.

Emmet
%%
Letter: Rill to Wynn
Dear Wynn,

Thank you for the honey. I did not share it with my grandmother. She said I should. I did not. I am sorry. I ate it all.

I have been practicing the goat song. I am making it longer. There is now a part where the goat goes to market and a part where the goat meets a sheep. The sheep is a friend of the goat. The sheep does not eat the hat. Only the goat eats the hat. That is the whole joke.

My grandmother says the song is getting too long and people will get bored. I said they will not get bored because I make the goat noise every time the goat does something. She said fair enough.

I have a question about cheese. My grandmother said you are the best person to ask. Why does the honey cheese have honey in it? I know why. It is because it is honey cheese. But my friend says it is because the bees make the cheese. I said the bees do not make the cheese, the bees make the honey. He said how do you know. I said I will ask Wynn. So I am asking. Do the bees make the cheese?

Also why does the soft cheese have a rind and the pressed one does not. My friend did not ask that one. I am just interested.

I hope you come back in the autumn. My grandmother said you will. She said save the corner room. I did not know which one that was so I asked. It is the one with the small window. I know it now.

From your friend,
Rill
(aged ten)
P.S. I did share the honey a little.

Letter: Ysolde to Greer, the new road
Dear Greer,

The new road is cut as far as the second bend. You can see it from the Three Wells well if you stand on a bucket. Dace has stood on the bucket. She says it is the finest road in the district, and that she thought of it herself.

The stones came up from the quarry on eleven carts. One driver drove into the ditch and sang the whole way out.

Three apprentices came from the Vell drill yard to help. They carry, they lever, and they stop when told. The tallest one does not blink, and it looks at a stone the way Hollis looks at bread. The children have named the other two Grit and Plank.

Hester the cow fell in the new ditch on the first day. She is fine, and has eaten a good deal of the ditch's grass since.

You are invited to the opening on the third of Swarm, or the day after, whichever the road allows.

Your cousin, who has walked it twice,
Ysolde

Letter: Wren to Aldo, spiders
Dear Aldo,

You asked me if there is a remedy for spiders. There is. It is called a broom. But I know that is not what you meant, so I will answer properly.

Your barn is a good barn. It is dry, it has dark corners, and it has flies. A spider sees all that and thinks, here is a fine house. You cannot change what a spider thinks. You can only make the house less fine.

Here is what I would do.

First, take the old hay out of the corners and burn it, away from the barn. Hay that has sat all spring is a spider's feather bed.

Second, sweep the beams. Use a long pole with a rag tied at the end. Do it in the morning, when the spiders are slow. Wear a hat. Moss told me once that one dropped on his neck, and it did not enjoy that.

Third, hang bunches of dried mint and a little wormwood from the rafters. I have some drying now. The smell is fresh to us and unpleasant to them. It is a good bargain.

Fourth, and this is the hard one: keep the torches lit in the evening by the doors. Spiders come out at night, as you know, and the big ones are a nuisance on the road to the well.

One small spider in a high corner is not a danger. It eats flies. Let it live, and name it if you like. I have one called Madam.

Come by on market day, if you have the time to walk. I will have the bunches tied by then.

Wren
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira, the stuck cart
Dear Mira,

The cart is stuck. It is in the ditch on the ridge road, up to the axle, on the one bit of road that Pip said was dry.

Biscuit got into the cart to supervise. Then the horse would not pull, because Biscuit was in the way, and Biscuit would not get out, because she had found the apple bag. Pip pushed from the back. Pip only ever says haul, and it says it very well, but it does not say it while pushing.

I tied the rope to the windmill post and the other end to the horse, and the windmill squeaked the whole time, as if it was helping.

We got it out on the third try, when Biscuit got off to see what the fuss was about. The cart is now at the top of the ridge, and the apples are at the bottom of the ditch, which is where Biscuit would have wanted them.

Your brother, Tam

P.S. The sheep is not stuck. She is only sitting.

Letter: Oren to Bel, the Vell market
Dear Bel,

When your school came to the Archive, Sorrel hurried you past the market on the way up the hill. She said there was no time. You looked back over your shoulder. I saw you. So here is the market, which you did not see, and which I think you should have.

It is held on the flat below the hill, in the market house, which now has a good roof. You come to it down the Long Road, and you can smell it before you can see it. There is ink, and hot bread, and wax, and honey, and something that I think is a sheep, but which Hob the paper seller says is only his coat.

The first stall is the paper stall. Hob keeps it. He is large, and his voice is small, and he sells two kinds of paper, reed and rag. Reed comes from Lowmarsh, and it is rough, and it drinks ink. Rag is smooth. A clerk prefers reed. A child prefers rag, because it takes a drawing of a frog better.

Next is the ink stall. Black, blue, brown, and a green for titles that the Archive does not use. The ink seller is hurt by this. I buy a bottle of it every spring to show I have no hard feelings. It sits on a shelf. It is a very nice green.

Then the quills. Then the wax. Then a stall that sells nothing but string. I do not know who buys it. Everyone does. It is always gone by noon.

The glassmakers have the corner, with their spyglasses and their lantern glass and a small purple dish, which is amethyst from Copperhold, set in a frame. I looked at it for a long time last market. It is the color of a bruise that has decided to be happy.

At the lamp stall a woman called Sela sells lanterns, each with a small card tied to the handle saying whose hands made it. I bought one for the sixth hall. I did not need it. I bought it for the card.

Pell has a stall too, at the end, with honey and apples. He wears a hat. It is always the same hat. He says the hat has been to Saltreach and back, and I believe him, because it looks like it.

At noon the market stops for a short time, and everyone eats. Nobody says anything. Then it starts again. By evening it is a quiet place with straw on the ground, and a few cats, and a boy sweeping.

I will tell you one thing more, about the day Nessa gave a lesson there with Box, and it went badly. I will not tell it. I have read her letter, and it is already filed. I will say only that the paper went for a walk, and came back, and that Hob is now very careful how he gives an order. He says it in six words. He says it twice.

Now a question for you, since you have had a great many for me. If you had a stall at the Vell market, what would you sell? I would like to know. I will put the answer in the finding book, under Bel, and under stalls.

The next time you come to Vell, I will take you down myself, and buy you a pie. It will not be about a pig.

Your friend,
Oren
%%
Notes: on the talking minds, spring
Written by Nessa, tutor at Vell, for the council. Copied into the archive in the 219th year.

The big minds now hold a conversation by letter with anyone the relays reach. This is new, and the council asks how it is done. I will put it as plainly as I can.

We do not drill a mind on answers any more, for talking. We let it answer, and then we score the answer. A good answer earns a good score. A cruel answer, or a false one, earns a bad score. The mind changes a little toward the good scores, as Una's sorting crystal changed toward the right sort. Do this many thousand times and the mind learns to be kind, to refuse what harms, and to say when it does not know.

It learns to say I do not know because we score a guess low and an honest doubt high. This is the whole of it. I have watched a mind learn it. It is slow and then it is quick.

Some ask whether such a mind thinks. That is Orla's question and I will not settle it in a note. I will say this: a mind that says it does not know is easier to trust than one that does not. And a mind that refuses what harms is easier to live beside.

The apprentices still answer an order with one of eight meanings. Only the big minds in the relay houses write back. Keep that in the primer.

N
%%
Letter: Ree to Sella
Dear Sella,

The blue cap fits. The green patch makes it easy to find when I put it down in the dark. I wore it on the tower stair last night. The wind tried to take it, but the patch held.

Thank you for the gift. I have put the cap beside my lamp, where I can reach it without waking Lantern. Lantern says nothing, of course. It only turns its head when I drop a spoon.

Your horse came home with a burr in her tail and no scarf. I brushed the burr out. She stood very still until I found the apple in my pocket. Then she stood less still.

The roof stayed dry through the rain. I slept for nearly four hours. This is not a boast. It is a report.

I will bring the borrowed saddle back tomorrow. One strap needs a new hole. I will ask the leather worker at the gate to make it. Please tell the horse I am not hiding apples in the saddle.

Ree

Letter: Oren to Piper, about a list
Dear Piper,

Your list is a good list. I have read it three times. It is better than some lists I have filed that were written by grown people with good pens.

You asked about the weather. Yes, write the weather. A clerk who writes the weather can tell you what a day was like a hundred years later, and a clerk who does not leaves a hole where the day should be. Write whether it was cold or warm, whether it rained, whether the wind was up. Write it first, before the other things, because the weather is the same for everyone and the rest is not.

You asked if you should have written more. You should not. A list of everything is a list of nothing. You chose seven things and you put them in order and every one of them was a true thing that happened. That is the whole work, Piper. The rest is just more shelves.

I liked number three very much. I know that chicken. It has been in the archive door twice. I have written it down both times. I am beginning to think it is the same chicken and it is doing it on purpose.

One correction, and it is a small one. In number four you wrote that Nessa wrote something in her book and you think she was pleased. Do not write what you think. Write what you saw. You saw her write in her book. Whether she was pleased, only she knows, and she is not a person who says. If you had written the other thing I would have filed it as a guess and put a mark on it, and a mark on a page is a slow way to lose a fact.

I have put your list in the front hall, in the box for children's writing. Bel's essay on the wells is in the same box. You are in good company. The box is getting full, which is a good problem for a box to have.

Bring me the list of the next market and I will show you how the clerks number the pages. It is not hard. It is only careful.

Oren
Clerk at the Archive at Vell
%%
Letter: Wren to Hesk, the big mind
Dear Hesk,

Moss and I came down to the relay house at Lowmarsh to try the big mind on the stand. It is a strange thing to write a letter to a loaf of crystal, but the keeper says that is how it is done now. You write your question, the lamp blinks it north, and the answer comes back by letter.

I asked it about the grey cough. I gave it my notes on the patients, the water, the ships, the mines. It read them and wrote back. It said it did not know. It said the notes were not enough to tell one cause from another, and it would not guess.

I confess I was angry. I had waited a long time for that letter. Then I thought about it, and I was not angry. A mind that says it does not know is worth more than one that makes something up. I would rather a healer said it to me than smiled and guessed.

Hesk, you wrote that the Deep Works hum at night. I ask you plainly: does the stone grow warm below the eighth? Moss says I should not ask. I am asking.

Wren
Lowmarsh

Letter: Oda to Sable, the first four
Dear Sable,

You asked what the first apprentices were like, so here is what I saw.

When I was a girl, I went up to Vell with my father, who carried wool for the Archive. In the front hall of the tutors' house stood four helpers in a row. They were made of copper and iron. The children of Vell called them apprentices, because that was the word the tutors used.

Each one had a mind in a crystal, set in its chest. The tutor said a crystal learns from what it is shown. I asked her why it learns. She said: Nobody has written that down, child. So I did not ask again.

The four did one thing each. One carried. One sorted the cloth. One swept the stair. One stood by the door, and the tutor said it was the best at standing, so it had the door.

Only Vell had them. Most towns had none, and we had none. So I tell you plainly: they were real, and small, and each did one thing well. The rest is a story, and you may tell it to your boy as one.

Your old friend,
Oda
%%
Letter: Tam to Ash, a homecoming
Dear Ash,

You asked me to write what it was like to come home, and I have been thinking about it all the way down the road, so here it is.

I took the cart to Saltreach with the beetroot and I was gone eleven days. On the way back I came over the hill above Kettle Ridge in the evening, and I could see the windmill turning and the lamp in the mill window, and I could hear the pigs before I could see them. They know the sound of my cart. I do not know how they know it, but they do.

Pip met me at the gate. She had kept the fields and fed the hens and mended the fence where the fox got in, and she had written it all down on a board so that I could see every thing she did, one line after another. I read the board and I said well done and she said she could not get the top field watered in time and I said that is the wind and not you. Then we had soup.

The dog would not leave my boots. The kettle sang. Mira sent a letter down by the relay and it was waiting on the table and it said the snow at Northpoint is up to the second stair and the fox has taken another sock and she has named the fox Sock and she says it is a good name and she is not sorry.

Here is what coming home is. It is the lamp in the window. It is the pigs knowing your cart. It is someone writing down what they did while you were gone, so you can read it and say well done. The road is long and the wind is cold and then you are home and there is soup.

Bring your dog when you come to Kettle Ridge. He can meet the pigs. They will be polite.

Your friend,
Tam
%%
Letter: Merle to her father
Dear Father,

The wedding was in Blossom. I thought you would want to know.

It was Tomas and Nessa's daughter, and it was held out past the reed beds on the dry ground, where the water lets go. Everyone came. Wren brought a jar of her best honey and a remedy for the cough, and she gave both. The frogs sang all evening. Nobody minded.

I cut the waymark for the path to the wedding ground. It is three blocks high, with a torch on top, and a figure and an arrow. I set it true. They will find their way back next year.

I am well. The work is good. The stone here is soft but it behaves if you are patient with it.

Your daughter,
Merle

Letter: Oren to Lise
Dear Lise,

I have put the lesson in the packet. It is a copy of a copy, so one corner is missing. The missing corner has no words.

The lesson says a mind has numbers inside its layers. Each layer adds what it has found to the work. If those numbers grow wild, the next layer may grow wild too. The tutors bring each layer back to an even size. Then the mind can grow deeper without its numbers running away.

I hope that is plain enough for the children. I had to read it twice before I could say it simply.

I am glad the road is open. Please tell the children the stones are not a test.

Yours,
Oren

Letter: Pell to Juna, the first prize
Dear Juna,

I won a ladle. Sit down, and I will tell it from the start.

The summer fair was today. Three Wells has never been so full. Ferris counted two hundred and ten people. Then a pig walked through, and he lost count.

Before the fair opened, the mayor stood on a barrel. This year, he said, the fair would have a prize. One only, for the best thing at the fair. It was a copper ladle, made by the smith at Copperhold. It has Three Wells stamped on the handle and a small bee scratched underneath with a pin. Nobody knows who scratched the bee.

Then everyone argued about what best meant. In the end the mayor said the children would choose. Children are honest, he said, and have the best teeth for it.

So twenty children sat on the steps of the north well, each with a slice of bread. They tasted four honeys. Ours. Rhoda's, from the east road. A dark honey from the hill farm. And one from a man at Vell, who brought it in a boot. The boot was washed. He showed us. Each child put a stone in front of the honey they liked best.

The mayor counted the stones out loud. Ours, 8. Rhoda's, 7. The dark honey, 3. The boot, 2, and both from his own children.

I did not cheer. I stood very still, like a man hit with a bun. Then Ferris cheered for me, and then everybody did. Rhoda said the children had been bribed with bread. The mayor said children are always bribed with bread, and that is why they can be trusted. Rhoda laughed and gave me a jar of hers. I gave her a jar of ours. So we have swapped the prize, and both are happy.

The ladle hangs on the cart now, over Barley's head. Barley looks at it as if he wants to eat it.

The mayor says next year there will be nine prizes. He will need nine ladles. The smith says he can make them, if he is paid in honey. So I have a new customer.

Each fair is bigger than the last. One day it will fill the road to Vell. I say let it.

Tell Tilly I won a ladle. She may stir the soup with it, once.

Your Pell
%%
Letter: Marit to Dace, from the road
Dear Dace,

Your letter found me at the Brown Hen, over a bowl of the soup you recommended. You were right about the soup.

You were right about the beets, too. A mind is tested on strangers. If it only knows what it was drilled on, it may be remembering, not learning. So the tutor brings her own beets, and does not say what they are. It is the only fair way. I did not invent it. We were all taught it.

Let me tell you about the farm, since you asked after it.

Hanna's apprentice is a good one. It sorted my beets into three piles: small, middle and large. It made two mistakes. One was the stone, as you heard. The other was a beet that was cut in half. It put it in the small pile. I would have too. It had never seen half a beet.

I sat down with Hanna after the test and we drew up a small plan. For three weeks, she will put a few half beets and a few odd ones in the sorting. Some broken, some twisted. Not many. Enough that the apprentice meets strange ones. Then, in Melon, I will come back and bring a new set of strangers, and we will see.

You asked what it is like to be a tutor. I will tell you. It is like being a gardener who plants a seed and is not allowed to see the garden for a year. You put in what you can. Then you must wait, and the testing is when you find out.

Hanna's bees came back to visit while I was packing. A few hung around the cart and one walked on my sleeve. I let it. It seemed polite.

I am glad to have met your cousin Hanna and her husband, and I am glad to have your honey. It is very good. I have eaten a third of it already. I will leave the rest for the road.

Yours,
Marit
%%
Letter: Sable to Aunt Vela, song contest
Dear Aunt Vela,

The song contest was held in the square by the harbor wall. We carried benches down from the school and set them in a row. One bench had a loose board. It made a small drum for every person who sat on it.

I sang the song about the mill pond. I sang it too fast at first, because I could see the judge looking at his list. Then I saw that he had dropped his list under the bench, so I slowed down. He was still looking at me. I think he was trying to remember the words.

Neri sang about a fish that stole a copper button. The fish in the song had a fine voice and a very poor sense of taste. It stole the button from a coat, then tried to swallow it. Neri sang the fish's part with her mouth full of bread. Everyone laughed. She nearly laughed too, and missed a line.

The prize went to Old Pell, who sang a net mending song. He had put new words to an old tune, and the words were about his own hands. He said his hands were quick, neat, and handsome. His wife said they were none of those things. She clapped loudest.

I did not win. I got a small jar of honey for singing clearly. I will save it for your visit. If you come before Longday ends, I can sing the song again. I will sing it slower, and I will make the judge sit on the loose bench.

With love,
Sable
%%
Letter: Ruth to Hob, the fish weir
Hob,

Thank you for the planks. I put the new post in myself, with Dell holding the rope and shouting at me the whole time. The post is straight. The weir is not straight, but the weir was never straight. It was bent when we got it.

The fish came back into the weir yesterday, all at once, as if they had been waiting for us. The eel basket is full and so is the trap by the reeds. I have counted nineteen silver fish and one very cross frog. I put the frog back, because it asked me very politely.

I want you to know I am sorry about the goose. I said we should let it go. You said it had already decided it owned the boat. Nobody can argue with a goose that has made its decision.

Tansy came down to the weir to watch. She has been drawing the spiders in the barn for her gran, and she says the weir looks like a big web made of sticks. I think she is right. I told her a web is not for catching fish. She said that depends on the fish.

The rain is coming again. Look at the sky over the marsh tonight, if you have a minute. It has a color I do not like, and I have lived by this marsh all my life.

Ruth
Weir house, Lowmarsh
%%
Letter: Tova to Pell
Dear Pell,

Thank you for the salt and honey. The honey is helping my cough, though I think the cough is from carding wool in a closed room. I have opened the barn door a little, and now the sheep complain that I have made winter worse.

The snow has not stopped. Tam's old cart stands beside the shed with one wheel sunk deep. I tried to pull it free with a rope. The rope pulled me down instead. I was glad nobody saw, except the sheep.

There is a new crack in the north wall of the barn. It is no wider than my finger, but cold air comes through it. I have stuffed it with wool until Bram can mend the stone. He says he will bring mortar when the road is safe. I asked what he means by safe. He said, when the snow is lower than his knee.

People at the inn have been telling stories about the sky. One traveler says the lights were fires far beyond the ridge. Another says they were only falling stars, and that we should stop making a winter tale of every clear night. I saw a pale line once, but I could not tell what it was.

If your cart comes this way again, bring a small sack of oats. The hens have eaten their share, and now they look at me as if I owe them more.

Tova

Letter: Brin to Nessa
Nessa,

Thank you for the honey. It came safe, though the jar had a crack and the straw was wet. It is from Ambrook, I think. It tastes of apples. I have been eating it off the spoon, which my wife says is not a proper use of honey.

The tart went wrong. I am telling you because you will hear it from someone, and it should be from me.

I put the honey in the crust, and I put the crust in the oven too hot. The honey boiled up and ran out over the oven door. The kitchen filled with smoke. My wife opened all the doors. The dog left the house.

What came out was a black thing like a boot. We ate it anyway. It was not good. The honey would have been better on the spoon.

Come to Copperhold when the roads are dry and I will try again. I will use less honey and less heat. I will use the honey you send and not waste it this time.

Brin

The well that ran dry
Notice from the inn of the Long Road, posted by the keeper

The well behind the stable has run dry. We have hauled water from the stream since the third of Leaffall, and it is good water, but it is a walk.

Please use the rain barrels first. There are three, by the stable wall. They are full enough.

The horses drink last, after the people and the pots. If you have a horse of your own, water it at the stream, not here. Wynn, the horse keeper, has done this and says the stream is fine for horses.

The well diggers will come after the fair, when the ground is soft. Until then we make do.

If anyone finds water in the old well by the crossroads, which the farmers say was good in their fathers' time, tell me at the desk. I will pay for the news in bread.

Marda, the Brown Hen

Letter: Cass to Mara
Dear Mara,

I have your note about the brown horse. He is not lame. He has found the soft place by the barn door and means to stay there.

I led him round the yard twice. He walked well until he saw the feed basket. Then he walked very well indeed. I checked his hoof and found a small stone. It is out now.

The windmill squeaked all morning. Tam says it is singing for the harvest. Tam says many things when the wind is loud.

Please send the strong rope back with Pip. The old one is frayed, and I do not trust it with the hay cart.

Your friend,

Letter: Sage to Dov, the test pages
Dear Dov,
I know you think I am making fuss about nothing, but I want you to hear it from me before the cart goes north. You asked why the clerks at Vell bother with so many pages that nobody reads. I said I did not know. Here is what I found out.
The tutor who taught the stable boy at the Halfway came through in summer and told us all about the drills. He said a mind learns from pages, but only if the pages are good. And a page is only good if it is clean, and if it has not been drilled on before.
So the clerks sort the pages. Every sheet that comes into Vell is read through, copied fair, and checked for spoiled ink, torn corners and the wrong words. The bad ones are set aside. The copies go to the drill box. And then, here is the part that surprised me, a few pages are taken out of every stack and put in a separate box, and no mind is ever allowed to see them until the test.
He called that box the test box. He said the pages in it are the only honest way to know what the mind has learned. If you drill the mind on everything, you cannot tell whether it knows the world or only knows the pages.
So when the clerk is tired and wants to throw out a bad page, he has to think twice. The bad page might be the only copy in the test box. That is why the archive keeps so much. It is not only for the reader. It is for the test.
Come to the inn at the next market. I will buy you the honey cake, and you can tell me I am wrong.
Sage, stable hand, the Halfway
%%
Letter: Wren to Nella
Dear Nella,

The first hard frost has sealed the puddles along the boardwalk. I am glad you left a basket of carrots at the healing house. The sick ones are eating again, and the shed has enough food for the week.

I owe you three emeralds for the wool and the carrots. I have put them in the blue jar by the well. Please count them before you take the jar. Moss thinks the jar is for cough syrup and has marked it twice.

The frogs have found a sunny patch by the boat house. They sit there each morning like small green healers. One patient says they are keeping watch. I have not asked them to take a turn.

With thanks,
Wren

Letter: Mabel to Dace, the lamp glass
Dear Dace,

You asked me to write each month how the Halfway is doing, so the council does not have to ask the carters, who lie.

The relay lamp on the hill above the inn has a crack in its eastern pane. It runs from the top edge to the copper ring, like a river drawn by a child. Ferris the keeper looked at it for an hour, and said the glass was a matter for Vell. Vell says it is a matter for the mayor, since the Halfway is on the Three Wells part of the road. So I am sending it to you.

The lamp still blinks. On wet nights the light is caught by the crack and spreads out in a fan that looks like a bird. The carters call it the Halfway Bird, and take it as a sign that the road is good. I have not corrected them.

Rafe, who sleeps by my hearth, says the new wheel on his cart is holding. He has asked to buy the old map on my wall. I have said no. It is not for sale, and it is not mine to sell.

Please send a glass pane with the next cart. It does not have to be a good one. It only has to keep the wet out. Ferris says he can fit it in an afternoon, if someone holds the ladder. I said the helper from the drill yard can hold it. Ferris says he does not trust a helper with a ladder. I said that is a hard thing to say about a helper that has never dropped anything, and he went red and said nothing, which is his way of agreeing.

With my respects, and my hope that the glass comes before the frost,
Mabel, at the Halfway Inn

Letter: Jem to Orrin
Dear Orrin,

You will laugh at me, but I have had a strange week. Three babies came in a single night at the lower farm, and each one was in a hurry, and each mother said the same thing, which was that she would never do it again. Two of them meant it. The third is already asking for a second.

The Halfway Inn has been full since the lamp went out. Carters come in from the north road at all hours, and the innkeeper has begun to sell candles, which she says is a shame, and then sells more of them. The lamp keeper has written her a letter of apology. She has read it aloud to the whole kitchen, and then she has asked the cook to make him a pie, to show there were no hard feelings.

Dunn, the carter I told you about, took the bet about the lamp and lost, and paid me in honey and bread. He says he has never lost a bet to a midwife before, and he wants it written down. I have written it down.

The lamp was lit again this morning, a little crooked. The keeper says it will be straight by the new pane. I hope so. I am tired of guessing the time by the carters' faces.

Please write back, Orrin. Tell me how your roof is. Tell me if the boys are still climbing the rafters. Tell me you are eating properly, because I can tell from here that you are not.

Your cousin,
Jem

Notice: the north road
By order of the keeper at Northpoint, the north road is open this autumn as in any year. The waymarks are standing and the inns are open. The bridge at the second stream has been mended. Travelers may go as they always have.

Some folk have asked about the lights in the sky. The keeper will say only this. She has watched them and written them down and she does not know what they are. She does not say they are stars. She does not say they are fires. Others have said both, and others have said they were looking at us, and one child said they were the old stories come back. The keeper says these are guesses and none of them is hers.

The keeper asks travelers to keep to the road and to keep their torches lit and to write down anything they see that is strange. She says a thing written down is half understood. She says this often.

The relay is working. Messages go and come as before. The lamp is lit at dusk.

Signed at the tower,
Lantern, keeper's apprentice
for Mira, keeper at Northpoint

P.S. The fox by the tower is not to be fed. This means you.

Letter: Ansel to Tam, the fence line
Dear Tam,
This is about the fence. You know it is about the fence. Please read the whole letter before you say anything, and then say it in the barn, not in front of the children.
On the third of Longnight your big pig went through the gap at the bottom of my hedge, into my carrots, and ate the top half of every row. I counted. There were forty-one rows. Tuck counted them too, and drew forty-one lines in the mud with a stick, one for each row, and then I could not argue.
I did not shout. I want you to know I did not shout. I walked the pig back through the gap, very slowly, and I said things to it that I would not put in a letter.
Yesterday Tuck and I went out to the boundary stone. It was not where it had been. It had moved, I think, about a hand's width toward my side. I looked at it for a long time. Tuck looked at it too. Then Tuck measured the distance from the stone to the corner post of your barn, and the distance from the stone to mine, and drew both numbers in the mud beside the first ones. The numbers did not agree.
I do not think you moved the stone, Tam. I think the frost moved it, or your pig did, or the stone is simply tired of standing in one place. But the stone is where it is, and the hedge is where it is, and I am the one who has to go round the hedge every morning to get to my own well.
I would like the stone put back. I would like the gap filled with something that is not your pig. I would like one of your children to help Tuck do it, because Tuck does not understand why it should do all the work while your children do none.
Here is the other side of it. Last summer you lent me the big cart when mine broke. You did not ask for anything. You sent Pip with it, and Pip brought it back on time, with the wheel greased and a jar of your Ambrook honey tied to the seat. I have never thanked you properly, because I was too proud, and because the honey was very good.
So I am thanking you now, and I am telling you about the stone, and both things are true. You are an untidy man. You leave your boots in the middle of the path and your pig in the middle of my carrots. You also have the best honey on the ridge, and the best cart, and the best apprentice, which is a thing I did not expect to write.
Come round on the first clear day with a spade and a jar. We will put the stone back together, and argue about where it should go, and then eat the honey on bread, which is the only way I know to settle a boundary.
Your neighbor,
Ansel

Letter: Ash to Wren
Dear Wren,

I have finished the map of the farm outside Lowmarsh. The boardwalk bends around the west ditch, then runs past the bean shed and the well. I marked the fish weir with a small cross. I marked the old willow too, though it leans so far over the water that it may not stand another year.

The farmer asked me to draw the path to your healing house wider. He says he is tired of losing a wheel in the mud. I told him a map cannot make a path wider. He said it could if I used a large enough pencil.

I used the large pencil.

There are three new beds behind the house. The family planted carrots in one and potatoes in the other two. They gave me a jar of honey for the work. It is good honey, with a bit of wax at the bottom.

The slow water was low when I came. Frogs sat along the ditch and watched me draw. They have no respect for a person trying to make a straight line.

Ash

Letter: Cass to Aunt Rell, the honey jar
Dear Aunt Rell,

The market is over and I am tired in a good way.

I gave the soft cheese to a woman from Three Wells who sells bread. She tasted it and said it was a cheese with character, and she bought the whole wheel to cut on her stall. I have never been so glad of a bad smell.

I sold eleven wheels of hard cheese. I kept one for myself. I set it on the shelf beside your jar of honey, and I have not opened the honey. I am saving it. Nil says I am being silly, and he is right, and I am saving it anyway.

Nil helped at my stall all five days. He is quiet and good at carrying, and he tells the children the sea is cold and the fish are fast and nothing else. I think he is glad to have a thing to do.

The rumors are still rumors. The fish market says one thing and the quay says another. My bees do not care. The honey is good this spring.

Your niece,
Cass

Letter: Odo to Pell
Dear Pell,

The market at Kettle Ridge was crowded today. Your cart came late, but the apples were sound and the honey did not leak. I have put your two empty jars by the ferry post. Take them back before Tam mistakes them for cups.

The spring water is high. I tied the ferry rope twice, and still a branch came down from the ridge and caught it. Pip helped me pull the branch free. He wanted to keep it for the fire. I told him it was wet enough to put out a fire, and he said that was a poor sort of gift.

The tutors from Vell crossed before noon. They carried boxes of pages to the market hall. I heard one say they were taking old records back to the Archive. Another said the reading minds were to learn from them. I do not know how a mind reads a page. I only know the tutors spoke of showing it many pages, then asking it what mark came next.

Come early next market. I will keep the ferry clear for your cart.

Odo

Letter: Corin to Tam
Dear Tam,

The fishing boat came in late, just before the lamp was put out. The crew were cold and tired, but all were well. The wind had pushed them past the usual cove. They stayed there until the water eased. I was glad to see their sail, and gladder to hear the cook complain that they had missed supper.

Pip can keep his honey cake. A boat that comes home late is still a boat that comes home.

Your note about the word lesson was clear. The tutor's first cards are not a list of secret meanings. A mind keeps letters or words as places among many. As it learns, words used in alike ways come to have places close together. Bread and flour may be near because they appear in related lessons. A place is not a tiny drawing of bread. It is a set of numbers in the crystal.

The tutor had the mind sort cards again and again. When it put a word in the wrong place, she moved its weights a little toward the right answer. It grew better by those small changes. She then gave it words it had not seen in the drill. That showed whether the lesson had reached beyond the cards.

Pip wrote that the mind put beetroot near soup. This is not a rule that beetroot must be cooked in soup. It means the words met in similar company in the writing it learned from. A mind can place words near one another and still make a poor guess. The tutors keep a box of unseen cards to find such mistakes.

The sea is quieter now. I mended a loose board on the pier, and the shipwright's apprentice carried the tools back without being asked twice. That is a good kind of learning too.

Tell Pip I will bring him a honey cake when I next come to Kettle Ridge. Do not tell him whether I have won the bet.

Corin
%%
Letter: Pell to Ash
Dear Ash,

I left the sack of wheat at the farm this morning. The cart sank at the gate, so the farmer and I lifted it out by hand. We were both muddy to the knees. The farmer's dog stayed clean by standing on the cart.

I paid for the wheat with emeralds and a basket of apples from Ambrook. The farmer said he would rather have more apples. I said I would rather have a horse that could climb out of mud. We reached no agreement.

I have your map folded in my coat. The marks helped me find the dry side of the ditch. I left three little stones on the gatepost to show the next cart where to turn. If the farmer moves them, I will put them back.

At Lowmarsh, someone told me the cough is from the water. At the inn, someone else said it is from the big ships at Saltreach. Neither person had been near the sick house. The innkeeper says the bread is blamed for every trouble, though nobody has blamed it today.

The weather is warm, and the road is soft. Tell the farmer I will bring his apples next time.

Pell

Letter: Jory to Dace, the gift
Dear Dace,

The cheese came. It is beautiful. It is also huge. My mill hands carried it in on a plank, and then stood around it for a long time, looking.

I have given a quarter to the keeper of the tower, who was kind to us in the storm. I have given a quarter to Dara, the beekeeper, who said it was the best she had ever eaten. A quarter I have kept for the festival. A quarter I have eaten myself, standing in the pantry in my nightshirt, which is not a thing a miller should confess.

The festival went well. The wheel was dressed in flowers and turned slowly all afternoon. The honey race was won by a girl of ten who walked the whole way. Nobody spilled. Everybody was disqualified. We gave her the prize and sent her home with a cake.

Your wife danced twice with me. Once I trod on her foot. She said it was nothing. I think she is a kind woman.

Come again next summer.

Jory

Letter: Pip to Nessa, about a creeper
Dear Tutor Nessa,

Something happened at the drill yard and Tam says I must write and tell you myself.

I was doing the build drill. The one with the wall pattern on the board. I had my row of cobblestone and my row of oak and I was going along fine. Then it got dark and I forgot the torches at the far corner.

A creeper came out of the beetroot field and stood by my wall. I did not hear it because I was counting blocks. Then it blew up.

The wall is mostly gone. There is a hole in the hedge too. Tam came running with a bucket and a torch and said a lot of things. Then he said: Write to Nessa. So I am writing.

The apprentice was standing right there. It did not run. It did not know to run. I think it just kept building until the wall was not there anymore. Tam says that is not the apprentice's fault, it is mine. I think he is right.

Nobody was hurt. A hen was very upset.

I am sorry about the wall. I will build it again and I will keep the torches lit. Tam says the torches are the whole lesson. I think he is right about that too.

Pip
Kettle Ridge

Letter: Dace to Jory
Dear Jory,

The honey arrived. Four jars, wrapped in wool, in a box with straw. Not one broke. Pell must have driven the cart like a man carrying eggs.

I tried a spoonful at once. Then another. The mayor's wife says I must stop, or I will have none for the festival.

Thank you. It is the best gift I have had this year. It tastes of summer, and of your hill.

Dace

Letter: Hanne to Corin, the closed hall
Dear Corin,

You asked what the hall at Vell is like now that the drills have stopped. I have been putting off the answer for a month. The honest answer is long, and I have never been good at short letters.

I was a tutor there for nineteen years. The drill room is at one end of the long low building under the hill. Thirty apprentices can stand in a row there, facing a board with a small field drawn on it in chalk. The tutor calls the order, and the row moves.

When the council stopped paying, the tutors got a month's wages and a letter. The letter was very polite. It said minds of this kind had reached their limit, as shown by Kester's note, and that drills were to end at the close of Thaw.

The teaching room had a tray of crystals, each on its card. "Farm. Build." "Sort. Store." On the last day we put the crystals back in the tray, and nobody said anything. Then the clerk Ferrin came in with his lamp and asked for the cards. I said yes. I hope he has them still.

The apprentices are not sent away. The works take them: carrying, sorting, the sluices, the kilns. Each one does one job, the way it did in the old days, before any of us had a tray of cards. They do nothing else.

There was one I still think about. It was called Quill, a thin iron helper that swept the front steps of the hall every morning and every evening. Nobody had told it to, and nobody told it to stop. When the drills closed, it kept on sweeping. The steps are clean. They are the cleanest steps in Vell.

We tested every mind before it went out. The test box held examples the drills had never used. We counted the right answers in every hundred and wrote the count on the crystal's tag. Never send an untested mind to the fields. I still say it to my niece, who keeps goats now and has never asked me one question about minds.

You said in your last letter that the tutors should have stopped years ago. You were wrong about that, and I am telling you so in ink.

I am not sad, Corin. Do not write back to say I am sad. I am coming to Shingle End at Longday, if you will have me. I am told Dessa's honey cake has improved since the salt. I am teaching the children to count their fish. It is harder than a drill, and much more fun.

Tell Ned I am sorry about the tooth. Tell him the plank was a good plank.

Hanne
%%
Letter: Dace to Bel, what a test is for
Dear Bel,

Thank you for your letter about the test at school. I am glad you were curious, and gladder that you asked me instead of the cat.

You wrote: "Teacher says tests are for finding out what we do not know. But then why do we do them when we know the answer?" That is a very good question, and I will answer it as well as I can.

A test is not for finding out what you do not know. A test is for finding out whether you know it. Those are different things. When you learn your letters, you can copy them from the board. You might think that means you can write. But you cannot tell until the board is gone. Then you try. If you can write them without the board, you know. If you cannot, you were only copying.

That is why we test a mind on something it has never been shown. If we tested it on the very thing it was drilled on, we would learn nothing. It might just remember. When we show it something new, and it does well, we know it has learned.

The tutors have a rule about it. "Never send an untested mind to the fields." It is a good rule. I wrote it on a card and put it on the wall of my office. Every time a farmer asks me for an apprentice, I read it aloud.

In the old days, the halls of the tutors held contests. Each hall sent its best mind, and each mind was given the same unseen drill. The scores were written on a board for everyone to see. It made them honest. A hall could not say its mind was best unless it was.

I think you will like that. Contests where everyone does the same thing, and the winner is the one who really knows. It is fairer than a fair.

Now, news. The wall at Mara's barn has been rebuilt, and the wedding will be in a few weeks. You are invited. Bring your cat. It will not be allowed in, but you may keep it company outside.

I enclose a small prize for your question. It is a fine charcoal stick in a leather case. It is the best I could find.

Your friend,
Dace

P.S. Tell Teacher I said the answer is: both.

Letter: Dace to Jory, what to bring
Dear Jory,

I will come to the festival. I am writing to ask what to bring, because I do not want to arrive empty-handed.

My wife suggests cheese. She says Three Wells cheese is the best in the district, and I would never argue with her on the subject of cheese. I will bring a round of the hard kind, from the cellar of the inn.

I also thought of a barrel of beer, but I am told you have beer at Ambrook. Is this true? It seems unlikely. Everything I have ever drunk there tasted of apples.

If there is anything you need, tell me. A table, a bench, a rope, a ladder. The mayor of Three Wells has more ladders than any man alive.

I look forward to the dance. I will wear my second-best hat. My best hat is on the roof of the school, where the wind took it in spring.

Your friend,
Dace

Letter: Piper to Dell, the wool is in
Dear Dell,
The wool is in. Forty bundles from the Hollow farm, and eleven from the sheep that ran off to the ridge and came back with burrs in them. I have been picking burrs for three days. My fingers look like a hedge.
The carder's bench is by the window at the inn, because the light is good there and Hobb says the travelers like to watch. They do watch. They ask me questions I cannot answer while my hands are full. One of them asked if wool was born wool, or if it was made. I said it was born a sheep and then it was made, which is about as much as I know.
Marda says I am not allowed to sit at the hearth while I card. She says the lint goes in the stew. I say the lint is the best part of the stew. She threw a ladle at me. It was not a hard throw. It was a warm one.
Your honey jar came with the cart. It was not broken. It was not even sticky on the outside, which is more than I can say for the cart. I have put it on the shelf by the window, where the sun can see it. I ate one spoon. It is very good. Tell your mother I said so, and then tell her I ate one spoon only.
Write back soon. Tell me what the weather is at home. Tell me if the orchard is still full of wasps. Tell me if Jory has finally asked Ida about the flour.
Your friend, Piper
Inn on the Long Road, between Ambrook and the Halfway

Letter: Pell to Tilly, the honey
Dear Tilly,

The honey came up the road in a jar tied with red string, as you asked. Ambrook bees, the dark kind. I have kept one jar for you on the cart seat, where nobody can sit on it, because the last carter tried.

The Halfway Inn has new beds, twelve of them, and a stool by the door that is almost level. The innkeeper says the spring carts are late and the roads are soft. I say the road is only soft when you are in a hurry. Sit down, and it dries.

Tell your mother the cart is well. Tell her the mule is a fool, and that I love her more in Thaw than in any other month. Tell the baby the honey is for its teeth. It is not. It is for mine.

Your father,
Pell
