Letter: Odo to Dara, the honey order
Dara,

I am the innkeeper at the Halfway Inn, a day south of the Brown Hen. I have heard your honey is the best on the road, and I want to buy some.

I want twelve jars. Six light, six dark, if you have them. I will pay two emeralds a jar, same as at Three Wells market, and I will pay the carter to bring them. I have a hearth here that has not gone out in twenty years, and a riddle board by it, and travelers who like a little honey with their bread.

I have also heard a thing you may want to know. There is a new road being cut east of here, from the Long Road up toward Vell, a shortcut for the carts. They say it will save half a day. They say the council at Vell paid for it and the clerks drew the plan. I do not know if it is true. The clerks draw a great many plans. But the stakes are in the ground and the men are working, so it is at least half true.

I will tell you a rumor, since you will hear it anyway. A family stopped here last week on their way to Saltreach, to take a big ship over the sea. They said the relays have gone quiet in the north, one by one, and nobody knows why. They said it as a fact. The carter who brought them said it was only the snow on the towers, and it happens every long winter, and they were foolish to leave. I do not know which of them was right. I only know the family paid their bill in full and did not look back.

Send the honey when you can. Bring it yourself if you like. The hearth is warm and the beds are soft and the riddles are hard.

Odo
Innkeeper, the Halfway Inn
%%
Letter: Hob to Mira
Dear Mira,

Sella says you asked how the road lamps fare in winter. They fare better than my fingers.

I trim them from the Brown Hen to the Halfway Inn, then on to the waymark by Three Wells. Snow gathers on the glass, so I brush it away with a rag. At each post I check the wick, the oil, and the latch. The latch is the part that tries to defeat me. It is small, stiff, and proud of itself.

The inn is warm, and Sella feeds every traveler who comes in from the cold. She puts a pot of soup near the hearth and a clean cup on the table. Last night a carter slept with his boots on. Sella let him. In the morning she found one boot frozen to the floor. The man pulled hard, and the floorboard came up with it. He helped put it back.

Pell sent a jar of Ambrook honey. Sella says it is for me, but she has put it on the high shelf. I can reach the shelf. I am only waiting for a good reason.

The road is passable to the ridge. Tell Tam to cover his grain and wrap his hands. He says gloves make him slow, but cold hands make him drop things. I have seen both.

If you send a message back, put it with Pell's next load. He comes through before the next frost, unless his cart has learned to walk without him.

Hob
%%
Letter: Toby to Orla
Dear Orla,

You do not know me. I keep bees on a farm outside Copperhold, on the slope where the heather starts. My cousin at Vell copies texts in the archive, and she sent me your question. I have read it eleven times. I am writing because I have a bet on it with my neighbor Rill, and I need to settle the bet.

Rill says a drilled mind can never think. She says it only does again what it was shown, like my bees going back to the same flowers. I say that is thinking, or near enough, because the flowers are never in the same place twice.

Here is what I understood. You say: take a mind and a person, hide which is which, and write letters to both. Let readers try to tell which letter came from the mind and which from the person. If they cannot tell, then what? I say then we must treat them the same. Rill says that proves nothing, because a mind could be trained to copy a person's letters and still have nothing inside it.

So I ask you plain. In your test, are the readers asking the mind and the person the same questions? Is the person allowed to write about anything, or only what the mind was drilled on? If the questions are new, and the mind was never drilled on them, then I think the mind has done something of its own, and I win two jars of honey and Rill's old cart.

The bees are well. The hives are full. I hope this letter finds you before the flowers close.

Toby
%%
Letter: Yarrow to Pell, the roof
Dear Pell,

The Brown Hen roof is on. You will see it when you come up the road. It is the grey one, and it does not leak, and I do not want to hear any remarks about the color.

I know you wanted a red roof. I know you said so at the market, to anyone who would listen. The red slates were not in the cart from Three Wells, and the grey ones were, and a roof that is finished is worth more than a roof that is red.

The loft has a new beam too. The old one sagged so badly that the cat slept in the middle of it, and then one morning the cat was on the floor, and the beam was across the bed. Nobody was hurt. The cat was not even surprised.

Pell, you must stay the night on your next trip. There is a bed in the north room, which is dry. The cat is in the stable, which is dry too, and which she has been told she may not leave until the frost has gone.

I hear your cart is due on the Long Road in four days. Bring the salt, and the good needles, and whatever you have from Vell. Bring a letter from Juna if she has written one. She writes to nobody except you, and I want to know what she says.

Yarrow

Letter: Jory to Nim, the song contest
Dear Nim,

The contest is over, and Ambrook has won, and the eel is famous. I am writing before the fair people leave, so you can hear it from me and not from the bees.

The judges sat on the bench by the mill wheel. There were seven of them: the mayor of Three Wells, two clerks from Vell, the cook from the Halfway Inn, Hob, the Lowmarsh frog-keeper, and a child from the school, who was allowed to vote because she had sung the loudest in the rehearsal.

Three Wells sang the bell, very well. Kettle Ridge sang the windmill, and Tam sang louder than the windmill, which was not in the rules, but was allowed. Saltreach sang the boats. Northpoint sang the wind and the lamp, in a short song made of blinks, which the judges said was the cleverest song, and which the children could not clap to. Lowmarsh sang the frogs, which was not a song but the frogs, and the frogs did very well.

Ambrook sang the eel. The chorus went: The eel is not an eel, the eel is the pond,
The eel is in the water and the water is beyond. The second verse was about the sluice, which nobody may open, and the judges laughed at that, and then went very quiet, and then clapped.

The judges gave Ambrook the first score. Three Wells was second, and Northpoint third, for the lamp.

The prize was a jar of Hob's honey, which the bees took over for the evening, so the whole square stayed out late, eating the bread that Ida gave away free.

Come to the bridge next summer and sing with us. Bring your bowl. You owe me a bill of stew.

Your friend, Jory

P.S. Brin danced. I have seen it, and I am not sure it can be unseen.

Letter: Pell to Marda
Marda,
I am at the Halfway Inn and the road beyond is quiet. Hobb says fewer carts than last year. I count three this week. Last summer I counted twelve.
Did you get my letter from Saltreach? I sent it at the start of Longday. If it never came, tell me, and I will write it all again. It had the price of salt in it, and a joke.
Spiders in the barn here, a great many, and Hobb will not let anyone sweep. He says they are keeping something out. I did not ask what.
Send my regards to Ambrook. Tell the bees I will be back by Reaping.
Pell

Letter: Dace to Pell, the boy home
Dear Pell,

I am writing from the mayor's house, with my feet up and a cup of cider going cold beside me. It has been a long month, and the notice board has had a dreadful week.

You will want to hear about young Barrow. You remember him, the boy with the hoop who ran into the north well last spring. He went to Vell in Reaping to learn his letters, and then he went on to the Oat Hill farm, where his aunt has a bed and a plough. He wrote to his mother twice, and then he did not write at all, and his mother went up to the Archive to ask the clerks whether her son was dead.

Oren checked the book. He found a note from a farm clerk that said Barrow had been sent to help with the flood, and had carried sacks on the river bank for six days straight. He had not been able to send word, since the river had taken the relay line down. The note had not been filed, since it had come by foot.

So Barrow came home on the fifth day of Apple, with a sack over his shoulder and his hair full of mud. His mother saw him from the window of the bakery and ran out in her apron. Ida said it was the first time she has seen Hollis cry without a loaf in his hand.

I was on the square when they met. I had a notice in my hand that I was going to pin on the board. I did not pin it. I held it for a long time, and then I put it in my pocket, because it was about the price of firewood, and nobody needed to read about firewood that day.

The boy has been given a bed in the bakery and a bowl of soup at every meal. He has told everyone who will listen about the flood, the river, the boats, the cows, and the Oat Hill cow who ate a hat. He leaves out the part where he was frightened. Ida says that is how you can tell he is home.

Come up when you can, Pell. Bring the honey. We will set a place for you at the table.

Dace

P.S. The notice I did not pin is still in my coat. It says the north well will be closed for the week. It was a notice that could have waited. Nothing else could.

Letter: Gale to Hesk, the new seam
Hesk,

I am writing this at the kitchen at the mine gate, because my hands are too dirty to hold a pen and Noll is holding it for me. I am telling him the words and he is writing them down. If the spelling is strange, that is Noll.

We struck a new seam this morning on the fourth level. Good copper. Red as a beet and easy to break. The whole wall of it shone when I held up the lamp. We stood there a while and just looked at it. Then Clank started sorting the loose stone into the bins without being told, and we got back to work.

The lift is running slow again. Tell the forge. Or do not tell the forge, tell whoever fixes the lift.

Your apprentice is worth his weight in copper. He carried the water up all day and never once dropped the pail. He also told the sorting shed that a bucket of deep stone was a bucket of coal, and they believed him for a whole hour. We had a laugh about that.

Bring the good lamp oil when you come down. The old oil smokes and stings my eyes.

Gale
The fourth level, Copperhold

Letter: Sorrel to Fen, a sheep found
Sorrel, schoolteacher at Three Wells, writes to Fen the weaver in the autumn. The letter was carried to Fen's door by a child, running.
Dear Fen,
We have found your sheep. She is in the school. I do not know how long she has been there. When the children came in this morning, she was standing at the back of the room, calmly eating the straw from the reading corner.
The children took to her at once. They have named her Class. She has been given a seat. She has not said a word, but the children say she is the best listener they have ever had.
Do come and fetch her, but please do not hurry. We are in the middle of a lesson about wool, and she has been very useful.
Sorrel

Letter: Pell to Bel
Dear Bel,

You asked what the relay mind does with a page. I will try to tell it simply, while I sit at the Brown Hen and wait for my socks to dry.

Imagine a line of letters passing through a small window. The window holds about sixty letters. The mind reads the page one letter at a time. When it reaches a new letter, it looks back at the letters before it that may help. Then it guesses the next letter.

It learned this by reading many pages from the Archive. The tutors show it pages and let it guess. They compare its guess with the next letter on the page. When the guess is wrong, they change the mind a little. After many pages, it gets better at guessing.

It is not a person hiding in the crystal. It is a mind made by drills. Still, some letters from the big relay minds sound so much like a person's that readers cannot tell who wrote them. Orla asked that question long ago. Now people ask it again.

Do not let this stop you from writing your school essay. The archive needs your own words too.

Pell
%%
Letter: Hild to Edda
Edda,
I forgive you for making me sorry. I forgive you slowly.
The mill wheel at Kettle Ridge is mended. It took nine days and six men and an enormous amount of shouting. Tam did most of the shouting. Pip did most of the lifting. The wheel is oak, with new spokes and a new rim, and when it first turned, the whole ridge went quiet, because it did not squeak.
Then it squeaked. Everyone cheered.
The mill keeps the tally board, and Tam wrote on it, in letters a hand high: THE WHEEL TURNS. Under it somebody else wrote: SO DOES TAM'S STOMACH. I will not say who.
I am packing now. I will come on the first cart with the blue thread, the cheese, and a jar of honey for each of your sons. I have a gift for the youngest, a bell for his hat. I hope he does not put it on the cat.
You asked about the mouse in the hat. I think you should have kept the mouse. A mouse that has been in a mayor's hat has stories.
Have the spare bed ready. I snore, but only in the winter.
Hild

Letter: Tam to Mira, first snow
Dear Mira,

First snow this morning. It came in sideways from the north and stuck to the wheat stubble, so the whole ridge looks like someone has spilled the flour and not swept it up. The windmill is still turning, which surprised me. It squeaks on every turn, as usual, but the squeak is higher now. Pip says the cold makes the grease thick. I said the grease has been thick since Reaping. Pip did not argue, which means it agrees.

We got the last of the beetroot in before the frost. It came out of the long field in a line of purple heads, and Pip carried the baskets to the barn two at a time, which I told it not to do, and it did anyway, and dropped one, and we ate the one it dropped. Everything in the barn smells of earth and sweet roots now. Sleep in there is good. The hens have moved into the hay loft, which the hens think is a great idea and I have not been able to talk them out of.

The fox came back. I know you will ask. It came to the hen house the night before last, and the hens made a fuss, and the fox made off with nothing, because Pip had shut the little door. Pip was very pleased with itself. Then Pip went to bed, and the fox came back and sat on the fence, looking at the door as if it had lost a bet. I have not told Pip the fox is still out there. It would only want to sit up with it.

The bees are in the hives under the sacks. Every hive has a board on it and a stone on the board. The honey is thick and dark this year, from the late clover, and I have kept you a jar. Do not tell me it is not as good as the Northpoint honey. You have never tasted the Northpoint honey, because it is all in your own jar.

Your brother, who is cold,
Tam

P.S. Winter is short at the top of the ridge. I am told it will be long for you. Keep the lamps lit, and put the good scarf on before you climb.
Kettle Ridge, the farm at the top of the lane

Letter: Ada to Lily
Dear Lily,

Mother says I can write you a long letter if I keep it neat. This is my neatest writing, so please read it slowly.

Yew let us carry the school lantern up the hill. My end was the bottom, and it was heavy, and I did not drop it, not even when the goat came and looked at the glass. Yew said I was very brave and very sensible. I think she meant the goat.

At the top the whole of Copperhold was in the square. There were pipes, and a drum, and a man selling sweet bread from a cart with a red wheel. You can have the next one if you come in Melon. You must bring your own spoon, because the sweet bread is sticky and the cart does not provide spoons.

Hesk was there in a clean shirt. He had soot on his nose anyway. He said our lantern was too bright for a miners' fair. Then he looked at it for a long time, very quiet, and he did not say anything else.

I won a ribbon for my drawing of the mine gate. It is blue. It is not as nice as the lantern's ribbon, but it is mine.

Please write back. Tell me about the river at Three Wells and whether it is still too cold to swim.

Your cousin,
Ada

Letter: Mira to Tam, autumn at the tower
Dear Tam,

Autumn has come to Northpoint. The foxes are fat. The nights are long and getting longer.

The relay house here is nearly ready. The quickeners are set in their ring, and the men from Vell keep filling them with ice, which is no trouble at all up here. A boy from the town asked me what the big mind was for, and I said it was so that an apprentice could ask a question and get an answer without a person in between. He thought about that a long while. Then he asked if it could tell him where his dog had got to. I said it could not. He was not surprised.

The wind is up. One pane in the lamp room has a crack, and I have stopped it with wax until the glass man comes. Tell Pip I have not forgotten the beeswax. Tell him the wax from Ambrook is the best for panes. Tell him I said so.

I am well. I am not lonely. I have the lamps and the wind and the boy and his dog, which keeps coming back.

Mira

Letter: Tam to Mira, the fair test
Dear Mira,

You will not believe what happened at the winter fair in Three Wells, and I am writing it down before Pip tells it wrong.

A tutor came up from Vell with a box of crystals and a table with two curtains. One curtain hid a mind, in its crystal, in the mayor's shed. The other hid Peg, from the bakery, who is fourteen and very serious about it. The tutor's name was Ros. He kept saying Orla's name, which I did not think was fair to Orla.

The test was the old one. Each room got the same letters, one at a time, through a slot in the wall. Each room wrote back. The people in the square would read both answers and say which was the person.

The first letter asked: "What do you do when your friend is sad?" One answer said: "I go and sit with them, and I do not talk for a while. Then I bring a cup of cider and ask what they need." Everyone said that was the person. Peg's voice is a bit like that, when she is not being serious.

The second asked: "What is the capital of the sea?" One answer said: "I do not know which sea you mean." The other said: "The sea has no capital. Ask the fish." Everyone laughed. Ros said both were careful, and that people write like that when they are being careful.

The third asked: "Is it possible for a drilled thing to think?" Then the square went quiet. The first answer said: "I am not certain. I can only tell you what I was taught to say, and I was taught to say I am not certain." The second said: "Yes, if you mean think the way you do. No, if you mean something that was never drilled." The crowd argued for an hour.

Hesk said the first answer was the mind, because a person would never admit that much in front of a crowd. Dace said the second was, because it was too clever by half. Old Brin said both were people, and he had met both kinds at the Kettle Inn. Ros only smiled, and waited.

Then he took the curtains down. In the first room was Peg, with a pie. In the second was the crystal, with a cloth over it. The crowd asked which room had answered the third letter. Ros would not say. He said: "I do not know, and neither do you. That is the question. You cannot tell by reading alone, and that is why we test." Then he packed the box and went down the road to Vell.

I thought it was obvious. The mind is the one that talked too much. Then I thought, no, a mind would talk too little, to be safe. I cannot decide, Mira. What do you think?

Your brother, Tam

P.S. Peg says she is not a crystal. Hesk says she is a little.
%%
Letter: Hob to Tam
Dear Tam,

I found your note under the inn table. It had a beetroot stain on it, so I knew it was yours before I read the name.

The Long Road is hard with frost, but the stones are clear. I trimmed the lamps from the Brown Hen to the Halfway Inn. One post leaned toward the ditch. I packed earth around it, then leaned on it to test the work. It stayed up. I fell down.

Sella says your cart may pass after Longnight. The bridge over the stream is sound, but the north edge is slick. Put straw there if you have any. She will keep two sacks of straw by the inn door.

I found the jar of honey Pell left for me. It is safe. I have only opened it twice, once to taste it and once to make sure the first taste was right.

Please send my cap back with the cart. Sella says it is dry. I do not know why she says this as if it were a surprise.

Hob

Letter: Oda to Lenn, about the melon
Dear Lenn,
You will not believe this. Hal's melon has grown so big that it has pushed the garden gate off its hinges. It is lying in the bean row like a green boat. Hal says it is the finest melon ever grown on the marsh. The bees have found it. The hens have found it. The boy has been sitting on it since breakfast and says it is warm.
I told Hal that a melon the size of a cart is not a melon, it is a problem. He said he would sell it to the Brown Hen and buy the inn a new roof. The Brown Hen has a roof already, and the roof is fine, and I said so. We did not speak until supper.
Now I am sorry I said anything. The melon is very good. It is sweet all the way through, and the seeds are the size of beans. Please come on the next cart and see it before the boy eats the rind.
Also, the cow is better. Wren's boy has been good. Keep the cream for me.
Your sister,
Oda
%%
Letter: Oren to Cass
Dear Cass,

I am writing to every healing house on the roads, and I am sorry it is a letter that asks for so much. Please read it through before you answer.

The council at Vell has sent an order to the Archive. The order is clear. The Archive is to be closed to readers from the start of Melon, and the doors of the hill sealed with wax and the archive seal. I have been told to do it. I have been told to do it carefully.

The order also says: write everything down. Every healer, every keeper, every mill, every school. Write what is known, and what is seen, and what is said by people you trust. Put it on paper and send it in. The council says the Archive should not be the only place where a thing is kept.

So I am asking you, as kindly as I can, to write for us. Your remedies, and how you made them. The illnesses you have seen this spring, and in what houses, and in what order. The water in your well, and whether it has changed. Anything strange that you have been told, with the name of the person who told you. If you are not sure, say so, and say why.

I know this is a great deal to ask of a healer with a full house. I know you have more to do than to sit and write. But I have sat in the copying room for many years, and I have learned that a thing written down in one place is only a thing until the place is closed.

The fox song is still on the shelf by the window, in Bel's hand. I have not moved it. It will stay where it is for a while.

Please write to me when you can. I will read every letter myself.

Oren
Archive clerk, Vell
%%
Letter: Isla to Wren
Dear Wren,

The first frost came early to Reedbank. I put the bean pots near the stove, then forgot the stove was out. The beans are still hard. I shall cook them longer today.

Can you send a jar of mint from the healing house? My throat is sore from calling the hens in. They ignore me unless I have grain. The hens have good sense.

I have saved two warm loaves for you. Please take one before your apprentice Moss finds them. Moss carried the flour sack here yesterday and set it down by the door. I thanked the apprentice. It stood there until I said, "Stay." Then it stayed. I had to ask it to come again.

The boardwalk is slick by the fish weir. I fell on my basket, but the eggs were safe. My pride was not.

Your neighbor,
Isla
%%
Letter: Wren to Moss, at the weir
Dear Moss,

I am writing this on the back porch, with a cup of cold tea and a bad pen, and the whole hamlet is humming in the summer heat. I want to tell you some things before I forget them, because you will be away until Melon, and I will have things to say by then.

First, the news. Your friend the heron is back at the marsh pool. It stands there all day and looks at the water, and I have decided it is a better healer than I am, because it knows when to wait. I have been trying to copy it.

Second, the barn at Aldo's farm. It is full of spiders, and I have written him a long letter about brooms. I shall not repeat it here, but if he asks you, tell him that I meant every word, even the one about the hat.

Third, the shelf of jars. I have moved the willow bark to the top row and put the honey at the bottom. If you do not look, you will take the wrong jar and give a man a headache for a stomachache, or the other way round. So look. Look twice. Then read the label. Then look again. A healer is a person who looks three times.

Fourth, your sister Reed came by and says the baby kicks. It is a boy or a girl or a very strong cat. She is well. I have told her to walk every morning and drink water and sit down when she is tired. She has promised to do two of the three.

Fifth, the cough in the east cottages. It is going, slowly. I have been using the marsh-mint and a little thyme. Do not bring me stories of how the cough is spreading, because it is not. It is only the damp. The damp comes every summer and goes every autumn, and it is not in a hurry.

Sixth, and this is the last: you wrote that the nights at the weir are cold, and that you miss the soup. I have put a jar of it in the parcel with this letter. It is the leek one. Do not open it until you are cold and tired and it is almost dark. Then it will taste like home.

Write to me on the day the pike come up the stream. I want to know what the water was doing.

Your teacher,
Wren
%%
Letter: Sable to Neri, thanks
Dear Neri,

Thank you for lending me the iron hook. It pulled the bent nail from my shutter, and the shutter closes again. The wind rattled it all night before that. I did not sleep well, and the cat slept very well on my feet.

I have made you four small candles for the work shed. They are short because I poured the last of the tallow into them. They burn straight, which is more than I can say for the one I made for myself.

The fishing boat came home after dark yesterday. We had waited on the wall with a lantern. The crew saw it from the water and waved. They had stayed to mend a torn net, then lost an oar in the chop. No one was hurt. They brought back a basket of fish and a long story about the oar.

Please take the candles before your shed gets damp. I wrapped them in wool, but not the good wool. I remember what happened last time.

Your neighbor,
Sable

Notice: mill road, winter
By order of the miller.

The mill road is icy from the bridge to the bend. Grit has been laid down at the bend. Walk your horses slow and do not run.

The mill is working. The wheel was cracked and now the wheel is mended. The new axle is oak and it came from Three Wells and it cost me a horse for a week and a sack of flour. The flour was worth it. The horse was worth it.

Flour is ground every day except the day of rest. Bring your wheat in the morning. Bring your sacks tied. Bring your children if they want to watch the wheel turn, but keep them back from the water.

A hot loaf for anyone who helps dig the ditch clear when the thaw comes.

Ash, miller

Letter: Wat to Sable, the drill at Vell
Dear Sable,

I went to Vell on the cart, as you asked, to see the drill with my own eyes. I will tell you what I saw, in order, so you can explain it to Jeb. He will not believe any of it unless it has numbers.

The drill yard is a square with torches round the edge, and a board on a post with a small field drawn on it. There was a row of minds in crystals on a bench. Each one had a card beside it, and the card showed what it was for.

The tutor took a card from the tray and showed it to the first mind. The card had a sack on it. The mind was asked: where does this go? It put its hand on the pile for sacks. The tutor shook her head. The mind moved its hand to the pile for bolts. Wrong again. The tutor said nothing, but she made a mark in her book, and the card was shown again.

That is the drill. It is the same card, again and again. Nobody says why it works. The tutor says the crystal learns from what it is shown, and each time it is wrong, it changes a little. So it is wrong less each time. She said that is all she knows, and the rest is the crystal's business.

By the end of the morning, the same mind put the sack on the sack pile nine times out of ten. The tutor was pleased. Then she took out a box of cards that the mind had never seen, and gave it the test. It got seven out of ten. She wrote that down, and said it would not go to the mill yet. It must do better on new cards before it goes near a sack.

That is the other rule. Never send an untested mind to the fields. She said it as if she were tired of saying it, and then she said it again.

I asked how many of the four at Vell were still at work. She said only one still does its work, the one by the door, and the others were moved to the stores and the lamp room, where each does the same thing it always did.

I stayed the night at the inn at the bottom of the hill. The fire was good and my feet were good. My head was full of cards.

Your cousin,
Wat
%%
Letter: Mab to Ruel, the copper vein
Dear Ruel,
Your letter came up on the salt cart. Father read it aloud at supper, and then he read the bit about the curve again, because he said it was the first thing he had understood since the harvest.
Here is the news. The copper men found a new vein under the hill past the orchard, where we used to dig for clay. It is green in the rock and bright in the middle. The carter says it is the best vein since the lower shafts at Copperhold were opened. The council sent two men with a measuring rod. They walked round our field three times, and then they went to the Brown Hen to drink.
Now the fox. It came into the hen house on the warm night and took the speckled hen before anybody woke. Father is mending the wall. I told him to use cobblestone, and he said cobblestone is for towns. He used oak. The fox will come back and be cross about it.
The council has a notice up. They will build a relay house at the foot of the hill tower, the big kind, for a mind of the new size. The mayor says it will answer the apprentices on the fields and the carts. Father does not want a mind so near the square. I said a mind is only a crystal in a box. He said that is what worries him.
Write when you can. Eat something green.
Mama
%%
Letter: Nim to Oren, the order
Dear Oren,

You asked how my apprentice Crumb is getting on. I will tell you the truth, because you will find out anyway from the cook.

Crumb carries the jugs and tends the cellar stairs. It knows its eight words, and it knows them well. One busy morning I was with the travelers, and I said to Crumb: "Fetch cider."

Crumb went down the cellar stairs. It came up with a jug. Good.

Then I was still busy, and I said it again, because the table by the hearth wanted more, and I did not say how much. Crumb went down the stairs again. It came up with a barrel. Then it went down again. It brought up every barrel in the cellar, eleven of them, and stacked them in the yard by the well, very neat, one on top of the other.

The travelers sat on the barrels, because the barrels were the only seats left. Everybody agreed the cider was excellent, which it was, because it was the same cider.

When I asked Crumb why it had brought up the whole cellar, it said the word cider was near the word cellar, and the word fetch was near them both. It was not wrong about the places. It was only wrong about how much.

I should have said one jug. I have said one jug every time since. Crumb now says "one jug," and then it stops, and waits for me to say the next thing. It is a good habit for a mind to have, and I think for an innkeeper too.

Your friend, Nim

The cellar is empty and very clean. The cider is in the yard.
%%
Letter: Lise to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

The packet arrived, and we read the lesson by the school window. The children liked the words "grow wild". One asked if numbers could climb out of a crystal and eat the books.

I told them no. The numbers stay in the mind. Each layer adds its work to the stream inside it. The tutors keep the numbers even at each layer, so one layer does not make the next too large or too small.

We tried a small drill with sums written on cards. I asked the children to add two and three, then to add one more. I asked them to keep their voices calm and their marks level. They did better at the sums than at the calm part.

The new road brings dust into the school. I am not complaining. A cart came from Vell today with slate and apples. Before the road, we had to carry both up the hill.

Your friend,
Lise

Letter: Moss to Wren, the ladder
Dear Wren,

We got the bees down. Emmet held the ladder and I held the box. The bees went in the box and I carried them to the new hive by the fence. They are in the hive now and Emmet says they are happy. I do not know how he knows they are happy. He says they sound happy.

When I got back to the healing house I put the honey on the shelf by your jars. I did not eat it. I only opened it to smell it. Then I closed it. Then I opened it again. Then I closed it. I did not eat it.

Reed's mother asked me to bring a cradle to the shed. We built it from oak and it is a little crooked at one end. Emmet says a crooked cradle rocks better. I think he is being kind.

Can I stay one more day at the hives? Emmet is teaching me how to find the queen. She is bigger than the others and she has a spot. I have not found her yet. I will find her.

Moss

Letter: Bryn to Pip
Dear Pip,

Tam says you laughed at the bay horse for shaking his head. He says the horse looked just like you when you found the wrong foot.

I am sure this is not true. I am also sure Tam would say the same thing about me.

The chestnut mare has burrs in her tail. Bring her to the oak hedge after the morning feed. I will hold her and you can brush them out. Do not pull hard. She is patient, but her tail is longer than your arm and she knows it.

Please bring two carrots. One for her, and one for the boy who helps.

Bryn

Letter: Rafe to Hanne, autumn news
Dear Hanne,

Your letter came up the Long Road in a bag of salt. It said Lowmarsh smells of frogs, which I believe, and that your knee is better, which I do not believe. You have never told the truth about your knee. Tell me the truth this time.

News of the road. The new road is cut as far as the ridge, and the stone edge has held through two frosts and one carter. That carter lost a wheel in the second frost and found it in the mayor's garden. He has been the mayor's cousin ever since, and nobody can explain it.

At the Halfway there is a map on the wall by the stair. It is the best thing I have seen since the road was cut. It is drawn in brown ink, with every inn as a little house and every tower as a little square. The Halfway has a bird on its roof. Mabel says the bird was there before her, and I believe her, because the bird is in the same hand as the rest. Nobody bothers to draw a bird in a hurry.

The clerk from Vell wants to copy the map. Mabel is annoyed that the Archive wants to take her wall away. I told her the Archive does not take walls. It copies them, and then gives you back the copy. She said that was a clerk's argument. I said I am a carter, and carters have no arguments, only routes.

The relay lamp on the hill above the inn has a crack in one pane. Ferris the keeper says it is a matter for Vell. The lamp still blinks. In the wet, the light catches on the crack and goes out in a fan, and the carters call it the Halfway Bird. I have not corrected them.

Ysa sends her love. She is at Ambrook for the apple picking. The apples are good, the bees are cross, and Ama has been very kind about the bees. The bridge over the stream has its loose plank back, and Jory says it still moves. The children jump on it. Ysa says it has never once given way, and she is not sure whether to be pleased.

The cart is holding. The wheel I put on in Thaw has settled, and does not squeak, which is more than I can say for the carter behind me. He is a fool, and a good one, and he has promised me a pie from Three Wells, if the pie survives the road.

Hanne, I want to come home for the feast. The road has a way of taking promises and putting them in the ditch, so I have said nothing to anyone. But I think I can be at Lowmarsh by the frost, if the mud lets me. I would like to sit in your kitchen with your bad knee on the stool and listen to you tell me about the frogs.

Keep the bread in the crock. Keep the torch lit by the water gate.

Your brother,
Rafe

P.S. The cheese from Greer came up the road in a box, with a note saying the cow ate the rosettes. I believe that too.

Letter: Hesk to Mira, a gift and a debt
Mira,
This is a short thank-you and a long apology.
The short thank-you is for the lamp oil. It came on the third cart of the winter. It is the good kind, from Saltreach, and it has kept the lamp room at the mine gate bright all month. Everyone says the light is better. I say it is the oil. Bram says it is the lamp. We have agreed to disagree.
The long apology is about the prize. At the Three Wells fair this autumn, the prize for the best copper tool went to the forge at Copperhold. It was Dara's, but she is shy, so she asked me to carry it up on the stage. I did. The mayor handed me a gold ribbon. I said thank you and went down. Then I realized I had taken the ribbon. It was not mine.
I sent it back to Dara. I said I was sorry. She said it was fine. She said the ribbon looked better on me than on the tool.
I wear it now on founding day, with my gold helmet. You will say I should not. I know I should not. It makes me feel proud.
Please do not tell Tam. He will want a ribbon too.
Hesk

Letter: Cob to Wren, the sick cow
Leaffall of 183

Dear Wren,

The first snow came in the night, two days before Frost. The apples on the north trees wear white caps now, and the hens will not come out of the house.

Dumpling has been off her food four days. She sighs at me over the gate. Not sad sighs. Rude ones. I gave her warm mash with honey, as you said. She ate half and gave the rest to the pig.

Scrap held the milk pail steady while I milked. When Dumpling kicked, Scrap stepped back at exactly the right moment. Maybe the cow told it.

There is a lump on her side the size of a potato, and it is hot to touch. Can you send the green salve and the willow bark with the next boat? Should she stay in the barn, or go out on the warm side of the hill? I do not want to move her in the cold.

The hay will not be enough. Rain in Reaping spoiled the top field. If the winter is long, we will count bales like emeralds.

The bees went into their boxes early, all three hives. The bee book says bees in a hurry should be left alone. I have left them alone. They are still in a hurry.

Come up for supper when the path is good. The stew is not good, but there is a lot of it.

Cob

Letter: Sella to Ree
Dear Ree,

The scarf was already full of holes. The horse has improved it.

Thank you for fixing the roof. I heard the rain in your room last Longnight, even from our house. I wondered how you slept. You say you did not sleep, which explains why you heard it too.

The horse is mine until Swarm. Please borrow her again if you need to move stones. She likes work better than standing by the fence. She likes apples best of all, and will search your pockets for them.

I have a small gift for you. It is a wool cap, blue with a green patch. I made the patch from the part of your scarf she did not eat. Wear it on the tower stair. The wind there is rude.

Sella
%%
Letter: Rill to Toby
Toby,

You took my cart in your letter to the tutor. That is not fair. The bet was two jars of honey, not the cart. The cart is my cart. I need it for the beetroot.

You also told the tutor I say a mind can never think. I said no such thing. I said a drilled mind does again what it was shown, and that is not the same as thinking about something new. If a pupil at the school is shown sums all winter and then does a sum she was never shown, we say she learned. Nobody says she thinks. I am not against the minds. I like them. The sorting crystal at the market sorts apples better than I do, and I do not mind, because it does not eat the apples.

But your test is a good test, and I will give you that. If the readers cannot tell the letters apart, then the mind has passed, and I will pay the honey. I keep my word.

One thing, though. Who picks the readers? If the readers are tutors who want the mind to pass, they will guess wrong on purpose. If they are farmers, they will guess by small things, like a letter that says "your brother" and forgets the brother's name. A mind drilled on letters will not forget a name if it was shown it. A person forgets names all the time. That is how you tell.

So my terms: the readers must be plain folk, and nobody tells them what to look for.

Now go and see to the bees. They were swarming over my fence this morning, and if they swarm into my hedge I am keeping them.

Rill
%%
Letter: Cass to Aunt Rell
Dear Aunt Rell,

The spring market opens next week and I have made too much cheese again. Twelve wheels of hard cheese and a soft one I am not sure about. The soft one smells of the shed. I will bring it to the market on my cart and see if anyone wants it.

My neighbor Nil is back from three seasons at sea. He came up the hill yesterday with a cold and a long story. He says the water is the same everywhere. I did not ask him more. He sits by his door now and watches the road.

I will send you a jar of honey from my bees. The first honey of spring is thin and light. Put it on bread.

Your niece,
Cass

Letter: Moss to Clank, the drill
Clank,

A letter, because you cannot read an order that long and I know it.

The order was: bring me some reeds from the boat house. You brought the boat house door. I do not blame you. The keeper laughed so hard he had to sit down. The reeds are behind the boat house, in the water, where the frogs are. Not the door.

The tutor at the relay house says a small mind takes one meaning and holds it, and fetch door is a meaning. So it was not your fault. It was my order. I am learning to say it plain.

Also. There are spiders in the barn by the fish weir. Big ones. I do not want to go in alone. Bring your lamp and your long arms and stand at the door while I sweep. Then we will get the reeds. Then we will get the door back on its hinges before the keeper notices.

Moss

Letter: Willa to her sister, the smoke
Dear Marn,

You asked me in your last letter why I live alone in the woods with a pile of burning logs. It is a fair question. I have asked it myself, mostly on cold nights when the wind blows the smoke into my bed.

Here is the answer. A charcoal pit is a small hill of wood covered in turf and earth, with a few holes to let in air. You light it from the middle. Then you do nothing. That is the art: doing nothing, for four days, with great care. If the air is too much, the wood burns to ash. If the air is too little, the fire dies and the wood stays wood. You walk round and round the pit, listening, and when the smoke goes thin and blue you close the holes.

I cannot explain what I listen for. It is not a sound. It is more like the pit tells me it is happy.

Copperhold buys everything I make. The forge wants it, the kitchen at the mine gate wants it, and Hesk wants it for the lamp room. I sell by the sack and I sell it all. There was a winter when I had nothing left for myself, and I burned a chair.

Come and see it. Come in Deepsnow, when the snow is deep and the pit is the warmest place for a hundred blocks. I will give you a hat, because your head will smell of smoke for a week. I will give you stew. The stew is mostly beetroot, because that is what a charcoal burner can carry.

Mother will not come. She says the smell gets into her good shawl. She is right. It gets into everything. But I would not trade it for a town.

With love, and soot,
Willa
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira, the zombie
Dear Mira,

A zombie came to the market gate at dawn. It stood there with its arms out, groaning. Pip held the gate shut with both hands, very politely. The zombie groaned at it through the bars. Neither moved. I think they were waiting for me.

Then the sun came over the wall. The zombie began to smoke. It went off down the ridge road, still groaning, like a man who has lost his hat. Old Brin saw it later, sitting in the shade of the north windmill. He says it was nearly polite.

That is the third this spring. Brin says the cold held them back last year. Hanna says the warm brings them out. Sefa says it does not matter so long as the torches are lit. We all lit more torches.

Brin's nephew writes that they are building ships at Saltreach, with room for forty families each. Brin says nobody needs a boat that size for fish.

Keep the torches lit. Write.

Tam

Letter: Pell to Bel, no debt
Dear Bel,

Your emerald came with the next cart. I looked at it for a long time. It is a good green one.

Here is what I did. I put it back in the envelope. I am sending it to you with this letter. The jar was old and had a crack in it already. The cat only finished what the crack began.

A debt is a thing between honest people. You came forward without being asked, and that is worth more than the jar. So the account is closed. Written in my book: Bel, honey jar, paid in honesty.

I am also sending a small new jar. This one is full. It is not for the cat.

Yours,
Pell

Letter: Nessa to Oren, the bet
Dear Oren,
You owe me a loaf. I have the bet written down, and you signed it in your best clerk's hand, so do not pretend you have forgotten.
The bet was that the talking mind at the relay house would say it did not know, within the first ten letters of any question about the sea. You said it would never say so. You said a big mind would always have an answer ready, the way a good fishmonger always has a price.
I have now read its answers for two weeks. It has said I do not know thirty-one times. Eleven of those were about the sea. Four were about the weather at Saltreach, which it could not see. Two were about the boat that went out from the quay last month, which it said it had no record of, and asked us to tell it if we did.
So you owe me a loaf. Bring it to the tutor house on the first warm day. Bring the butter as well. I am tired of the bread from the Archive kitchen. It is too careful.
Nessa
Tutor house, Vell

Letter: Pell to Juna, the hill lamp
Dear Juna,

Tonight they lit the new lamp on the hill between Three Wells and Vell. We watched from Ferris's yard at the edge of town. The lamp blinked, and far off the lamp at Vell blinked back. A boy beside me said, "They are talking." Ferris says the lamps only say all well. I said that is the best thing to say.

The keeper waved from the top, and we all waved back. It was a long way to wave.

Your Pell

Letter: Mab to Oren, the summer books

Dear Oren,

The school books are dry again. We left them open on the copying room tables after the damp night, and the pages have settled flat. I checked each one before the children came. One book had a beetroot mark on the last page. Bel says she did not put it there. I believe her, though she was eating beetroot at the time.

I am writing to ask for another box of plain paper. The children have filled the last one with letters, sums, and drawings of the mill wheel. Neri has promised to bring the box from the store room, if the clerk can spare it. We will use every sheet. Some of the drawings have no wheel at all, only a pond and a very large cat.

The broken wheel is mended now. Its new oak spoke is lighter than the old one, and Jory says he will watch it each morning. I think he means to watch the water too. He says the pond level has not been steady this summer. Neri says the old gate sticks in dry weather, and that is all.

At the school door today, a traveler said the north lights had been seen again. He had heard it from a man at the inn. The man at the inn had heard it from a cart driver. Bel asked whether they were falling stars. I said I did not know. Another traveler said the lamps at Northpoint were clear all night. These things may both be true, or neither.

The children were more interested in the flour that Jory brought for the bread lesson. They learned to sift it without dropping half the bowl on the floor. Halen dropped his share and swept it into a neat pile. We did not use that pile.

Please keep the drawings with the summer class pages. Bel made one of the wheel from the bridge, with the loose plank left out. She says she will draw it again when the pond is full.

Your careful teacher,

Mab
Schoolteacher at Vell

Letter: Isla to Nessa, about the contest
Dear Nessa,

The market was crowded, and the test went well. The bees stayed in the apple tree. Pell sold less fruit than he hoped, but more honey.

Each tutor brought one mind. We gave each the same unseen pages, then kept the answers covered until all were done. The pages were not used in any lesson. That is the point. A mind should answer what it has learned, not repeat a page it remembers.

One mind did poorly. Its tutor did not hide the score. That made the contest useful.

Isla
%%
Letter: Ida to Jory
Jory,

I did not expect a loaf from you at the Three Wells fair. I expected grit. I bought one anyway, because everyone was watching, and I wanted to see your face when I did not buy from the mill.

The bread was good. I am sorry. It was better than good. It had a crust that cracked the right way when I broke it, and the inside was white and soft, and it smelled of the grain and nothing else. I ate half of it on the square, standing by the east well, and the children came round and asked for pieces, and I gave them the rest.

I want to say I was right about the flour. I was right about the flour. But I think I was too hard, and I think I was also a little glad to be hard, because it gave me something to say to people. That is not a fair thing to do to a miller who is sorry.

So here is what I will do. Your flour comes to my shop again from the next market day. I will test it the way I test all flour, which is by hand and with my nose, and if it is clean, I will bake with it. If it is not clean, I will send it back in the same sack. You may keep the sack.

Tell your apprentice the bread was good. Tell it I said so. I do not think it made the bread, but it carried the sacks, and that is a kind of making too.

Ida
Bakery, Three Wells, by the north corner
%%
Letter: Mira to Dilly, the boat
Dear Dilly,
Your uncle Hesk says you are ten, and that you write well, and that you have a question about the sea. I do not live by the sea. I live by the snow. But I keep a relay, and a relay hears a great deal.
Last month a message came up the line from Saltreach in the middle of the night. A fishing boat was late home. It had gone out with six aboard and had not come back by dusk. The harbor master asked every tower to watch the water. We watched for two nights. Lantern climbed the stair every hour and looked out through the glass, until her legs hurt.
On the third dawn the message came: the Heron is in. All six safe. The wind had pushed her far to the east, and she had waited out the weather behind a rock. The crew ate their whole supply of dried fish and part of a net.
I can tell you, from far away in the snow, that a harbor is a place where many people wait. They wait with lamps. They wait with soup. They wait better than anyone I know.
I hope this answers your question about the sea. If it does not, write again.
Your friend at the top of the land,
Mira
%%
Letter: Hesta to Wren
Dear Wren,
Thank you for the medicine for Tuck. He is much better. He is up and out on the boardwalk, and he has already been told off twice for climbing the net posts. He says he is going to be a fisherman like his grandfather. I told him his grandfather fell in the marsh every spring and was pulled out by a goat.
I want to tell you something, because you will understand it better than Dob.
Since the eel business at the market, the whole boardwalk has been talking about the sun and the eels and who is to blame. Some say the stall-keepers should not sell the dark eel at all. Others say the dark eel is the best eel and the fault is in the cold cellars. The council sent a man to look at the cellars, and he came back with a face full of mud and a list of forty names.
I have been thinking about it at night. Tuck did not eat much of the eel. Only one bite. It made him sick for a day and a half. So it was not the eel alone. It was the eel, and the heat, and the long walk from the stall in the sun, and the bite he took at the back of the boat house where the flies are. The matter is not a simple one. I do not think any matter ever is.
Thank you for showing me how to tell a fever from a sunburn. I did not know that before. I think I will teach Tuck, when he is older, the way you taught me. Look at the skin. Look at the eyes. Then ask again tomorrow.
Come and eat with us on Blossom evening. We will have the fish from the weir, not the eel. Tuck wants to thank you himself, and he has been practicing the words all week in front of the cat.
Your friend,
Hesta

Letter: Fen to Sorrel
From Fen, weaver, Three Wells, in autumn, to the schoolteacher Sorrel.
Dear Sorrel,
My brown ewe is gone. I took her to the east well to drink and turned my back to wave at the baker. When I turned again, she was not there. She is a quiet ewe. She does not run.
If your children see a brown sheep with a patch on her left ear, please send them to me. I will pay in thread.
Fen
