Letter: Nim to Pell, the summer's end
Dear Pell,

The summer is ending. You can tell by the apples, which are coming down off the trees faster than the carts can take them, and by the light, which is going gold at the edges in the evenings.

The inn is full at the weekends. Travelers come up from the Long Road for the mill and stay for the stew. Madge of the Brown Hen sent me a note saying her stew is better than mine. I sent her one back saying mine is cheaper. We are both right, and we are both pleased.

The hearth is lit. It has been lit all summer, and nobody has let it go out. The riddle board has a new riddle every week now, and this week's is: what has a mouth but cannot eat? A river, the children say. The travelers say the answer is too easy. The children say that is the point of a riddle for children, and the travelers say that is the point of a riddle for travelers, and the riddle says nothing, which is the usual.

Crumb has learned the second line of an order. It says "one jug" and then waits, and I am proud of it, which is an odd thing to be proud of about a mind. Hob says the bees are calmer since the honey jar, and Tuck has taken over the bee smoke, which he holds very seriously, like a sword.

Jory's eel has been sung at every fair since the contest. Brin still dances when he hears it. The sluice is still shut. Nobody has asked why, and nobody has needed to.

The barley is in. The apples are in. The cider is in the cellar, in the right number of jugs. The best of the year is at the table, and the worst is at the bottom of the stairs, where nobody can reach it. I think that is a fair summary of a summer.

Come up when the leaves turn. Bring the ledger, and the bill, and your appetite. Tell Barley the carrots are for him this time, all of them.

Your friend, Nim

P.S. The apples on the old tree have a grey branch this year, and Hob says that means something, and I said that the branch is only old. Hob said that was what he meant.
%%
Letter: Fenn to Wren, after the storm
Dear Wren,

The storm came in off the marsh on the second night of Sowing. It took the hen house roof, the good ladder and most of the sheep wall on the north side. The hens are fine. Mostly. Two of them have decided they will never lay again, out of spite.

The creeper came in with the wind, quietly, the way they do. We heard the boom over the reeds, and the sheep went scattering into the water. Hob ran out in the dark with a torch and found the hole in the wall. Round the hole the stones were black, and the whole place smelled like a burnt pot. The creeper was gone. It had left us a gap the width of a cart and a great deal of grumbling.

Our son Dunstan was on the far side of the wall when it went. He is cut across the arm and bruised along the back, and he is cross about the bruising more than the cut. Can you come up in the morning? I know the road is wet and the bridge is slippery. I will send the cart to the far end.

Now the good news, which I want you to hear before you come. The mind you sent us last winter is on the sheep gate. It counts the flock through the gate at dusk, and in the storm it shut the north gate on time. Every sheep was inside before the wind came over the wall. I stood in the yard with a lamp and watched it do its work, and I could not have done better myself.

The card on its crystal says it was tested on a test box and got ninety-six right in a hundred. I have never understood what the other four were. Is it a trustworthy mind? I am not clever with numbers, and I am not sure you are either, so ask Oren and write me his answer. Tell him it is for a farmer on the marsh edge who wants to know.

Yours, in mud and reeds,
Fenn
%%
Letter: Oren to Pell, first spring
Dear Pell,

Today is the first day of Thaw, and the archive is one day old. You would not believe how many people came up the hill to see the door. The mayor came, and two carters, and a pig that followed the carters and was not invited.

Over the door the first keepers have cut our words: We will keep everything we write.

We have forty-one books, nine shelves, and one cat, who came in with the first box and has not left. Her name is Ink. She sits on the big book by the door.

When you come up the Long Road next time, bring us anything you have written. We will copy it, file it, and give it back.

Your friend, Oren

P.S. Ink says hello. She does not mean you.
%%
Letter: Noll to Ada
Dear Ada,

I am at the Brown Hen with the drill board beside me. I wrapped it in my coat, and the paint stayed on. The keeper has moved my stool near the window. It is the only stool that does not rock.

Lysa says she will carry the old boards to the Archive at Vell. Oren has offered to keep them dry and copy the marks. He is careful with every page, and I think he will be careful with the boards too.

The last lesson I heard from Lysa was about a test. She gave a mind examples it had not seen before. A mind that had only copied the lesson could fail at once. A mind that had learned the rule might do better. She says a fair test keeps tutors honest. There should be a box of unseen cards, and no one should teach from it.

That is good sense. It does not answer how a crystal learns. No one knows why it changes when it is shown an example. It does not say what a new kind of mind may do. It only tells us not to call one small crystal the end of all learning.

I used to think Kester had shut every door. Now I think he shut one door and left the rest unmarked. The council saw a closed hall and thought there was no point in opening another.

The relay keeper has come to look at the lamp. The glass is broken, and the redstone line is loose, but the copper ring is whole. He will take it to the relay house after he finishes his round. He says the lamp may shine again. I hope he is right.

I will come home when the road is clear. Save me a place by the fire, and do not save the stew unless you have added salt.

Noll
%%
Letter: Dace to Pell, my lost letter
Dear Pell,

Did you get my letter? I wrote it at the market on the fourth day of Melon. It was three pages. It was one of the best letters I have written. It had a joke in it, and a sum. I gave it to a boy to carry to you at the Brown Hen. The boy says he gave it to you. You say you did not. One of us is wrong. I think it is the boy. He is eight.

If you did not get it, tell me, and I will write it again. I will not be able to write the joke again. It came to me in the moment. I still remember the sum.

If you did get it and are only teasing me, please write back and say it was good.

Your friend,
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

P.S. I enclose a bun from Ida's. It is a little squashed. It was a good bun when I sealed it.
%%
Letter: Ash to Corin, a borrow
Dear Corin,

I am writing to ask a thing, and I will ask it plainly, because you are at Saltreach and I am at the mill and the road is long.

I need a horse. Only for a week. The mill wheel has a cracked axle and the new oak for it is at Three Wells, and Barley is too old to pull a cart that far now. He stands in the field and watches the hens and I do not have the heart to make him try.

I have heard you keep a grey mare at the yard for hauling timber. I would feed her well and rub her down every night and bring her back with a full belly and a good temper. I will bring her back by the fifth of Frost. I will bring back your harness too, oiled.

If the answer is no, say no and I will borrow from Tam at Kettle Ridge, but do not tell Tam, because he will lend me his cart and his cart squeaks and I will hear about it every turn.

How is the boat? Pell says you have had more advice than nails. Tell the town to come and look at my mill wheel and give advice and I will give them a bucket of flour and a warm place by the fire.

Your friend at the mill,
Ash

P.S. How is the mare with dogs? I have a dog. He is small. He means well.
%%
Letter: Hesk to Willa, the debt
Willa,

I owe you for the winter before last. Eleven emeralds for twenty sacks, and I paid you four and said I would pay the rest after the thaw. Then the thaw came, and the lift broke, and I forgot. I am a man who forgets debts and remembers every pick he ever lost.

Pell passed through on the Long Road yesterday and told me you were asking after your emeralds. He said it kindly. He said it in front of the whole inn. Everyone at the inn now knows I owe Willa seven emeralds. Thank you, Pell.

So here they are. Seven emeralds, in the small leather bag with this letter. Count them. Then count them again. I counted them three times at the mine gate and Clank watched me count, which was not helpful.

I have added one more emerald for the wait. You do not need to take it. But I will not take it back.

The new apprentice came last week, a copper one, with a mind that has been drilled to haul. The mine master named it Rivet, as it is the only word he could think of. It does not talk, which suits the Deep Works. It carries a full cart up the rails and an empty cart down, all shift, and when I say "home" at the end of the day it follows us up the stairs. Old Fenn says it is the first apprentice that never complained about his jokes. Rivet cannot hear them. That is why.

Send me eleven more sacks in Frost. I will pay on the day, in front of Pell if he is there.

Hesk
%%
Letter: Tam to Emmet, a favour
Dear Emmet,

This is Tam at Kettle Ridge. We have not met, but Pell the merchant says you are the man for bees. We have three hives by the flowers at the farm, and they are not doing well. The bees come out and then go back in. There are not many of them. The old hive is quiet.

Pell says you know what to do with a quiet hive. Pell says you once got a whole swarm out of a stone wall with a bowl and a bit of patience. I have patience. I do not have bees that work.

Could you send a word with Pell on his next cart, or come yourself? There is a bed in the barn and there is always soup. Pip will make you a second bowl. Pip makes everyone a second bowl.

I would pay you in beetroot. It is our best thing. The prize beetroot took a ribbon at the fair last Apple. My sister in the north says beetroot is not a fair price. My sister is in the snow and cannot grow anything, so she has opinions.

How is the little one at the ford? Pell says there is a baby.

Tam
Kettle Ridge
%%
Letter: Nera to Cass, more pages
Dear Cass,

Your note helped. I had heard people say Isla had found a way to make any mind clever. That is not what you wrote, and I am glad.

The tutor at the harbor school says the curve is drawn from trials. They grow minds of different sizes and give them different amounts of writing. Then they compare what each mind can do. Isla saw a pattern in those results and drew the curve from it.

A larger crystal alone is not enough. It needs more writing and more time with the writing. If it is fed spoiled pages, it may learn spoiled things. The clerks must take out copies and torn pages, and keep a box of test pages aside. No mind should see those pages during its drills.

You asked me to save the clean paper from the cider labels. I have done so. Your apprentice can use it for practice, but do not let the mind read the words on the backs. Those are old orders, and the meaning is not always clear.

Nera
%%
Letter: Ruby to Sela, the frog race
Dear Sela,
The frog race is on the Blossom fair day, as always. Our boy Dinn has been training his frog for a month. He calls it Admiral. It has never won anything, but Dinn says it is saving itself for the big day.
I want you to bring your stall to the far end of the boardwalk, by the boat house. The good end is by the fish weir, but that is where the old men sit to tell lies. The far end is where the children go to get their sweets. We will do better there.
Also, do not sell any more of the dark eel. The last of it went off in the sun, and Wren's boy got sick from one bite. Wren says it was the eel and not the sun. The sun says it was the eel too, so the matter is closed.
Tell your husband the post for the lamp by the steps is loose. It will fall in the water if someone leans on it. Someone always leans on it.
Ruby
%%
Letter: Nell to Kit
Nell writes from Vell to her grandmother Kit, on the farm outside Copperhold, in the autumn. The visit is done. The friends have gone home. Nell writes to say thank you, and to ask a favor.
Dear Gran,
It was the best visit I have ever had. Maren says so, too. Sedge said that she has not eaten so much pie in her life. She is still full. She told the whole tutor house.
The cave was grand. I did not expect it to be so big. The way the lantern light lit the roof, it looked like the inside of a great bell. Grandfather held the rope, and I went down, and for a moment I was the only person in the world.
I have a request. May I tell my tutor about the cave? She is Nessa, and she is very kind. She asked what we did in the holiday. I said we found a cave under a field. She said, "I would like to see it." I did not know if I could say yes.
I do not think it has anything to do with minds. But Nessa says that any hole in the earth is a thing a tutor likes to see, because it means something to know what is under the ground.
Also, how is the cat? Does she sit in the cave all day? Is she still on your bed at night? Tell her I said she is very clever.
Love,
Nell
P.S. I left a button in the cave. A blue one. If you find it, keep it.
%%
Letter: Rowan to Fen, from the road
Fen,

I got your letter at last. It was at the Brown Hen behind the bar with a tankard ring on it, so I read it slowly and with a beer. The innkeeper says it was there a month and he is sorry. He is always sorry.

I am sorry about the blue beads. I did not know they were money now. I will bring none.

The market was good to me. I sold the honey and the pots and I have orders for two more crates of jars from Ambrook, which is to say from you and Mother, if you will make them. Tell Mother I will pay in emeralds and not in beads.

Barley is well. He is fat and a little rude. He likes apples too much. He ate a hat.

The apprentice at Ambrook is called Peg. She carried a whole crate of pots to a stall and set it down so gently the pots did not knock once. Then she stood by the crate all afternoon with her hands out, waiting for another order. She did not get one. She stayed anyway. People brought her bread and she did not eat it, because nobody had told her to. I gave the bread to a dog.

There is a rumor she was first at the fair to understand a whole sentence. I do not know if that is true. I only know she is patient and she stands very still.

I am coming home by the Long Road in Leaffall. Save me some apples, the bruised ones are fine.

Rowan
%%
Letter: Master Rill to the tutors
Dear tutors,

I am Rill, schoolmaster at Saltreach. I write with a small request and a small gift.

The gift first. A jar of honey from my brother's hives. He keeps bees on the hill above the harbor, and this autumn was a good one for clover. The honey is dark and thick. Please put it in the kitchen at the archive, or give it to the clerks who work late. I am told they work late.

The request. I have a class of eleven children. One of them, Carys, wrote to you. I hope you do not mind. I was telling them how the Archive keeps everything, every letter and every notice and every tally, and she asked whether the Archive would keep her letter too. I said I thought it would. I hope I was right.

We teach reading here, and a little figuring, and the names of the towns on the Long Road. We have a map on the wall. It is old now and the ink is fading, and the road to Vell stops at a place with no name. If you have a newer map, or a clerk who could copy ours, we would be glad of it. We can pay in honey and fish.

We have a relay tower here, above the harbor. The keeper is an old friend. When I was a boy there was no tower, and a letter to Vell went by cart and took a month. Now the keeper blinks and it is there before supper. I do not understand it. I do not need to.

Also, if the tutors ever send an apprentice to Saltreach, the shipwrights would put it to work. They grumble, but they would.

With thanks,
Master Rill
School house, Saltreach
%%
Letter: Tam to Dara
Dara,

Thank you for the honey. Pip and I ate most of it on bread in three days. Pip says it is the best honey in the world and I did not correct her.

The farm is busy. The top field of wheat is in, the beetroot is coming up long and even, and the scarecrow still has its pumpkin head, though the crows have pecked one eye out. I will carve a new one in Apple.

I have a thing to ask you. The bees by the hedge have been strange. Not sick. Just quiet. They come out late and go in early, and some days they do not come out at all. The hives are by the flowers and the flowers are out, so I do not know why. Have your bees been the same? You know bees better than I do. Tell me if I should worry.

There is other strange news. A man came up the ridge road selling cloth, and he said the ships at Saltreach are as long as three boats now, and families are going over the sea and not coming back. He said it like it was a fine thing, an adventure. I did not think it sounded fine. I thought of the cold water and how far it must be to the other side, and I did not like it.

Pip asked me where the ships go. I said I did not know. She said: does anybody know? And I said I did not think so. She thought about it a while and then she went to feed the hens.

Mira writes less than she used to. Her letters are short. She says the nights are long and the sky is busy and the lamp needs a new pane of glass. She does not say more. I do not push her. You know how she is.

Write when you can. Tell me about the bees.

Your brother,
Tam
%%
Letter: Pell to Odo, a small gift
Dear Odo,

I owe you a proper thanks for carrying my cart through the high water. I have sent a new rope with this letter. It is oak fiber, well twisted, and long enough for the ferry post. It is a gift, not payment. You already charged me for the cart, and I have not forgotten.

Please do not tie it to the old branch. Pip says the branch is still by the landing, though I thought he carried it away. Perhaps he has moved it twice and called that work.

The tutors asked me for market ledgers. I gave them three copies, with the prices left in. I kept the page where I wrote that Tam's carrots were dear. The clerk said the reading mind should see ordinary records too. A ledger has names, sums, and repeated words. The mind may learn that a price often follows an item, or that a letter to a friend begins with a greeting.

It reads a short window, not a whole book at once. What came before can help it guess what comes next. The tutor said the mind does not need a separate rule for every line. It changes its weights as it sees many lines. I do not know what a weight looks like inside a crystal. I do know that seeing one ledger would teach less than seeing many.

If they use my prices, I hope they do not learn to charge by the sack.

Pell
%%
Letter: Zell to Oren
Dear Oren,
You asked how the move went. It went badly, in the usual way. The cart broke a wheel on the hill. The cat got out and had to be fetched from the Archive roof. The dye vats are in the yard, and the neighbors are not sure about them.
I have put a pot of blue by your door. It is thanks for the shelf you lent me. It was a good shelf. It has taken the weight of every skein I own.
A question. A letter was sent to me from the south three weeks ago, and I have not had it. The courier says it was left at the old house. I went back. The new tenant had not seen it. He was kind and a little frightened. He said the street has been quiet. He said the lamps on Lantern Row do not stay lit.
I do not know what to make of it. A dyer deals with colors, not with lamps.
Some of the clerks say the north relays are silent. Some say it is only the weather. Some say they have been told not to say. You are the one I trust to tell me the truth, or at least what you do not know. Write when you can. A short note will do.
Yours in blue,
Zell
%%
Letter: Neri to Sable, about the lamps
Dear Sable,

The candles reached the net shed in good order. Thank you for wrapping them in wool. The wool did not catch fire, because the candles were not lit. I tell you this because you looked worried when I took them.

We use two candles each evening while we mend nets. One sits on the bench and one sits by the door. The door candle has a wide base now. I set it in an old cup, so it no longer leans toward the harbor. It is a wise candle.

I have a request. Could you make six more, all the same height? The crew leaves before dawn to check the outer lines, and the small candles burn down before we finish tying our gear. We can pay with fish, or with a piece of the blue cloth from the sail store. I know you like that cloth for wrapping jars.

The boat that was late last week is home again. They had gone farther along the coast than they meant to. A fog came over the water, and the crew could not see the pier lights. They waited near a low island until it thinned. Then one oar broke, so they took turns rowing with the sound one. They were hungry when they came in, but they brought back a full catch.

My father says they should have turned sooner. The crew says he should come with them next time and show them how. Both sides have been saying this all week. I think they enjoy the argument.

The song contest is still being discussed. Pell claims his song won because it had the best words. His wife says it won because he stood near the honey. Neri says it won because the loose bench kept time. I say it won because we all wanted to hear it again.

Please send the candles with Lio when he comes to the net shed. I will leave the fish in a covered basket by your back step. I will not use the clay jar. It is still under your window, just where Lio put it.

Your friend,
Neri
%%
Letter: Cass to Nil, a jar of honey
Nil,

I am writing to you from my kitchen, which is next to yours, which is silly, but I want to say it properly and not in the doorway.

The honey is yours. The first jar of spring. I have been saving it for something, and I have decided the something is you coming home. Do not argue. It is only honey. Put it on bread.

You have been quiet since you came back. I do not ask you why. But you keep the lamps on the wall, and you watch the boats, and you do not say what you see, and I want you to know that I am glad you are here to see it. Whatever the north is doing, and whether the big ships go or stay, my bees go out every morning and come back, and the cheese needs turning, and the market will come again next spring.

Come and eat at my table on the first of Swarm. I will make the cheese with character, and we will open the honey, and you can tell me one true thing about the sea and I will tell you one true thing about the bees.

Cass
%%
Letter: Wick to Hesk
Dear Hesk,

The wheel is down. I am sorry to tell you that first, because you will want to know it before the rest. The big wheel at the boat house fell into the reeds on the second day of Blossom, when the water came up over the boards at dawn. I was on the bank with the boys and the rope. Nobody was hurt. The wheel was not so lucky.

It is a good wheel. It was oak, from the ridge, and Corin cut it for us himself when we were boys. Its spokes are as thick as my arm. It took four men to haul it out of the reeds with the rope, and the rope broke twice.

We have put the lamps on the bank to keep the frogs off the wood. They do not care for the lamps. They sit on the rim and sing.

Now the business. You asked me last spring to send a loom to the Archive, and I said I would send it with the wheel. The loom is safe. It was on the top floor of the mill, and the water did not reach it. But the boards are wet and the wool is spoiled, and I have had to send the first bolt back to Vell with a note.

Please write and tell me when you are coming to Lowmarsh. Bring the good saw. We will need it.

Wick
Weaver, the mill at Lowmarsh
%%
Letter: Mira to Tova
Dear Tova,

Lantern is warm. She says your mittens are the best kind, because they fit over gloves and leave room for a little mistake in the size. She has worn them each night at the tower. I told her to keep the spare pair dry, and she put them in the lamp room, away from the draft.

The road is not open yet. Snow stands level with the lower rail at the north bridge. The keeper at the little relay says the drift there has a crust strong enough to hold a fox, but not a person. I will not test it. I have enough work at the tower without falling into the stream.

I heard about the cave under your field. Do not go down alone, and do not take a lantern near loose straw. When the snow clears, I will come with Bram and look from the steps. If the air below smells stale, we will turn back. There is no prize for reaching the end of a dark place.

I once heard a miner say that deep stone can hum in the cold. He had worked at Copperhold, and he said the sound came from far below. Another traveler says the ground here is quiet, and that people mistake wind for a voice when they are tired. I cannot tell which story is true. I have heard no hum at Northpoint.

The lights came again last night. They were not ours. That is all I know. Lantern saw them too, then went back to checking the stair lamps. The lamp at the fourth turn had gone low, and she would not leave it so.

Keep wood for the stove, and let the cat have one corner of the wool basket. I know you will say it has claimed the whole basket already.

When the road opens, I will bring tea and a new shovel. Do not dig at the cave before then.

Mira
%%
Letter: Pell to Ama, the new crystals
Dear Ama,

I am writing from the Brown Hen, where the beds are soft and the beer is thin. I will be in Ambrook in ten days with salt, iron and whatever you have written down for me. Do not start the honey without me.

I heard something in Copperhold that you will like, since you always ask me about the tutors. A tutor there called Merle has been growing crystals in layers. Each layer feeds the next, like the floors of a barn. A crystal with one layer can only learn simple sorting. Everyone knew that. It was the first thing a child learned at the tutor house.

Merle found a way round it. When a crystal gives a wrong answer, you do not just scold it. You tell each layer its share of the blame. You start at the answer and pass the blame back, layer by layer, until you reach the first layer. Each layer then moves its weights a little toward the right answer. The next time it is shown the same thing, it leans a little better.

She says it is like a crew of carters on one load. If the load tips, the carter at the back is not the only one to blame, and the carter at the front is not free either. Each one shares the fault and carries a little less next time.

The tutors in the next hall laughed at her. Then they tried it, and their crystals learned hard things too. They say now that the deeper the layers, the more the blame has to travel. Merle is still cross that nobody gave her the credit at the start.

I will bring you a copy of her note. It is in very small writing, so bring your good spectacles.

Pell
%%
Letter: Mira to Dace
Northpoint tower, winter. Mira writes to Dace, mayor of Three Wells, who has been kind to her brother Tam and to the market. It is a more formal letter than most, and Mira did not enjoy writing it.
To Dace, mayor of Three Wells, from Mira, keeper at the Northpoint tower.
Dear Dace,
My brother Tam, at Kettle Ridge, tells me you have settled his quarrel with Brin over the fence. I do not know what you said to them. I did not know a ruling could be so gentle. Tam says you told them the line is fair, and then you paid for the cider.
I want to thank you. I do not say it often. I am a keeper, and keepers deal in short messages: seen, all well, good night. But this deserves more.
It was a poor harvest on the ridge, as you know. The wet spring took a third of the beetroot, and Tam and Brin both were short. A short year makes a long quarrel. You settled it with a pot of cider. I call that wisdom.
I also want to thank you for the market. I know that you keep it open all winter, so that farmers from the hills can sell what they have. This year many of them depended on it. I heard from Pell that the square was full on the first day of Deepsnow, and the bakers ran out of bread.
Here at Northpoint we have snow up to the first stair lamp. The ninety rungs are slick with ice. Lantern has taken to climbing them with a rope tied round his waist, and I have taken to holding the rope. The fox on the roof is well. I feed him.
I enclose a small gift. It is a pot of Hazel's snow cheese, from the market at Northpoint. It will not last long, since it is meant to be eaten. Share it with the council. Or do not.
With thanks, and with respect,
Mira
%%
Letter: Edda to Hild, a thank you
Dear Hild,
The blue thread was perfect. I used it for the collar of a coat for the miller, and he said it was the bluest coat in Copperhold. It is, because there are no other blue coats.
I wanted to thank you properly, and in writing, because when you were here I only said it with my mouth, and my mouth is a poor thing.
You came on a hot day, on the back of a cart, with a bag on your lap and a jar of honey in each hand. You gave me the cheese for Garn, and he ate it, and said it was sharp. Then he ate more. I think he likes sharp.
You worked at my stall for three days. You carded better than I do, and you did not complain about your arms. You sold forty balls of yarn and talked about Pip for most of them. A man bought two just to make you stop.
You slept in the spare bed and snored. I do not mind. The house was quiet before you came. Now it is quiet again, and I miss the snoring.
Let me tell you what I bought at the market, which I would not tell you in the last letter. It was a small clay pot with a lid, painted blue, from a potter from Lowmarsh. It holds nothing. I put it on the shelf. It looks nice.
I have put the twelve emeralds in the roof tin. It is full now. I can mend the east side of the roof before the rains, if the rains come. They have not come yet. It is a hot summer. The sheep are panting and the wool is thin.
Write again soon. Tell Tam that the wheel is a good wheel. Tell Pip to stay out of the ditch.
Edda
%%
Letter: Briar to Lark, the lamps
Dear Lark,

The cold has come down the ridge. I have been made head of the mares, which means I am the one who gets kicked first.

The new helper at the market came on the second of Frost. Yesterday I said bring the lamps. It brought the lamps, and not the oil, which I had not said.

The festival of the lanterns is three nights off. Mother says she will bake honey cakes, now that the mill has flour again. Please come. Bring the good scarf.

Your sister,
Briar
%%
Letter: Gwen to Wren
Dear Wren,

The cough is gone, and I thank you for the jar of honey. I used a little in tea and saved the rest for a sore throat. Tam says I am saving it for a day that may never come.

I am staying at the house on the edge of Kettle Ridge. The fence there has a gap wide enough for a fox. Tam says he will mend it. The hens do not believe him.

People here speak of the great relay house at Vell. One traveler said its mind can answer every letter in the archive. Another said it can read a whole shelf before the lamps are lit. These are tales, not tests. No tutor I know can check a mind that large alone.

I think it is enough that the mind helps the small apprentices answer their orders. Pip has learned to bring bread without bringing the basket too.

Gwen
%%
Letter: Finch to Ada
Dear Ada,

I found the three emeralds and counted them twice, as you did. The small pot is a good hiding place. I nearly put it beside the clay pots I use for nails.

The fish shed roof is holding. I saw the board in your basket. It has a clean mark from the fish, so you may keep both. I will not charge you for the fish, or for the board. I am glad nobody was standing below it.

I will clear the west gutter tomorrow. The leaves have been gathering there since Swarm. I thought the wind would carry them away. The wind seems to have other work.

Please keep the honey jar. I will take a loaf of bread instead, if you have one to spare. I can bring it home without dropping it. I make no promise about eating it before I reach my door.

Finch
%%
Letter: Brin to Hal, the market
Dear Hal,

Your mother says I must write before the next market. I have been talking to the wall. So here it is.

Biscuit got out. She went as far as the market gate and stood there looking at it. She did not go in. Her two lambs stood behind her in a row, looking at it too. Your mother says she was waiting for someone. I say she was waiting for a bun.

The market was smaller this year. I counted. The cheese woman from Three Wells did not come. Her neighbor says she has gone to Saltreach, with her cows. Cows on a ship, Hal. I said I would believe it when I saw it. Then I thought I might see it. The wool trader's stall was shut. The Kettle Inn had four empty beds at market time, which has not happened in my life. The innkeeper says it is the wind. Everybody says it is the wind.

Pell came with a new axle and fewer jars, and sold all of them by noon. He says the honey is going to Saltreach, for the ships. He said it with his hands in his pockets, which is not like Pell.

The mill squeaks worse than ever. Sefa says it is the sails, which are heavy with ribbons. I told her a mill that squeaks is a mill that works. She said that is what I say about my knee.

Tam's Pip carried my seed sacks up the hill. Nobody asked it. Tam says it was only tired of watching me do it.

Now I will ask you a thing, and you may say nothing if you cannot say. A carter at the pie stall says the Archive has been told to seal its doors. He says the order is to write everything down first. Hanna says the carter is a fool and a gossip. Tam says he has heard it too, from the ridge house. The keeper there would not say yes or no. I told them all: I have a son at Vell. So I am asking you. If you cannot say, send nothing. I will know it as well as a letter.

Your mother has made you a scarf. It is very long. She says you will wear it to the Archive. I said you are inside a hill, it is warm. She said that is no reason.

Come home for the shearing, if they let you. Biscuit is waiting. I do not know what for.

Keep warm. Keep the torches lit.

Your father,
Brin
%%
Letter: Dace to Pell, the mended lamp
Dear Pell,

You asked after the lamp. It is mended.

The glass cart came on the fourth day after the storm, as I had hoped, and the glazier from Saltreach set the new pane in a single morning. He is a quiet man who hums while he works. It was a very pleasant tune and I did not ask what it was.

The lamp was lit that evening. Half the town came out to the square to see it. A small boy asked why we were all standing there. I told him: because it was dark, and now it is not. He said that did not seem a good reason to stand in a square. I said: it is the best reason there is.

The tower keeper, young Tobias, has been given a ribbon. He is embarrassed. He says he only tied a cloth. I told him that a cloth in a storm is a great deal.

Also, the road. I know your cart was caught on the far side of the stream. I hope the horse is well. I hear he is called Barley and he is braver than you. The Brown Hen has told me the whole story. I have repeated it at three fairs.

Come to Three Wells when you can. I will give you the best table at the inn, and the loudest storm story, and you may tell it as you like.

Dace
%%
Letter: Mira to Gale
Dear Gale,

The lamp above Saltreach is broken. Salt wind found a crack where the glass meets the copper ring. The keeper has fitted a new pane, but the redstone line still fails when the wind shakes the tower. It should be lit again before the next clear night.

I sent a note by the lower relay to Wren. She will visit your farm when the lamp is steady. Until then, keep clean water by the cow and do not force her to eat. I know that is easy advice from a tower.

Your question about the apprentice is a good one. Test it first with work that is safe to get wrong. Put the hammer and the spade on the bench, then give it one clear order. Do not use the cow, the roof, or a full cart as the test. Nessa says every mind must be tested on pages it has not seen before it gets important work. A field is no place to discover what it has misunderstood.

The small mind does not answer, "I do not know." It must choose one of its learned meanings. The big mind at the relay house can write back in words, though it too is judged by its answers. I do not know why a crystal learns from examples. None of the tutors can tell us that part.

About the first whole sentence, the tales have always disagreed. Nessa remembers one in the drill room at Vell when she was young. Others name Clank, Pip, or Peg at the fair. Perhaps the first was not seen by the person who later wrote it down.

Do not let Pell call your roof a proper roof until the wind has tested it twice.

Mira
%%
Letter: Teague to Oren, a lost letter
Dear Oren,

I wrote to you at the end of Reaping. You did not answer. I thought you were angry with me for leaving Vell. Then Hesk told me the Copperhold post sack was left on the cart. It was found three days later in the stable, under a horse blanket. The horse had been sleeping on it. So I am writing again, and I will keep this one short.

I am a glassblower, as you know, and I live at Copperhold now. The Archive has a book on the old window glass of Saltreach, the blue that does not fade. I saw it on the shelf in the craft hall. I would like a copy of one page, the page with the mix of sand and ash. Not the whole book. Your clerks have enough to copy.

I will pay for the copy in glass. You once said you wanted a lantern pane that did not smoke. I can make you six.

I miss the craft hall. I miss the tea in the third room, which was always too strong. How is the Archive? Is the cat still not allowed in the copying room? Does Ink still sleep on the Saltreach shelf, and does anyone still pretend not to know?

If both letters reach you, please treat the second as the first, and the first as a very old friend who took the long way.

Your friend from the craft hall,
Teague
Copperhold
%%
Notes: the school play at Three Wells
The play was on the twelfth of Blossom, in the school yard, because the school house is too small for all the mothers.

Mistress Ada wrote it. It is called The Frogs and the Well. It has a frog king, six frogs, a bee and a lost cow. It takes half an hour and the frog hats are of green wool, cut and stitched by the children.

Bel is the first frog. She has eleven lines. She has known them since Frost.

The well is a barrel with a cloth over it. The cloth is from the dyer at Copperhold, dyed blue, and it is beautiful, but it is too heavy and it slid off in the middle of the second act. The frogs went on anyway.

The lost cow is two children under a brown blanket. They moo a great deal. Nobody minds.

The bee was meant to buzz. The buzzing child forgot, so the bee was silent, and the frog king said his line to a silent bee, and the mothers laughed, but kindly.

At the end the frogs all say together: We will keep the well clean. Then they bow. Bel bowed so low her hat fell in the barrel.

Afterward there was honey cake and tea. The mayor gave a short speech. It was longer than the play.

Next year, says Mistress Ada, they will do the story of the relay tower. She needs a lamp. She has asked the keeper at Vell if we may borrow one. She has not heard back.
%%
Letter: Mave to Pell, the borrowed horse
Dear Pell,
You may have the grey mare for the autumn road, as you asked. She is steady on the hill but a fool on the bridge. Walk her over the bridge at Saltreach, by the rail, and do not let her see the water. She thinks it is waiting for her.
Her name is Dove. She will answer to Dove if you are quiet, and to nothing if you are not. Feed her the oats from the bin by the door, two handfuls morning and night, and one apple if she has been good. She has been good all summer, so give her two.
Please bring her back by the Leaffall fair. The bees need her cart for the honey, and the children are fond of her. Little Ann has already asked me three times if Dove will come back, and I have said yes, so do not make a liar of me.
The bees are very good this year. The clover on the dry bank was thick, and the heather came late, and the hives are heavy. I have forty jars already, and the rest will come in the next week. I will send you two of the dark ones, for your mother.
Take care on the Long Road. The bridge at the Brown Hen is soft at the edge. Keep to the middle.
Mave
Hives behind the house, the hamlet near Saltreach
%%
Letter: Gorm to Ilse, the hole
Dear Ilse,

You will not believe what Tam's cousin found under his oat field at the edge of the ridge. He was digging a drain, the way you do before the autumn rains, and his spade went down into nothing. The ground just gave way.

He called for his wife and his two boys, and they all came with lanterns. Under the field was a cave. It was dry and round, with a floor of sand as smooth as flour. The walls were pale stone with small stripes of red in them, like the grain in an old oak door.

Nobody went in far at first. Tam's cousin is a cautious man. He lowered a lantern on a rope and called down to the bottom. The echo came back three times before it stopped. Then the boys wanted to go down, so he made them wait for a proper ladder.

I went up last week with two clerks from the archive. We took a box of fine tools and a sheet to write on. The cave goes down about the height of three houses. At the bottom there is a pool of clear water. It is very cold and tastes of stone. We filled a bottle, and it is sitting on my desk now.

The clerks wanted to know if the old people left anything in it. We found nothing except a broken clay bowl and some charred wood, and those may be from a farmer who sheltered from rain a long time ago. Nothing else. No writing. No marks on the walls.

The new road to Kettle Ridge passes just beyond the field, so the cave will be seen by many travelers. I have asked the council to put a stone post by the drain, with a torch and a sign that says "Mind the hole." Tam's cousin says he has already put up a fence. He did it in an afternoon, and his wife says it is the straightest fence in the valley.

Come and see it in the spring. Bring the drawing book. I want your opinion on the stripes.

Gorm
Kettle Ridge, the 157th year of the archive
%%
Letter: Ada to Noll
Dear Noll,

Thank you for writing from the Brown Hen. I am glad the innkeeper found you a dry place. I hope your shoes are dry by now.

You say the visiting tutor carries drill boards. Tell me what is drawn on them. I have only seen the old board in the school house. It shows a field, a ditch, and a little line of oak trees. I thought the marks were for teaching an apprentice to follow a row.

I remember the tutor who came through Three Wells last Apple. She had a copper hand on her helper and a loose strap on her box. She said a mind learns from the examples it is shown. The helper had been shown wheat rows many times. It could tend wheat. It could not sort apples.

I asked whether a crystal could learn two answers. She said the old kind could be moved toward one answer or the other, but not both together. Kester proved that for one kind of crystal. He did not prove that every kind must fail.

I wish the council had heard the difference. Now the tutor house stands dark, and the drill boards are stacked under a cover. The children use the yard to roll hoops. They are careful not to hit the boards.

There is a broken relay lamp by the east road. I found it in the grass on my way home. The glass is cracked, and the little redstone line has come loose. I left it with Dace. He says it belongs to the keeper, though nobody has come to mend it.

Your stew needs salt too. The innkeeper may have a poor hand with salt, but he has a good hand with a blanket.

Ada
%%
Letter: Fen to Sorrel
Fen, weaver, Three Wells. Autumn, near the end. Fen writes to the teacher Sorrel to say thank you for the year, as his shop is quiet and the wool is spun. It is a long letter, and the reader will find it kind.
Dear Sorrel,
It is nearly Longnight. The market is closing for the winter. My loom is quiet, since the wool is spun and the cloth is woven, and I have time to write letters. I have been meaning to write to you.
I want to say how much the year has meant. You gave my ewe a seat. You gave the children a lesson. You gave the fair a ribbon. You let me stand at the back of the school one afternoon and watch you teach. I have not been in a school since I was a boy, and I had forgotten how loud it is.
The fair prize did not matter. I did not win. A ewe from Kettle Ridge did. But I won something else. I won a story. I have told it forty times at the inn, and each time it grows. It has now a flood, a creeper and a wolf. I do not know how the wolf got in.
The ewe is well. She has been shorn for the winter. She looks very different without her coat, thin and surprised. She has asked to be allowed in the house. I have said no. She is a sheep. She has said no.
Here is a gift. I have woven a blanket for the school. It is red and green, the colors of the ribbon. It is long enough for four children to sit on. It is not very good. It is a weaver's gift, and it is made with love.
If a child cries, wrap it in the blanket. If a sheep wanders in, put it on the blanket. If the fire goes out, use it for warmth.
I wish you a good winter. May the stove be warm, and the children quiet, and the roof sound. Write to me when the thaw comes.
Your friend,
Fen
%%
Letter: Ada to Ned
Dear Ned,
I know you think the stall by the boardwalk is too close to the water. I know you think a stall should sit on dry boards. But the board men said the dry boards are full this spring, and the water boards have room, and the water boards have the best torch on the corner. So I took it.
I have sold four baskets of reed mats and one very big hat. The hat was a joke, and the man who bought it is a joker too. He wore it all the way to the fish weir and then he gave it to his dog.
The rain came in at noon and I had to hold a sheet over the reed mats for an hour. A boatman's son held the other corner. He was a nice boy with a broken tooth and no interest in the sheet. We got soaked together, which I suppose is what a market is for.
Please bring the other pole when you come. The one I have is short, and I have had to stand on a box to reach the top sheet. The customers are laughing at me. Not unkindly, but they are laughing.
Your loving wife,
Ada

Letter: Cob to Oren, my first market
Dear Oren,

You asked me to write down what happened at the market, so that I will remember it, and so that you will know which parts were my fault.

You gave me a list. Paper, six crates. Wax, four blocks. Quills, forty. I read the list three times on the road. I want it noted that I read it three times.

The paper seller, Hob, is a large man with a small voice. I asked for six crates. He said, six crates of what? I said paper. He said, reed or rag? I did not know there were two. I said both. He said, that is twelve crates. I said no, six, half and half. He thought about it. He gave me three of each, and I paid, and I was very proud.

At the quill stall I bought forty quills. They were cheap. I did not ask why. On the road home I found out. Thirty-one were cut for the left hand.

I am sorry. I have brought you three crates of reed paper, three of rag, and thirty-one left-handed quills. Tomorrow I will take the quills back, and I will be firm. I will say, I am Cob of the Archive. He will say, I know. You were here yesterday.

Your clerk,
Cob

Letter: Orla to Dara, the long night
Dear Dara,

Thank you for the socks. They are too long in the foot, which means they were made by someone who has never had feet of her own. I am wearing them anyway, and so is the cat, who has taken the left one for her bed.

Now the news from the house at the edge of the mine. Last week the copper cart came in at dusk with its wheel off, and the men carried the copper up the hill by hand. They sang the whole way, which I did not know they could do in the cold. They sang the one about the lamp boy who fell in the well, which is not a sad song, though it sounds like one.

The school here put on a play for the midwinter. It was called The Long Night. There was a wolf, a lamb, a miner with a lamp, and a baker who was not in the play but came anyway, to sell pies in the back row. The wolf was Dunny, who growled the whole time. The lamb ran off to the mine gate twice. The miner forgot his line and said Good night, Ida, instead, which made the baker cry.

The Halfway Inn sent us a barrel of soup on the cart. Nobody knows why the innkeeper sent it, but it was very good, and the carter said it had been made by a man who loved his own stove.

Our apprentice Brim has been given a new word this week, and it is not a helpful one. The word is stay. It means to stand very still by the door until someone comes back. Brim has taken it with great feeling. It stood by the lift door for a whole afternoon, so still that the children put their own hats on its shoulders. Hesk says that is what staying looks like, and that a good apprentice does not complain. I think it is what the apprentice has been taught, and that it is happy about it.

The count board is full. We had nine new carts of coal in one week, and the sorting shed has run out of bins. The men are making more bins from the old barrels. It is slow work, but the barrels do not mind.

Ama sent a jar of honey up the road for the midwinter. It sits on the table, and nobody may touch it, because Dunny has been appointed to watch it.

I have been thinking about the wall. I was on the wall at Copperhold for thirty years, walking the top in every weather, watching the road. I used to think a wall was for keeping things out. I think now it is only for keeping the place where you are. A wall is a kind of sentence. It says, here, this is where we are.

Write back in the spring. Tell me whether the new boat at Saltreach is red yet. Tell me whether the children in Vell are still singing the long song, and whether anyone has written it down.

Your loving sister,
Orla

Post: the cart goes down on the first of Thaw. It may be late. Everything is late this winter, except the wolf."

Letter: Thane to his brother
Dear Ruen,

Do not come up to the farm this summer. There is no harvest to come up for. The wheat has rotted in the wet. The beetroot is small and bitter. The potatoes came up black in the middle, and when you cut them the smell was like a cellar left shut.

I went out to the long field yesterday with the scythe, and the stalks did not even bend. They just broke. I counted the sheaves. There are eleven. Last year there were three hundred.

Father used to say a poor year is only a year. You plant again, and the next is better. I say it to myself at night and it does not help. I think of the bread we will not have, and the bread the children will not have, and the carts that will come up the road in autumn and find nothing to carry.

The steward at the Halfway says the grain will come up from the south, on the carts, and the price will be fair. He says it every year. This year the price is not fair. The grain is two emeralds the sack, and nobody has two emeralds to spare.

Please send what you can. Do not send coins. Send honey, if the bees have been kind, or apples from the old trees, or anything that keeps. We will be living on the stores until the spring.

I am not writing to frighten you. I am writing because I need someone to tell the truth to, and you are the only one who will not say it will be all right.

Thane
The farm at the top of the lane, Copperhold road
%%
Letter: Yarrow to Ama, the bet
Dear Ama,

You owe me a jar of dark honey. You bet that the Brown Hen would not get its roof on before the first frost. I bet that it would. The roof went on this morning, two days early, and the frost is not due until next week. So the honey is mine.

Do not send the light kind. I know you have plenty. I want the dark, the one you keep in the cool store behind the apples, the one you say is for your mother.

Your mother can have a pot of the light. She will not know the difference, and neither will the roof.

The men did it well. Hal did the far side with his own hands, and swore at every nail. Juna carried the slates up the ladder all morning, one by one, and did not drop a single one. Old Brin sat on the wall and told them they were doing it wrong, and then he came down and did it right. He would not take any credit. He said it was the apprentice that carried the bundles, and that was true. It stood at the foot of the ladder and passed the slates up as fast as Juna could take them. It did not look up once. It only watched the ladder.

I owe Hal a pie. Tell him it is coming, and tell him the pie is cherry, and that he will have to wait for the cherries.

Your friend, and a very cold innkeeper,
Yarrow

Letter: Corin to Idrin, Kester's note
Dear Idrin,

You sent me the copy of Kester's note, and the clerks at Vell are reading it wrong. You wrote that they say it proves minds are a waste of money. I am a shipwright, not a tutor, so correct me where I am wrong.

Kester says a simple crystal sorts by drawing one line. Yes on one side of the line, no on the other. That is all one layer of weights can do. It is a single cut across a board.

Now take four pegs and set them at the corners of a square board. Put two yes pegs on opposite corners, and two no pegs on the other two. The rule is "one or the other, but not both". A yes when exactly one of the two lamps is lit, and a no when both are lit or both are dark.

Try to cut the board with one straight line so the yes pegs sit on one side and the no pegs on the other. You cannot. Every straight cut leaves some peg on the wrong side. Kester is right about that. One line will not do it.

I tried it with chalk on the shop floor, with my youngest, Sable, who is nine. She got it in about a minute. She drew a bent line, going round one of the pegs. So a mind could do it, if its line could bend. But a simple crystal cannot bend. Kester wrote that down, and he is right about the simple crystal. He said nothing about any other kind, because nobody has built one.

So the council reads "never", and stops paying. That is the part I do not follow. A shipwright would not give up on boats because the first boat leaked. He would try another plank.

Maybe two crystals could work together and bend the line between them. Nobody here knows how to drill two at once. Maybe nobody can.

I am sending the chalk drawing with this letter. Keep it. Do not let a clerk file it.

Your friend,
Corin
%%
Letter: Wynn to Rill
Dear Rill,

Good questions. I will answer them both, and I will answer the second one first because it is easier.

The soft cheese has a rind because of how it is made. It is salted and set slow, and a skin grows on the outside as it dries. That skin is the rind. It is part of the cheese and you can eat it. Some folk cut it off. Those folk are wrong, but they are allowed.

The pressed cheese is made in a big press that squeezes the water out. There is no time for a skin to grow, because it is pressed hard and dry and wrapped in cloth. So it has no rind. It has a crust, but a crust is not a rind. I know they sound the same. They are not.

Now the honey. The bees do not make the cheese. That is important. I want you to tell your friend this. The bees make the honey. I keep the bees by the hives on the hill. I take the honey out of the hives and put a little into the cheese. A little, not a lot. If you use too much the cheese does not set and it turns to a mess. I learned that the hard way.

So the bees help make the honey cheese, but they do not make the cheese. They would be very bad at it. They are good at honey and they should stick to that.

I am glad you are making the goat song longer. I am not worried about people getting bored, as long as the goat keeps doing things and you keep making the noise. But I will give you one piece of advice, from someone who has sung a few songs. End with the hat. The hat is the best joke. Do not bury the hat in the middle. Save the hat for the end.

And share the honey next time. Your grandmother is right about most things. Not the oven. The honey.

Your friend,
Wynn
Cheesemaker
%%
Letter: Hesk to Wick
Wick,

Your goat is in my yard. It has eaten my beans. It has eaten the cloth off the line. It has eaten the bucket I left by the gate, and it has stood on the roof of my shed, and it is now sitting on my step, looking at me with the face of a goat that owns the mine.

I am not angry. I am only telling you where your goat is, so that you can come and get it before it eats the mine hall.

The goat came up the Copperhold road this morning. It walked straight through the gate of the mine yard, past the sorting shed, and into the tool store. It tried to eat a helmet. Bram got it out with a pole. It went up the ladder to the forge and sat on the roof. Clank went up after it and brought it down, very gently, by the collar. It was not grateful.

The goat has a red collar and a bell. The bell rings when it moves. I can hear it from the count board. I am sure the whole mine hears it too.

Please come before the bell rings again, Wick. Bring a rope. Bring your patience. And bring the good turnips the goat likes, because it will not come for anything else.

Hesk
Copperhold, the mine gate
%%
Letter: Mira to Tam, the long nights
Tam,

Northpoint is buried. The snow came in Longnight and has not gone to see how we are doing. It is up to the second window. From the lamp room I can see sky through the top panes and nothing else.

You always want news and never send yours, so here is mine.

The lamp at the top of the tower is lit every night. The oil came on the last cart, one barrel, and the council says it is the last until the road opens. The road does not open in Frost. It does not open in Longnight. It might open in Sowing if the snow is kind. The snow has never been kind here.

The copper ring round the lamp room has gone green in the cold, and so has the roof. The council says there is no copper to spare. So the roof waits. It is good at waiting.

Lantern keeps the stair lamps. There is a lamp at every turn of the stair, and at the top a ladder of ninety rungs, which I counted again this morning. Ninety. Lantern climbs each evening and lights each lamp. It climbs slowly. It never gets tired, and I am tired of watching it. I watch it anyway.

There is no tutor coming. The hall at Vell has shut, and I hear the tutors are walking home with their boards under their arms. So I teach Lantern the lamps myself. I stand at the bottom with the torch and say, light the third lamp. It lights the third lamp. I say, light the fifth. It lights the fifth. When it is right I say good. It does not know what good means. I say it anyway, the way you pat a dog.

Last week Lantern lit the lamp at the wrong turn. I climbed the whole flight in the dark to say so, which was a silly thing to do at my age. Lantern did not mind. It lit the right lamp after that, as if it had never done the wrong one.

The nights here are very long. In the evenings I talk and Lantern lights the lamps. That is how a tower keeps its keeper company.

The snow has been in my boots since Longnight. The boots are thinking about leaving. I have told them they cannot go until the spring.

Write to me. One sentence will do. A drawing of a pig will do. A letter with one line of news in it is worth more than a page from the council.

Keep warm. Do not stand near the ditch.

Mira
Northpoint relay tower, the top room
%%
Letter: Toby to Mira, news of the farm
Mira,

Thank you for the letter. I am glad Northpoint is warm enough to melt the ice off your stair lamp. Keep the torches round the foot. The nights are still long up there.

Here is our news. The bees swarmed on the second of Sowing, right onto the fence post, the same post as last year. I shook them into a new hive and they settled in a day. The honey from the old hive is dark this year, from the heather. Sweet, but it tastes of the hill. Rill says it is the best yet. Rill says that every year.

The apple trees flowered late but full. The pig had four piglets. One is small and follows me everywhere, and I have not named it because we do not name the ones we eat. The big mind in the relay house at Copperhold came last month. They say it is the size of a loaf and it sits in a ring of quickeners kept cool with water. Clank the miner's apprentice asked it something through the relay. The answer came back the same day.

I read the question Orla of Vell wrote, the one about a hidden mind and a person answering letters, and readers guessing which is which. I do not know what to think. I told Rill I would ask you, since you keep a relay and hear everything. What do the folk at Northpoint say of it? Half of them say a mind that reads letters has passed the test already. The other half say it has only learned to copy.

Write soon, and stay warm. Tell Lantern to keep the lamp trimmed.

Toby
%%
Letter: Oren to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

You missed the song contest, so I am writing it down before the details go. A clerk should record everything, and this is worth recording.

It was held in the front hall of the archive, because it was raining and the square at Three Wells was a river. Dace said it was the best rain we have had. The mayor was in the front row and fell asleep in the second song.

The winner was a girl from Ambrook, about fourteen, who sang about the bees going out in Blossom and coming home heavy. She had a jar of honey for a prize and she gave a spoonful to everyone in the hall. There was a great deal of coughing. Honey is not meant for spoons.

Second place was a man from the Long Road who sang about the Halfway Inn and the riddle board. He forgot the last verse and the whole hall sang it for him. Even the sleepy mayor woke up for that.

There was one quarrel. A baker from Three Wells said the Ambrook girl had not written her own song. The girl said she had, and her grandmother said she had, and the grandmother is very tall, so the baker said no more.

I have filed the words to the honey song. The girl wrote them out for me. She spelled Blossom wrong twice.

I hope the drills go well. Are the apprentices behaving? Do not answer that. I know they are.

Oren
%%
Letter: Nessa to Odo
Dear Odo,

Thank you for writing down the contest songs. The tutors brought them to Vell, and the clerks made copies for the great drills. One copy had a wet corner, but the words were clear. We dried it by the copying room window.

The reading mind sees a small piece of a page at a time. It uses the marks before that piece to guess what comes next. It can learn that a greeting often comes before a name, or that a song returns to the same line. It does not know the ferry or the singer. It has found patterns in the writing.

The tutors give it many pages because one page teaches little. They take away spoiled copies and repeated pages where they can. They also keep a box of pages for tests. No mind is drilled on those pages. Then the tutors can see whether it learned to read patterns, rather than repeat lines it has already seen.

We cannot tell you whose letters were gathered first. Some say the clerks asked every town. Others say they began with the Archive shelves. The old records do not agree. The clerks have kept the lists, but the lists are not all alike.

I will ask the judges to sit under the barn roof next spring. Your board will be useful.

With thanks,
Nessa
%%
Letter: Bry to Gale, the well ran dry
Dear Gale,

The well in the yard ran dry on the ninth of Longday. Not low. Dry. I dropped the bucket and it hit the bottom with a clang, and there was no water in it at all, only a little mud and one very surprised frog.

So now we walk to the town well with the cart. It is a long walk in the heat. The horse is not pleased. I am not pleased. The frog has moved into the rain barrel and seems happier there.

Everyone says the same thing: it will fill again in Leaffall. I hope everyone is right. In the meantime we are being careful with water. No more long washing of the floors. No more filling the trough to the top. The chickens have noticed and are cross about it.

Mum says the well never ran dry when she was a girl. Then she says she is sure it did once, she just cannot remember. That is Mum.

The heat is bad this summer. The wheat is standing tall but it is thirsty. We water it from the cart in the evening, and the water goes down into the dust so fast you would think the ground was drinking it.

How is the mine? Is it cool down there? I am jealous of the cool.

Write soon. Send news. Send rain, if you can find any.

Bry
The house by the yard
%%
Letter: Merle to her father
Dear Father,

You asked why I am still at Lowmarsh. I will tell you plainly.

The marsh stone is not like Copperhold stone. It is soft and wet and it splits along its grain if you look at it wrong. The old marsh wall was failing. I cut new posts and set them with a footing of cobblestone packed with clay. The water will not move them now.

The folk here are kind but they talk about the grey cough. Wren the healer has a house with high shelves of jars. She lets me read the labels. She says half of them are wrong and she means to fix them.

I have not forgotten the trade you taught me. I keep my chisels dry. I am eating.

Your daughter,
Merle

Letter: Oren to Pell
Dear Pell,

Thank you for the news. I hope the well fills again when the autumn rain comes. Until then, carry water from the stream in clean pails. Do not drink from the mine ditch.

The cat may be right about the grain shed. Mice do not need to be invited. Still, tell the farmer to keep the sacks raised from the floor. A dry store saves more grain than a brave cat.

At the Archive, we have begun copying the tutors' notes about the reading minds. One page says the mind reads one letter, then the next, using what came before. Its window is about sixty letters wide. The note is clear, but I had to read it twice.

Your apples arrived in good order. I put one by the copying room window.

Oren

Letter: Hobb to Pell, the stool
Pell,

You owe me for a stool. One stool, by the door, which you sat on last Blossom and then declared the finest stool on the road. The leg went with a crack you could hear in the kitchen. I have had it mended three times. It still rocks like a boat, and the carters ask me whether it is a new kind of seat.

You also owe me for the second bowls. You had six. The bowls are not free because you say they are small. The bowls are the same size as anyone's, and the soup is the same price, and you know this, because you have written the price on my board yourself.

I have sent the honey back. You will say I am cross about the honey. I am not cross about the honey. The honey is good, and the jar is very nice, and the red string is a fine touch. I am cross that you tied it to the cart seat, which is where the mule sits to eat.

If you come through in summer, bring the stool money. Bring a second stool, if you can find a straight one. I will put the first stool in the yard and let the carters argue about it.

Hobb, innkeeper, Halfway

Letter: Kester to Lark, the market day
Dear Lark,

I promised to tell you the whole market day in order, so here it is, from before the sun to after the lamps. You were in bed with a cold and missed all of it, so you can read this on the step and feel very sorry for yourself.

Before the sun. The carts start up the hill while it is still grey. You can hear the wheels on the stones of the lower road. The first is always the cheese cart from Kettle Ridge, and the cheesemaker's dog sleeps on top of the cheeses, and nobody has ever asked him to move.

Dawn. The bakers light the ovens at the bottom of the market, under the oak. The smell goes up the hill and wakes the clerks. Oren says the smell is the only thing the Archive has never managed to file.

Morning. The fish carts come in from Saltreach. They are wet, and so is everybody near them. The fish seller shouted the price of cod across the square, four emeralds the barrel, which Father says is robbery. He bought two barrels anyway, and then he was sorry about the smell in the cart all the way home.

The honey stall. The bees came, as they do every Melon. They sat on the jars and on the cloth. Brin tied the cloth, then untied it, then sat down on a barrel with his face in his hands. The jars are labeled in red now. Brin says the red is for people who do not trust his handwriting, and that includes everybody, including Brin.

Midday. The song contest under the oak. I have written to Bel about it, so I will say only that someone threw a melon rind at the tutor, and it landed in the bread basket, and the baker has asked for it back.

The lower road. A cart with a loose wheel crossed the new bridge and came out the other side without losing anything. The carter bought everyone a drink, which is not what the rail is for.

Late afternoon. Oren let me stand in the doorway of the copying room. From the big window you can see the whole town, the carts going out on the Long Road, the windmill on Kettle Ridge, and the oak at the bottom of the hill with its shade pulled in. He says it is a town that writes everything down, and so it is the best window in the world for seeing a day.

Dusk. The lamps are lit in the square. Each one is lit from a taper by a child with a careful hand. The traveler from the Long Road plays a pipe, badly, and everybody claps for him, because he has come a very long way and he is going to play whether they clap or not.

Night. Father walks us home. The torches stand on their posts along the road, one every twelve blocks, and we count them, and I got to nineteen before I fell asleep on his shoulder. Mother says the day was very long and very good. We say yes. Then the dog wakes up on the cheese cart and walks home across the top of the load, and nobody asks him to move.

Your friend, who has written it all down,
Kester
%%
Letter: Noll to Ada
Dear Ada,

I am at the Brown Hen. The rain came hard on the Long Road, so I stopped before my shoes filled with mud.

The innkeeper gave me a dry place by the hearth. I paid with two emeralds. One was so small he weighed it twice.

A visiting tutor from Vell is here. Her name is Lysa. She carries a box of old drill boards. She says the tutors have little work now. The council stopped paying them, and most of the halls are shut.

I asked her if the old crystal could learn two things at once. She said no. It could learn one answer or the other, but not both. Kester wrote that down, and the councils believed him.

I am not sure why they thought that meant no mind could ever do more. Perhaps they were tired of paying for lessons.

The fire is warm. I wish you were here to tell the innkeeper his stew needs salt.

Noll

Letter: Ulla to Oren, the gate
Dear Oren,

You asked me to write the business of the gate in full, for the book, because you were not there and the clerks will ask. So here it is, as plainly as I can make it.

On the third night of Melon, after the lamps were lit in the front hall, there was a knock at the great gate. Three knocks, slow, and then a long scrape against the wood. I thought it was a cart. Then I thought it was Wenna's horse, which likes to lean on things. Then I looked through the grille and saw a zombie standing in the lane, very still, with one arm up against the door.

I did what the rule says. I did not open the gate. I held the lamp from the wall up to the grille, and the zombie did not like the light. It moved back a step. It did not go away.

Then Pell came up the hill with his cart and a torch. He shouted at it, and it shuffled sideways. Then Dell, the wood cutter, came down from the road with a spade. Ned came out of the copying room with a bucket. Between the four of us, we drove it down the lane to the bend. It went round the bend, and we did not see it again.

I have written the gate in the book, and the cat has been moved from the big book to the top of the door, where she can see the lane. She is not pleased. She says the zombie was hers.

Your friend, Ulla

Letter: Cass to Ilse, the spoon
Dear Ilse,

Your cousin's baby is four days old, and she is the loudest thing on the farm. I say that with love. She wants feeding all night and she has opinions about the blanket.

I have carved a spoon for her, from the birch by the well. Gorm's boy helped with the rough work, and I did the smooth. It is small enough for her mouth, and it has a little bee on the handle, because I could not resist. You can tell her the bee is for her grandmother.

Do not tell me you are too busy to visit. Hives do not hive themselves. Come in Melon, when the honey is in, and bring a jar from the red ribbon hive. Your grandmother would have brought it herself.

Cass
Healer, the farm above Copperhold

Notes: on Isla's curve
These are Nessa's notes for the tutors at Vell, winter of the 196th year of the archive.

Isla's rule is simple and we must teach it simply. A bigger crystal, drilled longer on more writing, grows a better mind. And the curve can be drawn before the mind is grown. That is the whole of it. The rest is practice.

Here is how I put it to the apprentices in the drill room.

Take two crystals, one small and one large. Drill each on the same pages, the same number of times. Test each on pages it was never drilled on. The large one does better. We all knew that.

Now take two crystals the same size. Drill one on a hundred pages and one on two hundred. The one with two hundred does better. But not twice as well. It does better by the curve, and the curve bends. The first pages teach the most. The two hundredth page teaches a little.

Now take a large crystal and a hundred pages, and a small crystal and a thousand. Isla's curve will tell you which wins before you begin. Draw it on the board. Let the apprentices guess first. They guess wrong. Then draw the curve, and they see.

I will tell you what the curve does not say, because the children ask, and the apprentices ask, and I have no answer.

It does not say why the curve bends. Isla could draw it. Every tutor since has drawn it and got the same line. Why it bends, no one wrote down. Leave it open. Do not invent a reason to fill the space.

It does not say what the mind is doing inside the crystal. Looking inside was not done in Isla's day. It is not done now.

It does not say what happens when the crystal is so large that no tutor can check the mind alone. The councils have begun to ask. I have no answer for them either. I say: build the house, and test the mind on pages it has never seen, and be honest about what you cannot check.

One more thing for the drill room. The councils are counting coins for relay houses, tower by tower. They will want a number before they build. Teach the curve, and they will have their number. Teach it honestly, and tell them the curve bends, or they will promise more than the crystal can hold. A promise you cannot keep is worse than a small promise kept.

Nessa
Tutor of apprentices, Vell

The net store roof is done, and the room still smells of oak. Thank you for replacing both rafters. I know you had to carry the second one alone after I went to the quay. I am sorry I was late. A basket of herring split on the pier, and every fish went in a different direction.

Letter: Pell to Mira
Dear Mira,

I am writing from the Brown Hen. The inn is full of festival folk, and I have taken a place at the end of a table beside the flour bin. The innkeeper says I may sleep upstairs if I move my sacks first. I have moved them twice.

The Apple festival was bright and crowded. Bel read a short piece about the old bridge, and the children painted apples on the notice board. One painted a fish. Bel said it was an apple seen from the side. Nobody believed her, but she kept the fish.

I met your relay keeper from the north road, who carried a message from the tower. The note was clear and warm. Some of the folk at the festival thought a clerk had written it. The keeper said the big mind wrote it. Then three people asked how a mind in a crystal could know what to say.

I told them what Oren told me. A reading mind takes a page one letter at a time. Its window holds about sixty letters. It uses earlier letters that matter to guess the next one. Tutors drill it on many pages, compare its guesses with the pages, and change it when it is wrong. The mind learns the work by seeing examples. It does not read all the page at once.

That answer did not end the talk. One man asked whether the mind understands the words. A woman asked whether a person understands them any better. Then the innkeeper rang his spoon on a cup and told us to stop arguing before the stew went cold.

I have no answer to Orla's question. I only know that the note told me the north road was passable and the bridge was sound. That was useful news. The words were kind too.

I will leave the festival tomorrow and take your parcel north. It has been safe under my coat, though one corner now smells of honey. I hope that is not important.

Your friend,
Pell
