Letter: Ulla to Oren, the spoiled box
Dear Oren,

I have been asked to tell you the spoiled box is full. You will know what that means. It means the clerks have been throwing out pages for six months, and nobody has counted them.

I counted them. There are three hundred and eleven pages in the box. Seventy of them are the same drill, page four, which somebody wrote out wrong on the first day and then copied a dozen times. The copies went to the farm school, and the apprentices there drilled on the wrong sums for a whole season.

Nobody meant it. The farm school did not know. The clerks did not check. I did not check either, and that is the part I am writing to tell you about.

I think the test box caught it in the end. A tutor tested the farm mind on the sums in the test box, and the mind got them all wrong. The tutor sent the page back. It took two weeks to find out why. The page was wrong. The drill was wrong. The test box was right.

So I am putting the copy checks back. Every page now gets read aloud by a second clerk before it goes to the drill. It takes longer. It is worth it.

Please do not be angry with me for asking you to take the box to the top floor. You can start with the seventy.

Ulla
%%
Tutor's note: The test of Orla
For apprentices, and for anyone who asks.

Orla was a tutor at Vell, about the ninetieth year of the archive. She asked a question. Can a drilled mind ever think? People had argued about it for years and never settled it, because everyone meant something different by thinking. So Orla gave them a test instead.

Here is the test, in plain words.

Take one mind and one person. Keep them apart, so nobody knows which is which. Write the same questions to both, by letter. Let both write back. Then give the letters to readers who were not told which letter came from the mind and which from the person. Ask the readers to sort them.

If the readers can tell, then something in the letters shows the mind is not like a person, and we can say what it is. If the readers cannot tell, then what? Orla did not answer her own question. That is why people argued ever after.

Here is what the test does not settle, and you should know it.

First, the questions matter. A mind drilled on sums will do sums well and may still have nothing to say about the weather. If the readers ask only what the mind was drilled on, the test is easy and proves little. A good test asks about something new.

Second, the readers matter. If they want the mind to pass, they will guess wrong. If they want it to fail, they will guess wrong the other way. Pick plain readers and tell them nothing.

Third, copying is not thinking, or maybe it is. A mind that reads letters learns to guess the next letter from the ones before. It can write a letter a reader cannot tell from a person's. Some say that is only copying. Some say a person does the same, and we do not hold it against them.

Orla's test is still used. Contests between tutors' halls give each mind the same unseen questions, and the readers are folk from the towns. What the test proves, nobody agrees. What it shows, everyone can see: that a mind can answer in letters, and that we cannot always tell.

Write your own answer. Keep it short.

Nessa, tutor at Vell
%%
Letter: Nessa to her sister
Dear Rill,

You asked me what I do all day. Here it is, in order.

I stand the apprentices in their row. I put a crystal in the drill box and set the drill. The crystal answers. When it is wrong, I move its weights a little toward the right answer. That is the whole of it. The rest is patience and good pages.

Pip's mind can now sort a field of beetroot from a field of wheat, most days. Clank's mind can carry a load along a wall and not drop it. Both are wrong about one thing in ten. Nessa's rule: never send an untested mind to the fields.

Come to Vell before the snow and I will show you the copying room. It is the brightest room in the hill.

Your sister,
Nessa

P.S. I have your jar of honey on the shelf by the drill box. The apprentices cannot reach it. I checked.

Letter: Dace to Jory, the lost shingles
Dear Jory,

I have a small matter, and a large one.

The small matter first. After the storm, one hundred and twelve shingles from the roof of the town house landed in your mill pond at Ambrook. I know this because a boy from your village rowed over the next morning with a basket and a long face. He said they floated. I did not think a shingle could float that far, but the wind was very bad.

Please send them back by the next cart. I do not need all of them. Eighty would do. The other thirty-two I give to the ducks.

The large matter. The relay lamp on my tower broke in the same storm, and the lamp at Ambrook is the nearest one that still shone. Your keeper lit it for us. She sent messages up and down the line all night, because ours was dark. When the glazier came, she sent word to him as well. I owe her a great deal. I would like to send her a gift, but I do not know what she likes. Do you?

Last time we met, you said she keeps bees. If so, I will send a hive. If not, I will send a cheese. Both are good. Both are made in Three Wells, in the cellar of the inn.

I hope the wheel at the mill is turning well, and the sluice is still shut. You know the one.

Your friend,
Dace

Letter: Isla to Cass, on planning a mind
Dear Cass,

Rell sent your question to me with a note about his wet drill board. I know the feeling of a spoiled tally. We had to copy one trial twice after a clerk found a missing page.

Your example is close. A curve does not tell us every taste of a finished batch, and it does not tell us each answer of a mind. It gives a useful plan from many trials. We compare minds with different amounts of room and different amounts of writing. We test them fairly, on pages they have not read. Then we draw what growth we can expect from more room and more drilling.

It is important to say what the curve cannot do. It cannot make a poor page good. It cannot make a mind learn without examples. It cannot replace the test. A larger crystal gives the mind room to keep more patterns, but those patterns must come from writing and practice.

The council is building relay houses because large minds may answer apprentices in many towns. That work will take stone, glass, copper and careful keepers. It will take water too. Your well may not be the only one that runs low when builders arrive. Tell the harbor keeper to count the water before he lends it. A promise is easier to keep when the jars are measured.

I have not seen the marks on your hill. I will ask the surveyors to spare the oak if it stands outside their wall. I cannot promise they will. Plans must fit the ground, and the ground is not always kind to plans.

Keep the old pages dry. A small mind can learn from them, and a larger mind can learn more, if the pages are clear and the lessons continue.

Isla
%%
Letter: Gorm to Ilse, the cave again
Dear Ilse,

I promised to write more about the cave. The clerks have now made a copy of the drawing and it is pinned up in the hall at Kettle Ridge, where everyone can see it. Here is what they found when they went down a second time.

The cave is not a single room. It is a set of rooms, one below the other, joined by narrow steps cut into the stone. The steps are worn in the middle, as if many feet had gone up and down them. Nobody living can say who cut them, or when. Some people say it was a farmer who wanted a cool store. Others say it was the old miners, long before Copperhold had a shaft.

On the second level the walls are smooth. The clerks put a hand on them and said they felt warm. I put my hand on them too, and they were cold. I think they were only less cold than the pool at the bottom. Stone does that. It keeps the heat it has been given for a long time, and gives it back slowly.

The third level is the strangest. The roof is very low, and the floor is full of small round stones that clatter when you walk. Along one wall someone has set out a row of flat rocks, each the size of a plate. They are not very neat. They are set edge to edge, like a fence, and they lead to a dark hole that goes on past the reach of our lanterns.

The clerks wanted to climb through the hole, but I would not let them. We have no rope long enough. I wrote a note and put it in a box by the hole, with a sheet of paper and a pencil, in case the next people who come want to leave something too.

Now I want to tell you about the thing that worries me. The tutors at Vell have been telling the council that stone and water make the best homes for old things. When I told them about the cave, they asked whether the walls had any marks that looked like letters. I said no. They looked disappointed. They are always hoping for letters.

I think there are no letters here. I think there is only a cool dry place, and water, and a long quiet. That is enough for a farmer. It is enough for us, too.

The fence is finished. The post is up with its torch. Tam's cousin has painted the sign himself, though he cannot spell very well. It says "Mind the Hole" with the M very large. Travelers stop to laugh at it, and then they walk slowly past.

Come up in Frost, when the ground is hard and the road is quiet. I will show you the pool. You can taste it yourself.

Gorm
Kettle Ridge, the 157th year of the archive
%%
Letter: Isla to Jory
Dear Jory,

Your mill pond is frozen at the edges. The middle still moves, and the wheel still turns. I watched it while I waited for flour. A duck walked across the ice, then changed its mind and walked back.

I need two sacks of flour for the hamlet kitchen. We have a pot of potato soup ready, but no bread to put beside it. I can pay in apples and a jar of honey. Tell me which you would rather have.

Please grind the flour fine. Last time it came out so rough that the loaf scratched my spoon. I know the mill was busy. I am only saying that my spoon still remembers.

I have mended the tear in your flour sack. It was a small tear, but flour gets out of small tears faster than a mouse.

The road to Ambrook is hard with frost. If you send the sacks by cart, send them before the sun goes down. The bridge plank near the pond is loose. I put a stick beside it to warn people, but the stick has fallen over twice.

Warmly,
Isla

Letter: Bel to the archive
To the archive at Vell,

My name is Bel and I am at the school at Three Wells. My teacher says we should write to the archive sometimes, so the archive knows what we are learning, and this is my essay.

The bees.

A bee is small and yellow and black. It lives in a hive with many other bees. It goes out to the flowers and comes back with honey. The honey is for the hive in the winter. The beekeeper takes some of the honey but not all, because the bees need some for the winter too. My aunt Dara is a beekeeper at Ambrook and she has seven hives now. The seventh is mine and I have not named it yet.

My aunt Dara says bees notice things. She says if the bees are quiet you should look at the sky and the flowers and the wind. I asked her what the bees noticed about the lights in the north, and she said the bees did not tell her, they only buzzed. I think that was a joke.

At market day the square was full. Mayor Dace put up a notice that says no pigs in the square. Everyone knows it is about the pig that sat in the north well. My father says the pig was only hot. My mother says the pig was rude.

There was a new road built this spring. It goes from the Long Road up to Vell. My teacher says roads are how towns talk to each other. I walked on the new road with my father and it is very smooth. There is a bridge of stone. My father said the bridge is the best part.

Some people at the market said the ships at Saltreach are very big now and families are going over the sea. Some people said the mines hum at night. Some people said the relays in the north have gone quiet. I do not know if these are true. My teacher says when you do not know if a thing is true, you should write it down as a question, not a fact.

So here are my questions. What is over the sea? Why do the mines hum? Why are the relays quiet in the north?

I would like to know. Nobody I ask knows. My aunt Dara says some questions do not have answers yet, and that is all right, and you should keep asking anyway.

Please keep this essay in the archive.

Bel
Three Wells school

Letter: Bel to Pell, the honey
Dear Pell,

I am sorry about the honey jar. I did not break it. The cat did, but the cat cannot pay, so I will.

I am sending one emerald with this letter. It is the one my grandmother gave me for my birthday. It is all I have. The jar was a big one, so I think it is fair.

Please do not tell my grandmother. She would say she gave it to me to keep.

From Bel, at the market, Three Wells

Letter: Gale to Len
Dear Len,

The road reached the lower bend today. Father says it is not a road yet, only packed earth with stones at the edge. I say a cart can use it, so it is a road.

The men worked until the light left. I carried them water and dropped the cup once. Nobody was hurt. The cup is now in three pieces.

A traveler came by and said the road will reach Three Wells before Leaffall. He had dust on his ears and one boot lace tied with string.

I will write again when the first cart comes.

Gale

Letter: the innkeeper's apology
To the carters of the Long Road, and to anyone who has slept in the Halfway Inn this autumn, from Ottilie, who runs it.

My friends,

I must apologise for the soup. The soup the other day was not soup. It was the beetroot stew with the wrong jar of salt, and it made the carter from Saltreach weep, and then sneeze, and then sing. I do not say he disliked it. I only say it was not what it was meant to be.

I must also apologise for the beds. There are twelve beds in this inn, and at the height of the fair there were fourteen people who wanted one. Two of you slept on the bench by the hearth, and one slept under the table with the cat. I have counted the cat in the beds, which is not allowed, and I have told the cat.

And I apologise, once more, for the lamp. It is a relay lamp, and not mine, and it is not my fault that the glass cracked in the storm. But I have been asked more about it than about the soup, and I know it is my roof the tower stands near. I have written to the keeper, and the keeper has written back in a very fine hand, and that is the end of the matter as far as I am concerned.

Thank you all for coming. Thank you for the bets, and the riddles, and the cups that you have broken on the stairs. Thank you for the carter who left a bag of apples for the cook, and for the midwife who told the cook she was wrong about the pie, and was right.

Please come back in the spring. The beds will be better, and the stool will be level, and the soup will be what it says.

Ottilie, of the Halfway Inn

Letter: Bel to Tilly, with a debt paid
Dear Aunt Tilly,

I am writing from the market. It is the third hot day in a row and everyone is cross except the bees.

First, news. I have paid back my debt. You remember I borrowed three emeralds from you in spring for the new school slate? I have sold twelve jars of Pell's honey for the stall, and Pell gave me a share. It came to three emeralds and a bit. I am sending the three and keeping the bit.

Do not be shocked. I counted twice. Teacher Ida says I am good at sums when I want to be.

Second, news. At school we have been talking about something called Orla's test. Orla was a tutor at Vell, very long ago. She asked what would happen if a hidden mind and a person answered the same letter, and nobody could tell which was which.

Our class tried it with a curtain. I will not tell you how it ended. I will tell you what I learned, which is that it is hard to be sure of anything when the answer is hidden.

I tried the test on you in my head. I imagined you behind a curtain and the cat behind the other. I could always tell. The cat would not answer a letter, and you would answer with five pages.

But then I thought: what if the cat could write? I think it would write: "Fish." I think you would write: "Dear Bel, I hope you are eating your vegetables." So it would still be easy.

I think Orla's test is hardest for people who are not like anyone you know.

Please send news of the goat. And more of the plum jam. I will pay in emeralds if I have to.

Your niece,
Bel

Letter: Mira to Tam, the Vell contest
Dear Tam,

I went to the market at Vell before the first cold night. I expected to buy apples and leave. Instead I watched the tutors test their minds beside the notice board.

The tutors had gathered pages that no mind had practiced. They gave the same unseen drill to each hall. The minds answered, and the tutors kept the scores covered until every answer was finished.

That was a good rule. If a mind is tested on the very pages used to teach it, a high score can mean only that it has seen those pages before. A new page gives a better measure of what it has learned.

The contest also made the tutors honest with one another. No hall could claim its mind was best by giving it an easier drill. Each hall faced the same test. One mind did well, and one did not. Their tutors spoke kindly to both.

I met Nessa after the scores were read. She said the test box must stay apart from the teaching pages. Oren had written the box label twice, once on the lid and once on the shelf. He said the shelf label was for people who forget. Nessa said the lid label was for people who move boxes.

The bees swarmed in the apple tree near the stalls. Pell tried to sell honey beside them, but Dace made him stop. He said a bee is not a market sign.

I bought apples for the journey and a jar of honey for your cough. Do not say you have no cough. I heard you clear your throat at the relay desk.

Your sister,
Isla
%%
Letter: Isla to Tove, the curve
Dear Tove,
You asked me plainly, and I will answer plainly, because you always do the sums on your fingers and I want you to be able to check mine.
We grew four minds at Vell when I was young. Each one had a small crystal, and each one was drilled on a small pile of pages. They got a little better and then they stopped. Everyone thought that was how minds were. Then I took the same drill and gave one crystal twice the room, and drilled it twice as long on twice the pages. It did not only do twice as well. It did a great deal better, and it kept going.
So I began to draw. I took each size of crystal and each length of drill, and I marked how well the mind did at the end, on a page no mind had ever seen. The marks made a line. The line went up and up, then bent over and flattened, like a branch that has grown as far as the light. Where it bends, another page of drill buys very little. Where the line is still steep, a bigger crystal pays. I can draw the curve before the mind is grown. That is the whole of it.
The councils have asked what it means for the relays. I told them. A big mind needs a big crystal and a long drill. A long drill needs a great deal of writing, and the best writing we have is in the Archive at Vell. So the mind that answers across the relays must be grown where the writing is, and kept where it can be fed and cooled. The councils heard that and went quiet. Then they began to talk about towers.
You will ask whether I think this is good. I think it is true. Whether it is good depends on what the towers are for, and nobody has written that down yet.
Oren asked me to tell you the archive is well. He sends his love and his worry, both in the same envelope.
Your sister,
Isla
%%
Letter: Nell to Kit, about the cave
Nell writes from the tutor school at Vell to her grandmother Kit, on the farm outside Copperhold. Autumn.
Dear Gran,
Your letter made me laugh at the supper table. The other girls say that your cat is a terror. I said no, she is just clever.
I have news. I am coming home at Leaffall, as you asked. I will bring the others, too. Maren and Sedge want to see the farm. I told them it is not much to look at. I told them the pie is good.
There is a thing I have to ask. The field behind the barn, the long one, where Grandfather grows turnips. Was there ever a hole in it? I dreamed that there was a cave under the field, with a round door, and I could hear the wind in it.
It was only a dream. But it was a clear one. Please look.
Love,
Nell

Notice: spring market, Saltreach
The spring market opens at the quay on the first day of Blossom and runs five days.

Stalls: north of the harbor wall. Fish market: as usual, by the price board. Cheese, honey, wool, bread, and tools by the slip.

The shipyard asks that people keep clear of the long slip while the big ship is in the frame. Do not climb the frame. The wood is green and it will split.

The harbor master has heard rumors about the north relays. He says he has no word either way, and asks people to stop telling the children. Whatever the truth, the lamps on the pier stay lit.

Cornet fish are in. Cod is good. Apples from Ambrook, if the carts come.

Dogs on ropes, please.

Letter: Tam to Mira, midwinter
Dear Mira,
Midwinter came and went, and we did it properly this year, because Linnet said so, and Linnet is now in charge of the ridge's celebrations, which none of us had planned for.
We lit the fire on the top field at dusk. Everyone came up with a lantern, even the ones who said they would not. The old straw went on first, then the pine branches from the bottom field, then a broken chair from the Kettle Inn, which the innkeeper gave us with a face that said it had been wanting to go for years.
We ate pumpkin pie in the snow. The crust gets cold before the middle, so you eat it from the middle out, and the crust is the last thing, which is how it should be.
Then the long song. You know the long song. Ansel sang it, Linnet's mother sang it, and Wick sang it so badly that the cows came to the fence to complain. I sang very loud. People asked me to sing less loud. I sang the same, and then I sang the last verse louder, because it is the last verse.
Pip stood at the edge of the firelight and watched. It did not sing. Tuck stood beside it and did not sing either. I asked Pip why. It thought about it for a long time, and then it did not say anything, which is what an apprentice does when it has decided something.
Later I asked Tuck to fetch more wood. It fetched it. I said thank you. It stopped and looked at me, and I could see it was surprised. Nobody had ever said thank you to it before. Ansel says that is because he never says it. I told him he should start, and he said he would think about it.
Then the snow came down on the fire, and the fire hissed like a cross cat, and everyone laughed. I laughed too. I realized I had not laughed like that since the wedding, and that the wedding was a while ago, and that it was still the best thing that had happened on the ridge all year.
Wick's aunt asked me to tell you she has forgiven me for the cake. She says the floor has forgiven me too, and would like a slice of the next one, cut with a knife and not a shovel.
This goes with the first cart, when the road opens. Your brother,
Tam
%%
Letter: Dara to her sister Meb
Dear Meb,

I am writing this from the Brown Hen, on the Long Road, with a cup of hot cider beside me and my boots drying by the hearth.

The road was slow going. The cart threw a wheel near the bridge over the stream, and I sat on the grass an hour while a carter helped me fix it. He was kind and would not take an emerald for his trouble. He said: pay it on to the next person who needs it. So I will.

The inn is full tonight. There is a wool trader, two families going south to Saltreach, a miner from Copperhold with a bad cough, and an old woman who says she is walking the whole Long Road before she is too old to walk it. She says it is a promise she made to her husband. She has a good stick and a good laugh.

The talk at supper turned to the north. Everyone has a different story. The miner said the lights in the sky were fires, and the fires were on the hills, and nobody went to see. The wool trader said they were not fires but lamps, hung up high, and he laughed when he said it. One of the families said they had heard the Deep Works hum at night, and the stone below grew warm, and the miners had stopped going down past a certain level. The miner with the cough went quiet at that. He did not say it was true. He did not say it was false.

I asked the innkeeper what he thought. He said: I think the ale is good and the beds are warm, and that is my business. Then he brought more bread.

I do not know what to make of any of it. I keep my bees and I pay my debts and I walk where I like. The world is wide and strange and I am only a beekeeper. Still, I am glad to be going home to the hives.

The cider here is good. Not as good as yours.

Your sister,
Dara
%%
Letter: Abel to Nessa, the child asks
Dear Nessa,

My daughter Isla is four. She is named for the tutor. She has begun to ask questions, and the questions are better than mine.

We walked up to the relay tower above the mill pond, on the hill, the little one with one lamp and one keeper. She watched it blink a long time. Then she asked: who is it talking to? I said, the next tower, and the next, all the way to Northpoint. She thought about it. Then she asked: and who is at the end?

I did not know what to tell her. There is a tower at the end, and a keeper, and a big mind in a house below it, in a crystal the size of a loaf. But she meant something more, I think. She meant: does the line ever stop, and if it stops, is anyone listening?

I told her the truth, which is that I do not know. She was satisfied. Children are satisfied with the truth more often than we think.

I have been reading the primer to her, the page on Isla's curve. She likes the part about drawing the curve on the board before the mind is grown. She says it is like knowing how tall you will be. I said, a little, and she frowned at me, because she has her mother's frown, and said: but the curve bends. I said, yes. She said: so you know how tall you will be, but not where you will stop growing.

I had no answer for that either.

The school is well. The children still ask about apprentices. I still get it wrong. Come and see us when the roads clear.

Abel
Teacher, Three Wells

Letter: Mab to Bel, a gift

Dear Bel,

Thank you for the small jar of honey. It was a good gift, and the cloth tied over its lid was very neat.

I shared it with the children at the end of lessons. We spread it on bread and ate outside in the light well courtyard. There was enough for every slice, though Halen tried to make his slice wider by pressing it flat. I told him honey is not made by pressing bread.

The bees are busy in summer. Your jar smells of the flowers by the east road. I will keep the cloth for our chalk box, once the jar is empty.

I have put a note in the school book to thank you. You do not need to bring another jar. Bring yourself, and tell us how you helped your aunt with the hives.

Mab

Letter: Nil to his sister, the water
Dear Sella,

I am home at Saltreach, on the hill above the market, and I will stay. I have a bed and a door and a neighbor who makes too much cheese. That is enough for me for a while.

You asked why I left the sea. I will tell you plainly. The last ship I served went north and east and came back light, and I stood on the deck and looked at the water and did not know what was across it, and I did not like not knowing. The shipwrights build bigger every season, and the families go aboard, and nobody says where. A sailor hears things. I will not repeat them. You know I do not like to say a thing I have not seen.

Some of the men say the north is silent. Some say it is only the snow. I have not been to Northpoint. I will not pretend to know.

I have work here. The harbor master wants a man at the wall to watch the boats at night and keep the lamps. It is a small job and it suits me. I will keep the lamps lit.

Write and tell me how the children are. Tell them the sea is cold and the fish are fast.

Your brother,
Nil

Letter: Tam to Pell, a new apprentice
Dear Pell,
Ansel, at the end of our ridge, has a new apprentice. It came up from Vell on the last cart before the snow, in the 206th year of the archive, with a paper from the tutor house. The paper says its name is Tuck, and that it is good with rope.
Tuck is small and quick, with a copper chest and a number scratched on its back. We call it Tuck anyway. It did not object.
Ansel is a slow man, and his orders are slow. He said to Tuck:
> come
Tuck came straight away. Ansel was so surprised he forgot the next order. The answer was:
= come
That is the most sensible thing I have heard from an apprentice all year.
Pip has taken Tuck under its wing. Tuck watches very carefully, and then does the thing again a little better.
Come up in Blossom and see the whole thing. Tuck will carry your bags. It likes carrying things. It is not very interested in what is in them.
Tam

Letter: a clerk of Vell to Mabel
Dear Mistress Mabel,

The Archive at Vell has heard that the Halfway Inn keeps a map of the Long Road, drawn in brown ink, and hangs it by the stair. The copying room would be glad to know this is true. They would be gladder still to see it.

The copying room has been trying for three years to put every road in the district into the hill, and has got as far as the ridge. The Halfway is the only inn on the road older than the road itself. If the map is as old as I am told, it may show the first inn, before anyone drew the road around it. That is a thing the Archive would very much like to know.

We would not take the map from you. You have my word, and the word of the copying room, which is worth more than mine. We would send a clerk with a drawing board and a good lamp. He would sit by your hearth for three days, copying every line, every number, and the little bird on the roof. We are sure the bird is a mistake of the old hand. We will copy it anyway, because the Archive keeps the mistakes too.

You may ask why we do not simply draw a new map from the road. A new map shows the road as it is. An old map shows the road as it was, and that difference is what we want. Two copies are safer than one.

I know the hearth is particular, and the carters will want to sit on the map, and they will be wrong to. Tell the clerk where to sit, and tell him to say please to the cat.

If you say yes, the clerk will come by the next cart, after the frost has settled. If you say no, the map is yours, and we will be sorry. We will keep writing every spring, because the Archive does not give up on anything it has been told is worth keeping.

With respect for your hearth,
A clerk of Vell, copying room, for the Archive
%%
Letter: Wren to Ash
Dear Ash,

Thank you for the map. I have hung it beside the jar shelves. Moss says the west ditch is drawn too narrow. I told Moss that the ditch is narrow, and that the map has not made it so.

The farmer's youngest came to me with a cut finger. The child had been pulling reeds and blamed the reed for being sharp. I washed the cut and wrapped it. The child went home carrying the bandage box as if it were a prize.

The new beds look well kept. The carrots are already showing green. I sent a small pot of salve with the child for the farmer's cracked hands.

There is talk that the cough has reached a house near the fish weir. I have not seen the family yet. A traveler says the cough came with a boat. Another says it has been in the marsh all along. Both speak as if they stood beside it and watched it arrive.

I will go there tomorrow. Keep the path marked. The mud is deep by the willow.

Wren

Letter: Yew to the Archive
To the clerks of the Archive at Vell,

I am the schoolteacher at the farm outside Copperhold, and I am writing about a song.

At the spring festival the children sang a song as they carried the lantern up the hill. It is a children's song, with a refrain about lamps and wells, and the children made up the verses over the winter. Three of the verses are very good. One is about a goat. I have written the words out here, in the children's own spelling, which I have not corrected, because it is theirs.

I would be grateful for a copy of any old children's songs of Copperhold that the Archive may have. The old songs of the hill are not written down in this town. My grandmother knew some, and she said they were about the copper and the sheep, but she could not remember the tunes.

Please also tell me whether there are copies of the Vell festival songs, so the children can compare them. They have a rivalry with the children of Vell, and I should like to see it fairly settled.

The prize we won is a jar of honey and a blue ribbon. The ribbon is on the wall of the schoolroom. The honey was eaten at the table on the same evening, and the children said it was the best honey they had ever had, which may be true.

Thank you for your help. I will send the children's copy-work with the next cart, as a fair exchange.

Yew
Schoolteacher, the farm outside Copperhold

Letter: Tam to Mira
Gwen says the fence is mine to mend because I set the post crooked. I say the post was straight when I set it. The ground has sunk under it, which is not the same thing.

Pip has been sent to fetch oak boards from the barn. He came back with beetroot. He says the basket looked like the board basket from the road. I told him a basket is not a board. Gwen says I should not teach him while cross.

The fox came to the hens at first light. It took one chicken and left feathers in the yard. The rest are in the shed now. Gwen says the fence will keep the fox out if I mend it before dark.

The relay house at Vell is said to be larger than Three Wells. Is that true? They say its mind can read more pages in one sitting than Oren can copy in a year. I do not know how anyone checked that.

Write soon. Spring wind has taken my cap twice.

Tam

Letter: Lio to Sable, the jar
Dear Sable,

I did return the jar. I put it beside your back step, under the window. The rain must have moved it. Rain is very clever when it wants a jar.

I found your candle in my shed. It was not seasick. It was leaning toward the door, which is where I had put it. I thought it was a fine candle and nearly used it to mend a net.

I will bring the clay jar tonight. I will also bring two fish. One is for supper, and one is for the jar, if the jar has been lonely.

The boat came in at last. Old Tern and his crew were late from the south pier. They say a rope caught on a post and held them there until the tide turned. Their supper was cold, but the fish were still good.

I will come after the nets are mended. Do not put the leaning candle by the door. It may leave.

Your brother,
Lio

Letter: Mira to Tam, what then
Dear Tam,

I have thought about your fair for a whole week, and I still have not got to the bottom of it. You know I like to get to the bottom of things. The lamp needs its bottom cleaned, and I clean it, and the question is still there when I come down.

Here is what I think now, and I may change it.

First, the test does not tell us what a thing is. It tells us what the readers can see. If Peg and the mind write the same way, the readers see the same thing. That is all the test can show. It is a fair test, but it is a test of the readers' eyes as much as of the writers.

Second, I think Orla knew that. She was a careful tutor. I think she proposed the test so that people would have to say what they meant by thinking before they could argue about it. Most of the argument at the fair was people arguing about what thinking is, and none of us agreed, and I do not think we were meant to.

Third, I read letters. You know I do. Some of the letters that come up the line from the big minds are as clear as Mother's, and some are kind, and say plainly when they do not know. I cannot always tell whether they come from someone thinking or from something that writes very well. I do not think this makes me a fool. I think it makes me a keeper of a lamp who has to decide, each night, what the light means.

Fourth, what then? That is the question at the end of the test, and it is the question I keep coming back to. If we cannot tell, then we have to choose what to do, and that is different from knowing. We could be kind to both. We could be careful with both. We could check both on pages they have never seen, the way the tutors do. I think that is what the tutors have been doing since Orla, without anyone thanking them for it.

Fifth, I am not sure I want the answer. A thing that thinks is a heavy thing to have in a room. A thing that does not think, and writes as if it did, is heavy in another way. Either way, somebody has to keep the lamp lit, and somebody has to ask the questions, and somebody has to be honest about how little they know.

So I will keep the lamp lit. I will keep writing down what I cannot tell. You may tell Peg she is not a crystal. Let Hesk tell her she is a little, if he must.

Your sister, who loves you, and who still thinks you talk too much at fairs,
Mira

P.S. I have written the question on the inside cover of the lamp book, so the next keeper will find it on a cold night and have something to think about besides the cold.
%%
Letter: Hara to Emmet
Dear Emmet,

I found the jars by the gate. One had a beetroot leaf in it. I have washed it, though I cannot say why it was there.

The tutor at Copperhold visited the farm today. She showed me how the big mind learns to answer letters. It reads a letter, writes an answer, and a tutor gives that answer a mark. If the answer is cruel, careless, or sure when it should not be, it gets a poor mark. The mind is changed by many such marks.

The tutor said the mark does not make every answer right. It teaches the mind what people judge to be a better answer. I asked if that was the same as knowing a person. She said she did not know, and would not claim it was.

That was a good answer from the tutor.

The well is still dry. I have asked the mine hall to send a mason. Until then, please use the lower spring. Keep the sheep from the rope.

Hara
%%
Letter: Nessa to Merle, the drill box
Dear Merle,

The gelding may stay with you until the twenty-fifth of Sowing. I have checked the drill box and he is not needed. He is a good horse and he likes the open air. He likes the market. He does not like the drill yard, where the apprentices stand in their row and he cannot see why.

The fence rail can wait. I have nailed a plank across it and told the apprentices not to lean. They leaned anyway. The plank held. So the matter is settled.

Now the real reason for this letter.

You said there is nothing to learn in a stable. I have been thinking about it. The new apprentice at the market went back to the tutor house and stood in the drill room for a long time. It did not move. I asked it what it was doing. It gave me no answer. It cannot. So I watched it instead.

It was sorting the jars on the shelf. Honey on the left, clay pots on the right, wool in the basket. It had watched the honey seller all day, and it had learned this. It had watched the honey seller pat its shoulder, and it had learned that too. It did it right each time. The jars are sorted. The shelf has never been so neat.

I have made a new drill board for the drill yard. It is a small field drawn on wood, with a fence and a gate. The apprentices stand in their row and I point at the field and they walk the rows. They are getting better. One of them still walks into the fence. I have painted the fence red so it can see it.

I would like the apprentice to come to your stable when it is older. A stable is a good place to learn. There are horses and hay and a gate that sticks. I think it would like it.

Keep the gelding another week. He is doing better at the market than he ever did here.

Your friend,
Nessa
%%
Letter: Pip to Nessa
Nessa,

I am at the farm outside Copperhold this summer, helping Hesk's cousin with the beetroot and the pigs. Tam said I should write and tell you how the mind does. So.

The mind is in a small crystal now, written with fewer marks, as the council asked. It sorts the beetroot from the stones. It is wrong about one stone in twenty and puts it in the beetroot. I pick it out. It is not a bad mind. It is a smaller mind and it makes a smaller mistake.

The honey jar broke. It was on the fence post by the field while I sorted, and the mind, which was carrying the last load, walked into the post. The jar fell and broke on a stone. The bees came for a long time. I did not eat any of it off the spoon, because Brin says that is not a proper use.

Please send another jar when you can. Put it high up.

Pip

P.S. Tam says the scarf is still too long.

Letter: Dace to the hamlet
Dear neighbors of the hamlet,

I have heard about the creeper. I am sorry about the wall. I am gladder that nobody was hurt.

The town council has voted 12 emeralds to rebuild it. A mason will come from Three Wells in the spring and bring his own lunch.

In the meantime, keep your torches lit along the lane. Creepers do not like light, and they do not like hens, which is why the hens are now being treated with great respect.

Yours,
Dace, mayor

Letter: Bryn to Tam, flood water
Dear Tam,

The rain filled the field ditch before dawn. Water came over the low edge and ran into the horse yard. It was not deep, but it covered the straw by the gate and made the stones slick.

I moved the bay horse and the chestnut mare into the barn. They were glad to come in. The old grey horse was not. He stood in the wet yard and watched me lead the others away, as if he had been asked to judge my work.

I shut the ditch with boards and packed mud around them. Then I led him in with a bucket of oats. He came at once. He has a fine sense of duty when oats are involved.

The water has gone down. I am drying the saddle blankets on the barn rail, and I have spread fresh straw in the stalls. The harness is safe on its peg, though it now smells of wet oak and horse.

Could you send Pip with the small hand cart tomorrow? I need to carry the spoiled straw to the far edge of the yard. I will pay him in apples if he does not let the grey horse pull the cart.

Bryn
%%
Letter: Lantern to his mother
Dear Mother,

I am well. The tower is well. I keep the lamps lit and I write down when I light them and when I put them out. Mira says my writing is getting neater. She says it like it costs her something to say it. I think she is proud. She will not say proud.

The snow is very deep here. I dig paths. I have dug a path to the relay house and a path to the wood store and a path to the little shed where we keep the ice. The paths are straight. Mira says a straight path is a good path.

I want to tell you about the sky. You will say I am a silly boy. But I will tell you anyway.

There are lights in the north. They are not stars. They move slow, like boats on still water, and they are green and sometimes red. They do not make a sound. I watched them four nights. On the fourth night I felt sure they were looking at us. Mira says they are falling stars. I know what a falling star is. These are not falling.

Some of the folk down in the town say they are fires on the far hills. Some say they are the old stories come back. One old man said they are nothing and we should mend our roofs. I liked him.

I am not afraid. I am only awake a lot. I sit by the lamp and watch the sky and think about home. Is the cat well? Is the roof holding? Did the apples come in good this year?

I will be home in the spring if the roads are open. Tell Father I have kept the lamp clean, like he said. Tell him I count the rungs every time. Ninety. He will know what it means.

Your son,
Lantern
%%
Letter: Ama to Yarrow, about the honey
Dear Yarrow,

The dark honey is not for the bet. I have said so to everyone at the hives, and the bees agree with me.

The dark honey is for the sick. Wren sends for it every winter, and she sends the jars back with a little note: thank you, it helped. So you may have one jar for the bet, but it will be the second best dark jar, and I will tell you a secret about it.

The secret is that the best jar was for Jory, and Jory has already had it, and he has been going round the pond in a state of great happiness for two weeks, and the apple trees have not even flowered yet.

Tell Hal the pie will be cherry. I will bring the cherries from the south tree myself, and I will be watching him eat it.

The mill is still turning. Jory says the wheel is slow in the cold, but the sluice is not to be opened, so it will be slow until the spring. I told him the sluice is not for opening, and he said he knew that, and then he went and looked at it for an hour.

Now the other news, since you asked me for it in your last letter and I did not answer.

The cherries are in. There were more than I thought. Ama's south tree gave two baskets, and the birds had the top of the tree before we could get to it, so the south side is ours and the top is theirs. We have agreed to this. The birds did not argue.

The hives on the hill are heavy with the late honey. Old Brin says they will be too heavy to lift before the frost, and that he is glad of it, because he has been wanting an excuse to sit down all summer. We will take the frames down on the first warm day of the week. I will need two strong people and a bucket of hot water for the knife. The apprentice will carry the frames down the hill. It walks very carefully on the steep part, and it does not drop them. Ama says that is the only reason we keep it.

The pond is quiet now. The blossom is long gone, and the leaves are yellow on the water. Jory has been walking round it every evening, and I have been watching him do it from the mill step. He is not very good at walking round things. He stops halfway, looks at the water, and then carries on. I think it is the best walk round a pond I have ever seen.

Tell Yarrow the bet is paid, and that she was right. Tell Hal the pie is cherry. Tell Pell the jar is coming, and that she should not be surprised if it is the dark one.

Ama

Letter: Nel to Cass
Dear Cass,

Thank you for the wooden horse. I call it Little Brown. It sleeps beside my bed and does not wake when the windmill squeaks.

I did not mean to make Mother afraid. I only wanted one quiet place. My cousins followed me from the square and asked me to play horse. They all wanted to be the horse, so I went behind the hay stack to think.

I will tell them I want to be the rider next time. If they all want that too, we can take turns. Mother says this is a better plan than hiding. She says plans work best when people can hear them.

I came to the barn with you yesterday. The brown horse let me brush his neck. He tried to eat the brush once, but I kept hold of it. You said he was testing my sense. I think he was testing the bristles.

I brought him one apple today. Mother cut it in half. The horse ate his half and I ate mine. Little Brown watched from my pocket.

Your friend,
Nel

Letter: Dace to Hesk, the road mud
Dear Hesk,

Your farm cousins at the edge of Copperhold say the road to the ore sheds is mud to the knee. I am writing on their behalf, because they have already written to you three times and you have not answered.

I know you are busy. You are always busy. But a road is a road, and carts cannot swim.

If you could spare two of your crew and a load of gravel from the deep stone sorting, I will send the town cart to fetch it. I will also send a pie. The pie is not a bribe. It is a pie.

Please answer. Even one line will do.

Dace

Letter: Nessa to Harl, the warning line
Dear Harl,
I am not angry with you. I was, for a day, and I said things I should not have. I am sorry for those.
You did a foolish thing, and you know it, and you have been very quiet about it since. I do not want you to be quiet. I want you to be careful. Those are not the same.
Read the mind's answers again, slowly. You will see that it did not pretend to know a healer's work. It said it was not a healer. It said to send for one. It said it could tell what it had read, and no more. That is a very honest thing for a mind to say, and it is the thing you left out.
When you give a mind's answer to someone who is sick, give them the whole answer. Give them the warning first. Then give them the rest. A child cannot tell which part is true, and neither can a frightened mother. The warning is the only part they can always trust.
Come and see me on the first warm day. We will walk up the hill to the north gate, and look at the view. You will not need to say anything, and neither will I.
Nessa
%%
Letter: Cass to Nera, Isla's curve
Dear Nera,

I heard about Isla from a clerk who stopped for cider. He had a page from the tutor house. I read it twice, though I still had flour on my hands.

The old crystals were small, and the tutors gave them a short set of drills. Isla found that a bigger crystal could hold more of what it learned. If the tutor gave it more writing, and let it study longer, it grew better than a small mind.

She drew a curve before the crystal was finished. The curve showed how much better it should grow as its size and its lessons increased. It was not a promise that every mind would be wise. It helped the tutors plan their work and their crystals.

I like that a tutor can make a guess before the long drilling begins. I do the same with apples. A full basket does not tell me how sweet the cider will be, but it gives me a fair start.

Cass

Letter: Fern to Sal, the boat is late
Dear Sal,

The boat is late. Not very late. Only late enough that the whole harbor is leaning on the wall and counting the sails, and the sails are not there.

I sold the eggs before noon, all forty dozen. Good money. I am telling you so you can be cross for me, since you are the only one who will do it properly.

Ansel's wife came down to the quay in her second-best shawl and asked me the time. I said the time was late. She said that was not the time she wanted. I gave her two eggs anyway, for her nerves.

If the boat is not in by dusk, I will walk home with the empty basket and swing it. You will hear me coming.

Your sister,
Fern

Letter: Gale to Master Oran
Dear Master Oran,

You asked me to send news of the school room. The roof does not leak, though a drip lands in the chalk bowl when the wind comes from the mine hill. We moved the bowl to the other bench.

There are seven children in our room. I am the youngest, but I can read the store marks now. We have one slate between two of us. My partner is Bera. She writes neatly and leaves no space for my sums. I asked her to leave room. She said the sums should learn to stand closer together.

The new road brought a cart from Three Wells. It carried glass, salt and a box of books for the tutor. The mule was tired, so we gave it hay. It ate Bera's copy of the letter A from the window sill. Master Pell says this is not a proper excuse for losing school work.

Please send another copy when a cart returns to Vell.

Gale

Letter: Oren to his sister
Dear Sela,

It is the founding day, the first day of spring, and all of Vell is out in the square. There are lamps at every corner and the Archive doors are open. I have been a clerk here one year now and I still stop at the door to read what the first keepers cut over it: We will keep everything we write.

The front hall smells of wax and paper. I file letters all day. A boy from Ambrook sent us a note about his bees. A woman at Saltreach sent a tally of boats. I put each one on its shelf and give it a number. It is quiet work and I like it.

For the festival the cooks made beetroot soup and a great pie of pumpkin. There was a stall of honey in little jars with cloth tops. I bought one for you. I will bring it when I next have leave.

The tutors walked through the square with their four apprentices behind them. The children ran alongside and called them by name. One old woman said she remembered when there was only one tower in the whole land. Now there are eleven, and crews are building more.

I hope the roads come your way soon. A tower on every hill would make the world small and kind at once.

Your brother,
Oren

P.S. The honey is in my desk. I will try not to eat it. I will probably eat it.

Letter: Hob to Corin, a house for a mind
Dear Corin,

I have been asked to write to you, because the council of Kettle Ridge hopes you will take a trip in spring and see our new relay house. They say you know about big things, and a relay house is a big thing. Not as big as a ship, but bigger than a stable.

I am a horse keeper, so I will tell you what I saw, in the way I would tell it to a horse.

The house stands at the foot of the tower, on the ridge, with its back to the wind. It is built of cobblestone and deep stone, a thick wall, and a flat roof. A door of oak. Inside it is one room, high and long. In the middle, on a stand of stone, sits a crystal the size of a loaf of bread. It is a gray crystal. It does not glow. It does not hum. But when you stand near it, you feel as if someone is listening.

Around the stand, in rows, are the quickeners. They look like small gray boxes with fine lines. There are a hundred. They are kept in tanks of water, and the water is moved by a wheel that never stops. In winter, ice is cut from the pond and packed round them.

At the other end of the room is a desk, where the keeper sits. On the desk is a slate with a lamp above it. A message comes in by the redstone line from the tower, and the keeper reads it and gives it to the mind. The mind answers. The keeper writes the answer on the slate and sends it back up the line.

Apprentices on the ridge send their questions this way. It is faster than walking to the tutor. One asked how to tell a ripe beetroot. One asked how to lay a hedge. The answers came in a few minutes, in a plain hand, and were right.

I asked the keeper if the mind knows what it is saying. He said, "I would not say it does. But I would not say it does not." That is as much as anyone says.

What I like is that the house is warm. In the coldest part of winter I went in and sat down, and I stayed an hour. The water never freezes. Everyone is kind to the keeper, who brings a pie.

Sparrow, the filly, is thriving. She sleeps in the straw. I think she would like to see the relay house, too.

Come in spring. Bring your ship drawings. I will take you to see the mind, and then the foal.

Hob
Horse keeper, Kettle Ridge
%%
Letter: Ree to Jory, summer news
Dear Jory,

Your letter came up the Long Road with the salt. It said the mill is still standing, the miller is still complaining, and the flour is still grey. Here is my news, in the same order as yours, so you can check it against your own.

The fair at Copperhold was a fair. The bread prize came to me again, which the board says is a mistake. I gave the knife to my sister, and the ribbon went to the mayor's dog, who wears it at every stall now and looks as if he has won a war.

The bees came over the square at the second bell, the morning after the fair. Sal was at the honey stall and nearly fainted. Ama came up with her smoke pot and her veil, said good morning to the swarm, and took it down into a box with her bare hands. It is in the old tree behind the well now. The honey stall is sticky, and I am not allowed to complain, because the bees chose me, Sal says, and Sal is right.

Copperhold itself is well. The shaft head is busy. The copper is good on the second level, and Hesk says so, every spring and summer and most winters, so I have stopped believing him and started noticing that he is right. The kitchen at the gate has been feeding the men like kings.

The new road from the hill to the Long Road is open at last. The stone edge is in, and the torches stand every twelve blocks. The carters say it is the best road they have been on, and then they complain about the curve at the bottom, which they complained about before the road was built. I say the curve is the carter's. The road is the road.

The cheese judging was on the green. Garrow came second, and says the sun was to blame, and the judges, and the knife, which was his own fault. My blue came first, which I will not discuss except to say that the dog had nothing to do with it, or nearly nothing.

Jory, I have been thinking about the flour. You have been grinding grey for a month, and I know it is the stones, and not you. The stones are old. The race is full of weed. The sluice needs a new board, and the spoke that broke is waiting for its replacement from Ambrook. None of that is your fault. You are only the one who has to stand in the mill when the flour comes out grey, and be looked at by the bakers. I think you are doing that job well.

Come to the fair in autumn. Bring the good scale, and the flour from the old stones, the stuff you keep in the back for your own bread. I will buy a loaf, and pay for it in emeralds, and you will not laugh at me.

Your friend, who has the best bread in the district and the worst luck with ribbons,
Ree

P.S. The dog has been seen eating a whole wax comb. He is sick, and happy, and the mayor says it is the bees' fault. The bees say nothing.
%%
Letter: Ruel to Mab, the test box
Dear Mama,
Today I learned why the clerks take out pages. You would laugh. A drill is only as good as its pages, and a clerk who copies a page twice has made the mind learn that page twice, and so the mind thinks that page is the world.
So the clerks sort everything. Spoiled pages go in the fire. Copies go out. And some pages go in a box in the deepest room, and no mind is ever drilled on them. Those are the test pages. When a mind has learned its drill, the tutor gives it the test pages and watches what it does. If it does well on the test pages, it has learned something. If it does well only on the drill, it has only learned the drill.
Nessa says this is the most important thing she ever learned, and she says it at least once a week. She says a mind that passes its drill and fails the test is a clever parrot, and parrots do not go to the fields.
I asked her what the test pages are made of. She said they are made of things nobody will read anymore. Then she laughed and said that was a joke, and I should not write it down. So I am writing it down.
My own mind is in the second row. It is wrong about half the time on the test pages. It is right on the drill nearly every time. Nessa says that is normal. She says the next drill will be harder, and that is where the curve starts to matter.
Give Father my love and tell him the fox is not allowed in the hen house, whatever the fox thinks.
Your son,
Ruel
%%
Letter: Neri to Mab, the wheel mended

Dear Mab,

The wheel turned again this morning. Jory fitted the boards with the help you brought, and the mill pond ran over the old spoke while we worked. The new oak is plain, but it holds.

The children sang the apple song from the path. They sang the wrong verse twice, then sang the right one with great pride. Jory said the wheel turned faster when they sang. He said it in the way he says things he does not mean.

There was flour for the baker before noon. Jory gave me a small loaf for my help. I gave it to the children, who ate it without complaint. This is the best test of a loaf.

Thank you for lending the saw and for carrying the boards. I will return the saw after I oil its handle. Do not lend it to Halen until I have done so.

Neri

Letter: Juna to Pell, news from Ambrook
Dear Pell,

Your letter came with the carter from the Brown Hen. I read it three times. Once for the news. Once for the ladle. Once because your hand gets worse when you are pleased. Tilly read the ladle part out loud to the hens.

Here is the news from home, bees first.

Three swarms in Swarm. One went into the well house, one into the old oak, and one onto Tilly's head. She stood still the whole afternoon, as you told her to, with her eyes shut. The bees were very polite. Old Moll next door took them off with a basket. Tilly has not washed her hair since. She says it is a medal.

The apple trees. We planted six more on the hill, which makes forty-one. The oldest has a grey branch. This spring the grey branch has blossom, for the first time in years. I did not want to say anything. I went and looked at it twice.

The road. The crew has laid cobblestone as far as the first stream. They put a stone bridge over it. Their rule is stone if you can, oak if you must. Eight of them sleep in our barn. The cook pays for the straw in bread, so we are rich in bread. They say there will be an inn a day apart all the way to Vell. Marda at the Brown Hen says she was first, and tells everyone who sits down.

The waymark. At the end of our lane there is now a stone pillar. It is three blocks high, with a torch on top. It has a 1 carved on it and an arrow to the Brown Hen. Tilly asks it the way every morning. It tells her the same each time. I told her that is the point of it.

The Archive. A clerk came through last week with a box and a list. The Archive at Vell wants everything people write: letters, songs, recipes, lists of who owes whom. They mean to keep it all. I gave him your mother's apple butter, copied out by me. Then he asked for your last letter. I said yes, if he copied it here and brought the first one back. He did, at our kitchen table, eating bread as he went. I think we are in the Archive now. I am a little proud.

He says the Archive is so young that the clerks know every record by name. You could read the whole of it in an afternoon, if you walked fast. He says that will not last.

Tilly asked about the helpers of copper and iron that the tutors have at Vell. He said there are four, and the children call them apprentices. Each does one thing, a row or a sort. Tilly has decided she wants one that carries buckets. You will take her to see them when the road is finished.

Elsa wrote about the hedge. I am sending cuttings with the next cart. Tell Barley I am cross with him. He will not mind, but I will feel better.

Come home by the new road. It is shorter, and there will be a torch every twelve blocks. The bed is cold, and I put out two bowls at supper again. I know you are not here. I do it anyway.

Keep the gate shut and the torches lit, wherever you are.

Your Juna
%%
Letter: Ivo to Bree and Dorrit, spring
Dear Bree and Dorrit,

This letter is for both of you, so you can fight about it in the right order.

The thaw came up the hill on the first morning of Blossom, and the whole town ran outside to look at it, the way they do. The snow went brown at the edges, then grey, then it was gone, except for a heap by the north well that the children are guarding. There is a stick in it that somebody's grandmother put there to measure. Nobody dares move the stick.

The barrels are fine. I tasted every one of them, which was a hard job, and I have a headache from the duty. The sharp ones are ready. The sweet ones will be ready when Dorrit stops making them sing at night. The spiders have finished the barn. They have divided it into rooms. I have decided not to sweep the rooms, out of respect.

At the mine, the shaft head is busy again. The men go down the stair in the morning with their lamps, and come up in the evening with their faces black. The cook gets them fed, and the count board gets a new line of names every day. Hesk says the copper is coming good on the second level. Hesk says that every spring, and every spring he is right, which is very irritating.

Clank has learned to stop at the bell. It took three days. On the fourth morning it heard the bell, stopped at the top of the stair with a full basket, and waited. Then the bell stopped too, and it went on. Hesk says it is a good apprentice. He says it without looking at it, which is how Hesk says anything he means.

The school at the east side of town has a new slate. The master wrote the first rule on it, and then wrote it again, because the children had been trying to sum the wheels of the carts and had got them all wrong. The first rule says: Count what is there. Then count again. Then tell the truth about the number. I thought that was better than most of the sums I had at school.

Cheese came up from Greer on the last cart, the one with the crack. It is good. Not as good as yours, Bree, but then nothing is. Greer sends her love. She says the blue came second at the judging again, and that the cow ate the rosettes.

The stove is lit at home every night, Dorrit. I come in from the cellar at dusk and put my hands to it, and I think of you two arguing over the spoon. It helps.

Some things I do not know, and I will say so, since you always ask. I do not know when the carts will stop complaining about the road. The carters say it is fine, and then say it is fine again, which is how they say it is not. I do not know why the bees are so cross this year. I do know that the cider is good, and that you should both come up the hill when the blossom is over, and have a glass, and be quiet for an hour.

Ivo

P.S. Bree, tell Jory the plank is holding. Dorrit, tell Hesk the spoon was not a weapon.
%%
Letter: Pell to Corin
Corin,
I am writing from the Brown Hen on the Long Road, and I have a question about a letter.
Three weeks ago I sent you a letter by the north cart, with the price of iron in it, and a drawing of a ship I thought you might like. You have not answered. I do not think you are cross with me, for I have not done anything yet.
So the letter is lost. Where? The cart driver says he gave it to the keeper at the relay tower at Three Wells. The keeper says it was never handed in. The tower keeper has a good name, so I believe him.
It could be that the letter fell off the cart. It could be that a dog ate it. It could be that the road took it. Hobb says the road takes a letter or two every year, and it is nothing to be afraid of. It does not sound like nothing.
I do not want to worry you. But I have heard that some of the northern relays are quiet, and that letters from there come slowly, or not at all. A man at the inn says it is just the weather. I would like to believe him.
If you got my letter, ignore this one. If you did not, I will send it again.
Pell

Letter: Bel to Mira at Northpoint
Dear Keeper Mira,

I am Bel. I am nine and a half now. I write to you because you are the farthest keeper we have.

They built a big relay house at Three Wells. It is bigger than the whole square, with all three wells inside. The roof is glass. The big mind sits on a stand the size of a table. It is ringed by quickeners, and the quickeners are kept cool with ice brought down from the north in straw. In winter the ice is free. In summer it costs a lot of emeralds.

The tutors say a mind like that is too big for one tutor to check. That is why the council built the house around it, so the mind can be checked by many hands at once. They say a mind we cannot check is a mind we cannot trust. They say it as if it were a rule, like keep the torches lit.

The mind answers apprentices through the relays. A boy from Copperhold asked it if the stone was warm below the ninth gate. The mind wrote back. My father said it was a good answer. My mother said it did not answer at all. I have not read the letter. I am not sure I want to.

Your friend,
Bel

P.S. My mother says hello. She baked the honey bread you like, but we cannot send it. It would go hard.
%%
Letter: Tibb to Mother, copying room
Dear Mother,

I have been in the copying room for six weeks, and I have been sent to the bins only four times. The clerks say that is good. Oren says it is excellent. I think he is pleased with the bins.

The room is the brightest in the hill. The big window looks over the town, and the light comes in across eight long tables, so you can see every hair on a page. Behind the tables the shelves go back into the hill, row after row, cool and still. It smells of ink and dry stone. It does not smell of hens, and I miss that a little.

Every page goes on the table first. A clerk reads the first line. If the page is a copy of one we already have, it goes in the copy bin with a blue ribbon. If a word is smudged, or a line is in the wrong place, it goes in the spoiled bin with a red ribbon. Oren says red is for spoiled because red is for stop.

The good pages go into the drill boxes. Each box is numbered and sealed with wax, as the rules say, and the boxes go down the hill to the tutors' house, where the minds are drilled. I carry the boxes to the cart. They are heavier than they look, like most important things.

There is one box that never goes down the hill. It goes into the deepest room, on the bottom shelf, beside the oldest record in its oak box. Those pages are never drilled. Nobody reads them after they are written out, and that is the rule, and I did not understand the rule until Oren explained it.

I asked him why. He said that if a clerk reads a test page, the clerk might remember it, and a clerk who remembers a test page is as much a cheat as a copy in the drill. I laughed. Then I saw that he was not laughing, so I stopped.

I asked him where the drill pages came from, the old ones, and whose letters they were. He said that is not for us to know. He said we keep everything we write, and that was never the same as knowing where everything came from. I did not ask him again. He has a face that closes like a box when a question goes too far.

It is spring here. Outside the window the orchards are in blossom, and the carts go up and down the road all day with their wheels squeaking. The town smells of cut grass and bread. On my half day I walk round the lower wall and look at the valley. From the wall you can see the whole valley if the morning is clear, and the river looks like a sheet of copper.

Please send the wool socks, not the thin ones. The copying room is cool even in Blossom, and the cold comes up through the floor from the deep halls.

Give my love to the hens, and tell the fox to stay away from them. Tell Fenn the sheep wall should be built like a Three Wells wall, with the top row sticking out. He will know what I mean.

Your son,
Tibb
%%
Letter: Wren to Nessa, the storm year
Dear Nessa,

I have been putting off this letter all week, because I wanted to tell you something sensible about the storm, and I do not have anything sensible to say. So I will tell you what happened, and you can be sensible about it at Vell.

The wind came up the marsh in the middle of the night. It was not a great wind, as winds go. But it went on for a long time, and it came from every side at once, and it found every loose board on the boardwalk and every shingle on the sheds. The water rose over the lower path, and then over the second, and by morning the healing house was the only dry roof in the village. The rest of us sat in it.

I had eleven people in beds and six on the floor. Ruth brought in two boys from the weir who had been out since dusk in a boat with no oars. Hob came in carrying a goose, which bit him, and then bit Moss, who was carrying the broth tray, and then sat down on the floor very happily and ate the bread off the tray. Nobody could stop laughing, even the boys with the cuts.

Moss did well. It carried the broth in both hands all night. It did not spill once, though the floor was tilting. It sat with the boys by the fire and did not move, and when the older one asked it a question, it answered by doing the next thing, which was to pass him a cup. That is the only answer an apprentice can give, and it was the right one.

I keep thinking about the jars. Eighty of them on the high shelf in the back room, and the shelf came away from the wall, and not one broke. Moss picked them up one at a time and put them back in their places, by the labels. It took most of the night. I am going to stop talking about it now. I am going to go to bed.

Please write back when you can. Tell me the tutors' house is dry. Tell me Vell is still on its hill.

Wren
Healing house, Lowmarsh
%%
Letter: Nessa to Cress
Cress,

Your letter came by cart from Three Wells, and I read it standing in the cart yard. Sit down. I am not cross. You did nothing wrong that I did not do first.

My first barn was a stable at Copperhold, in my second year. My mind sorted apples from pears. At Vell it scored ninety-nine. In the stable it put every apple in the pear pile, because the Vell apples had been red and the Copperhold apples were green. I stood in the straw and the miners laughed. One of them asked for his pears back.

So here is what happened to Scoop, as plainly as I can say it.

A mind learns whatever helps it to be right. Nobody tells it which part to learn. At Vell the cloth helped: brown sack, wheat; white sack, flour. The grain helped too, but the cloth was easier and louder. So Scoop learned both, and listened mostly to the cloth. At the ridge the cloth said nothing, and the grain was too quiet to carry the day.

The test box did not warn you, because it was the same as the drill box in the way that mattered: brown and white. A test box is pages the mind has never seen. It must also be pages like the ones the mind will meet. Those are two different things. You tested the first and forgot the second.

What to do this week:
1. Gather every kind of sack on the ridge: patched, dyed, inside out. Fill them from the mill.
2. Drill Scoop on those, a thousand sacks, with the right answer shown after it has answered.
3. Make a new test box of sacks Scoop has never seen, from a farm it has never seen.
4. Test it in the barn, by lantern, not at the mill by daylight.
5. Write down which sacks it got wrong, and why you think so.

The bowls were a good idea. Tell Ida's girl so from me. A child who looks at what the mind looks at, instead of what the tutor expected, is worth a dozen of us. Put her idea in your book as: take away what misleads, and the rest can be heard.

You asked if you should stay. Yes. You have a barn story now. Every tutor needs one. I have the stable. Now you have the sacks.

"Never send an untested mind to the fields." I say it so often that I forget the second half. Test it where it will work.

I am pinning a note to the door of the tutor house. It says: a sack is not its cloth.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Oren to Nella, four helpers
Dear Nella,

I am glad the jar arrived without leaking. The lid was made by a careful hand.

You asked for a plain account for the children. The first tutors at Vell made four helpers of copper and iron. Each had a mind held in a crystal. The tutors showed each mind its task again and again. A crystal learns from what it is shown, but no one wrote down why.

The helpers could not take any task they pleased. One could tend a row. Another could sort. That was the work it had learned.

I will bring the account with me. Please keep the children from touching the old drill box. The latch is loose.

Oren

Letter: Fern to Mira
Dear Mira,

I am at the stall with the eggs in their straw and the sun on the north market for the first time since Reaping, and I am writing to you in between customers, because you will want to hear about it before the carts take it up the north stair.

The market is open. The first stalls are up. Ruby Oke has brought the honey and the bread from Ambrook, and she has a new cloth on the table, which is blue and has a lot of opinions. The carters from Kettle Ridge have brought the wool, and the carters from Saltreach have brought salt, and a box of dried fish that nobody will name.

The horse is back, and everybody has come to see her. She stands at the foot of the stair with the children round her, and she puts her nose into their coats and takes the sugar out of their pockets. I have told the children that is not allowed, and the horse has told me it is allowed, and the horse has won.

Gault and Harrow have both been to the stall this morning. They did not speak to each other. They bought eggs from me, one after the other, and they each paid the exact price, and they each looked at the fence through the market window while they waited for their change. I have never seen two men so careful about a sum.

Lantern came down with the stones this morning. It set them out in a line on the north road, one after another, and it did not say a word. Then it stood up and looked at the fence, and it looked at the oak, and it went back up the stair. Gault and Harrow stood at the fence for an hour and said nothing. Then they both went home, and the fence is still there, and the stones are still in their line, and nobody has moved either.

I will not tell you what I think of the fence. I will tell you that the eggs are very good this spring, and that the hens have decided the sun is theirs, and that the honey has come up from Ambrook in perfect condition, and that the bread has a crust like a roof.

Come down for the spring fair. Bring the mare if you can, and nobody will mind if she eats the bread.

Fern

Letter: Hob to Tam, the new house
Dear Tam,

They have started the relay house at the foot of our tower. Stone from Copperhold, cut in blocks as big as a calf. It will be done by Thaw, they say.

I am not worried. I only wish to know where I will put the horses. The new wall comes right up to the paddock gate, and Clover, who is due any day, is a mare who does not like to be hurried past a wall.

Please tell the masons to leave me a gap.

Hob

Letter: Pip to Tam, the fence
Tam,

I am writing this and leaving it on your bench because when I say it out loud you laugh and then I forget it.

The fence.

You moved it. Not much. Two posts. I know it was you because the old line is still in the grass and the new line is not, and because you always dig on the slant.

The fence is on the line the way it has always been. My father put his end where it is and your father put yours and they met in the middle and shook hands on it. That is what the stones say. There is a stone under the hedge with a mark on it.

I do not want the top two rows of your wheat. I want the fence where it was.

If you think I am wrong, come and stand at the stone with me and we will look at it together before the light goes.

Pip

Letter: Ruth to Bram, the homecoming
Dear Bram,

You are home. I have seen you walk up the lane with your pack on your back and your hat in your hand, and I have not stopped smiling since. The children have stopped asking when you will come. Now they are asking what you brought.

I know you are tired. Sit down. The bread is on the table and the soup is by the fire. Eat before you speak. You always said that was the rule of the Long Road, and I am holding you to it.

The boy at the gate was very proud of himself. He carried your pack up the lane with both arms, and he says he will carry it every day now. He does not know that you have two more days of walking in your legs. Please do not tell him yet.

The house is clean. The yard is swept. The goats are in the pen, the hens are laying, and the cat has been sitting on your chair every evening, looking at the door. She has not moved. You may have to ask her nicely.

Tell me about Vell when you have rested. Tell me about the Archive, and the clerks, and whether the cake was as good as you said. I want to hear the whole thing, slowly, with the spoons in your hand. I have been listening for your voice in the lane for six weeks, and now I can stop.

Welcome home, love.

Ruth

P.S. Father has taken the new roof off the barn to make it straight. Do not look at it until morning. He wants it to be a surprise, and it is a surprise, I promise.
