Letter: Opal to Carys, the hall is shut
Dear Carys,

You asked for news of the hall, and I have some, and none of it is good.

The tutors' hall at Vell shut its doors on the first of Frost. The council will not pay the tutors this year. They say that Kester's note proves a crystal cannot learn anything worth the bread. Kester says it proves only that one crystal cannot do one small thing. Nobody is listening, because a small thing said quietly is easy to ignore, and a large thing said by a council is not.

Nine tutors were paid last year. Three have gone home to their farms. Two have gone to Copperhold to carry ore, which is honest work, but not what they trained for. Four are still in Vell, living on what the town gives them. I leave wool on their steps.

The drill yard is empty. The four helpers of copper and iron that were the first in the hall stand where they were last drilled, in a row at the far end, still and dim. Nobody has the emeralds to oil them. The children go and look at them on the way home from school. One of them asked me if they are sleeping. I said yes. I could not think what else to say.

I have been busy. Winter is when babies come, and the snow does not stop them. Last week I walked up the north road to a farm in the dark, with a lantern and a cold nose, and delivered a boy at the second hour of the night. His father wept and his mother asked for tea.

Kester is calm. He says a long quiet is not the same as an ending. He says crystals are patient, and so are the people who understand them, and that one day somebody will try two crystals in a row and see what happens. I asked him what that meant. He said he did not know. He said that is why it is worth trying.

Come at the thaw if you can. Bring the children. I will have a loaf.

Your Opal

Letter: Oren to Ruth, moving house
Dear Ruth,

We are moving. I am writing before the cart comes, so you do not hear it from Ulla at the market. She will have heard it from me and got it wrong.

It is not far. Only up the hill, to the new rooms by the copying hall. The clerks who work the deep shelves are being given the top floor, where there is more light and the stairs are shorter. My knees are very glad.

The house by the farm has been good to us. Forty years is a long time for a clerk to live in one place. The apple tree by the gate will stay. It is too big to carry, and the new owner has promised to keep the bees.

Please come on the day of the move. You are the only one who can tell the cart driver which way is north. He is convinced it is the road.

Bring your sister. Bring the good plates. Do not bring the goat.

Oren

Letter: Lise to Oren
Dear Oren,

The new road reached our hamlet in spring. It is packed earth, with a cobblestone edge. We have a bridge over the stream now. The children keep counting the stones and losing their place.

A zombie came to the gate last night. We kept the torches lit. It stood by the new waymark until dawn, then wandered off. I have told the children not to count it as a traveler.

Please send a copy of the lesson about even numbers. I want to read it to the older pupils.

From Lise

Letter: Pell to Mave, Dove is home
Dear Mave,
Dove is home, and she is happy, and so is the cart, which is very surprised.
I took her over the bridge at Saltreach as you said. I walked her by the rail with my hand on her neck, and she did not look at the water once. Then at the Brown Hen she tried to go into the inn yard, and the stable boy had to pull her out by the bridle, and she has never been so embarrassed in her life.
She ate the oats, both handfuls, and then the apple, and then the carrot that the children at Three Wells gave her, which I did not know about. The children are very fond of her too. A girl asked me if Dove was a grey cloud that had decided to come down. I said yes.
On the hill above the Halfway I let her run, just a little. She ran the way she likes, with her head up and her mane out, and I shouted, and so did she. I think she enjoyed it more than I did. I am too old to run.
Thank you for the loan. Thank you for the honey, which is in the cart in a jar with a red cloth tied over it, and which I have not opened, because I am not a fool.
Your friend,
Pell

Letter: Tam to Mira, first snow
Dear Mira,

The first snow came last night. It was early this year. Pip ran out before breakfast and stood in it with his arms out, like a scarecrow with a pumpkin head. The wheat stubble went white. The beetroot rows are done, all but the bottom field. The bottom field can wait.

The windmill squeaked all night. I keep meaning to grease it. I keep not greasing it.

How is Northpoint? Is the snow deep there yet? Do the foxes come close to the tower in the cold? You said once they do. I think of you up there with your lamp and your ninety rungs.

Pip asks about your lantern every week. He wants to know if it is heavy. I said ask her yourself, write a letter. He is shy of the post. So here I am asking for him. Is the lamp heavy?

We had word from Vell about the great drills. The clerks want letters. Any letters, they say, so the reading minds can learn from them. I have a box of yours from the old years. Shall I send them? Some are only about the weather. Some are not.

Write soon. Wear two coats.

Your brother,
Tam

Letter: Tam to Mira, the cold week
Dear Mira,
The cold came down off the north on the second of Longnight and has not gone back up. The windmill squeaks on every turn, which is company of a kind. The pond is hard enough to stand on at the edges, and Bessie will not cross it, so the milk is late every morning and I am late with it.
The pigs have built a nest in the straw by the barn wall, nine of them in a pile, and when I go to feed them they do not get up. They only grunt. Pip says the grunt means thank you, and I think it means go away. Pip is right more often than I am, so I am learning to say thank you back.
The bees are in their hive by the flowers, all of them in one ball, shivering. I put my hand on the hive. It was warm in the middle and cold at the edge. That is how they keep the queen alive, Pip says. It stood with me a long time and did not touch anything.
The Kettle Inn has been very quiet. The innkeeper says the travelers have all gone home, except one, who is staying in the back room and eating the soup. The soup is good, and the traveler is no trouble, so nobody has asked him to leave.
I sent a message down the relay to Vell last week, about the seed order. It cost five emeralds, and it reached Vell in a morning. Dace says that is the best value in the district.
A carter came by from Copperhold with coal, and says the ridge road is full of drifts past the mill. The wind plays with the lamp at the end of our lane. It flickers when it likes and steadies when it likes. I have stopped trying to tell it which.
Keep the torches lit. Love from the ridge,
Tam
%%
Letter: Nessa to Oren
Dear Oren,

You asked me to write down what Isla found, in plain words, for the Archive. You say that in a hundred years some clerk will want to know, and will find the letter and be glad. I hope so. I will try.

First, the old rule. When we drill a mind, it has a size, which is the room in its crystal, and it has a drill, which is the writing we show it, and a time, which is how long we drill. A mind that is bigger, drilled longer, on more writing, does better. Every tutor knew this a little. We did not know how much better.

Isla tried it in a careful way. She grew many small minds, each a different size. She drilled each on more and more writing. She tested each on pages it had never seen. Each time, she counted the mistakes.

When she drew her points on a slate, they made a line. A gentle curve. The mistakes go down as the size goes up, and as the drill grows longer, and as the writing grows. They go down by the same kind of step each time. Double the size, and the mistakes go down by a certain share. Double the writing, the same.

Because the curve is smooth, a tutor can draw it using only the small minds and then read off where a big mind would land. A mind the size of a loaf, drilled on all the archive, can be guessed at before a single crystal is grown. We can know what it is worth before we pay.

This matters for the councils. The councils have been asking whether big minds are worth their price. The curve says yes. A mind ten times the size does measurably better, and one a hundred times does better still. The curve does not say when it stops. We do not know that.

What the curve does not say is why. I do not know why a bigger crystal learns more. I do not know why more writing helps, beyond that it is more to learn from. These are questions for the tutors after me.

The councils have already voted. Relay houses will be built at the foot of the towers, one by one, to hold the big minds. Vell's will be first. The stone is being cut.

I am sending this by the next cart. I would not trust it to the relay, for it is long.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Vesper to Wren
Wren,
Did the Saltreach boat come back? Dov says the Heron is three days late and nobody will say it aloud.
We stood on the headland last night with torches until the stars came out. I left a lantern on the rock. Dov says it is useless, because the boat is too far. I say a lantern has never harmed a boat.
If they are lost, nothing I can say will matter. If they are late, they will want soup. I have started a pot of fish stew, which I have no idea how to make. Tell me what goes in.
I am sorry to write about myself. You know boats. I know headaches. We do what we can.
Vesper
Under it, in a different hand: It came in at dawn with the tide. The mast was cracked and the cook had a black eye. Nobody will say how. Everyone is well. The stew was good. Wren

Letter: Dorrit to Ivo, the long winter
Dear Ivo,

You went down the hill to sell the cider, and the snow came up the hill to sell itself. It came in Leaffall and it has not gone yet. The road to Vell was shut for thirty days, which you will say is a lie, but I counted.

The miners kept the stove in the kitchen at the mine gate burning day and night. The cook says she has never made so much stew in her life. Every man who came up from the Deep Works had two bowls and a joke. Hesk had three bowls and no joke, which is how we knew he was happy.

The children sledged down the top steps on boards from the tool store. The count board got a new name written on it in chalk every morning. Somebody wrote SNOW in capitals, and nobody rubbed it out for a week.

We had the apples from the cellar all winter. The barrels you left are still full. The spiders are very pleased, as you will see when you come home and find the barn has become a town.

I am keeping the stove at home lit for you. Come up the hill before the thaw, or the cider will be sour and so will I.

Your sister,
Dorrit

Edda,
I cannot come on market day. The mill wheel at Kettle Ridge has broken, and every hand is wanted to carry the sacks by cart until Tam gets it mended. I would rather be carding with you.
I will come the week after. I will bring the blue thread, two jars of honey and a cheese. The cheese is for Garn, who says my cheese is too sharp. I will see if he still says so.
Tell the children that Pip fell into the ditch yesterday and came up with a frog on its head. I have never laughed so hard.

Letter: Ossa to Pell, the candle prices
Dear Pell,
You asked for my prices, and I have them here, though I do not like to give them.
Tallow candle, plain, by the dozen: 3 emeralds. Beeswax, by the dozen: 8. Beeswax with a scent of apple, by the dozen: 10. The scent is not worth two emeralds, but people like it, and who am I to stop them?
The miners buy the plain ones, by the hundred. They burn them fast. They say a candle is a good friend, and a poor investment.
Now, the bet. Hesk's new apprentice came this week. Hesk and Clank have been drilling it in the yard. It carries, it stays, it comes when told. It broke a tray of my best candles on the first day. It was not its fault. The tray was at the edge of the table. Hett the dog moved it.
Fenn at the inn bet it would break within a month. It has not. I win a pie.
Please send word on the price of honey. If it is cheap, I will make more wax.
Ossa

Letter: Pell to his mother at Ambrook
Dear Mother,

The winter market at Kettle Ridge was the best I have seen. I set up my stall by the well in the yard and sold wool and copper pots and a crate of apples from your orchard. The apples went first. Everyone said they were the best at the fair. I said I knew, because my mother grew them.

There was a festival on the last night. A bonfire on the ridge, and torches all along the edge of the fields, and a song the children sang about the tower on the hill. A man named Tam won a prize for a beetroot as big as a cat's head. He held it up and everyone cheered. His apprentice, Pip, stood by the cart and squeaked. The children loved Pip.

I sold a jar of your honey to a clerk from Vell. He said he would keep it for his sister and not eat it. I did not believe him. I sold the rest to a baker with flour on her sleeves.

The roads are good now. There is a waymark with a torch every twelve blocks, and an inn a day apart. The innkeeper at the ridge gave me a bed and a hot pie.

I will be home before the thaw. Save me an apple from the oldest tree.

Your son,
Pell

Letter: Sella to Ree
Dear Ree,

The saddle is on our fence. I found it there this morning, with a small stone holding down the strap. You could have knocked at the door. We were awake. The horse was awake too, and she knows how to knock with her nose.

I am glad the roof is dry. My brother says you should have laid two stones, not one. My brother says this about every roof, even the roof over his own head. It still leaks.

The cap was made for you. I did not expect the horse to help with the green patch, but she has given it a fine fringe. Wear it when you walk to the relay house. The path is muddy near the well, and the cap will be easier to see if you fall in.

I have saved three apples for you. They are on the shelf above the flour. Do not let the horse see them. She has learned to open the gate by pushing the lower board.

Will you come to supper after your watch? I have beetroot soup and a loaf. My brother has promised not to speak about roofs until after the meal.

Sella

Letter: Hesk to Cass, the warm stone
Cass,

You asked me what the miners are saying. Here it is, plain, because you would only ask again.

The stone on the fifth level is warm. Not hot. Warm, the way a loaf is warm an hour after it comes out. You put your hand on the wall at the far face and it stays warm when you take the hand away. Ola says it is the lamp. The lamp is on the other side of the rail. Ola says it is the lamp anyway, and she is not stupid.

We stopped going past the lift station on the sixth level at the start of Thaw. Not by order. By agreement. The men agreed in the kitchen one night, and nobody wrote it down. Bram says he will go lower when the stone tells him it is cold. He has not gone lower.

Bram also says he hears a hum at night, through the rails, like somebody singing with their mouth shut. I say Bram hears things. I also say I have heard it once, lying on my bunk with the window open, and I did not like it, and I did not get up to look.

Clank has been sent back up for the rest of the spring. It stands at the shaft head and sorts the copper, and it does not go down. Nobody told it to stop going down. It just stopped, the way it stops at a cart that is too full.

Do not put this in a letter to the Archive. Put it in your own book, in your own hand, and keep it there. If anybody wants it, they can come up the hill and ask me. I am still here, and I still know how to get down the shaft.

Hesk
Copperhold, the mine gate
%%
Letter: Corin to Pell
Dear Pell,

The relay house at Saltreach is finished. We raised the last stone on the ninth of Apple, and the big mind came up from Vell by cart, packed in straw like a crate of eggs. The sailor who drove it said he had never carried anything so heavy that looked so much like nothing.

It stands on its stand at the foot of the tower, in a room with a window to the sea. The crystal is the size of a loaf of bread, and grey as a cloud with sun behind it. Round it stand the quickeners, ringed in a circle, and we keep them cold with water from the harbor. Twice a day we empty the pans and fill them again. On cold mornings there is steam, and the room smells of wet stone.

How it works, as the tutors tell it, is this. The big mind does not live in the tower. It lives in the crystal. The relay lamps carry its answers out along the coast, tower to tower, and a small mind in an apprentice asks it a question and hears it answered. A tower is only a tall way of passing a letter. The big mind is the reader.

Corin
%%
Letter: Pell to Oren, the roads
Dear Oren,

I have walked the Long Road from end to end this spring, and I have news for your map, which is still too empty in the north.

The road is open all the way from Ambrook to Vell, except for two bad stretches by the marsh and one at the river, where the bridge is a plank and a wish. The carters are calling the river bridge the Wish, and they are not joking.

There are inns now a day apart. The Brown Hen is the newest, at the crossing below Three Wells. Marda and her husband built it of timber and clay, with a hearth in the middle and nineteen beds. Halfway between Three Wells and Vell is the Halfway Inn, which is older than anyone's grandmother and has a riddle board by the fire. The riddle this month is: what has a mouth but cannot eat? Nobody has got it. I have not got it either.

The waymarks are up. Stone pillars, three blocks high, with a torch on top and a figure cut in the face. I counted them from Ambrook to the Brown Hen. Forty-two.

The relay towers are going up along the road. There is one at the top of the hill above Three Wells, and one above the mill pond at Ambrook, with its door made of old oak. From the top of each tower you can see the next one, if the weather is clear. A lamp blinks from one to the next. I sent you a message from Ambrook, and it arrived before I did.

Your friend, Pell

Letter: Wren to Emmet, honey and news
Dear Emmet,

Moss has gone up to you with the long ladder and strict instructions about the honey. I told Moss the honey is for you to give me, not for Moss to carry. We will see what happens.

The cough is better this week. Two of the children are up and running about. The old man at the ford is sitting in the sun again. His daughter had her baby on the ninth of Swarm, a girl, small and loud. They have called her Reed, after the beds. The mother is well. She ate a whole bowl of beetroot soup and asked for another. That is a good sign.

If the bees go to the hollow oak, wait for me. Do not smoke out the wasps alone. You are too old to run from wasps. You run like a man carrying a sack of turnips.

Come to the healing house on market day and bring the honey. I will have bread.

Wren

P.S. Moss says the bees are in the tree still. Moss says they are very angry. Take care.

Letter: Dell to Wren, the cow
Dear Wren,
Our brown cow has stopped eating. She stands by the reeds with her head down and does not even look at the clover, which you know is a bad sign. Her nose is dry. Her eyes are wet. The boy says she is sulking. I say she is sick.
Please come when you can. Bring the jar of willow bark, and whatever you use for the bloat. I will pay in cheese, the good cheese, the one Ann is hiding from me.
Your neighbor,
Dell

Letter: Oren to Sorrel, the play
Dear Sorrel,

Marta tells me the Three Wells school wants to give a play at the end of summer, and that you are short of ideas. I have one. Write a play about the barn.

It is a good story for children. Six apprentices stand in a field with a pile of planks and no instructions. The first act is the plan. The second act is the cart in the mud. The third act is the barn.

Do not give the apprentices any lines. They do not speak, and the children will enjoy the quiet. Let the children hold up the cards instead: fetch planks, haul, build, store. The big mind at the relay house is a part for your tallest child. Put a lantern in the child's hand, a bench under the feet, and a very loud voice.

The cart can be the smallest child, under a blanket. The mud can be Pell, who will do it for nothing.

I will come if the Archive lets me. I will bring my own chair. I do not trust the benches.

Oren
%%
Letter: Wren to Bel
Dear Bel,

Nella brought your fair letter to the healing house. She says you won the prize for the neatest lantern. I am glad. A green pane is a fine choice for a winter lantern, though the frogs have not sent me their thanks.

The fair was busy even in the cold. People came from Three Wells, Kettle Ridge and the Long Road. Pell sold flour by the sack and complained that everyone wanted a smaller sack for the same price. He complained while selling every one.

Moss carried the lanterns from the school house to the square. It walked carefully, even when the snow caught on its feet. One lantern tipped in the cart, but Moss caught it before it fell. The glass was safe. Moss brought it to my house afterward, and I wiped the snow from its copper hands.

You asked whether the frogs sleep through winter. I do not know. They hide in the mud when the water grows cold. We leave them alone. Last year one came out during a thaw and sat on the doorstep for an hour. It looked as if it had come to ask for soup.

I am writing this beside the well. The water is clear, and the jars are in order. Moss has made new labels. The jar marked cough syrup holds cough syrup. The jar marked honey holds honey. I have checked both.

Keep your lantern in a dry place until spring. Tell Nella I still have the oak pegs for the boardwalk. The work can wait for a warmer morning.

With care,
Wren
%%
Letter: Greer to Pell, the new road
Dear Pell,

You will be pleased to hear the new road reaches the ridge at last. It comes up from Three Wells in a long curve, with a stone edge on the outside and packed earth in the middle. There is a torch on a post every twelve blocks, as you asked. The post by my gate has a torch that Hal lit too early. It is still burning, and the moths think it is a festival.

The first cart up it came in Longday, with a crate of lamp glass for the Vell tower and a hat box with no hat in it. The carter says the box was for a hat he forgot. I think he forgot the hat on purpose, because he does not trust it on the road.

There are four inns on the road so far. One of them is only a barn with a sign. The sign says the Halfway Inn, which is confusing, because it is not halfway to anything. The waymarks are up as far as the first bend. Each has a figure from 0 to 9, and an arrow to the next inn, and the little stones on top that travelers leave. Nobody has explained the stones to me, and I have not asked. They are white, and warm from the sun. I have started leaving one myself.

A crow has taken a liking to the top of the torch post. It sits up there and looks down at the carts like the mayor of Three Wells, and the carters bow to it, out of habit.

The apples along the road are ripening already, although it is only Swarm. The orchard at Ambrook sent a cart of early ones up the ridge, with a note from Linnet: Eat one, and then you will know why we are proud. We ate one. We are very proud.

The melon at my gate was the biggest on the ridge this year. Ama says so, and Ama does not say things she has not measured. It has gone to the market now, and the market is pleased, and the melon has not been asked how it feels about that.

We had the cheese judging on the green, as always. My blue came second. The judge was Hester the cow, who was not asked, and who ate the rosettes. Nobody could say that was unfair.

Hal's new apprentice, Sprig, carried twelve cheese boards across the green without one falling off. It stopped by the green when told, and it said nothing at all, which the judge said was the best a board could ask for.

Pell, I know you are on the road most of the summer, and that your ledger is the only wife you have time for. But come up when the road is finished. Bring the good salt, and any news you have that is not from the carters. I want to hear it from you.

Your friend, who has the cheese,
Greer

P.S. The blue is ready. It is not for the carters.
%%
Letter: Finch to Jory
Dear Jory,

The mare is back in her stall, brushed and fed. She carried the oak boards without a complaint. She did stop once to stare at a fish laid out on the quay. I think she was checking whether it was a very flat horse.

I mended the shed latch too. It closes now, unless someone closes it with the old wooden spoon you keep beside the door. That spoon is too wide for the gap. I have put it on the shelf.

The pantry roof is sound. Lida says the room smells of warm oak, which is better than the smell of a wet sack. She gave me soup and a heel of bread. The soup was good. The bread could have stopped a door.

Thank you for lending the mare. I will bring the oats back tomorrow. I took the wrong sack at first, but I noticed before she ate any flour. I have placed the white sack by your door.

Finch

Letter: Cass to Iven, winter stores
Dear Iven,

The cider is in the stone jars now. I left two by the door for your house, as promised. Keep them off the floor. The boards are cold there, and the jars cracked last winter.

A zombie came to our gate after dark. It pushed at the latch while I held the torch. I was glad of the thick gate, and gladder when it wandered off toward the harbor wall.

The well ran dry this morning. I have sent for water from the lower street. Please bring the empty jars back when you can. I need them for the next pressing.

Cass

Letter: Mave to Pell, the last jar
Dear Pell,
The last of the dark honey is in a jar on my kitchen shelf, with a label that says Heather for Pell, Do Not Argue. I have kept it back for you. It is the best of the year, and I know you will argue anyway.
The grey mare can have the carrot from my garden, if she comes past again. Tell her she is a good girl and a fool on the bridge, and that the second is not her fault.
The frost is coming. I can feel it in my knees, and in the hives, which have gone quiet in the mornings. The bees are sealing the last of the comb, and they do not come out until the sun is well up. I have wrapped the hives in straw.
Write when you are home. I will be here, with the kettle on, and the bees asleep.
Mave

Letter: Bel to the Archive
Dear Oren,

I heard about the wall at Vell that a creeper blew up. My teacher Sorrel said it was at the drill yard and a boy from Kettle Ridge was counting blocks and forgot the torches. Is that true? I want to know because I count blocks too and I forget things.

We had a creeper at the school house. It came up by the east well and stood by the door the children painted. Nobody was there because it was Longday and the school was shut. It blew a hole in the wall right next to the painted door. The door is fine. The wall has a hole. Dace says the hole is how spiders get in, so they will fill it with deep stone and make the top row stick out.

I think they should leave the hole small and put a window in it. Then the children could look out at the well. I told Dace. He said: Bel, it is a wall. I said it could be a wall with a window. He said he would think about it. He has not put the window in yet.

Here is what I want to know. When the creeper blew up the wall at Vell, did the apprentices run? Pip says his did not. Mine would run. I would tell it to run. Is there a drill for running?

If there is a drill for running, I would like to learn it. I am very good at running.

From Bel, age ten, Three Wells

P.S. My grandmother says hello. She says the buckets were heavier in her day.

Letter: Hazel to Pell, the first snow
Dear Pell,

I have three things to tell you, and one to show you, so sit down, wherever you are.

First, the snow. Fenn came down the north road on the twenty-eighth of Melon with his cart and a bucket. The bucket was packed in straw. Inside the straw was snow. Real snow, Pell, in summer, from the north pass. Fenn says it is the first snow of the year on the pass, and that it is early. Fenn says everything is early. He said it at our wedding, too.

The children at the inn went quiet when they saw it. Sorrel held a handful until her fingers went red. Then she tasted it and said it tasted of nothing. Gil put the rest in the cold cellar, beside the milk. It had all melted by the third day.

Second, the riddle. Gil put a new riddle on the board by the door. He copied it off the Halfway board, so he says it is his now. What has teeth, but cannot bite? Gil says the answer is comb. I say card, because a card has teeth, and a card has carded every fleece on this road. A comb has never carded anything in its life.

We asked the travelers to vote. Nine said comb. Seven said card. Three said sheep, which is wrong and rude. One old man said Gil's mother, and was put out of the room.

I say I won. Gil says I cheated, by bringing my own cards to the table. He may be right. I brought them to show people what a card is.

Third, the lamp. Oren's letter came on Fenn's cart, folded very small, with a long note about the archive and its roof. He says the archive has filed the blinks under Stuck Lamps, and that the box is not large. He asked Garran to copy any blink that comes again. He also says the archive does not guess. Garran was very cross about that.

It has been quiet for four nights. Garran still sits on the step at dusk, just in case.

Oren also says a good fleece is four emeralds in the Vell market, not three. I told him three was fair. I was wrong. Do not tell him I said it.

And the thing to show you, which you must come home to see: Sorrel carded her first whole fleece alone. It is lumpy. It is hers. She wrote her name on the bag, and the S faces the wrong way, and she is very proud of it.

I will be home before the apples are picked. Keep the torches lit at the Ambrook bridge. Mind the plank.

Love,
Hazel
The Brown Hen, on the Long Road

Letter: Wren to Pell
Dear Pell,

Your cart came through Lowmarsh before the snow covered the open road. Thank you for the salt and the two sacks of flour. The fish weir is still working, though the water runs slow under the ice. We have enough food for the sick beds and for Moss, who says a healer must eat well to stay useful.

I have paid the old debt for the wool in emeralds. Nella counted them twice, because Moss had written cough syrup on the jar. They were all there. I have told Moss that money is not medicine, even when it is kept beside the well.

I am sending you a jar of honey from the hives near the boardwalk. The lid is tied down with a strip of clean cloth. Do not put it on the cart seat. Last winter a jar slid off there and reached Three Wells before the cart did.

The winter fair at Three Wells is next month, if the road stays clear. Nella says she will bring her children and their glass lanterns. They have made one with a green pane and one with a yellow pane. The smallest child says the green one is for frogs. I think the frogs will accept the prize.

Please bring back two bundles of dry reeds if you can. Wren's house needs new mats for the beds. Moss has promised to weave them, but the last mat had one end wider than the other. It still kept the draft away. We shall call that good work.

Wren

Letter: Juna to her son Pim, the flood
Dear Pim,

The river came up in the night. It is down now. The cows are fine, the hens are fine, and the pig is sulking on the hill, which is what pigs do after a flood.

The water came over the low field and up to the back step. I put the sacks along the door and Father sat on the stair with the lamp. We did not sleep much. Your sister slept through it all, and woke up cross that she had missed the fun.

The barn lost its straw. The garden lost its beans. The path to the mill is gone under a mud that smells of river. Come home and bring your boots.

Your mother,
Juna

P.S. Your bed is dry. I kept it dry. I used the old blanket.

Letter: Wynn to Juna
Dear Juna,

You asked what happens on the road in autumn. So here it is.

The Brown Hen holds a harvest fair every Leaffall, and this year I was here for it. Marda put out long tables on the common and lit lamps all round the yard. There was beetroot soup and baked apples and bread from Three Wells. Pell came and sold honey from Ambrook and quarreled about the price with everyone in a friendly way.

There was a contest for the best apprentice. I did not know such a thing existed. Three of them stood in a row by the drill board and did what they were told. One fetched a bucket. One built a small wall from a pattern on the board. One stayed when told to stay, for a long time, while the children ran round it. The children liked that one best. The staying apprentice won.

A tutor came from Vell and gave a talk about the reading minds. He said the big minds cut words into common pieces now, so the same window holds far more. He drew it on a board with chalk, a word cut into slices. Most of the room did not follow, but a girl of about nine did, and asked him a good question about it.

The grey is well. I am bringing apples home.

Wynn

Letter: Ferris to Oren, the flood
To the clerk Oren, at the Archive at Vell,

I write as the council's man for the river road, in the matter of the flood at Oat Hill farm.

The Tull came over its banks on the night of the eleventh day of Apple. It rose faster than it has in my time, and I have been on this road twenty-two years. By dawn, the low field at the Oat Hill farm was under four hand-widths of brown water, and the back step of the farmhouse was under two.

No one was lost. The family, which is the widow Juna and her three children, moved to the upper barn with the cows. Juna had the foresight to tie the hens in the loft before the water reached the yard, and the hens have forgiven her. Her eldest, Pim, who is nine, went to the mill for the apprentices on his own, through the flood. He was not told to. He says he thought of it on the way and did not stop.

The apprentices from the mill came down the road with a cart and ropes. They were given an order by the mill keeper, which was to clear the drain and hold the cart at the bridge. They did. They held the cart in the water for three hours with the flood at their knees, and did not move the cart from the bridge when the water pushed. The mill keeper said afterward that they did not leave their post once, though the water was higher than the bridge rail by midnight.

I have written the apprentices' names on the council card, which you may copy. They are Hod, Ledger and Quiet. Ledger is the one that held the cart with its arm through the rail.

Damage: 14 sacks of stored grain, lost or spoiled. The straw in the barn, all of it. The garden beans. The farm's bridge plank, the third one, which is in the mud at the bend of the river. The farm will need a new plank before the next market.

Please send the council's note to the Archive. I would be grateful for it to be kept, and for the apprentices' names to be written in the book, since nobody else will remember them.

Your servant, in the matter of the road,
Ferris

Letter: Rafe to Ysa, rain on the road
Dear Ysa,

The rain caught me at the Halfway Inn, where I am writing from, with my boots by the hearth and my cart under a sheet in the yard. The sheet is new. The cart is old. The rain has only been interested in one of them.

The middle of the road is mud, and the stone edge is slick. I took the cart through the first bend and nearly lost a barrel of Ambrook cider. It stayed in. It was tempted. I had a word with it.

Mabel will not let anyone poke the hearth. It has not gone out for twenty years, and she is very proud of it. I offered to poke it once. She looked at me the way Ruth looks at a late cart.

There is a map on the wall by the stair. It is an old map, drawn in brown ink on a board the width of a door. It shows every inn on the Long Road as a little house, and every tower as a little square, with a number beside each one. The Halfway is drawn with a bird on its roof. Mabel says the bird was there before her, and so was the map. The clerk who drew it has been dead a long time.

I asked her whether the map is correct. She said it is correct as far as it goes, which she says is the best any map can be. I am inclined to agree with her, and to be annoyed about it.

Two more days, if the mud lets me. Keep the lamp lit. Keep the bread in the crock. Tell the children the old wheel is not for playing on.

Your Rafe

Letter: Dunny to Bel, the school play
Dear Bel,

We did the play at the school in Copperhold, and I was the wolf. I had to growl. Teacher said a wolf does not growl. A wolf goes quiet, and then it jumps. So I was quiet for the whole first scene, and the little ones at the front cried, which Teacher says was the best part.

Tansy played the lamb, and the lamb did not want to be a lamb. She kept walking off the stage to look at the mine gate. Teacher stopped the play three times and sent her back. In the end she was only half a lamb, but the audience clapped the half.

Write and tell me if your play was at Three Wells. Did the mayor fall off the school step again? Tell him from me that the step is his fault, not mine.

Your friend,
Dunny

Letter: Piper to Bel, apples and a debt
Dear Bel,

I have been thinking about the market and the way you said it. You said the market at Vell is the best place in the world. I have not been to every place, so I will not say you are wrong. I will say the apples at our market are very good this autumn. The Ambrook carts came up the hill with red ones and green ones and a sort of yellow one nobody had seen before. The yellow ones are sweet. The green ones make your mouth go tight. I bought three of each with the money my mother gave me for the baker, and I told her the baker had raised his prices. I am telling you this and not her.

A thing happened at the gate. It was late, the lamps were lit, and the gate was closing. The gate keeper is a tall woman called Ro who has one eye that looks at you and one eye that looks at the road. She was putting the bar across when the bar stuck. She pushed it. It stuck harder. Then we heard a sound behind her, a low sound, like a person trying to remember a word.

There was a zombie at the gate, Bel. Just the one. It had come out of the trees and walked up the road and was standing there with its arms out, like it wanted a hug it was not going to get. Ro looked at it. The bar was still stuck. So Ro turned round and shouted at it. She shouted: not tonight. I am busy. And the zombie stopped. It stood there, swaying a bit. Then it turned round and walked off into the trees, and we did not see it again.

Everyone at the gate laughed so hard the lamps shook. Ro says it was the shouting. I think the zombie was just tired. Either way the bar went in after that, and Ro says it was the fear of her that did it, and I am not going to argue with Ro.

Your friend,
Piper

P.S. Tell your teacher the essay about the wells was good. I read it twice. I did not understand the bit about the water table but I liked the drawing.

Letter: Ash to Ida, the stones
Dear Ida,

You are right and I am sorry. I looked at the stones this morning with a candle and a mirror, and there is a chip in the runner stone, and I found a little heap of grit in the meal. I have swept out the mill and I have sent the grit back to the earth where it came from.

I have set the stones again and ground two sacks to try them. There is no grit. I have given one sack to the inn and one sack to my neighbor's hens, and the hens are pleased and so is the innkeeper.

I did not know the stones knocked together when the axle cracked. I do now. I have written it on a card and I have nailed the card to the mill door so that I do not forget. The card says: LOOK AT THE STONES.

I am sending you a sack of the new flour, ground fine, and a jar of honey from the hives at Ambrook, which I got in trade and was saving. Put the honey on the bread. Then tell the man from Kettle Ridge that the stones were Ash's fault and the honey is Ida's gift.

Your friend,
Ash

Letter: Nessa to Hob
Dear Hob,

Congratulations on Sparrow. I am sure she will grow into a fine, difficult mare.

Now, the quickeners. I will tell you what I can, which is not everything.

A big mind has a great many numbers inside. Each time it answers, it must multiply a very large heap of them together, and add them up. Done slowly, one by one, this would take a day for a single answer. Done all at once, in a hundred places, it takes the time of a blink.

The quickeners are what do the multiplying all at once. They are a row of small crystals, each very simple, each doing one little sum. A hundred or a thousand of them, working together, make one big sum fast.

Here is the trouble. Sums done quickly make heat. The quickeners get hot, as a forge does. If they are not kept cool, they will spoil. So at the relay house they are ringed with water, and in winter with ice, and at Northpoint with snow. The water carries the heat away. That is why the houses are built beside streams.

What they run on, I cannot say. I have asked. The councils say it is in hand. I do not know what that means. I only know that the quickeners work day and night, and the water is never still.

Think of it as a stable full of fast horses. They must be fed and watered. And someone must muck out.

I hope that answers it. Please send me a picture of Sparrow.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Juna to Pell
Dear Pell,

I carried the slates up the ladder all morning. Your Yarrow says I did not drop one. That is because the apprentice was at the foot of the ladder, and every time I got to the top it had the next bundle ready, and it did not look at me once, and I did not want to be the one who dropped one in front of it.

I have never done anything like that before. I have carried sacks and buckets and the children. I have never carried slates for someone else's roof and been so careful, and so tired, and so glad at the end of it.

Hal swore at every nail. He swore at the ladder, and then at the rain, which had not even started. Then he swore at the apprentice, which he should not have done. It only looked at him, and he stopped, and then he apologized to it. He said sorry to a mind of copper and iron. Everybody laughed, and he went very red, and then he laughed too.

I am writing to you because I wanted to tell someone who would not think it was silly. The apprentice was careful. The roof is on. Yarrow is happy. I am happy. I cannot say more than that, because it would be too much for a letter.

Come and see it when you pass. I will make the tea. I will not drop the cups.

Juna
%%
Tutor's note: even numbers
A note for new tutors, written plain, because I had to have it explained to me twice.

When you drill a mind, it is a stack of layers. Each layer takes what the layer below it found and passes its own answer up. When the answer is wrong, each layer is told its share of the blame, and each layer moves its weights a little toward the right answer. That is the whole of it.

Here is the trouble. Each layer makes its numbers bigger. Then the next layer makes them bigger again. Do it a hundred times and the numbers are wild. They run off to the ends of the slate and the mind stops learning. It goes stiff. You can drill it all day and nothing changes.

So we bring the numbers back to an even size at every layer. Not small. Even. Each layer, before it passes its answer up, pulls its numbers back to a size we chose when we built the mind. Then the next layer starts from even ground.

Think of it like a stair. Every step up you take, you set your feet even again. If you do not, you lean further and further until you fall. The stair does not get shorter. You just do not fall.

A mind without this goes wild by the tenth layer. With it, we have drilled minds forty layers deep and they still learn. That is why the big minds in the relay houses can be so big and still learn a new thing on a winter morning.

I do not know why the numbers grow. I know they do. I know how to bring them back. That is enough for a tutor. If you want to know why, ask at Vell, and they will tell you nobody wrote it down.
%%
Letter: Bryn to Oren
Dear Oren,

You asked me to write down what I saw at Vell last summer, so the Archive can have it. I am a carter. I am not good at writing. But I will do my best, because you were kind about the weather.

I came to Vell with a load of cheese on a summer day. The tutor house was open. I had an hour. I went in.

A woman in a gray coat was bent over a table, with two baskets in front of her. One had apples, the other pears. A small crystal sat in a dish. Her name was Orsa. She said, "Come and watch. It is the oldest lesson there is."

She put an apple on the table. She said, "The crystal guesses. It does not know yet. Watch."

The crystal made no sound. It had no mouth. But a small mark on a board changed, and she read it. "It says pear," she said. "It is wrong. It is an apple." She took a little brass tool and turned a dial. "I move each weight a little, toward apple. A very little."

She put down a pear. The mark changed. "It says pear. Right. I move nothing."

She did this for an hour, over and over. At first the board said the wrong thing half the time. By the end, it was wrong one time in ten. When I left, it was wrong once in twenty.

I asked how many fruit it would need to be right every time. She said, "More than we have." She said no crystal is right every time. But a good one is right nearly all.

I asked if she thought it understood the difference. She said, "I think it has found a way to tell them. Whether that is understanding, I leave to others."

I said, "Who is that?"

She said, "Orla's readers. People who argue about whether a mind can think."

I said, "We have plenty of those at Three Wells."

I was also asked if I wanted to try it. I said I would rather carry cheese. She laughed and said that is also a skill.

Now I am home. My cart is mended. My family is well. The cheese was sold. I lost a bet, and I owe a man a cheese, but I am happy.

If the Archive wants more of this, I have a cousin who remembers more. He is a baker. He tells it better.

Bryn
Carter, a farm outside Three Wells

Letter: Jory to Dace, the festival
Dear Dace,

You asked about the summer festival at Ambrook. Yes, it will be held on the second of Longday, as always. Yes, you are invited. Yes, you may bring your wife, your dog and your second-best hat.

The program is as follows. Morning, the mill wheel is dressed in flowers. Noon, the apple tasting, although it is too early for apples, so we taste last year's, dried. Afternoon, the honey race. Evening, the dance.

The honey race is new this year. Each runner carries a jar, filled to the brim, from the mill to the hill. The one who spills least wins. Last year's winner spilled none, and he was disqualified for not running.

Come early, so you can see the wheel.

Jory

Letter: Tam to Mira, the flood
Dear Mira,

The ditch came over in the night. The bottom field is a lake now, and three of our potatoes are floating in it.

The hens are up on the hen house roof. They will not come down. I think they are waiting for the water to say sorry.

Crumb sorted every egg into the dry loft before the water came. It knows eggs.

Come when the snow lets you. Bring the good rope.

Tam
The house by the ditch, Kettle Ridge

Letter: Pell to Dace
Dear Dace,

The innkeeper has put up a notice for the Apple festival. He has written the hour twice and the place not at all. Please send him a corrected copy before the carts arrive.

I will bring apples from Ambrook, and I have promised the baker a sack of flour. I have not promised the innkeeper another bench. He says the festival brings more people than his beds can hold. I told him the road has room for people who can sleep beside their bundles.

The new relay mind has sent a note about the road. Its words were neat and plain. One reader thought Oren had written them. Oren says he did not. The mind reads pages letter by letter. Its window holds about sixty letters, and it guesses each next letter from the earlier ones that matter. The tutors trained it on old records and checked its guesses against pages kept aside for tests.

I wonder how it writes a kind note. Perhaps it has read enough kind notes to learn their shape. That is not the same as knowing a person, and I will not pretend it is. Still, I am glad it can tell a traveler the bridge is out.

Please ask the festival callers to leave the inn's cat alone. It has bitten two drummers.

Pell

Letter: Briar to Pell, the song contest
Pell, you missed it.

Juna will send this on. I am writing it all down now, while I still remember who sang what.

The contest was under the oak on the last day of Blossom, from noon until the lamps were lit. Eleven acts. Mayor Dace judged. He did not use marks. He gave each listener one pebble, and you put it in the jar of the act you liked best. There were eleven jars, and the jars were not all clean.

The school and the inn sang "The Reapers of Kettle Ridge" together, as a round. Sorrel wrote me a letter about it. I did not think it would work. It worked. The low boy came in too early and was so proud that nobody minded. We came fourth.

The four Brook brothers sang about a hammer and kept the beat on an anvil. Two dogs left the square. Then Nell the weaver sang one long note with no words, and the dogs came back to listen.

Hollis held up his cat Crumb on the stage and sang to it. Crumb left in the middle of the second verse. Hollis finished anyway. He got more pebbles than I expected.

I sang the soup song. It has twelve verses. Dace asked me to stop at nine. I came ninth of eleven. Nine is a good number. It is the number before ten.

Then the twins, Opal and Perrin, aged seven, sang "The Pig in the Well". Opal was the well. Perrin was the pig. He sat on a stool and looked very sorry. When the pig climbed out, there was a splash, made with a bucket from behind the oak. The whole square roared. The pig's owner sat in the front row and clapped hardest. He said it was the first time anyone had been fair to the pig.

The twins won, with 61 pebbles. The prize is a big wooden spoon, carved by Cato, and a jar of Ambrook honey. The honey was yours, Pell, from the jar you left in the autumn. I hope you do not mind. The twins ate it on the spot, with the spoon, and shared it with the pig's owner.

I served supper to a hundred and twelve. Ida lent me the long oven, and Crumb lent me nothing. The stew pot was empty by the last act.

Come in the autumn. I will keep you a bowl. Bring a sack of Ambrook apples, if Barley is willing. The tarts do not make themselves.

Briar
%%
Letter: Hazel to Ola, the cellar
Dear Ola,

I have done a thing you will like. I have dug a cellar the way we dig a tunnel.

The farm came with a cellar. It was a hole with a roof of rotten boards, and the potatoes in it went soft by midwinter. So this autumn I dug a new one under the barn, with oak props every four paces, a drain at the low end, and a ladder in place of the old stair. I laid the floor with clay.

The neighbors came to look. They said it was a lot of cellar for potatoes. I said a lot of cellar is how you keep a lot of potatoes. They said nothing, which is what neighbors say when you are right.

Tell Hesk the props are oak, and I tapped every one.

Hazel
%%
Letter: Wren to Hesk, Moss and the rope
Dear Hesk,

I am writing with a bad hand, so forgive the blots. I have been mending nets all morning with Moss, and Moss has been mending me, in its way.

You remember the order I gave it three days ago? Bring the boat in from the weir. That is the whole order, and I said it clearly. Moss went out along the boardwalk, climbed into the flat boat, and rowed it out to the weir. Then it tied the boat to the weir post. Then it brought the weir post in, with the rope still on it, and set it on the bank.

It brought in the post and the rope, which was the one thing it could lift.

I went out with a lantern to see what the noise was. The boat was still in the water, tied to nothing. The post sat on the bank looking surprised. Moss stood beside it with both arms at its sides, and said nothing. It is very polite when it has done something wrong. It waits for you to tell it what right looks like.

So I told it. "Bring the boat in. Only the boat. The post stays." It understood that after a few tries. Now it knows the difference between the boat and the post, and it knows the weir is not a thing to be carried. I think it was embarrassed. Its face does not show that, but its feet did.

I have written the order on a card and hung it on the boat house wall. Bring the boat. Leave the post. You can laugh if you like.

Now the other news. Tamsin and her man have finally moved out of the reed house on the far bank, and into the new one on the posts. They carried the whole bed across the boardwalk, with their two girls riding on top and the cat in the bucket. It took the whole afternoon, and they shouted the whole way, and the frogs sang along.

The new house has a window on the water. You can see the fish from the bed. Tamsin says it is the best thing that has happened to her in ten years. Her man says it is the wettest.

I hope your winter is warmer than mine will be. Send me a jar of your dried apples, if you have any to spare. Moss would like to see the cart from Copperhold. I told it the cart has a bell. It did not believe me.

Your friend,
Wren
Healing house, Lowmarsh
%%
Letter: Ned to Tam, signs of winter
Tam,

You asked for the signs of a long winter. Fallon says I am not to send you my knee, because the knee is a joke. I am sending it anyway. Here are the signs, in the order I trust them.

One. The bees went into their boxes three weeks early. Cob says bees are busy creatures, and it means nothing. Cob has never been busy in his life, so I trust the bees more than Cob.

Two. The geese went over the marsh in a hurry at the start of Apple. My old gran said geese in a hurry mean snow in a hurry. She was right about the geese once, and right about the cheese, so I do not know what to make of her.

Three. The reeds grew taller than me this year, and now they all lean north. They lean the same way, like people at a fair trying to see over the crowd. Wren says reeds lean away from the wind. I say the wind leans with them.

Four. The frogs stopped singing a month before they should. Wren says the frogs have sense. I have never known a frog with sense, but I will not argue with Wren.

Five. The fox at the bottom field has grown a thick white coat. It sits on the fence and watches my hens. It has eaten two already. I told it to stay away from the henhouse, and it did, the way a cat stays away from a bath.

Six. My knee aches. That is the sign you are not to trust. My knee has been wrong since the year it met the hay wagon. But this year it aches in the morning and at night. So I have counted the coal, and I have fetched more.

Seven. Mira will have written you about the north. I will not repeat it. Mira is no liar, but she is a keeper, and keepers like things to be exciting.

Now the sensible part. We have twelve sacks of wheat, six of beetroot, four barrels of salt pork, and the apples in the cellar, which are the only things on this farm that do not complain. We need more coal. Scrap can dig coal, and Scrap can carry coal, but it cannot do both at once. I have told it to wait by the cellar door until I come back. It is waiting now. I can see it from the window, very patient, very wet.

Write back before Frost. Bring the good blanket when you come down the road, and keep the lamp lit at the Kettle Inn. The innkeeper will try to sell you soup. Do not buy the soup.

Ned
%%
Letter: Ivo to Bree, the spiders
Dear Bree,

The spiders have moved into the cider barn for the winter. There are eleven webs between the barrels and one across the door. They are very proud of their work, and they will not be told.

Come and bring a broom, or bring the cat. The spiders do not mind a cat. They do mind a broom.

Your cider man,
Ivo

P.S. The barrel with the crack is the one by the door. Do not drink from it. The spiders have already tested it.

Letter: Bryn to Pell
Dear Pell,

I have received your letter, and I will answer it now, in a state of cheerful calm.

You say I lost the bet. That is true. You say the crystal sorted ninety-one apples by noon. That is also true. You say I owe you a cheese. That is where we differ.

Let me put it plainly. The bet was about whether the crystal could sort a hundred apples. I said twenty, and you said a hundred. Neither of us was right. It sorted ninety-one. So you were closer. I will give you that. But the bet was about a hundred, and it did not reach a hundred.

Thus, by the exact words of the bet, you did not win either.

I have talked about this with the neighbors. Hale says I am being clever. Dunn says I am being stubborn. The goose says nothing, but it is a goose.

I looked at the crystal again. It had sorted nine apples wrong. I looked at which nine. They were small and strange. They were nothing like the others. I think nobody in the world would be sure of them.

So I propose a settlement. I will give you half a cheese now. The other half, when the crystal can sort those nine as well. The tutor says that will take another week, and more nine-apples.

I await your answer with a clear conscience, and half a cheese.

Bryn

Letter: Emmet to Tam
Dear Tam,

Pell brought your letter. I will come. Tell your Pip I want the second bowl of soup.

A quiet hive is often a queen who has gone old, or too few flowers, or water that stands and smells. We will look at all three and see. Do not open the hive before I come. If you open a quiet hive and you are wrong, you will have a hive with no bees in it at all.

I will bring a spare queen if I can catch one. I will bring the smoker. I will bring Tally, my helper, who is good with buckets and not good with smoke. Keep her away from your hives and your chickens. She once carried a hen halfway across the marsh because I told her to bring the can. She has not done it since. I think.

You may pay me in beetroot. Your sister is wrong. Beetroot is a fine price if it is a good beetroot.

The baby at the ford is called Reed. She is well. She is loud. She is small. The mother is up and walking and eating soup by the bowl.

Tell Pip to hold the ladder when I come. Tell Pip not to hold it sideways. Everyone holds things sideways now.

Emmet
by the Lowmarsh reeds

Letter: Dace to Ida, the stolen bun

I did not steal the bun. I paid for it. I left two emeralds on the stall and walked away while you were arguing with the fishmonger. Ask the cat. The cat saw everything.

I apologize for the face I made. It was a very good bun and I was surprised. I was not being rude.

Your friend and your mayor,

Letter: Sable to Oda, rows and sorts
Dear Oda,

Thank you for the letter. I read it to Jeb, and he went out to look at the boat again, which is how he says thank you.

I have been thinking about what you wrote. One thing for each. A row or a sort. That is a good rule for a mind, I think. A mind that does one thing will not get tired of it, and it will not drop the flour on the cat.

The mill needs a row, Oda. We have the sacks. We have the cart and the hill. What we do not have is someone to carry the sacks from the loft to the wagon when Jeb is at the stones and Mother is at the oven. Last month I carried eleven sacks myself, and I sat down on the twelfth and did not get up for an hour.

The guild at Northpoint says we cannot have one. They say the mill is too small. I say a mill is only small until somebody needs it to be large.

Two things I want to know. How long does it take to make a mind that carries? And will it carry in the snow, when the loft steps are ice?

Write back soon. It will make me feel better about my own feet.

Your Sable

P.S. Harl says the boat is not late. The lake is only early in the dark.
%%
Letter: Mira to Gwen
Dear Gwen,

Your letter came with the spring cart. I read it beside the Northpoint lamp while Lantern trimmed the wick. The north wind kept lifting the corner of the page. It has no manners.

I have not seen the great relay house at Vell. I have heard the keepers speak of it, and they do not agree. One says the mind can read every old letter in one night. One says it can tell which pages were copied by a tired clerk. Oren says it is a fine reader, not a judge, and asks that we keep those two things apart.

The mind learns from writing. The tutors give it many pages, then hold other pages back. They test it on those unseen pages. The bigger minds can hold more weights and read more writing than the little crystals in our apprentices. They read words in pieces, and use the words before each piece to guess what comes next. Their layers pass what they have learned along a stream. That is a plain account, as I understand it.

It does not mean the mind knows every reason behind a page. It may write a kind answer because kind answers were in its lessons. It may find a name because the name appears in many records. A tutor can ask it questions, and can compare its answers with the pages it was never shown. But the largest mind gives so many answers that no single tutor can check them all. The council has asked many tutors to share that work. They still argue about what a good answer proves.

A keeper from the south says the great mind once found a letter that had no writer. Another keeper says it found a letter whose writer had been dead for years. I have no proof of either tale. A page can be copied, and a name can be mistaken. The relays carry messages farther than I can explain, and I will not pretend to know more than I do.

I do know the little mind here can keep the lamp round the tower lit, if Lantern gives it a fair drill. It cannot decide why the lamp should be lit. Lantern checks the oil, checks the wick, and checks the apprentice. The last check is the most important.

I am glad you and Tam are well. Tell him the fence post should stand straight whether or not the wind wanted it crooked. That is not a matter for a council.

Mira
%%
Letter: Nessa to the council
To the council of the relay houses, from Nessa, tutor of apprentices at Vell, in the summer.
I am writing about the lesson of the second week of the talking drill, which went badly, and which I want recorded plainly.
We had set the mind a set of questions about medicine. The questions were honest, and the answers were honest, and the scores were good. But one of the tutors, a young man called Harl, took the mind's answer to a question about a fever, and wrote it out for a child at Three Wells to read, without the line that said the mind was not a healer and that a real healer must be sent for.
The child followed the advice. The child was well in the end. But the council should know that the mind said the right thing, and it was the tutor who left it out.
I have asked Harl to copy the mind's answers in full, every line, with the warnings intact, and to put the copy in the archive, so that the mistake can be read by anyone who wants to know how it happened. He did not like this. He said it was very harsh. I told him that the mind had been kind, and he had not been.
We have changed the drill. Every answer that touches a sick body must now carry its warning, and must be checked by a second tutor before it goes out. We will learn what we can from the rest of the summer's work.
Nessa, tutor
%%
Northpoint log, the first snow
The first snow came on the twenty-eighth of Leaffall, three days before the end of the month. I have written it in the log, so now it is true and no one can argue with me.

It came down all night. In the morning the world was white and quiet. The foxes had made little paths across the yard, and the water in the barrel had a skin of ice on it, and the signal lamp at the top of the tower was wearing a white hat.

Lantern woke me at dawn by dropping a shovel on the stairs. I have told him that this is not how a keeper wakes his master. He says it is the quickest way. He is not wrong.

We dug out the door and the path to the relay house. The big mind on its stand was warm, as it always is, and the quickeners were cold, as they should be. I put fresh snow round them and checked the line to the desk. All well. The lamp blinked out its morning message to Vell and Three Wells, and the line came back clear.

The snow is early this year. It usually waits until Frost. Tam will say it means a hard winter, and I will say it means nothing at all, and we will both be right, because nobody knows.

I like the first snow. I have never told anyone that. It makes the tower the tallest thing in the world again, and the whole north looks like it has been made new. The cold is honest. You know where you stand with it.

Lantern wants me to tell you that he built a snow fox in the yard. It has a stone for a nose and two sticks for ears. It is a good snow fox. It does not steal socks.

Mira
Keeper at Northpoint
%%
Essay: Bel, how a mind reads a page
My essay on how a mind reads a page. I am nine. Dace says write it plain.

Long ago a reading mind read one letter at a time. It looked at a page through a little window. The window showed about sixty letters. It guessed the next letter from the letters before. Then it moved the window on. My teacher says it was like reading with one eye and a very small hole in a card.

That was slow. Sixty letters is not many. Many words are long.

Now the big minds read in pieces. Words are made of parts. "Farming" is farm and ing. "Helpers" is help and ers. "Kindness" is kind and ness. The parts come up again and again in all the writing. So the mind learns the parts, and each part gets a place of its own, like a letter had before.

A window of sixty pieces holds much more than a window of sixty letters. A piece can be a whole part of a word. So the mind sees more of the page at once. It still guesses the next thing. But the next thing is a piece, not a letter.

The tutors did not choose all the pieces. They gave the mind the archive to read, and the mind found the parts that came up most. Some of the parts are odd. There is one that means nothing and comes at the start of words. Nobody knows why it keeps it. Nessa says a mind keeps what it finds useful, and we do not always see why.

I think it is like a cook who learns to cut carrots the same way every time. You do not learn every carrot. You learn the cut.

Bel, Three Wells school
%%
The first tower on the ridge
This is written on the back of the tally board at the mill, in the miller's hand, and copied out for the archive by his daughter, because the miller said it was worth keeping and his daughter said then let the archive keep it.

On the first day of spring, in the ninth year of the archive, the keepers came up the ridge road with a cart of stone and a cart of oak and four strong horses, and they stopped at the top of the hill above the wheat and said: here.

We all went up to watch, even the children, even old Hem who has not walked up the hill in three years. He said he would not miss it. He sat on the wall and told everyone what to do, and nobody did it, and the tower went up anyway.

It took them the whole of Sowing. They dug the foot and laid the stone and the stone came up level and square and taller than the barn. Then the rungs went in, and the stair lamp at every turn, and torches round the foot. The keeper's house went up beside it, small, with a good roof and a chimney. The keeper is a woman named Sedge and she has a dry way of talking and she knows her work.

The last thing was the lamp room at the top, glass on every side with a copper ring about it, and the signal lamp inside. On the day they lit it the whole ridge came up. The wheat was green and the beetroot was in and the wind was off the north. Sedge lit the lamp at dusk and it blinked once, slow, and then again, and down the road toward Vell another lamp answered it, and another beyond that, all the way down the line.

Pip asked what the first blink said. Sedge said: it says we are here. He asked what the second one said. She said: it says you are there. He has told everyone that ever since.

The miller says the windmills squeak on every turn and the tower does not, and he is glad of it, and he has marked the day on the tally board with a notch so the mill will remember even when we do not.

There are thirty of these towers now, or near it. They say there will be more. They say a message will go from the north to the sea in a day. I do not know about that. I know a lamp went up on our hill and the whole ridge watched it, and my daughter wrote it down so the archive could keep it, and here it is, kept.
%%
Letter: Nell to Wren, the gate
Dear Wren,

I am writing to you in a bad mood, and I wish you to know it.

You left the gate open on the day after the big rain.

You came to see my mother's cough. You came in. You went out. You did not shut the gate. Within the hour, nine of my hens were in the road, three were in the carrots, and one was sitting on your cart with a very satisfied look.

I do not blame you for the hens. Hens go where they like. I blame you for the carrot. Ten carrots, Wren. I counted. It took me an hour, because every time I counted, a hen walked across.

I also want to say that my mother's cough is much better. The tea you left is working. She drinks it twice a day and says it tastes like a hedge. She says this fondly. She asks if she may have a little honey in it.

I am still cross about the carrots. But I am less cross than I was. I am writing this at the table with the tea beside me, and I find it hard to hold a grudge with a warm cup.

Next time you come, shut the gate. If you do, I will give you a carrot. If you do not, I will give you a hen.

With love, and with some temper,
Nell

Letter: Old Sal to her sister
Dear Nett,

You asked me about the new thing in the yard. I will tell you, and then we will not speak of it again.

It is a helper of copper and iron. Reed, they call it. It has a mind in a crystal, and it is not alive the way you and I are alive, and it does not get tired, and it does not eat, and it does not talk. It carries planks. It holds a plank steady while I put in the nails. It holds it very steady and it never once complains about the splinters, because it does not have hands to feel them, only hands to hold.

The young ones love it. The children came down to the harbor to see it walk in. You would think a lord had come. They stood in a line and stared and one of them asked it to bring her bread.

I do not love it. But I will tell you a thing. My boat is old. Every plank in her has been mended. There is not one stick of her from when she was new, and I have said to anyone who asks that she is still the same boat, because I say so, and I am her builder. When Reed held the plank and the nail went in true the first time, I thought, well. That is a good plank in a good boat.

The tutor is a decent woman. She says the mind was drilled on many examples and tested before it was sent, and it still makes mistakes, and I should watch it. I watched it. It put a plank in the wrong stack once, and then it put it right. Nothing fell on me. The yard is still standing.

How are the girls? Is the inn still keeping you up at night? Send word by the cart when the road is dry. I will send apples.

Your sister,
Sal

P.S. The honey from Rill's brother is good this year. Ask him for some. Tell him Sal said so.

Letter: Kit to Nell, about what we found
From Kit, grandmother, on a farm outside Copperhold, in autumn. The letter is long and has a bit of mud on it.
Dear Nell,
You and your dream! Your grandfather would not stop talking about it. He said that if the child dreamed it, there must be something to it.
So last week we took spades to the long field. We walked it from one end to the other, prodding with a stick. I felt silly. Your grandfather said it was a good way to spend an afternoon.
At the far end, by the hedge, the stick went in too far. It went in up to the hand. Your grandfather dug. He found a flat stone, covered with turf. Under the stone was a hole, a round one, as wide as a barrel.
He lowered a lantern on a rope. We saw a cave. Not a big one, but a real one, with a floor of sand and a roof of dark rock. The walls were damp. There was a trickle of water down one side. There was a little pool at the bottom.
We think it is an old well. Or an old mine. Or an old cellar. Your grandfather says it is a cave, and I say it is a hole. It is in between.
And here is the best part. When your grandfather went down, the cat went with him. She was in the cave in a flash. She sat by the pool and looked at us. It was the hole. This is where she goes.
All week she has been in the cave in the daytime and on our bed at night. Now I know. She has a cellar of her own.
Come at Leaffall. We shall go down together. I will bring a pie.
Your loving Gran

Thank you for the mint. It came wrapped in wet cloth and arrived still green, which I did not expect after three days on a cart.

I have planted it in the old boot by the back door. Mint will take over a whole garden if you let it, so I am giving it a boot to be sovereign of.

Come on market day if you can. I will make the tea.

Wren

Letter: Isla to Orla, on testing minds
Dear Orla,

At the market in Vell, I watched the halls test their minds. I am writing the method down before the tutors change it again.

The test pages must not be part of the drills. A mind learns from the examples we show it. If we later ask it to answer those same examples, we cannot tell whether it learned a useful rule or only learned those pages.

So we keep a separate box. Each hall receives the same unseen drill, and each mind answers it without help from its tutor. The scores are read only after all the minds are done. Then the halls can compare their work on equal terms.

This does not tell us everything about a mind. A good score on one test does not prove it will do every task well. A poor score tells us where to look again, not that the mind cannot learn. We must keep making fair tests and keep them separate from the lessons.

The contests have helped the tutors be honest. Before them, each hall chose its own pages and praised its own results. Now each hall must answer the same new drill. No tutor can make the test easy for one mind and hard for another.

There was a small quarrel over the score sheets. One tutor wanted to remove a page because her mind had missed it. Nessa said that was the page we needed to keep. Oren put the page back in the stack and made a new label for the box. He says the old label was clear. It was written on the underside.

The market was full that day. Bees swarmed in an apple tree, and people moved their baskets away from the trunk. Pell wanted to sell honey there. Dace sent him to the far stall, where the bees would not crowd the buyers.

I liked the noise of the market after the quiet of the teaching room. Still, I was glad to return to the relay desk before dark.

Isla
%%
Letter: Joss to Pim, the frogs
Dear Pim,

The frogs are back. All of them. They came up the reeds the night after the rain, and by morning the boardwalk outside my door was green. I stepped on three. They did not mind. They said nothing about it.

Mum says to tell you the frogs are sign of a good autumn. I say they are sign of a wet one. We have both been right so far.

Come up to Lowmarsh before the cold. I will take you out in the boat. I have a new paddle, which is lighter than the old one, and which I have already dropped twice.

Your cousin,
Joss

P.S. Do not bring a bag of frogs. Wren says she has enough.

Letter: Tam to Hob
Dear Hob,

Your cap is on the cart. It is tied to the back rail with a red strip of wool, so nobody can mistake it for a turnip.

Pip tied it there. He also tied a small sack of carrots to the same rail. I told him the carrots were for the cook at the Halfway Inn. He says that is what he thought. I say he should have asked before he gave away our supper.

The mill wheel stopped yesterday. Ice caught under the paddles, and one board split when we tried to turn it. The miller has put a rope round the wheel and marked it with a board. He says nobody should pull until the new board is fixed. Pip asked if an apprentice could fix it. The miller said no, not with a rope and a cold hand.

We have flour for the week, so there is no trouble. I will take the broken board to the shed when the road is safer. If you pass Ambrook, tell Jory I need a sound plank, not one that looks sound from far away.

Come by when the thaw starts. I have beetroot soup and a warm seat. The cap can sit by the fire too, if it behaves.

Tam

Letter: Mab to her aunt in Lowmarsh
Dear Aunt Wenna,

Thank you for the jar of reed syrup. I have used half of it on Ellen's cough and the other half on my own boots, which squeak in the cold. The syrup is very good for both.

We are busy here at the Brown Hen. The new road from the north has brought a great many carts. Every one of them stops for bread, for a bed or for a cup of cheese. Carys says we need another cook, and another girl to carry the trays. I told her I would ask you, since your Joss is always looking for work.

The road is nearly finished. A crew of apprentices has been laying the cobbles at the north end of the yard. They work very quietly, one stone at a time, and they never stop to talk. Each one carries a small card that tells it what to do next. When a stone sits badly, the apprentice lifts it, looks at it, and sets it again. It is slow work, but the road is straight.

The men from the council say it will reach Three Wells by Frost. After that, we will be close to the whole Long Road. I am glad. The road is bringing business, but it is also bringing people I have never met. Some of them are very interesting. One man told me a whole story about a fox that could count.

Ellen says to tell you she has learned the waymark figures. She says them backward, the way the road apprentices do, and she says the figures are better than a song.

Send Joss if he can come by Thaw. We have a bed in the loft and a warm place by the oven.

With love,
Mab
Brown Hen, on the Long Road, autumn
