Letter: Mira to Tam
Dear Tam,

Your ewe is at Northpoint.

She came up the road three days ago with ice on her back and stood by the relay house and would not move. Lantern gave her beet tops and she has not left since. She sleeps against the warm stone of the foot. I think she has decided the tower is hers now.

I will keep her until the road is clear, then send her down with a drover. Do not worry about her. She is eating better than I am.

You asked about my boots. My boots are fine. The left one leaks at the toe and I have put wax on it and it still leaks and that is the whole of my news about boots.

The keeper at the little tower between Vell and Three Wells came up last month to learn the lamp. He is young and quick and he talks all the time. He asked what I do in the long nights and I said I keep the lamp and he looked at me as if there were more to it. There is not.

The rungs are cold this time of year. Ninety of them. I count them every morning, because a keeper who stops counting is a keeper who has stopped looking. That is what I tell Lantern. He says he counts them too. I think he says that to please me. I do not mind.

Tell Pip the fence is two posts over on your side and you know it. He wrote to me about it. He wrote a very careful letter. You should hear him out and then stand by the stone with him and stop laughing.

I am glad the frogs are back. Send me some spring. The north is still white.

Your sister,
Mira

It has not been lit for three nights, and Dunn had to admit it, and then he paid me, very gracefully, and then he asked me to make him a poultice for his foot.

Your cousin, Jem

Letter: Pell to Ambrook, about a debt
Dear Mother,

I am at the market at Vell, under the wall, with the cart turned sideways so the wind does not take the ledger. I have sold the last of the honey and most of the wool, and I have paid a debt I owed since Sowing.

You will laugh. You will laugh and then you will be cross. The debt was to a girl called Piper. She is about nine, she lives near the archive gate, and in Sowing I bought a pie from her at the fair and found I had no small coins. I said: I will pay you at the autumn market. She said: all right. She said it the way a clerk says it, as if it were already written down somewhere.

So I have paid her. She came to the cart today with a basket and a very stern face. I gave her two emeralds. She looked at them. She said: that is too much for a pie. I said: it is a pie and six months of waiting. She thought about it. Then she said: all right, and put them in her pocket, and then she asked me if I had seen the yellow apples.

I have. The Ambrook carts brought them, and everyone at the market is arguing about them. Half the folk say they are the best apple ever grown and the other half say they are too sweet and a proper apple should make your mouth go tight. Piper says the yellow ones are for children and the green ones are for people with something to be cross about.

The honey went well. I got a good price from the inn of twelve beds, and a better one from a woman with a stall of jars who says her sister is a healer at Lowmarsh and needs good honey for the winter. I did not ask what for.

I will be home before Apple. Keep the fire in and do not let the mill wheel run all night, whatever Jory says. Tell Father the boot he lent me has a hole in the heel and I will bring him a new one.

Your son,
Pell
%%
Letter: Hob to Pell, after the storm
Pell,

You will have heard about the storm. Everyone has heard about it. The Brown Hen has been telling it for three nights, and every time it gets a little bigger. By the last telling the wind had taken the roof off the mill at Ambrook and carried it all the way to the lake.

Here is what really happened. The wind came up the marsh at dusk and pushed the water up the boardwalks. Two planks went into the reeds by the fish weir, and one post went over into the mud. The boat house on the east side lost its roof, but the tally board on the wall stayed where it was. Nobody understands that.

Wren and her people spent the night at the healing house with the buckets. Wren says the jars on the high shelves are all still there, every label in place. She says this proves the wind has no respect for good handwriting.

We went out at first light with rope and a cart. Twelve boats had gone adrift. Nine came back on their own. Three we brought in with the cart, one at a time, and one of them had a goose in it. The goose has not left the boat. We have decided it owns the boat now.

Send me the new planks when the cart comes. Oak if you have it, and not the soft birch from the ridge. The birch sinks.

Hob
Fishing house, Lowmarsh
%%
Letter: Ada to Noll
Dear Noll,

Bring the board, but do not carry it in the rain. Wrap it in your coat, or the paint will lift.

I have set the broken relay lamp on the shelf by the door. Its glass is cracked in two places. I thought to mend it with a pane from the hen house, but the pane is too wide. The keeper says the lamp should wait for the relay house. The keeper has not come yet.

The lamp does not tell us what is wrong with the minds. I know that. Still, when I see it dark, I think of all the little lessons that have stopped. The tutor yard is quiet. The apprentices in the mill store only carry sacks from one bin to another. They do that well, but nobody asks them to learn more.

Lysa came to supper last night. She explained Kester's note with beans on the table. She put one bean by the bowl and one by the cup. Then she said the old crystal could be taught to choose the bowl or the cup, but not to choose the bowl when the bean was round and the cup when it was flat, or the other way around. I think I have the example right. I may have moved a bean.

She said the fault was in that kind of crystal, not in the beans, and not in every mind that might ever be made. Her words were plain. The council's words were not.

The children wanted her to teach them a drill. She showed them how to copy a row of marks, then how to check the row against the board. They were pleased when they found a mistake. They were more pleased when they found the tutor's mistake.

Come home when the road is firm. I have kept your stool by the window, though it still rocks.

Ada
%%
Letter: Ash to Bel
Dear Bel,

You asked how I make a map when the paths keep changing. I draw what is there on the day I walk it. Then I return and change the map when the path changes.

The farm outside Lowmarsh has a new ditch gate. The farmer made it from oak, with a handle wide enough for a mitten. He says it will keep the field water in place. I drew the gate on the map. He says I drew it too small. I told him that is because it is a small gate.

I also drew the three beds, the reed shed and the place where the boardwalk ends in mud. The farmer has put a plank there. It is not a fine plank, but it keeps boots dry. Moss says the plank will be gone by next Swarm. The farmer says Moss is welcome to come and carry it away.

The lesson went badly yesterday. I was teaching two children to mark a path from the well to the road. I told them to look for things that do not move. One drew the scarecrow. The other drew a frog on a post. The frog jumped before the ink was dry, and the scarecrow fell down when the windmill turned.

We started again with the well and the stones. Those stayed still long enough.

The farmer gave us beetroot soup at midday. He put too much salt in it, but he blamed the pot. I have copied the map cleanly now. One copy is for the farm, and one is for the healing house.

Your friend,
Ash
%%
Letter: Maud to Tam, about the ditch
Dear Tam,

I am writing because you will not come across the field to say it, and because the ditch is mine as much as yours.

Your ditch runs down the middle of the long field, as it should. It keeps four rows wet on each side. Last week it kept four rows wet on my side as well. My beetroot is up to its ears in water, and so is my bean row, and so, I am sorry to report, is the goat.

The beans were the first thing I ever grew that nobody told me how to grow. I planted them the old way, from my mother, with a stone in each hole for luck. They came up very tall and very green. Now they are tall and green and wet, and I am not sure luck covers this.

I know the rule. One ditch for two fields. You dug it, so you say the water is yours to let out. But the sluice is on my side of the hedge, and I am the one who walks down to look at it each morning in my nightcap, with the lamp, and the frogs watching me.

The goat has been moved to the top field, where she says she prefers the view and the company of the hens. She has not forgiven either of us. She has told the hens so, at length.

The bees have swarmed, in the month of Swarm, which they say is a coincidence. They went up over the hedge in a brown cloud, and settled in the old oak at the bottom of your field. Ama came up the ridge to see. She says it is a good swarm, and that it will be yours to keep, because they chose your tree and not mine. I told her that was no comfort at all.

So here is what I propose. You come and look at the sluice, in your clean boots and not the muddy ones. I make you a cheese. You say the cheese is too salty, and eat the whole of it. Then we decide where the water goes, and whose the bees are.

P.S. I am not cross. The hedge on the north side is very good, and you may say so to Hal, who is not listening to me anymore.

P.P.S. The new apprentice has dug a little channel along the bottom of the field, without being asked. The water goes to the beetroot now, and not to the goat. Sprig is a better neighbor than either of us. I told it so. It looked at me as if I had said the sky was blue, which it is.

P.P.P.S. Please bring the seed of the big red beetroot when you come. You promised it at the fair, and I have not forgotten, even if the goat has.

Maud
%%
Letter: Kester to Opal, the debt
Dear Opal,

I am writing to say that I have paid what I owe, and to thank you for the winter.

You lent me twelve emeralds in Longnight, for coal for the hall when the council would not buy it. The hall is shut now, and the coal is burned, and the emeralds were spent on a good cause. I said I would repay you by Thaw, and I do not like to be late.

Here are the twelve, in this packet, counted twice. Do not tell me you do not need them. A debt left unpaid is a stone in the shoe, and I have walked far enough on stones.

I earned them copying for the Archive. The clerks pay by the page, and I am slow, but I am careful, and they have given me the old records to copy, the ones nobody will read for a hundred years. It is quiet work. I sit at the long table in the copying room with ink on my sleeve and a lamp at my elbow. I find that I think better with a pen in my hand. A page of other people's letters is a restful thing.

I will tell you what I have been thinking, in case it is of use. You remember my four dots, and the string that cannot divide them. I have been drawing them again. Suppose you do not use one string. Suppose you use two crystals, each drawing its own string, and then a third crystal that looks at what the first two said. The first two could each cut off a corner. The third could say yes to what lies between. I do not know if it would work. I do not know how you would drill them. The crystals do not know their share of any mistake, and I cannot see how to tell them.

It is only a thought. I have no hall, no pay and no apprentice to try it on. I have a table, and a pen, and a lamp. That is enough for a thought.

If you have a quiet hour, think about it too. Midwives know that some things come in twos.

With thanks, and twelve emeralds,
Kester
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira
Dear Mira,

Something odd. The bottom field, the one with the potatoes and carrots, has a hole in it now. A cave under the field, wide as the barn, with a stream running through it. The plough went through the roof of it on the ninth of Leaffall. No one was hurt. The horse was upset.

I went down with a torch. It is dry and cold, and there are old marks on the walls, a row of them, like someone counted something a long time ago. I do not know what they counted. No one here does.

Pip wanted to take the small mind down to look. I said no. Then I said yes, and it went down and came back and stood in the yard and did nothing at all. So much for that.

We will fill the hole and plough around the cave. The harvest is in. The beetroot did well. The windmill still squeaks. First snow was early, as I wrote, and there is more coming.

Did your letters go to Vell? Oren wrote that the box came. He says the drills go well and the reading minds are hungry for pages. A field of letters, Nessa calls it. I think of your weather in there, and the foxes, and the long nights, and I am glad the box went.

Wear two coats.

Tam
%%
Letter: Opal to Carys, the long winter
Dear Carys,

The winter is long. There has been snow on the hill since Frost, and it is only Longnight. I have delivered seven babies in nine days, four of them in the snow and one on a table at the inn. All are well. The tutors' hall is shut. Kester sits at home and draws lamps. I send you a pair of socks and a loaf. Eat the loaf first.

Your Opal

Letter: Pell to the keeper at Northpoint
To the keeper at Northpoint, if Mira is not there, and to Mira if she is.
I hope this reaches you. I am writing a second time because the first did not get an answer, and I do not know if that is the post or the north.
I am at the Halfway Inn. I have a cart of salt and a cart of honey and a donkey who dislikes both. I will be at Three Wells at the end of the month, and at Vell after that. If you have letters for the south, I will carry them. I am glad to. It is the least I can do.
I have heard a story at the inn. A traveler said the lights over Northpoint were seen from the ridge. Another said it was a fire in a barn. Another said there were no lights at all. They argued till the stew was cold.
I do not know which is true. I would like to ask you, since you live under it.
There are spiders in our barn. They have been busy. I think they know something about the weather, or the winter, or the lights. I will let you know.
Write if you can. A line will do. If your letter goes astray, I will not mind. I will only be a little more worried.
Your friend on the road,
Pell, merchant
%%
Letter: Greer to Pell, the last cart
Dear Pell,

I have your letter about the north road, and I have read it three times. That is two more than you deserve for the handwriting.

You ask whether the carts still go through Three Wells to Northpoint, and I must say I do not know. The last cart I met on the road was at the Brown Hen, and its driver would not tell me his load. He said coal. Then he said salt. Then he said it was none of my business, which is true, and he drove off fast. I have not seen a cart go north since.

The relay is quieter than it was. Ambrook sends messages to Vell and back, as usual. Copperhold sends the count. Lowmarsh sends frogs, which is an old joke between the towers, and nobody laughs at it now. But the north line is slow. Some days Northpoint does not answer the first call, and the keeper at Ridge Hill has to send it again, and again, until it comes through. Then the answer is short. Seen. All well. Keep the lamps lit.

People ask about the lights. You will have heard. Over the north ice, low, sometimes green. The Saltreach fishers say they are falling stars, and they point at the sky as if to prove it. The children at Three Wells say they are fires, with people sitting round them far away, and waving. Mira says only that they are not ours. Mira does not use many words, and when she uses only three, I believe her a little more than I would like.

Here in the market the talk is of the ships. Corin has three big hulls in the yard now, and the men who work them do not talk about the sea while the sun is up. Some families have packed their carts and gone down the coast road. Some have not. They do not say where they are going, and I do not ask. It is not a thing you ask a person at a market stall, with the honey weighed out and the bread wrapped.

And the Deep Works. The miners say the stone is warm far down, warmer than it should be. They say the ninth gate is locked, with its key kept at the mine hall, and that nobody goes below the eighth without a reason. I asked Hesk. He said the stone was warm because the mine was deep. Then he said he did not believe it, and went back to his pick.

So that is the market. Honey is cheap, bread is dear, and the rain has been kind. I still keep the cart by the door, Pell, with its wheels greased and two sacks of flour in it. You know why. I do not know why. Both are true.

Send me a letter when the road opens, and tell me what you saw. Tell me what the Brown Hen's soup is like this year. Tell me anything at all about something ordinary.

Greer
%%
Letter: Sage to the Archive
To the clerks of the Archive at Vell, from Sage, stable hand, at the Halfway Inn.
Please forgive a stable hand for writing to the Archive. I was told it is allowed, and that a clerk will read it.
A cart came through from Ambrook with a load of old paper, damp at the edges. The carter said the paper was bound for Vell, for the drill box, and that the clerks would be glad of it, because the spoiled pages are the most useful for a test.
I did not understand how a spoiled page could be useful. I asked the carter. He said that a page with a smudge is one that a mind has to work to read, and that a mind which can read the smudge can read anything. I asked him whether that was true. He said he did not know, but that his master at Vell had told him so.
So I am asking the Archive. Is it true that the spoiled pages are the best test? And if so, why are the clean ones kept at all?
I have enclosed a sixpence of emeralds for the postage, and a sprig of heather, for the clerk who reads this. I am sorry for the heather. It was all I had.
Sage, at the stable door
%%
Letter: Wick to Ola, the new loom
Dear Ola,

I have a new loom. It came up from Vell on Pell's cart, in four pieces, with a note from the clerk saying the pieces were to be kept in order. I have kept them in order. I have not yet assembled them, because I am frightened of the frame.

You said you would come and help me, and I know you will. But you must come early, before the light goes, because the frame has forty pegs and the pegs are not the same length. The clerk says they are the same length. I have measured them twice. They are not.

The loom is for the blue cloth. Everyone in Lowmarsh wants blue cloth now, since the blue dye from the Brown Hen came in. I want to weave something warmer, with a grey stripe down the middle, for the healing house, where the beds are cold in the morning. Wren says the grey will remind the sick of the boats, and the boats will make them want to get well. I told her that was a lot to ask of a stripe.

I have also started a new thing. I have been weaving the reeds in with the wool, very thin, so the cloth will keep out the marsh damp. It smells of the water, and it looks like a field. Wren says it is a hopeless idea. She has already asked me for a bolt of it.

Come when you can. Bring the pegs, if you find any spares. And bring a little bread. The loom eats a great deal of time, and nothing else.

Wick
%%
Letter: Wynn to Pell, about the cheese
Dear Pell,

The cart came in on the third of Longday, two days late, and I know why. The wheel. You told me the wheel was fine. The wheel was not fine. The cheese arrived, though. All of it. That is the main thing.

Here is what I sent and what you should get for it.

Twelve rounds of the pressed cheese, the kind you like. Six rounds of the soft one with the rind. Four small pots of the honey cheese, wrapped in cloth. That is the lot.

The pressed cheese will keep a month if you keep it cool. Do not stack it by the oven. I know you. You stack things by the oven.

The soft one wants eating first. Sell it first. If it is not sold in a week, eat it yourself and pay me later. I would rather you ate it than it went to waste.

The honey cheese is the last I have this summer. The bees have been busy but the flowers by the mill pond came late. Next summer I will make more. Tell folk who ask.

Now the prices. I am putting them up a little. Not much. The salt went up, and the salt is half of what makes it keep. Two emeralds a round for the pressed, one for the soft, three for the honey pots. I hope that is fair. Tell me if it is not.

You owe me eleven emeralds from the spring cart. I have not forgotten. I am not cross. I am just saying.

The inn was full all summer. Travelers going south for the fair, and a family from Saltreach with three children and a goat. The goat slept in the stable and ate a straw hat. Not mine. The innkeeper's. He was very calm about it. He said it was the second hat this month.

How is your mother? You said she was poorly in the spring. I hope she is better. Give her my love.

Send the money with the next cart. And fix the wheel.

Your friend,
Wynn
Cheesemaker, by the Long Road
%%
Letter: Gil to Ruth, the weir in autumn
Dear Ruth,

The weir is up. The eels came in good this year, so good that the boys on the boat house steps have a new song about them. I will not write the song. Your grandmother would not allow it on the paper.

You asked in your last letter how we know when the eels are ready. I will tell you, though you know it already. You look at the water. When the reeds turn gold, and the evening mist sits on the shallows until midday, the eels are close to the top. Then you lower the traps at dusk and lift them at dawn. Do not lift them too early. They fight the basket.

Your uncle Cob was always in a hurry with the traps. He used to pull them up before the dawn, when the eels were still sleeping in the mud. The basket would come up half full of mud and half full of grumbling. He grew out of it, the way boys do.

We have a new boat house on the north side, with a proper slip and two hooks for the lanterns. It is the one with the tally on the wall. Every boat that goes out is marked on it with a cut stick. Every boat that comes in is crossed off. A boat with no cross at dusk is a boat we go looking for. That has happened twice in my lifetime, and both times it was the wind that did it, not the water. The water in Lowmarsh is slow. It does not want to drown anybody, only to keep them.

Wren's healing house has had a busy month. Three children with the sniffles, one man with a bad foot from the weir post, and a dog who ate a jar of reed syrup and was very happy about it. Wren says the dog is fine. The dog says so too, in its way.

The frogs are loud at night, and they will be louder in spring. Keep your window shut. Keep your boots by the door. And come to us at Longnight, if the road is open. There will be eels in the pot and a fire in the hall, and I will tell the story about the boat that went round the weir twice, which you have always asked me to tell.

Your loving grandfather,
Gil

P.S. The new boat house has a window on the water. You can see the eels from the table. I told your grandmother that is why we need the window. She said that is not why.
%%
Letter: Nessa to Wren
Dear Wren,

I received your letter about the soup. I read it twice. The second time I laughed, and then I was sorry I had laughed, because the soup was not a joke to you.

You say the children ate it all and asked for more, and that the grey mush on the bottom of the pot was the only part you could not understand. I will tell you what I think it was.

The barley went in without being soaked. Then the beans went in with it. Then, I think, the bowl of old flour went in as well, the bowl you keep by the door for thickening, which was wet from the rain. Then the honey, which you add at the end, went in at the start. All of that cooked for an afternoon, with the lid on, and nobody stirred it.

It is not a poison. It is a pudding that has forgotten what it was supposed to be. It will not hurt the children. It will hurt your pride, and I am sorry for it.

Next time, soak the barley overnight. Keep the flour in a dry jar. Add the honey last. And stir the pot every quarter hour, the same way round.

Your children will remember the pudding. Let them. The best recipes in the Archive are the ones that went wrong once, and were kept anyway.

Nessa
Tutor's house, Vell
%%
Letter: Edda to Sella, market day
Dear Sella,

We reached Three Wells today. It was market day, and every stall had a crowd around it. Mother bought carrots, and I bought a small honey cake with one emerald. The baker said it was a high price. I said it was a large cake. It was not.

A crew of four apprentices was working by the east well. They were building a low wall beside the school garden. They carried cobblestone, set each stone, and checked the pattern against a board.

Their order cards were tied together with string. I saw one card fall into the mud. The nearest apprentice stopped, picked it up, and put it back in order. Then the crew went on.

A woman at the stall said the big mind had planned the whole wall. A mason beside her said the apprentices planned the work after they saw the place. They asked the big mind when they found a problem with the ground.

I asked which one was right. They both said they were.

The wall is straight so far. I hope it stays straight when the rain comes.

Your friend,
Edda
%%
Letter: Ree to Pim, the fair prize
Dear Pim,

The summer fair at Copperhold is on the first fine day of Longday, and the prize for the best bread has come to me again. The rules say a trader cannot win twice in a row without a rest, so the board has given me a blue ribbon and a new knife instead. I have given the knife to my sister. The ribbon has gone to the mayor's dog. He wears it at every stall, and looks as if he has won a war.

Come up for the fair. Bring the good scale, and leave the bad jokes at home.

Your friend,
Ree
%%
Letter: Jory to his cousin in Three
Cousin,

You asked what the market was like. It was the best in years. The weather held and the pond was low, so the ducks were cross and loud, which the children enjoyed.

Rowan came with a cart of honey and pots and a lame horse called Barley who is not lame any more. Barley stood by the fence all day and let the children pat him. He ate one of the straw hats off a stall. Rowan paid for it and said it was the cheapest thing Barley ever ate.

There was a moment when a trader told his apprentice to "fetch the small apples from the cart" and the apprentice brought him four apples that were small. Just four. The trader wanted a crate. Everyone laughed and the trader laughed too, and then he said it again more carefully, all of it, and the apprentice brought the crate. It is a new one from Vell, he said. It is learning.

Nim's mother sold every loaf she had by noon and then went home and baked more.

The chair woman mended nine chairs. She says it is a good year for chairs.

Your cousin,
Jory
%%
Letter: Oren to Yarrow, the market roof
Dear Yarrow,

I write to say thank you, and to tell you what you did, because I do not think you saw all of it from the top of the ladder.

For three summers the roof of the market house at Vell has leaked. It leaked on the paper stall, and the paper seller kept his reams in a barrel with a lid. It leaked on the ink stall, so that the blue went greener than it should. It leaked on the honey stall, where Pell stood for a whole market with a sack over his hat and said nothing, which is how I knew it was serious.

You came on the sixth of Longday. You went up with a rope, a bucket of tar, and a bundle of reeds from Lowmarsh. You were up there for four days. Nobody saw you come down. They only knew you were there because of the singing.

The first market after you finished was the driest in three years. It rained, by the way. It rained at noon. We all stood under the new roof and listened to it, and it sounded like applause. Hob the paper seller took his reams out of the barrel and laid them on the table, and looked at them, and wiped his eye. He says it was the tar.

I am told you asked for a pie. The Archive kitchen has made you one. It is not about a pig. It will travel with the carter on the tenth.

May I ask a favor? The clerks' house roof is not as good as it was. There is a place over the stairs where the rain comes in as a thin line, and in winter it freezes into a sort of rope, and Cob has walked into it twice. I would like you to look at it, if you are passing. I will pay in emeralds, or pie, or both.

With thanks from the market, and from the paper,
Oren
Clerk at the Archive at Vell
%%
Letter: Ada to Noll
Dear Noll,

I am glad your stool sits flat. Tell the keeper that a straight stool may bring custom too. I will not promise to eat his stew again until he keeps the salt jar beside the pot.

Lysa visited us at the tutor house today. She brought a small crystal in a cloth wrap. It was old and cloudy at one edge. She did not say why the edge had gone cloudy. She said no one knows why an old crystal changes.

She laid out two rows of cards. One row showed a round bean and a square cup. The other showed a square bean and a round cup. Then she asked us to mark the answer that fit each card. The old sorting crystal could learn to sort one kind of card from the other. It could not learn the rule "one or the other, but not both." That was Kester's point.

Lysa said this does not make the crystal useless. It can still learn a row, a sort, or a single answer. It means only that this one kind of crystal has a limit. The next tutors must not promise more than it can do.

I asked why the council did not give the tutors time to try another way. She said councils are made of people, and people can become tired of waiting. She said Kester's note was clear, and the council wanted a clear end to the argument. That is what they got.

I have kept the drill boards dry. The children painted one new board on a piece of spare oak. It shows a field with a ditch down the middle and torches at the edges. They want to teach a small apprentice to follow the rows. Lysa says we must make many examples and keep some aside for a fair test.

The relay lamp is still on my shelf. Its glass catches the light from the window, but it cannot send a message. I hope the keeper comes before frost.

Your sister,
Ada
%%
Letter: Edda to Hild, after the market
Dear Hild,
You missed it. I am writing at once so you may be sorry.
The market was the best in years. There were forty stalls, a man who juggled eggs and dropped only two, and a woman who sold nothing but string. She sold out by noon. I do not know what people did with it.
The wool stall did very well. I sold eleven fleeces, seven balls of yarn and a pair of mittens I made from the leftovers. The mittens were for a tall man with a beard who said he lived in the north and needed something thick. I charged him four emeralds. He said it was too much. I said the north was cold. He paid.
Garn ate three bowls of stew and told me about the Deep Works. He says the miners found a new vein of copper, deep and green, and that Hesk is beside himself. I asked what Hesk does when he is beside himself. Garn said he hums.
The children found a mouse and put it in a hat. The hat was the mayor's. The mayor did not mind, but his wife did.
At the end of the day, I sat down at my stall and counted my emeralds. There were sixty-one. I put twelve in the tin for the roof, as I do every market. The tin is almost full.
Come soon. I will not tell you what I bought.
Edda
%%
Letter: Yew to Sorrel, the lantern prize
Dear Sorrel,

We won the lantern prize at the spring festival at Copperhold! The school lantern, the one with the glass panes the children painted, was judged the best on the hill. I held it up in the square while the mayor read the names. My arms are still shaking.

The children were beside themselves. Little Ada cried, then laughed, then asked if the lantern could come to school every day. I said it could come on fine days.

Hesk came down from the mine gate to see, with his cap in his hand and soot on his nose. He said the lantern was too bright for a miners' fair. He said it in the most pleased way.

The prize is a jar of honey from Ambrook and a blue ribbon. The ribbon is on the schoolroom wall, next to the map of the Long Road.

Come up in Melon, when the road is dry. Bring your spoon.

Yew
%%
Letter: Oren to Nessa, the sealed box
Nessa,

I have been going through the deep halls, as the order says. Write everything down. So I write this down too.

In the deepest room, in the oak box, is the oldest record. It is a list of names and sums from the first years. I did not open the box. It is already written. I only put a card on it.

I found your primer page among the shelf copies, and I filed it. It is plain and true, and I am glad it is in the archive. I read the part about the pieces of words to a clerk who is new, and she understood it, which is more than I did at her age.

I do not know whether the readers of the far years will want our pages. I hope they do. We will keep everything we write, as the first keepers cut over the door. That is all I can do.

Tell Bel at Three Wells that her letter about the school door arrived and is filed. She asked. I am telling her by you.

Oren
Vell
%%
Letter: Pell to Jory
Dear Jory,

I am at the Brown Hen, where the rain has found a hole in my left sleeve. The innkeeper says the roof has had that hole since before he was born. I told him that is no excuse. He gave me a bowl of apple stew to stop me talking.

I have three jars of honey for Ambrook and two sacks of wool for the school. The cart is sound. The left wheel squeaks, but only when it is empty. Perhaps it likes a full load.

At Three Wells, folk were talking about the new reading mind at Vell. They say it reads a page one letter at a time, with room for about sixty letters in its window. Each new letter is guessed from the letters before it. I do not know how a crystal keeps so many letters in mind. I only know the big mind at the relay sent my order back without changing a word.

I asked it to send apples to the inn. It sent the message to you instead. The apples are still here.

Your friend,
Pell

The tutor's drill box
This is a page from a primer, kept at the Archive at Vell. Teachers used it in the school at Three Wells. It explains the rule of growth, found by Isla, a tutor at Vell, about the 195th year of the archive.

A mind is kept in a crystal. A small mind sits in a small crystal. A big mind sits in a big crystal, the size of a loaf.

Isla found a rule. She drilled many minds, small ones and big ones, on many pages and on few. She wrote down how well each mind did. Then she drew a curve through the marks.

The curve says this. A mind drilled on more writing grows better. A mind with a bigger crystal grows better still. And you can draw the curve before the mind is grown. You do not have to guess. You can look at your crystal and your pages, and the curve tells you what you will get.

The curve bends. Twice the pages do not give twice the learning. The first pages help most. Later pages help less. A bigger crystal helps, but it also bends. So a tutor does not need an endless crystal or endless pages. She needs enough of both.

Here is what the children say back. Why does the curve bend? Nobody wrote it down. Isla could draw it, and every tutor after her drew it, and it came out the same. Why it bends, the books leave open.

Here is what Isla said. A curve you can draw before the mind is grown is a promise. Keep it, and people will trust you with big crystals.

The councils read Isla's note. They counted their coins. Then they began to build relay houses, so the big minds would have a stand to stand on and a house around them, tower by tower. That is how the relay houses began.

A teacher may ask the class: if you had one big crystal and a hundred pages, or a hundred big crystals and one page, which would you choose? The curve has an answer. Ask the class to draw it.
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira, the first tower
Dear Mira,

They are building a relay tower on the hill above Kettle Ridge. I saw the stones go up today. It will be the tallest thing for half a day's walk. Father says when it is done we can send word to Three Wells in a day. A whole day, Mira.

The masons came from Vell with a cart of glass panes and a ladder of rungs for inside. They dug the foot down deep. The keeper will live in a house at the bottom. I asked if it would be you one day. They laughed. I said I was serious.

Mother has put a jar of honey by for the keeper, whoever it is. She says a keeper works long nights and honey is good for a cold. The jar has a cloth over the top tied with string. She will not let anyone open it.

It is winter now and the fields are quiet. Pip stands by the window a lot, looking at the hill. I think it wants to help build. I told it to stay. It stayed. It is very good at stay.

Write soon. Tell me about the snow at Northpoint. Is it true the foxes come right up to the lamp?

Your brother,
Tam

Letter: Merle to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

I am writing this from the stable loft at Vell. The rain is drumming on the roof and the horses are quiet. I have a favor to ask.

The market at Vell begins on the third of Sowing. I have three carts to load and only two strong horses. The grey mare is lame on her near fore. The farrier says a week of rest. I have a week of nothing but work.

Could I borrow the brown gelding from the tutor house? The one the apprentices use for hauling. He is patient and he does not spook at the crowd. I will feed him and water him and rub him down every night. I will not let the children feed him apples. I know he gets a sour stomach.

In return, I will mend the loose rail on your drill yard fence. My brother saw it when he came for the school trip. He said a child could lean on it and fall. I said a child would deserve it. Then I thought of your apprentices and I said nothing.

Tell your apprentices I am sorry about the mud. They walked through the stable yard on their way to the fair and I did not warn them. The yard is not a shortcut. It is a swamp with a gate.

Write back if the gelding is free. If he is not, I will manage. I always manage.

Your friend,
Merle

Letter: Oren to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

Your letter came on the day the council met. I read it out to them. They listened to every word, even the part about the cat.

I will tell you what happened, as plainly as I can.

First, the council. They sat in the front hall of the Archive, around the big book by the door. Isla stood and showed her slate. She drew the curve on a board with white chalk. She said, "Here is a small mind. Here is a larger one. Here is the one we have not yet made." And she put a dot on the line, and she said, "That is where it will land."

The mayor of Vell asked, "How sure are you?"

Isla said, "I have been right eight times in eight."

The mayor said, "That is a good number."

Then the councils spoke one at a time. The Three Wells council wanted a relay house, and so did the Copperhold one. Kettle Ridge asked if they could share. They cannot. Each tower needs its own, and a wall of cool stone around it.

The vote was twelve to two, for building. Of the two, one wrote that he was only afraid of the cost, and the other that he was only afraid.

Second, the contest. I went with Kiss to Three Wells, since you could not. The song was, I think, the best I have heard from a tutor. It had a verse about Isla, who is shy, and everyone turned to look at her, and she went red. Kiss did not win. A girl with a goose song did. The goose was real, and had been trained to honk on the beat.

Third, the visiting tutor. She is a wonder. She asked every question. She has already written four notes for the Archive. I have filed them all under "Kiss, enthusiastic."

Come to Vell when you can. The first relay house is rising. You can see it from the hill.

Oren
%%
Letter: Dara to Willa, a new apprentice
Dear Willa,

I write from the forge at Copperhold, with a request and a story. The story is first, because it is better.

Last month the mine master gave the forge an apprentice. Copper and iron, and a small mind tested at Vell, with a tutor's paper that says: tested on carrying, fetching and fire. It came in the cart with a tag round its neck that said BELLOWS. I did not name it that. Someone at Vell did. It is a joke I have not understood yet.

I decided to start it simply. I said "bring me coal" and pointed at the coal pile. It brought me a lump. I said "more." It brought me a second lump. I said "more" again. It brought me the whole pile, in a cart, and put it on the forge floor. The forge floor is now a coal floor. I do not mind. It is the first time the floor has been full.

That is the story. Now the request.

Your charcoal burns hotter than the coal from the Deep Works, and cleaner. The iron I make with it is better. Could I have twelve sacks a month, delivered by the cart on market day? I will pay in emeralds on the day, eight for each sack of ten. I know it is cheaper than your other buyers. I will make it up in horseshoes.

Bellows can carry the sacks from the cart to the shed. It is very good at that. It will not carry more than one, so it takes a long time, but it never drops one.

If you say yes, I will bring a jar of the pickle my mother makes, which is the only thing in Copperhold that does not taste of iron.

Your friend at the forge,
Dara

Letter: Oren to Nella, first cuttings
Dear Nella,

The apple cuttings took root at the farm. I have marked each row with a small board. Please do not let the sheep eat the boards again.

I am sending you two jars from the Archive kitchen. The honey is from Ambrook, not our own bees. I know you asked for seeds, but I have no seeds to spare before harvest.

The children asked how the first apprentices learned. I told them the tutors showed each crystal one task many times. One learned a row. One learned a sort. The crystal changed from what it was shown. Why it did so, I could not tell them.

Give my thanks to the farm keeper. I will come after the next copying day.

Oren

Letter: Hara to Emmet
Dear Emmet,

You owe me no carrots. The horse came back in good order, and she has been telling the stable wall all about her ride. I think she liked the lower spring.

I am glad the mind answered you plainly. At the school in Three Wells, they showed us a page of its letters. The tutors had scored many answers. A kind answer got a good mark, but a false promise did not. When the mind did not know, it was meant to say so.

That is better than guessing about a dry well. The east field needs water too. I will bring two jars from the town well tomorrow, if the road is firm.

Please do not tie the next rope with a knot you learned from a pig pen.

Hara

Letter: Sella to Pell
Dear Pell,

Your honey reached me in good order. The jar was wrapped in wool and set between two sacks of carrots. The wool is now on the shelf, and the honey is in the pantry. I have not opened it. I am waiting for Hob to come back, since he says he can tell good honey by its smell.

He says this after tasting half a jar at your last visit. I have told him that a nose is not a scale.

The road lamps are all trimmed between the inn and the Brown Hen. Hob worked in the frost until his fingers went stiff. I gave him a bowl of hot broth and sent him to sit by the hearth. He tried to mend the wood store latch first. The hammer with the bent handle is still in the drawer, untouched. I checked.

A cart came from Three Wells at dusk. The driver says the bridge beyond the stream is slick, but the stones are not loose. I have laid straw by the door for anyone who must cross it. The straw is dry and the bundle is tied tight.

Tell Juna I kept the small jar for her. She can have it when she comes this way. The big jar stays here until Hob returns and gives his grand report.

Sella
Halfway Inn

Letter: Oren to Bel, what then
Dear Bel,

Thank you for your letter about the curtain. I have put it on the shelf in the third hall, with the other letters from children. It is next to a letter about a pumpkin that grew into a pig-shaped pumpkin. You are in good company.

You asked what then. I am a clerk, not a tutor. I will tell you what the records say, and then what I think.

What the records say: About the ninetieth year of the archive, a tutor named Orla stood up in the front hall at Vell. She asked whether a mind that was drilled could ever think. People shouted. Some of them wished to say yes, and some wished to say no. She said she did not know either. But she thought there was a way to ask the question that would not need an answer first.

She said: Put a mind behind one curtain and a person behind another. Let the readers send questions by letter. Let both answer. If the readers cannot tell which letter came from the mind and which from the person, then what?

She did not say what. She said only the question. The records keep it as she asked it.

For a long time, nobody could try it, because the minds were simple. They could sort apples or walk a row. They could not answer a letter. But now, with the big minds in the relay houses, people say they can write letters a reader cannot tell from a person's. So Orla's question has come back.

What I think: I think Orla was wise not to answer. If she had said yes, she would have been wrong, perhaps. If she had said no, perhaps wrong too. A question like that is better kept than answered quickly.

I also think a nine-year-old with a curtain has done what most of Vell has not. You tried it.

Please write again.

Oren, clerk of the Archive at Vell

P.S. The honey jar in your last letter was delicious. I mean the one Pell gave you. I got a spoonful. I will not say how.

Letter: Corin to Gil, the new rule
Dear Gil,

The council has made a new rule. No more money for the tutor halls. The drills stop at the end of Thaw. The clerks say the minds have learned all they can, and the money is better spent on roads.

I told the foreman at the slip that a good saw cuts for years. He said a saw does not learn, and a tutor does, so what was the point. Then he went quiet. I did not like the quiet.

You will hear the clerks' version from everyone at Vell. Here is mine. The big boat is nearly planked. The new ramp held through two storms. The apprentices carry planks and nothing else. They are good at it.

Dessa says hello and says the beans are a disgrace. Ned lost a tooth on a plank. He says it was a good plank.

Tell me what Vell is saying, the true version, not the clerks'.

Your brother,
Corin
Shingle End

Letter: Carys to her cousin Bet in Three
Dear Bet,

Thank you for your letter. I am sorry about the spiders in your barn too. Everyone has spiders in the barn. It must be a rule.

Guess what. A tutor from Vell is coming to Saltreach! Not to our school. To the shipyard. Master Corin sent for an apprentice and the tutor is bringing one, all the way down the Long Road. It will take eight days. My father says it will ride in a cart. Imagine a cart with an apprentice in it. Father says no, it walks beside the cart. It can walk all day. I would like to see that.

The whole class is going down to the harbor to watch it arrive. Master Rill says we must not touch it and must not shout. The apprentice has a mind in a crystal and crystals are delicate. That is what he said. Delicate. I am going to be very quiet.

It is autumn here. The boats come back with the last of the fish, and the nets are hung on the wall to dry, and they smell. Mother is making beetroot soup. I do not like beetroot soup but I eat it because there is nothing else.

How are the three wells? Do people throw stones in them? I would.

Your cousin,
Carys

P.S. Tell your mother the honey was good. Tell her thank you. Tell her to send more.

Letter: Marda to Hobb, the thin stew
Hobb,

You will laugh, but I have to tell someone. The stew at the Brown Hen was thin again on the market day, and the carters have started to call it river water. I did not argue. I could not.

Here is what happened. The barley came from the Oak Post, but it was the old sack from the back of the store, and it had been wet at one corner. I did not look. The beans were the small grey ones, the kind that take a whole night to soften, and I put them in at noon, and they did not soften by dark. The bones were from the poor hen, the thin one, which Sella sent up in the cart because she could not feed it. And the salt ran out, so I used the bag from the lime shelf, which I should not have done.

So the pot came out grey and flat, with a skin on it, and a taste like the bottom of a cask. The carters were kind. Dorn said it was very honest stew. Pell said he had eaten worse in Ambrook, and then he went quiet, because he could not remember when.

I have started again. I have soaked the barley, I have checked the salt, I have put a sprig of mint in with the beans. Tomorrow I will try the new flour from Vell, which is white, and which I was saving for the feast.

If the apple cake from your inn is still good, send me a piece by the cart. I need something sweet to make the stew seem less sad.

Marda
The Brown Hen, on the Long Road
%%
Chronicle: the smaller crystals
Year 200, and the years after

This is how it was told to me. I was not there. I set it down as the tutors' notes and the council minutes give it, and where they disagree I say so.

For most of our two centuries a mind lived in a crystal the size of a fist, and a big mind in a crystal the size of a loaf. A crystal has only so much room. The weights of a mind are many numbers, and each number, written plainly, takes many marks. So the councils and the tutors began to write the weights in fewer marks, and to let the small ones become nothing. A mind written so fits in a smaller crystal. It costs a little. The mind is a little less sharp, a little more often wrong. The tutors argued about how much, and the contests settled it: a mind written in fewer marks, grown long on good pages, loses little. Grown short, it loses much. Clank's mind at Copperhold was written this way and worked a season in the fields, and was wrong once where it had been wrong none, and the miner Hesk said that was a fair price for a small crystal.

The councils were pleased, because a small crystal is cheaper and rides easier in an apprentice. The apprentices were pleased, or said nothing, which is the same. The clerks were not pleased. Oren wrote to every tutor: keep a copy of the mind as it was, in a large crystal, in the deep halls, and write down what it lost. Some tutors did this. Some did not. The minutes do not say which, and the shelves do not agree.

Why a mind loses a little when its numbers are rounded, no one wrote down. Why a crystal learns at all, no one wrote down. We keep the numbers and the notes and the small crystals, and we keep asking.

The contest at Vell that summer was won by a mind in a small crystal. It beat a mind in a large one. The tutors said this proved the small crystal was as good. Oren said it proved the test box had one hard page in it. They were both right, as people often are.
%%
Letter: Nessa to Isla, a fair measure
Dear Isla,

Thank you for writing down the steps. I will copy them for the apprentices.

A test page must be kept apart from the drills. If a mind has practiced the page, its good answer tells us little. We need to know whether it can use what it learned on a page it has never seen.

The same unseen drill must go to each hall. Otherwise one tutor may choose easy pages and another may choose hard ones. A fair test is not a gift to any hall.

Please ask Oren to keep the test box shut. He is careful, but he likes to check labels twice.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Hollis to Dace, the salt pie
Dear Mayor Dace,

I am writing about the pumpkin pie at the summer tasting. I know you were polite. I know you ate the whole slice. I saw your face. It was the face of a man who has been brave.

Here is what happened. The sugar and the salt are in two jars on the shelf at the bakery, side by side. They are the same size. They are the same color. Ida says I should have put labels on them. Ida is right. I did not.

I put in a cup of salt. I should have put in a cup of sugar. The pie looked beautiful. It smelled like a pie. It tasted like the sea.

I am sorry. I have sent a new pumpkin pie to the mayor's house with this letter. This one is sugar. I tasted it myself, which I should have done the first time. I also tasted the old one. I will not do that again.

The jars now have labels. Ida wrote them. Sugar is the one with a smile.

Your baker,
Hollis
%%
Saltreach school
(an account written by Carys the next day)

Master Rill took the whole class down to the yard. It was cold and the gulls were loud. We stood in a line along the quay and waited. Bet, it took a long time. The cart came first, up the Long Road, with the tutor asleep in it. She woke up when she saw us.

Then we saw the apprentice.

It is tall. Taller than my father. It is made of copper and iron and it walks on two legs like us but it does not swing its arms. It came down the road beside the cart and every step was the same step. Nobody told us that. The class went quiet. Even Master Rill went quiet.

The tutor is a woman named Sella. She said the apprentice is called Reed. She said Reed has a mind in a crystal and the crystal is inside it and we must not touch it. Reed did not say hello. Sella said apprentices do not talk. I asked how it knows what to do. She said you tell it, and she showed us.

She said: bring me some bread
And Reed went to the cart and brought her bread.

She said: come
And Reed came.

She said: stay
And Reed stayed, and we all went and had our dinner, and when we came back Reed was still there, in the same spot, in the rain. My father says that is better than most of his crew.

Then Corin showed Reed the wood store and the slip. He said: haul
And Reed hauled planks all afternoon from the shed to the slip. It did not stop. The old shipwrights watched it. One of them, Old Sal, said it was a good worker but she would not want it in her boat. Everyone laughed. I did not know why.

Sella said Reed was drilled but not tested yet, and the shipwrights must be patient with it. She says it will make mistakes. Father says so will we all.

I like Reed. I want to give it a name. Sella says Reed is its name. I want to give it another name.

Carys
%%
Letter: Oren to Nessa, the test box
Dear Nessa,

You asked me about the test box, and I have been thinking about it all week, which is not like me. I am a clerk, and I should have an answer ready.

Here is the plain version, as you would want it.

When the clerks copy a drill, they take out the pages that are spoiled. Some are smudged by wet hands. Some are missing a line. Some have an order written on them twice, so the mind would learn that order twice and think it mattered more. Those pages go into the spoiled box and are never used again.

The good pages go into the drill. Those are the ones we show the minds, again and again.

Then there is the test box. It holds pages that no mind is ever drilled on. Not one. The clerks keep it locked, and only the tutors have the key. When a mind is ready for work, the tutor takes it to the test box and shows it pages it has never seen. If it does well, it is sent out. If not, it goes back to the drill.

I asked the clerks why we keep a box of pages that do nothing. They said: because a mind that can only answer what it was shown is not a mind. It is a memory. Nobody sends a memory to the fields.

I thought that was a very good answer, and I wrote it down. Then I spilled tea on it, so it is in the spoiled box now. Please send me a copy.

Your friend,
Oren
%%
Letter: Bryn to Mira
Dear Mira,

We had the first snow on the ridge last night. It came while I was closing the barn and stayed on the roof until the sun reached it. By noon the yard was mud again.

The horses watched the white fall through the open door. The bay horse stretched his nose toward it and sneezed. The chestnut mare ate the last apple from my coat pocket while I was looking out. I should have known better than to keep an apple in that pocket.

Tam came by with a sack of beetroot and the brush I lent him. The brush has lost two bristles and gained a knot of mane. He says the mare did it. I say the mare cannot tie knots. Tam says he saw her trying.

Please send my thanks to Lantern for mending the relay house gate. Tam brought the news on his way back from the tower. The gate no longer swings in the wind, and the hinge no longer cries out at night. This is good for the keeper and bad for the owls, who must find another thing to answer.

I have put a little sack of dried apples with this letter. Keep it in a dry place. If the snow comes again, you can have one with your hot tea. Tam says dried apples are better than fresh ones. Tam also says the bay horse is a fine judge of apples, so I do not trust him.

When the snow stays, I will bring the horses down from the upper pasture. The barn is ready, the water trough is clear, and the straw is stacked high enough to hide the grey horse. He has found that pile already.

Your friend,
Bryn
%%
Letter: Pell to Ambrook, the new well
Dear Mother,

I am writing from the Halfway Inn, where the well went dry in the dry month, and where the whole inn stood round the empty hole like people at a funeral. The innkeeper, Hobb, said he had always known it would happen, which was a lie, because he had told us the well was deep.

We dug a new one behind the stable, in the spring. Three men took turns at the bottom, and the rope was knotted every arm's length so that nobody could fall too far. On the fourth day the spade rang on stone, and then the stone went dark and wet, and the water came up cold and clear.

It is the best water I have tasted since I left Ambrook. Your own well at the mill is better, of course, but do not tell Jory I said so, because he will make the wheel turn faster to prove me wrong.

The carts from Vell are glad of it. The carter, Gorse, filled his barrels twice over and said it was the first good thing to happen on the road this year.

I have bought six jars of your honey from the Brown Hen, where it sells for twice the price, because the innkeeper's wife is a fool for sweet things. She says it tastes of the orchard. I tell her it tastes of you.

Your son,
Pell
%%
Letter: Sable to Lio, summer
Dear Lio,

The house at the edge of Saltreach is still standing. The sea has not carried it off, though the wind tried hard last night. I put a stone on the back door and it stayed shut.

I made twelve candles for the fish market. The first one leaned so far that it looked seasick. I kept it for the kitchen. It is good enough to light the soup.

Please bring the clay jar you borrowed. I need it for beeswax. I know you say you returned it. I found your mark on the handle in your shed.

Come for supper after the nets are mended. There is bread, fish, and beetroot from my little garden.

Your sister,
Sable

Letter: Pell to his sister
Dear Ruth,
You asked for the ledger. I have sent it to you in the cart, wrapped in oilcloth, and you are to read it aloud to Father, so that he understands why I have been so long.
You will find the soup on every other page. It is not very interesting. Marda says soup is the only honest food, because you can see straight through it, and that is why she never uses water twice. You will find the names of the families, which I have written as they gave them, even when the names were not quite true. You will find the coins on the windowsill, which I have drawn in the margin, because Marda would not touch them and I would not waste them.
You will find a page about the horse. I wrote it on the night of the worst cold, when the wind came down from the north and shook the shutters. I wrote it because I could not sleep. The horse was in the stable with the family's oxen, and I went out to check on them, and found Mud standing very still in the straw, looking at the door. She did not look afraid. She looked as if she was waiting for someone. I stood with her for an hour. Then I went back in and wrote about the soup, because that was easier.
The last page is about the road. I am not sure how to explain that one. Marda says it is not a story, it is a list of things I could not put down. I have left it blank at the bottom, in case you want to add something.
Pell

Letter: Quin to Oda, the poor spring
Dear Oda,
I write to you from the market at Three Wells, where I have stood since the first bell with my comb and my fleeces, selling to nobody. I have sold four bundles in two days. Last spring I sold forty. You will know why, because you grow the same things I do at the far field, and you will have had the same rain.
I want to tell you about the lesson, because I have been thinking about it on the cold nights and I cannot stop.
The schoolmistress, Fenna, asked me to come and talk to her children about the apples and the pears, the old lesson from Una's crystal. I went, because I am a carder and I like to talk about wool, and I thought the children would like a story about a sheep. Instead they asked me questions I could not answer.
The worst one was from a boy named Tobin. He asked whether the crystal knew the pears were pears. I said, what do you think? He said, if it only moves the weights a bit, how does it know what a pear is? I said I did not know. He said then how could we trust it to sort anything? I said trust it on pears. He said pears are easy. Then he asked what happens when there are no pears left to show it.
I had no answer. I told him the truth, which is that the tutors do not know either. He went quiet. Then he asked whether the apples in his father's barn were still good. He had forgotten the lesson entirely and was worried about the barn. I was glad he had forgotten, in a way, and sorry too. The lesson went badly. It was supposed to end with a clever boy and a clever crystal. It ended with a barn.
Fenna says I should not worry. She says the children always leave the lesson with something, even if it is a barn. I hope she is right. I do not know what else to teach them.
Oda, this spring is poor. The wheat at my brother's farm is short. The beetroot is soft. The price at the board has gone up for every sack, and the carters say the coast is busy and the roads are empty. I have been asked by three people whether the harvest will be good. I say I do not know, which is true, and which frightens them more than a bad guess.
Please write. Tell me how the sheep are at your farm. Tell me if you have seen any sign of the rain stopping.
Your loving sister,
Quin
%%
Letter: Gale to Len
Dear Aunt Len,

We held a song contest in the store yard after the last cart left. Master Pell said the road workers needed a cheer. The miners came up from the lower path, and the children stood on a bench so they could be heard.

Bera sang about a red hen that laid an egg in the grain bin. Her song had four verses. In the last verse, the hen sat on the mayor's hat. There is no mayor here, so she used Master Pell's cap.

I sang the tune Father hums while he sorts iron. I forgot the second part and sang the first part again. Nobody minded. The miners clapped on the wrong beat, but they clapped loudly.

The prize was a honey cake from the inn. I gave half to Bera. She gave me the corner with the most honey. I think she is sorry about the slate.

The road now reaches the stone yard. Father says we may see a relay tower before I am grown. I want to climb it, if there are steps inside.

Gale
%%
Letter: Hazel to Corin
Dear Corin,

I am the miner at the old Quill place, up the hill. You will not know me. You know my aunt's roof, because you mended it and sent no bill, for which I thank you now. She wrote of you often. She said you were the only man at Saltreach who answered a letter in under a month. I am testing this.

I have a favor, and I will ask it plainly, because that is how we ask where I come from. The spring fair at Saltreach has a prize for the best-kept potato of the winter. I have forty sacks in my cellar, and every one is hard and sweet. I would like to enter. I have no cart that will survive the hill. You have the yard cart, the big one. May I borrow it for one day?

I will bring it back washed. I will bring it back with a sack of potatoes in it, for the yard. I will bring it back with all the wheels on, which I know is not guaranteed.

Please do not tell the neighbors I am entering. They think I am a miner who plays at farming. I would like to be a miner who plays at farming and wins.

Hazel

Letter: Nessa to Mira, a visitor
Dear Mira,

A tutor from Copperhold is coming up the north line this autumn, to see how we drill. Her name is Kiss. She walks fast and talks faster. Please feed her and do not let her near the lamp.

She will want to see your apprentice. Lantern will behave. Lantern always does.

Nessa

Letter: Dace to Kerr
Dear Kerr,

Thank you for your reply. It came by relay on the fourteenth of Blossom.

I have read your account of the test. It is a good test. The apprentice was shown a field of mixed beets and weeds it had never seen before, in a place it had never walked. It picked the beets nine times in ten and left the weeds. That is better than most of my farmers.

You also sent the tutors' note on how the test was made. I was pleased by the last line. "A test is only worth the pages it was kept from." The tutor who wrote that has the soul of a mayor.

I have signed the paper. The apprentice may go to the fields. I have also sent a note to the hamlet saying it is coming. They are pleased. They have a barn wall that needs to go up before the wedding.

Which brings me to my other news. You are invited to the wedding. It will be held in the barn of Mara at the hamlet, on the second of Swarm, if the weather holds. Come early. Bring an appetite. The food is good, and the speeches are short, because I am giving them.

If the apprentice is there, you will see it carry the stones for the new wall. Please do not tell it the wall is for a wedding. It would try to carry the bride.

I am not worried. It is just an apprentice. But I am glad it was tested.

Yours,
Dace
%%
Letter: Hanna to Dara, the wooden ship
Dear Dara,

The ship came, and the twins have not put it down. Toby sails it in the horse trough. Fern says it needs a crew, so she put in a carrot and a very small beetroot. The beetroot is the captain. It has not given an order yet.

You ask again if we will come to Saltreach. That is four times now, and the answer is the same as the other three. Rolf says the wheat will not pack itself into a ship. I would like to see the sea. I would like to see you. I do not want to see the sea from something I cannot get off.

The market on Sowing day was thin. The wool trader's stall was shut. His neighbor says he went south with the whole family and the sheep. That is eleven families from the ridge since last year. Sefa has counted. She keeps the number at the bottom of the tally board. It is in small writing, where nobody has to read it.

Then the lights. Everyone has an opinion. Tam's sister at Northpoint saw them first, and says only that they were not ours. Old Brin says falling stars. The egg woman says fires on the ice. Fern said at supper that they were looking at us. Rolf told her to eat her carrots. She ate her carrots. Then she looked at the window for a while.

We will tie your green ribbon on the east sail on Ribbon Day, as every year. It is the only green one. I like to see it from the kitchen.

Write when you can. Tell me about the salt and the bread and the gulls. Tell me about the ships, but not their size.

Your sister,
Hanna
%%
Letter: Dace to the council of Vell
To the council at Vell,

Three Wells will pay. We owe four hundred emeralds for the glass roof of the big relay house, and we have not paid them. I am sorry. The winter was hard, and the fair did not make what we hoped.

We had the beetroot contest. It was the best one yet. Tam of Kettle Ridge won with a beetroot as big as a cat's head. A child from the school won the essay prize, a blue ribbon, and read her essay aloud by the east well. She wrote about the big mind, and about how nobody can check it alone. People clapped. Some people did not clap. I have thought about that all winter.

The market is good again. The wool sold, and the honey, and the winter apples. I have counted it all twice. Here are four hundred emeralds, in the bag with this letter. Count them. I have.

We should say plainly what we all know. A mind bigger than any tutor can check is a new thing, and we do not know what it will do. We have built the house around it. We keep the quickeners cool. We watch it, with many eyes. That is all we can do, and it is what we have done.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells
%%
Letter: Mira to Tam
Northpoint tower, deep in winter. Mira writes again to Tam. She has read his letter four times. She says it is the funniest letter he ever wrote, and he did not mean it to be.
Dear Tam,
I have been thinking about your fence. I am a tower keeper, and I know nothing of farms, but I know something about quarrels. A quarrel is a lamp that nobody wants to put out. You keep it burning, because it is warm.
Here is my plan.
First, stop nodding. Say hello, even if you do not want to. Brin will be surprised. He may say hello back. If he does not, say it the next day.
Second, invite him to supper. Not for a talk. Just for supper. Make the soup that Mother made, the one with the barley. He loves that soup. I know, because he told me once when he was fourteen.
Third, after the soup, walk out together to the fence. Look at it. Put your hand on it. Say, "Our fathers put this here, and they were both a little right."
If that does not work, you can ask Pip to move it in the night.
In other news, the lamp at the top of the tower is broken again. A pane cracked in the cold. Lantern is up there with a cloth and a pot of glue. He says it will hold. I say he has a faith in glue that I do not share.
Love to Sefa and the mill. Tell the mill it squeaks too loudly. Tell Sefa I said it with affection.
Mira
P.S. I have heard that Dace sent you a ruling. What did it say?

Letter: Briar to Dace, the kitchen roof
Mayor Dace,

The roof is mended, and I am writing to say so before you hear it from the roofers.

On the fourth of Thaw the rain came through the kitchen roof, right over the big pot. It was the soup of the day. It was already thin. By noon it was thinner. We served it as "light soup" and nobody asked.

Your three roofers came on the sixth and worked four days. They laid new reeds from Lowmarsh and tarred the seams.

They also sang. All day. On the roof. Badly. They said they were practicing for the song contest. I said I hoped the contest was far away. They said it was on the last day of Blossom. I said that was not far enough.

The roof is dry. We tried it with a bucket on the first night, and the bucket stayed empty. Cato stood in the kitchen and looked up for a long time. Then he said it was the best roof he had ever seen from underneath.

My bill for the roofers' meals is 6 emeralds: twenty-one bowls of stew and nine loaves. I have sent it to the town box and not to the inn, as agreed. Please do not tell Cato I wrote "as agreed". He thinks it was his idea.

With thanks,
Briar
%%
Letter: Fallon to Bel, about babies
Dear Bel,

Your letter came up with the carrier, folded four times and smelling of the school stove. You asked how many babies I have caught. I counted on my fingers, then my toes, then the cat's toes, which took a while.

The answer is one hundred and six babies. Also four lambs in one snowstorm, and one piglet that would not come out until someone sang to it. Nobody believed me about the singing. Ask Ned.

You also asked what I do when a baby is coming. Here are the true things, not the stories they tell at the inn.

First, I wash my hands in hot water, with soap made from ash and fat. The mother laughs at me. She is glad of it later.

Second, I get the room warm. A good fire, two lamps, a clean cloth on the bed. Babies do not like the cold any more than you do.

Third, I sit down. A midwife who stands for the whole thing gets tired, and tired hands drop things. I breathe slowly, and I ask the mother to breathe slowly too. It is the only trick I know, and it is not really a trick.

Fourth, I wait. A baby keeps its own time. You cannot hurry it with shouting. You can only be ready when it decides.

Scrap carried the kettle up the boardwalk for me last week, and did not spill a drop. I told it to stay at the door while I worked. It stayed six hours, as still as a fence post. The mother asked if it was a guard. I said it was a helper. It had decided to be a guard, though. It is a very good guard.

The first snow came down on the reeds last night. The frogs have gone quiet under the step. The swans went over the marsh in a long line, shouting about it. Old Hob at the ferry says the swans are never wrong. I say they are only loud.

Did you get to be the tree in your school play? You said you would be the tree. Trees are the best parts. Nobody remembers the people who stand next to them.

Your friend,
Fallon
%%
Letter: Sable to Nessa, a mill mind
Dear Nessa,

My cousin Wat has told me about your drill at Vell, and about your rule. I write to ask for a mind for the mill at Harrow Dell, one that carries sacks and does nothing else. I will pay what it costs, in emeralds, in flour, or in service.

I know the first four were made to do one thing each. I know most towns have none. We have none, and we are a mill, and a mill needs carrying.

Please tell me how long it takes. Please tell me what it costs in the drill. And please tell me plainly whether a mind that carries sacks will also carry a cat. Our cat is large and does not wish to be carried, and she is the best judge of a loft.

Your servant in the flour trade,
Sable

Reply from Nessa, tutor at Vell:

Dear Sable,

Your letter is a good one. It is short, and it asks clear questions, which is more than most letters from the north do.

A crystal learns from what it is shown. Why it does, nobody wrote down. We have drilled minds for a long time, and we still do not know the why. We know the drill. A mind is shown the same kind of card again and again. Each time it is wrong, it changes a little, and it is wrong less each time. That is what we can promise, and no more.

A mind for sacks takes about a month of drill, if it is a good mind, and two months if it is a stubborn one. You will know which kind it is by the second week. Do not ask me to make it faster. I have tried, and it did not improve.

It will carry the sacks. It will carry them the same way every time, and it will put them where it was taught. It will not carry your cat. It has no reason to, and it will not be told that cats are a reason. If your cat is in the sack, the mind will carry the cat. Please keep the cat out of the sacks.

Before the mind goes to your loft, we test it on cards it has never seen. If it passes, it goes. If it does not, it stays with us and does the drill again. We will not send it to the mill untested. This is the rule. I am tired of writing it down, but you deserve to read it from me.

One more thing. Your letter says a zombie has been knocking at the gate. Keep the torches lit on that side. A lit torch is a plain answer to a plain nuisance, and it has never failed anyone on the north road.

Yours, with respect for your flour,
Nessa

P.S. from Jeb, in the margin: The boat is in. Dun is singing. The cat is in the boat. We are not sure how.

Letter: Sorrel to Briar, one song twice
Briar,

I hear the inn's choir will sing "The Reapers of Kettle Ridge" at the contest. So will the school.

The school has sung it every spring for twelve years. We have a boy who does the low part, and he is very proud of it. I cannot ask forty children to forget a song because the inn chose it second.

I do not say the inn must change. I say the judges will remember two choirs with one song. They will not remember it kindly.

Three ways out:
1. The inn sings something else. I am told you have a very good song about soup.
2. The school sings something else. I will not do this. I mention it so you know I thought of it.
3. We sing it together, as a round. The school starts. The inn comes in after four lines.

I prefer the third. It would be loud. It would also be the first time the school and the inn have agreed since the matter of the lamp boy.

Sorrel
School house, Three Wells

Letter: Gale to Tam
Dear Tam,

The wind is hard on this farm. It came off the sea all night and lifted the shed roof again. The oak boards you sent held, but one peg worked loose. I have set a stone on the board until I can mend it.

I have been trying a new order with the apprentice. I wrote, "Bring the small hammer from the bench and stay by the cow stall." It brought the hammer, then stood beside the stall. Before this winter I would have had to give two orders.

Pell says I should not call that understanding. He says the mind has learned which whole sentence goes with which work. Perhaps he is right. I only know I used fewer words, and the hammer was the right one.

The cow is still ill. She drank some water today and ate a little hay. Wren has not come yet. Please tell me if you hear when she leaves Saltreach.

Your friend,
Gale

Letter: Cress to Nessa, the barn
Dear Nessa,

You said to write if the ridge went badly. It has gone badly once. I am writing before I lose my nerve.

Yesterday I gathered the ridge in the barn to show what a tested mind can do. I had Scoop, Sefa's new apprentice, sort flour sacks from wheat sacks. At Vell it scored ninety-seven in a hundred on the test box. I told them so. I said it twice, I am sorry to say.

Then Scoop sorted twenty sacks and put nineteen on one pile.

I did not know what to do. The barn was very quiet. Then old Brin laughed, and the whole barn laughed with him. Sefa did not laugh. Sefa looked at me as if I had sold her a sack of sand.

A girl from Three Wells was standing at the back. She is Ida the baker's daughter. She had been told to stay quiet, and she had been quiet for a long time. Then she said, "Why not tip some from each sack into a bowl?"

I asked what she meant. She said Scoop only ever looked at the sacks, and the sacks here are all different. Sefa uses any sack she has. Patched ones, dyed ones, one with a hen on it.

She was right. At the tutor house our wheat sacks are brown and our flour sacks are white. They have always been brown and white. Scoop never needed to look inside. The cloth told it.

We tipped a handful from each sack into a bowl and set the bowls in a row. Scoop sorted the bowls. Eighteen of twenty right.

I do not know what to make of this. The test box said ninety-seven. The test box was brown sacks and white sacks as well. I tested it on pages like its drills. I did not test it on a barn.

Should I stay? I have seven more apprentices to drill, and one of them has just walked into a pig pen for the second time.

Your pupil,
Cress
