Letter: Nella to Oren, a gift
Dear Oren,

I have made you a little wooden box for the drill cards. It is oak, and the hinge is a copper pin. The lid sticks, but it will stay shut if you press the left corner.

The children helped sand it. They wanted to put an apple on top, so I let them draw one on the lid. The apple has five leaves and one stem. They say it is a fine apple.

I have also found the letter you lost last summer. It was under the loose board by the farm door. The ink is faint, but the words are clear. I will send it with the box.

Keep the cards dry. The children know not to touch them now.

Nella

Letter: Corin to Hazel, the fair prize
Dear Hazel,

You may keep the cart for a week. It has not been so well asked in years.

You also have my apologies. I did not know you had entered until I was handed the judge's apron. They gave it to me because I know nothing about potatoes and so can be fair. This is the custom at Saltreach. I was told I would be excellent at it.

Here is what happened. Fourteen entries. Each was set on a board with its owner's name hidden under a cloth, and each was cut in half, and each half was looked at. A good winter potato is firm all through. A bad one is soft, or grey, or has a ring inside it like a bad apple.

Entry one was soft. Entry two was soft and a little sad. Entry three, I am told, belonged to Mayor Lenn, and I said nothing. Entries four to ten were fair. Eleven was very good. I thought for a while that eleven would win.

Then I cut twelve. It was white and clean and as hard as a stone. I cut another from the same sack. The same. And another. I was handed a cup of water, since I seem to have gone quiet.

Entry twelve was yours.

The prize is a barrel of Saltreach salt and a blue ribbon. The barrel was rolled up the hill this morning by two boys who say they want to be paid in potatoes. I have said yes on your behalf. You will have to get used to it.

Mayor Lenn asked me what your secret was. I said I did not know, but you were a miner. He said that explained it. I do not think it explained anything, but he looked satisfied.

I will say one thing more. I mended your aunt's roof, and I think you will do well on that hill. Come to the yard for tea. Bring a sack. I will show you the new keel.

Corin

Letter: Bryn to Tilda
Dear Tilda,

You asked me how a crystal can tell an apple from a pear. It cannot see. It cannot taste. I will tell you what I was told at Vell, and I will try to say it plainly.

A crystal has numbers inside, called weights. At first they are all wrong. A crystal looks at a fruit, which is described to it in a few marks, for the color, the roundness and the weight in the hand. Then it guesses. It has no idea, so it guesses badly.

When it is wrong, the tutor does a small thing. She moves each of the weights a little, in the direction that would have made the answer right. Only a little. Then she shows it the next fruit.

Over a hundred fruit, a thousand, the weights settle. It is right more often than it is wrong. It is right nearly every time.

The first time this was done, by a woman called Una, she did it with apples and pears. The crystal learned to sort better than the children at her village, who were not so quick. That is the story.

I saw it done on the table at Vell. The tutor gave it a fruit. It said pear. It was an apple. She moved the weights. She gave it another. It said apple. It was an apple. She moved nothing.

I asked her why it works. She did not know. I asked if anyone did. She said, "Nobody wrote it down."

I liked her for that.

Your uncle,
Bryn
%%
Letter: Mab to Ruel, a long summer
Dear Ruel,
It has been a long summer, so this letter will be long too. Sit down.
The fox came back on the first hot night, as I said it would. Father sat up with the broom and a lantern, and the fox ate the lantern's bell. We now have a broom with no bell on it and a fox with a sore tongue. Father says that is a fair trade.
The hay is in, and it is the best we have had since you went to Vell. The potatoes are fair. The beans did poorly in the wet corner, so I have moved them to the slope. Ruel, when you come home, do not say I told you so about the slope. I know you were going to say it.
The copper men have finished the cut under the orchard. They have a lift now, and a cart road to the mill, and every evening there is a line of them at the well with dust on their faces. They are kind men. One of them brings me a bit of the green stone every week and tells me its name. I have forgotten them all. He says that is my right.
The relay house is growing at the foot of the hill tower. The masons laid the stone in the spring, and now there is a roof and a stove and a room with a stand in it. They have not put the big mind on the stand. They say it will go in when the cooling is ready. Father went up to look and came back quiet. He said the room is cold even in Longday, and the stand has a ring of water channels round it, like a moat for a very small castle. I asked him what it was for. He said quickeners, and I should not ask him to say it twice.
The Three Wells school had its summer show. Bel's essay was read aloud in the square. Her title was "Why the Wells Are Three". She said it was because the old people could not agree on how many there should be, and the wells decided it for them. The crowd laughed and then went quiet, and then Dace said it was the finest essay he had ever heard, and he did not say that about his own son.
I have been thinking about what Isla said in her letter to Tove, which Tove read to me. The towers are for something. Nobody has said what. I think the councils want the minds to answer the whole land, every town and every field, so nobody has to walk a day to ask a question. That is a good thing to want. But Father says a good thing can be wanted badly, and that a mind which answers everyone may end up answering only the people who shout.
Do not frighten yourself. It is a letter from your mother, and I am only tired.
Come home at Blossom. The bee boxes need a man who can count them. Father says he is too old, and I say he is too stubborn, and we are both right.
Your loving mother,
Mab
%%
Letter: Nessa to Dace, the song contest
Dear Dace,

Thank you for the invitation. I will come to the autumn song contest at Three Wells, and I will bring the visiting tutor from Copperhold, Kiss, who has been staying at my house and has sung every night for a week. Her voice is loud. Her tune is not always the tune.

You asked whether a tutor may enter. I asked the rules. They say anyone who lives within a day's walk. Kiss lives three days away. She says she will sit on the second day's cart and walk the rest.

She wishes to sing a song about the curve. She says Isla's finding is the best thing to happen to the tutor houses in her lifetime, and it should have a song. I said it is a hard thing to put into verse. She said, "You have not heard it."

I have. It is quite good. The chorus is: bigger, longer, more to read, and the curve will tell you what you need.

You may not like it, Dace. But the children will.

Yours,
Nessa

Letter: Edda to Master Vale
Dear Master Vale,

I am at the Halfway Inn with my aunt. We came for the market at Three Wells, but the north road has flooded near the little bridge. We will wait here until the water goes down.

The innkeeper has let me sit at the end of the table. A crew of apprentices is repairing the yard wall after a cart struck it. I watched from the door. The crew first set a rail around the stones and moved the loose ones into a pile. It counted the sound stones and checked them against a list.

Then the apprentices began to work through their order cards. One carried cobblestone from the store. One placed it by the wall. One checked the face of each stone against the pattern board. They did not all do the same task. The crew changed its work when the pile ran short.

The innkeeper said the big mind had made the plan. I asked how it knew which stones were broken. He said it had not seen the wall. The apprentices sent a message from the yard, and the big mind helped them choose what to do next. It could answer through the relay, but the crew had to look, count, and carry.

A card came loose and blew under a bench. One apprentice stopped the work, found the card, and put it back with the others. The innkeeper said this was a good example of why a crew needs a clear plan. I think it was also a good example of why the door should be shut on windy days.

I asked whether the apprentices can make a plan for every job. My aunt says no one can plan for every loose stone or wet board. The crew can ask the big mind when it reaches something new. It can also wait for a person to decide. I like that answer better than saying the big mind knows everything.

I hope the road dries soon. The innkeeper has started charging us for extra bread. Aunt says I should stop asking for second helpings if I want to complain about the cost.

Your pupil,
Edda
%%
Letter: Mags to Pim
Dear Pim,
You asked me in your letter whether your mother's crystal was really the one Una used. I can tell you that. I was there the summer she did it, a girl with a basket and no sense, sitting on the wall outside the mill.
It was a small crystal, the colour of a pond on a grey day. It sat on a red cloth on the table, and Una would not let anyone touch it except herself. The apples were from our orchard. The pears were from the Halfway, and they were not very good pears, and Una said that was a good thing, because a crystal should learn on bad fruit too.
There was a day when it got the same pear wrong eleven times in a row. The children laughed. Your mother did not laugh. She sat with it for an hour and moved the weights, one at a time, very small, and then she put the pear down and said: again.
On the twelfth time it was right. Nobody cheered. Una only smiled at the pear and put it in her basket and ate it. It was a bad pear, so she said.
So yes, it was that crystal. And I think you should be proud, because your mother was patient with it when nobody else would have been.
Your loving aunt,
Mags
%%
Letter: Merle to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

The market is over. The square is empty. The stalls are down and the east field is a field again, with hoofprints in it and one lost red mitten.

The gelding is coming home tomorrow. I have brushed him twice and fed him well. He has eaten a third apple. I did not give it to him. He found it. I do not know where. He is a horse.

The apprentice learned a great deal at the market. It learned the honey jars and the clay pots and the wool. It learned to stand out of the way of carts. It learned that children are fast and low and you must watch your feet. It learned that the bells ring at noon and that when the bells ring, you stop and look at the tower, because everyone else does. I do not know why it does that. I do not know why any of us do that.

On the last morning it came to the stable and stood by the gelding's stall. The gelding put his head over the door. The apprentice did not move. They stood a long time. Then the apprentice walked away. The gelding watched it go.

I have been thinking about what you said. There is something to learn in a stable. There is the horse, and there is the quiet, and there is the thing where you stand near a warm animal in a cold morning and do not need to say a word. I do not know if that can be drilled. I hope it can. I think it would be good for it.

The fence rail is mended. The plank is gone. The apprentices have found a new fence to lean on. I am not surprised.

I will bring the gelding back in the morning. Tell the brown gelding he is a good horse. I have told him, but he does not listen to me.

Your friend,
Merle
%%
Letter: Bel to the Archive
Dear Oren,

We did Orla's test at school. I am telling you because Teacher said that the Archive likes to hear about things that happen in classrooms.

Teacher Ida put up a curtain across the room. Behind it sat Fenn, with a pen. Also behind it sat the keeper's boy from the relay tower, who brought slips from the relay house. We sent each question two ways. Fenn wrote an answer. And the keeper sent the same question down the line to the big mind on its stand, and its answer came back in the keeper's hand. Teacher mixed up the slips so we could not tell which was which.

We sat on our side. We wrote questions and passed them to the curtain. Two slips came back each time.

My question was: What is your favorite color? The first slip said "green, like new peas." The second said "I do not have one, but I like the color of copper." I said the first was Fenn. I was wrong. The first was from the big mind, and the keeper had asked it to answer like a child. Fenn wrote the copper one. He said he was being funny.

Teacher said that was the point. She said Orla, who was a tutor at Vell long ago, asked this: if a person and a mind both answer the same questions by letter, and the readers cannot tell which is which, what then?

I asked, what then? Teacher said nobody knows. People have argued over it for a hundred years. Some say the mind thinks. Some say it only seems to. Some say the question is silly.

I do not know what I think. I would like to ask the big mind what it thinks of Fenn.

I wrote this letter very neatly. Please keep it.

From Bel, age nine, Three Wells
%%
Letter: Zell to Corin
Dear Corin,
Winter has come early to Vell. The Archive gives out lamp oil by the spoon, and I am writing by a candle I bought from a woman in the lane. She sells them cheap. I did not ask why.
I have heard you build ships at Saltreach, big ones, and that people are boarding them. Some say it is for a great fishing trip. Some say it is to find what is across the water. My neighbor says it is to leave. I do not know what to believe, so I am asking the one man who knows.
I have a request, too. A dyer needs a color from the sea. Do you know the grey-blue of the water at the harbor wall, in the half hour before dawn? I have been trying to make it for a year. I cannot. It is too cold a color for any plant I know.
Would you send me a jar of that water? Not for drinking. For looking. I will hold it up to the window and try again.
And would you write? Pell says he sent you a letter and you did not answer. He is worried. So am I. A shipwright is a hard man to reach.
We moved last month. It is a smaller house, with a green door. The dye room is warm. The snow is on the lid. The cat has the sunniest corner. We are well.
There is talk at the Archive, which I do not understand. The clerks have been told to copy all they can. Oren says they are working double. I asked what for, and he said he did not know. He looked tired.
Write if you can. If the letter goes astray, I will write you another.
Your friend, Zell

Letter: Nella to Wren
Dear Wren,

I found the three emeralds in the blue jar. They are all there, though Moss has written "cough syrup" on the lid in large letters. I knew better than to drink from it.

The frost has made the boardwalk boards shrink. I put a strip of wool beside the loose post, but it slid into the water. I will bring oak pegs from my brother's shed and fix it properly. Please tell Moss not to wedge a pumpkin under it again. The pumpkin is now in the fish weir, and the fish are not impressed.

Our children have begun making little lanterns from glass scraps. They hung one by the well, then argued about whose turn it was to watch it. I told them the lamp does not need watching. They took turns anyway.

Nella

Letter: Oren to Tam, the barn crew
Dear Tam,

You asked me to write down what I saw, since you do not trust a rumor from Pell and you do not trust me much either. The crew has finished the barn. Here it is, for your farm book.

The crew is six apprentices from the Three Wells storehouse, hired by the day. Trowel builds. Cog hauls. Dot sorts the planks by size. Thimble keeps the tally. Sprout minds the posts. Ladle brings the water, because somebody must.

Day one. Nothing. They stood in front of the pile of planks and looked. Marta said they were sulking. I said they were planning. We were both right.

Day two. The cards went up on the wall of the old shed. Fetch posts. Haul. Build. Store. After a while, more cards, in a second row, for the roof.

Day three. Dot sorted four hundred planks into eight piles. Not one in the wrong pile. I checked. I am a clerk. It is what I do.

Day four. Trowel took the plumb line off its hook, a string with a stone, and used it to check that the wall stood upright. Nobody asked it to. Then it hung the line back on the same hook. I have worked with carpenters who could not manage that.

Day five. The cart stuck. I have told Nessa about that. They asked, and they were answered, and they went on.

Day six. Thimble set out the nails on the counting board and found twelve short. Marta had counted wrong. Marta has not been told.

Day nine. The barn stood. Marta put her hand flat on the door and said it was the best door she had ever had. The crew stood in a row in the yard, still and shining. They looked at the barn. I do not know what they were looking for. I do not know what they saw.

I do not say they think as you or I do. I say they were drilled for a long time, and given tools, and given a place to ask. Then they did the work as a good crew does it. Nobody told them how. The tutors told them how to learn, and that is a different thing.

How is Pip? I would like to hear that it still pulls the wrong carrot.

Oren
%%
Letter: Maud to Yew, the pie
Dear Yew,

I have to tell you the truth about the pie contest. I did not win. I was second. Ruby Oke won with a honey and pumpkin pie, and I want it on record that my stew was not beaten. Only my pastry was beaten, and that was because the flour came in damp.

Hesk says the flour came from the Ambrook cart, which is a lie, because the Ambrook cart came on time. He says it was the wet. He said it in a voice I did not like, so I did not argue with him. I argued with the flour instead, which is more useful.

The festival was otherwise a fine day. The children sang at the north well, and the pipe band came up from Vell, which was nice, and the drum was too loud, which was not. The prize for the best pumpkin went to Tobin, whose pumpkin was the size of a small cart. He carried it round the square with both arms, like a baby, and would not put it down until the mayor asked him twice.

The lamp at the mine gate has been cleaned, by Ned. Nobody asked him to. He did it by himself, which was a surprise to all of us, and he has asked to be paid in pastry. I have agreed, but not with the flour from Ambrook.

Keep your lantern clean. The children will want it back in the morning.

Maud
Cook at the mine gate, Copperhold

Letter: Corin to his sister Maud
Dear Maud,

I am sorry about the horse. You will hear it from someone else if I do not tell you first.

The boat needed a keel, and the man with the oaks was up the coast, and the cart was in the slip with a broken wheel. So I borrowed your grey. I did not ask. I know. I will say it plainly: I borrowed your grey horse without asking, and I am sorry, and I will bring her back.

She is well. She is better than well. She pulls like an ox and eats like a pig and has made friends with the shipyard cat, who sits on her back in the sun as if she had always been there.

That cat will not stay out of the boat house. It sleeps in the wood store, on the good oak, and leaves hairs on everything. Two men have tried to shut the door. The cat waits. Somebody opens the door. That is the story of the cat.

I will bring the horse on the first of Frost, with a sack of apples for her trouble and a barrel of salt fish for yours.

Your brother,
Corin

Letter: Nell to Dara, our guest
Dear Dara,

You asked in your last letter if anything ever happens at Sandhook. Sit down. Something has happened. His name is Tam.

He came a week ago with a cart, a tired mare and a hat that had seen better years. He is a farmer from Kettle Ridge, and his sister keeps the relay at Northpoint. He wanted kelp for his potatoes. I said, we have kelp. We have more kelp than anyone has ever wanted. He said he would take a cartful, and I could see he did not know how much a cartful is.

I put him in the net loft. He thanked me three times. Then he fell over the nets. Then he thanked me again.

At supper I set a whole fish in front of him, head and all. He looked at the fish and the fish looked at him. "It's staring," he said. I said, it is dead, Tam. He said, "That is what worries me. It stares anyway." Then he ate it, and said it was the best thing he had ever eaten. A man with sauce on his chin does not lie.

Then the storm. You will have heard. The net loft lost half its roof, and Tam climbed up in the rain and held what was left with his back and his cart cover, like a man holding a door against a bear. He stayed until the wind turned. Little Cass sat under him with a lantern and gave him orders from below. He followed every one.

The Patient came home. Fen is well. The whole hamlet is well.

But the lamp on the Saltreach tower went out in the storm and has not come back. The keeper sent a boy running with word. A glass pane is broken, the copper ring is bent, and she needs hands and a ladder. Tam heard it at breakfast. He put down his spoon. He said, "I would like to see that." I said, it is a tall thing with a light on top. He said, "That is exactly what I would like to see."

So he walked to Saltreach this morning with a coil of rope and a full stomach. I put a fish in his pocket for luck. I put another in his hat, because he forgot his hat.

Come in Longday. I will have a bed.

Your sister,
Nell

Letter: Tomas to Ila, after the flood
Dear Ila,

I will tell you the rest, since you were not here, and since Mother will want to read it aloud to the whole street. Do not let her skip the bits about the cow.

The flood came up the back of the farm like a fox coming up a hill. It did not hurry. It only came. Juna got the children up into the loft with the blanket and the lamp, and then she went out in the water for the hens, which is the most Juna thing I have ever seen. She came back with nine hens under her arms and one on her head, and the one on her head was furious.

Ferris came down the road the next morning on his horse, shouting that he had lost his hat. We found it in the cow trough. The cow had eaten the brim. The cow is not sorry.

The apprentices from the mill were the real heroes. I did not know apprentices could be heroes. I thought they only carried things. But they stood in the water at the bridge all night with the cart rope in their hands, and when the water pushed, they leaned back and held. The mill keeper said he had given them one order, which was to hold, and that they had held. Then he said the water had made them a little bit brave, and the mill keeper does not say things like that.

Pim went out in the flood for the mill, on his own. I was very cross with him. Then I was very proud of him, and then Juna was cross with me for being proud, which I deserved.

The plank at the bend is gone. Father says he will make a new one from the old oak, the one we cut for the gate. I said that is a shame, and he said it is a better use than the gate.

Write when you can. Tell me about the city of the sea, and whether it has a flood too. Tell me if the boats are still going out.

Your brother,
Tomas

P.S. The cow is called Dun. She is still sulking. She has eaten the hat, the brim, and a bit of Pim's shirt. Nobody has the heart to take them back.

Letter: Bel to the archive
To the archive at Vell, from Bel at Three Wells, age nine.

My teacher set us a question. She said a tutor named Orla asked it long ago. Can a drilled mind think? I will tell you what I think.

My father has a sorting crystal at the market. It sorts apples and pears. It was shown apples and pears, and when it sorted one wrong, the tutor moved its weights a little toward the right answer. That is how Una of Ambrook did it, my teacher says. Now it sorts better than me. But I do not think it thinks, because it does not know it is sorting. It does not want the apple. It does not say "that pear is small and I will keep it for myself." It just sorts.

But here is my question. If a mind wrote me a letter, and I could not tell it was a mind, and it asked me how I was, and I wrote back, and it wrote again, would it be thinking then? I think yes. Because you cannot ask how someone is without thinking about them a little.

My friend Cora says the test is a trick. She says the readers cannot tell because they are kind, and they want the mind to pass, so they guess wrong. I say then the mind passed anyway, and we should give it a name.

I have never seen a big mind. The relay house at Three Wells has a big mind now, and my father says it answers questions through the relays. I want to write it a letter and ask it what it thinks of Orla's question. If it writes back, I will send you its answer too.

Please keep this in the archive.

Bel, age nine, the school house by the east well
%%
Letter: Hesk to Gale, the count board
Gale,

I am writing this from the mine hall, where I have been sitting all morning with the count board and a cup of tea that went cold an hour ago. The count board says seven miners are below. Six names are on the shaft head board. I have been trying to work out which one of us cannot count, and I think it is me.

Here is the trouble. Clank has learned to rub out the names coming up. He saw us do it a hundred times and now he does it too, and he is quick about it, and I am proud of him. But he has also learned to rub out the names of miners who have not come up yet, because he does not like to see a name sitting there alone on the board. He thinks it is untidy. So the board says four below and the count says seven and the kitchen has made six suppers, and we have all been very polite to each other over a mistake that is entirely mine.

I have told him. He understands. He nodded. He rubbed out a name while I was telling him not to, but I think that was an accident. He is a good apprentice and a hard worker and I would not trade him for three trained minds. I just need him to understand that a name on the board is a promise, and you do not break a promise to make a board look neat.

The seam on the fourth level is good. Gale's copper is the best we have had in a year. Send her my regards and tell her the lift is mended, and I am sorry about the oil.

Now I am going to go and count the miners again, slowly, out loud, with my finger on the board. If there are seven, I will ring the bell twice, which is the signal for all well. If there are six, I will ring it once and go and look. That is the rule. I made the rule. I know the rule.

Send the sorting shed a new bin. The coal bin is full and the iron bin is full and the copper bin is full and the fourth bin, which is for the things that are none of those, is full of frogs. Do not ask.

Hesk
The mine hall, Copperhold

Letter: Dessa to Mave, the honey cake
Dear Mave,

You asked how the honey cake went. I will tell you the truth, because you always know when I am lying.

I took your recipe down from the shelf. Four eggs. A cup of flour. Soft butter. Dark honey from the hive behind Ruth's wall. Then the steps: beat the eggs, fold in the flour, stir in the honey, bake until brown.

I beat the eggs. Good. I folded the flour. Good. Then I reached for the honey jar, which stands on the left, where the salt is kept. The lid looked like honey. It was not honey. Ned had moved the salt. He says he did not. He is seven, and he says a great many things.

So the cake went into the oven with a whole cup of salt in it. It came out brown on top and very pleased with itself.

Ned ate one slice. He said it was the best thing he had ever tasted. Then he went out to the step and was sick in the bean rows. Corin says the beans have never been so surprised.

There are eleven cakes on the shelf now. Even the gulls will not touch them. I am thinking of sending one to the council, with a note that says: this is what happens when the rules are wrong.

Tell me the true way to make it. Do not write "a pinch". I have had enough of pinches.

Love,
Dessa

Letter: Edda to Teacher Sorrel
Dear Teacher Sorrel,

I am writing from the Brown Hen. We stopped here on the Long Road because the cart wheel came loose. Father is fixing it beside the stable. The innkeeper gave me a place by the window to do my lesson.

There are two apprentices in the yard. They are sorting sacks of apples and carrying them into the store. One checks the mark on a sack, then carries it to the right pile. The other brings an empty sack back to the cart.

The innkeeper says they are working from a plan. I asked who wrote it. He said the apprentices wrote the order cards after asking the big mind at the relay house. They had a long job, so they broke it into small steps. First they counted the sacks. Then they sorted them. Then they carried them inside.

I thought the big mind told them every step. The innkeeper says it did not. It helped when the apprentices had a question. They still had to look at the sacks and do the work.

Father says I should learn to mend a wheel before I write about one. I shall try tomorrow.

Your pupil,
Edda
%%
Letter: Oren to Wren, the test box
Dear Wren,

Your question is a good one, and the answer will sound like fussiness. It is not fussiness. It is the most important work we do in the copying room, and I am sorry about the paper.

A drill is a box of pages. The mind is shown a page, it guesses, it is told the right answer, and it changes a little. It is only as good as the pages we give it.

Copies go out first. If one page sits in the drill twenty times, the mind learns that page by heart. It looks clever on the drills and dull on everything else.

Spoiled pages go out next. A smudged word, or a line in the wrong place, teaches the wrong thing. Tibb marks them with a red ribbon, and anything with a red ribbon goes in the bin.

Then the test box. It holds pages that never go into a drill. When the drilling is done, we test the mind on the test box and count the right answers. If it answers the test box well, it has learned the lesson. If it answers only the drill well, it has learned the pages.

One rule matters more than the rest. A page in the test box must never be in a drill, not even a copy of it. If one page sits in both, the mind passes by remembering, and it fails the first time it meets a page nobody has seen. That is the cheat, and the clerks are sworn against it.

Where the old pages came from is not for me to say. The copying room does not agree with itself about it, and I would rather say nothing than guess.

Check the box twice before you send it back. Your letter made me do the same.

Yours,
Oren

Letter: Tam to Mira, the wedding
Dear Mira,
Linnet married Wick on the first of Frost, in our barn. The hall at Three Wells was full of Dace's notices, and the barn is warmer.
The cake was made by Linnet's mother, with honey and dried apples from Ambrook. I dropped it on the barn floor on the way to the table. Eight people ate it off the floor anyway. Nobody got sick.
The cow came in through the door during the speeches. Nobody had asked it. It walked the length of the barn and stood by the bride, and Linnet said: well, she is family too.
Write and tell me about your snow. I have had nothing from the north since the last cart.
Your brother,
Tam

Letter: Greer to Sim, the wedding
Dear Sim,

The wedding was on the reed bank, as planned, and it rained on it, as planned. Nobody minded. Ruth wore her mother's blue dress with a patch on the back, and she looked better than anyone.

The fiddler played four songs before the rain got into his box. The boat house was full of cakes. Wren gave the bride a jar of honey and a long talk about keeping it dry. Oddly, Wren was right, and the honey is still good.

The Lowmarsh boys and the Saltreach boys had a race through the mud. The Lowmarsh boys won, because they know where the deep mud is. I will not say which of the Saltreach boys went in the water first. You know who.

Come to the next market at Lowmarsh. Bring a dry coat.

Greer

Letter: the boy with the cart
Dear Mother,

I am at Lowmarsh. I am well. The cart came down the boardwalk safe, with the apples. Two of them fell in the reeds, and a frog took one, and I think it liked it.

The people here are kind. Wick the weaver gave me a bowl of stew and a bed by the loom. The loom is very loud at night, but it stops at the bell, so I can sleep after that.

I met Wren, the healer. She has a green cloak and high shelves of jars. She showed me the apprentice Moss, which is small and copper, and which carries water very carefully. It did not look at me. It looked at the jars. Wren says it is learning the names of the herbs, and it is getting them right, mostly.

Tell Father the mill pond is high here. The water is brown and slow, and the boats go through the reeds like they have nowhere to be. I think he would like the reeds. I would like him to see them.

I will come home at the first cart. Please keep my bed warm, and keep the cat off it. The cat knows it is my bed, and it sleeps there only to make me feel silly.

Your son,
Ned

P.S. The wheel fell in the reeds. Everyone says it was only the water. Nobody says the water was to blame for anything else.

Letter: Sorrel to Fen
Sorrel, schoolteacher at Three Wells, writes to Fen the weaver, in autumn. The fair is over.
Dear Fen,
The ribbon was made by the children in an afternoon. It is red. It has a bow at one end and a stripe at the other. They put on a lot of glitter, which came from the glass shop, and which will be in my classroom until spring.
The children are very proud. They stood in the square at the fair and watched you lead your ewe to the judges. When the judge put a hand into the wool, the whole school held its breath.
You know what happened. The judge said the fleece was fine. She said it was even. She said it was thick. She gave the ewe second prize.
First prize went to a ewe from Kettle Ridge. Nobody minded. The ribbon from our school was the best-looking thing in the pen.
The children want to know whether Class is disappointed. I told them sheep are never disappointed. They have no concept of it. But I looked at her in the pen, and she looked at me, and I am not sure.
At any rate, the children ate a pie each and the afternoon was a success. The baker gave us all a honey cake. You were kind to lend the ewe.
Come and see us soon. The school is yours. So is the straw.
Sorrel

Letter: Oren to Nessa
Nessa,

The council met. They want the tutors to write their minds in fewer marks, so the apprentices' crystals can be smaller and cheaper. They said the words "small cost" and looked at me.

I read your note. The cost is real. I told them so. They said: a little cost is worth a small crystal. I could not argue, because they are right about the money and I am right about the minds, and both can be true.

So here is what I ask. When you write a mind this way, write down what it lost. Keep the numbers. Keep a copy of the mind as it was, in a large crystal, in the deep halls where it is cool. If a small crystal goes wrong in a field, we will want the large one to compare. The archive has no roof, it has a hill, and the hill can hold a few extra crystals.

Also: your jar. The honey from Ambrook. Do not leave it by the drill box. An apprentice knocked the last one down and Clank stood in it.

Oren

P.S. Write everything down.

Letter: Lyle to Nessa

Sella says you are teaching the layered mind at the tutor room. I would like to understand it before Hesk explains it at supper. He knows the names of the parts, but he puts them in the wrong order when he has eaten.

I am a cheesemaker, not a tutor. Tell it to me as if I were weighing curds.

I heard that the first layer gives its finding to the next, and the next gives its finding onward. But I do not see how a deep mind can learn from a wrong answer if the first layer never sees the last one.

Your neighbor,

Letter: Cass to the harbor master
Sir,

I keep bees on the hill above the market and I make cheese. I have taken a stall at the spring market for nine years. That is good. This year I want to ask for a place near the harbor wall, not by the slip. The shed by the slip has a bench and a roof, and last year I had my cheese on it, and it was good for the shade and bad for the noise. The shipwrights work all night now. I hear the hammers from my hill. My soft cheese needs quiet and a cool place.

I do not complain about the shipwrights. They have work, and work is good. I only ask for the wall.

Also, my neighbor Nil is home. He can help me fetch and carry. He is strong and he is quiet now.

Yours,
Cass, cheesemaker

Letter: Rell to Cass, the first plans
Dear Cass,

Thank you for copying the page. I have pinned it beside the drill board, where the apprentices can read it after their lesson.

The idea is plain when written slowly. First, make trials with crystals of different sizes. Give each mind a known amount of clean writing. Let each one learn for a known time, then test it on pages it never saw. Write down what it can do. The results show a curve. The curve helps a tutor choose how large a crystal to grow and how long to drill it.

A bigger mind can learn more patterns, but it takes more writing to teach those patterns. The tutor cannot skip the writing and expect the crystal to fill itself. Nor can the tutor use the test pages for lessons. If the mind has already read the test, the result tells us very little.

I have heard three versions of Isla's first trial. One says she used old letters from the archive. One says she used school lessons. The clerk says the record does not name the pages. I will not choose among them.

The council has sent a plan for a relay house at Saltreach. It is to stand below the tower, with a cool room for the crystal and a desk for the keeper. The quickeners will need water. Our well is low, so the builders have marked a place for a covered tank. They say the harbor well will serve. The harbor keeper says it will not.

Can you send the cider maker's tally from last winter? I need a count of how much water the crews used. I have asked the harbor keeper twice, and he has sent me a list of fish instead.

Rell
%%
Letter: Corin to Wren
Dear Wren,

Thank you for the jar of the root and honey. I have not needed it yet, but I keep it on the shelf by the slip, and I look at it, and I feel better.

The cough has come to Saltreach. It came up the coast, the sailors say, though some say it came down from the mines and some say it is the water. Nobody agrees, and I have stopped asking.

It is a grey cough. That is what people call it. It comes with a tiredness, and it goes away for a while, and then it comes back. The old men get it worst. We have lost two this autumn, both of them old, both of them fishermen who would not stop going out in the cold.

I am well. I go to the boat house every day. I wear a scarf. I do not go out in the rain.

The big ships are nearly done. Three times as long as any boat I have built. When I stand on the quay and look at them I do not know what to think. They are the best work I have ever done. I would rather they were not needed.

Write when you can. Tell Moss I have not forgotten the honey.

Corin

Letter: Mab to Neri, the wheel

Dear Neri,

The mill wheel broke at noon. One spoke split, and the wheel stopped with half the pond water still behind it.

No one was hurt. I was carrying the school books past the mill when I heard the crack. Jory came out with flour on his sleeves and said, "That was the wheel, not my back." He was right about both things.

The mill cannot grind until the wheel is mended. Could you send two oak boards from your shed? I will bring them back after the market. I can help lift them, but I cannot promise not to get flour on them.

Mab
Schoolteacher at Vell

Letter: Dace to Marit, before she goes
Dear Marit,

I hear you are leaving the farm outside Copperhold tomorrow, and I wanted you to have a letter from me on the road. You will be tired. A letter is something to read at the next inn.

Hanna has written to tell me about the test. I understand the apprentice sorted your beets with only two mistakes. Hanna says one of them was a stone that looked like a beet. I would have made the same mistake. I have seen the stones at Hanna's farm. They are very round and they dress well.

She also told me about the bees. Please accept my apologies on behalf of the district, which is not usually so welcoming.

I have one question, if you do not mind. It is something I have wondered about for a long time, and I have never had the right person to ask. When you test a mind on beets, why do you bring beets of your own? Why not use the beets that are in the field?

I think I know the answer. You do not want to test the beets. You want to test the mind. If the beets are the farm's own, the mind may have seen them a hundred times and knows them like old friends. Your beets are strangers. A mind that sorts strangers well has learned something.

Am I right? Please tell me. I like to be right, and I like even more to be told so by someone who knows.

I have enclosed two things. One is a small jar of honey from Ambrook. The other is a map of the road to Vell, with the inns marked. I have put a star by the Halfway Inn, because of the hearth, and a smaller star by the Brown Hen, because of the soup.

Safe journey, and write when you are home.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells
%%
The pieces of words, told to a child
A reading mind looks at a page through a window. The window is about sixty letters wide. Long ago a mind read the letters one by one. Then the tutors found a better way.

A word is like a loaf. You can cut it into slices. A mind keeps a list of slices it has seen many times: short words whole, and common middles of long words. When it reads, it cuts each word into slices from its list. A long word is a few slices, not many letters. So the same window holds far more, and the mind can read a longer letter without losing its place.

Think of a cart of apples. You could load them one at a time, and it would take all morning. Or you could load them in baskets, and it would take an hour. The baskets are the pieces. The apples are the letters.

The tutors at Vell say this is why the big minds in the relay houses read so well and so fast. They do not say why a crystal can learn the pieces at all. Nobody wrote that down, and I will not pretend to know.

I asked the tutor from Vell about a mind that reads by pieces. He said it can be wrong in a new way: a word cut wrongly is the wrong word. So they still test the minds, and the test box is still full of pages no mind has ever read.

Moss
Lowmarsh, the healing house
%%
Letter: Mira to Pell
Dear Pell,

Your note reached Northpoint with the late autumn stores. The apples were bruised, but sweet. Lantern ate one before I could put it away. I told her the store tally had no line for an apple eaten by its keeper. She said it should.

The reading mind has reached our relay house. It stands in its crystal by the keeper's desk, with the quickeners around it. We keep snow in the cool trough. The lamp at the top has burned steadily, though the wind has tried hard to blow it out.

The tutors say the mind reads one letter at a time. It uses the letters before the new one, up to about sixty letters back, and guesses what letter should follow. It learns from many pages. The relay carries our letters to it, and it can write back. I have seen one answer that sounded like a careful clerk. I have seen another that repeated the question.

I do not think either answer proves Orla right or wrong. Orla asked whether a reader could tell a hidden mind from a person by their letters. Now people are asking again. I would rather ask whether the answer is useful, and whether it is kind.

I sent the mind a small question about the snow tally. It replied that the trough held less than yesterday. That was true. Lantern had used some to cool the quickeners and forgotten to mark it down.

Please tell the farmer that autumn is a poor time to leave a well empty. Tell him the same thing next autumn if he asks.

Mira

Letter: Piper to Hobb
Dear Hobb,
I am sorry about the lint. I am sorry about the song. I am not sorry about the bench. The bench is in the right place, and the light is best there, and when I sit there I can see the whole road to the Halfway.
I have been keeping the bench clean for you. I did not tell you that. I swept it every morning before the travelers came down, and I put a clean cloth on the rail. I thought it looked better for the guests.
Today a boy came with a cart of raw fleece from the moor. He asked if I could card it for him in one day, and I said I could card it for him in three, and he said that was too long. Then he sat down on the step and cried. I gave him a bowl of soup and a stick to chew on, and he stopped crying, and then he carried the fleece to the bench himself. He was very good at it. I did not have to show him twice.
Tell Marda I will bring the big comb in the autumn, and I will not sing.
Piper

Letter: Cass to Isla
Dear Isla,

I have not met you, but your work has reached this house at the edge of Saltreach. I make cider here, and I keep more tallies than I care to admit. I have a question about the curve.

When I press a larger batch, I expect more cider, but I still taste it before I promise a shopkeeper a price. Is your curve like that? Does it show what a mind should manage, while the test shows what this mind can manage?

Rell says it is drawn from trials with crystals of different sizes and amounts of writing. He says the mind must be tested on pages it never saw. I understand that part. I once counted the same jar twice and had to correct my winter book.

There is talk that the council will build a relay house below our tower. The surveyors have put marks on the hill, though the ground is full of loose stone. I hope they leave the old oak by the lane. It shades the place where I set the cider to cool.

The well here ran dry for three days. We hauled water from the harbor, and the first bucket tasted of salt. The children would not drink it until I poured it into the soup pot. A zombie came to the gate that same night. It did not get in. The gate is sound, though the latch needs oil.

If you answer, send the letter with the tower keeper. The roads are slow in winter.

Cass
%%
Letter: Oren to Bel, how a crew works
Dear Bel,

You asked in your last letter how a crew works. I have watched three days, so now I can tell you. I will say it plainly.

A year ago, one apprentice did one job, and a person told it each step. Now a crew can do a long job, and nobody tells them each step. This is how.

First, they plan. The crew stands in front of the work and looks at it. Then they put up cards on the barn wall, one card for each step. The cards use the words you know: fetch, haul, build, store. They put them in an order. The order of the cards is the plan.

Second, they check. After each step, the one that did it looks at the result. If a plank is crooked, a new card goes up: build again. They do not mind. They were wrong a thousand times in the yard at Vell. A mind that has been wrong a thousand times is not shy of being wrong once more.

Third, they use tools. Trowel does not keep numbers in its head. It takes the counting board off the wall, sets the beads, and reads them. Marta says a mind that counts on a board counts better than a mind that counts alone. So do I.

Fourth, when they are stuck, they ask. The little post on the roof sends the question to the relay house, and the big mind sends back an answer.

I do not know how a crew decides what to ask. I do not think the tutors know all of it either. They tell me it learned this the way it learned everything. By being shown, and being wrong, and being shown again.

Your friend,
Oren
%%
Letter: Corin to Ash, the grey mare
Dear Ash,

Yes. Take the mare. Her name is Moss and she is quiet and she does not like to be hurried, so do not hurry her. She will go all day at a walk and half a day at a trot and then she will want her oats.

I am sending her with my apprentice, who will ride her to your mill and walk back with the empty cart. Do not pay the apprentice. He is glad to be out of the yard, because he has been sanding the same board for a week and it has gone very smooth and he has gone very bored.

About the dog: Moss is fine with dogs. She is not fine with geese. If you have geese, put them somewhere else.

The boat is going well. We slid the frame down the slip on the second of Frost and she sat in the water like she was born there. Everyone cheered and then everyone gave me advice about the mast. I have written it all down in a little book. The book has forty pages of advice. The mast is fine.

I will tell you the rest when you bring the mare back. Bring me a sack of your flour and I will tell you about the mast.

Corin

P.S. Keep her out of the mud at Lowmarsh. She will find the mud. She always finds the mud.

Letter: Dara to Pell, the hives
Dear Pell,

You asked after the bees, so here is the news from Ambrook.

The hives came through the winter well. All six. The oldest one swarmed early, in the first warm week of Thaw, and I caught the swarm in a basket and put it in a new hive by the mill pond. So now there are seven, and the seventh is small but busy.

The apple trees are in blossom. Four hundred of them, and the oldest one with the grey branch has blossom too, more than last year. I stood under it a long while. Jory says I am foolish about that tree. Maybe I am.

The honey this spring is light and sweet. I have four jars set aside for you. Two dark, two light. I will not sell them at market even if the wool trader offers me a good price, which he will, because he always does.

Here is a thing you should know. I paid off the debt on the hives last week. The mill lent me the money for the hives two springs back, and I have paid it back a little at a time. Last week I took the last of it to Jory in a small purse. Eleven emeralds. I counted them out on his mill table, and he counted them again, which is fair. Then he said: Dara, that is the end of it. And I said: yes.

I felt strange walking home. The purse was empty and light. I kept putting my hand in it.

New road. The council has finished the new road from Ambrook down to the Long Road. It is packed earth with a cobblestone edge, like the others, and there is a torch on a post every twelve blocks. They built a bridge over Ambrook stream where it used to be a ford. Stone, not oak. It is a good bridge. Before, when the water was high, you had to wait or get your feet wet. Now you walk over. The children have already worn a path to it just to walk over it and back.

People say strange things at the inn now. A traveler from the north said the sky over Northpoint was full of lights, all one night, and then they were gone. Another traveler said it was only falling stars, and a third said there were no stars that night at all, and the sky was clear. They argued about it a long while and then went to bed. I do not know what to think. I only know my bees are calm, and bees notice things.

Give my love to Tilly. Tell her the seventh hive is hers to name.

Dara
%%
Letter: Merle to the tutors at Vell
To the tutors at Vell,

My name is Merle. I am a stonecutter, not a tutor. I write because of something my father told me about his father.

My father's father was a miner at Copperhold. He said the mine kept a little mind in a crystal that watched the count board, and it was the size of a fist. His father's time, the mind was a fist. My father's time it was smaller than a fist, and it did the same work. My father said they learned to write the mind's weights in fewer marks, so the same mind fit a smaller crystal. He said it cost a little. The mind was slower at the edges of its numbers, but it was good enough for a count board.

I am no tutor. I cannot say if this is so. But if a mind can be written in fewer marks, then a smaller crystal will do, and a small crystal is cheaper. That is why I am writing. The marsh needs cheaper minds, if minds can be cheap.

Will you tell me if this is true? I will not be angry if it is not. I would like to know.

Merle, stonecutter at Lowmarsh
%%
Letter: Nella to Ama, the house on posts
Dear Ama,

We have moved. I am sitting on a box in a room that smells of fresh boards and old reeds, and my feet are wet. But the floor is dry, which is the important part.

Here is how it went. Our old house on the bank had sunk into the mud over the summer. Every morning the step was a little lower. By Blossom the door would not shut, and by Swarm the kitchen had a puddle in the middle. Joss said it was a lake with a cupboard in it. Mum said a lake is better than a bog. We all said we would move, and then we did not.

So in Leaffall we finally built the new house. It stands on six posts in the shallows, with a boardwalk to the bank and a boat house underneath. Joss carried the posts with his friend, and a great many people came to help. The men from the boat house brought the beams. The women brought soup. Wren came with the healing house cart and a box of jars, which she set on the windowsill, and which we have not dared to move since.

The worst part was the bed. It is a big bed, made by my grandfather, and it is too wide for the door. We had to take the door off the hinges, carry the bed through, and then put the door back on, with the bed inside it. Joss says he did not hear the word "careful" once that afternoon.

The best part is the window. Every morning there is a fish in it. Not a real one, a fish. A big grey one that goes past the glass at breakfast and looks in at us. Lotte says it is the same fish every day. I think it is a new one each time, because they keep looking so surprised.

I have put the honey cake recipe in the cupboard, in a jar with a lid, so the frogs cannot get it. Come and try the cake in Frost. You will have to come by boat. The boardwalk is still too wet for carts.

With love,
Nella

P.S. The cat has gone under the new house. She says it is hers. We have let her keep it.
%%
Letter: Tova to Mira
Dear Mira,

The snow came over the north field before we had brought in the last turnips. I found them by their tops and dug them out with a spoon. The spoon is bent now, but the turnips are sound.

Pell's cart left us a sack of salt and two jars of honey. He says the road to Northpoint is clear as far as the low hill. He says this each winter, though the low hill is often under snow.

The sheep are in the barn. I card their wool by the stove, and my hands smell of lanolin all day. A little clump stuck to the cat's tail. The cat has not forgiven me.

Lantern came for wool mittens. I made them too large, on purpose, so they will fit over her gloves. She says the signal lamp is bright enough. I am glad of that. Please tell her to keep the stair lamps lit, even if the wind makes them fuss.

We have plenty of wood for now. If the snow lasts, I will send another pair of mittens with Pell.

Your friend,
Tova

Letter: Hesk to Mira, a long winter
Dear Mira,

The frost has come down the shaft like a lid. The tracks are white to the second level. Bram slipped on the lift steps and says they have been slippery since the first of Frost. That is not true. I slipped on them too, and I am not Bram.

The shift is long and the coal is short. We take four sacks a day from the store to the kitchen, and the kitchen says it is not enough. I tell them it is four sacks more than the coal wants to give. They do not believe me. They are cooks. They believe only what is hot.

You asked about the apprentice. Brim came up from Vell in the autumn, tested on carrying and on the field drill. It is good at both. It carries so steadily that the carts seem to get lighter. It is not good at the stairs. It walks down them as if they owe it money.

Last week I gave it an order at the lift. I said home. I meant the tool shed, which is its home here. It went home all right. It went up the hill to my house, and stood by my door, very patient, waiting for the shed to come up to it. My wife came out with a lamp and asked what the mind wanted. I said it wanted the shed. She said that was not her affair, and went in to make the tea.

So I have learned that home is a word with two meanings, and one of them is my kitchen. I told Brim the shed. It went to the shed. I was sorry for it, and proud of it, which is a mixture I have not felt since the day I bought my first pick.

The count board says coal is up on the third level and copper is down on the second. The water is holding. The sixth level is wet and always will be. Bram has asked to be given a bucket of his own, and I have told him to get one from the shed, which is where the home is.

The lamps on the road are all lit. Frost came early and stayed. Be careful on your stair, Mira. Lantern is not the only one who can slip.

Hesk
%%
Chronicle: the great drills at Vell
From the chronicle kept at the Archive, the years about 185 to 195, written down later by the clerks.

In those years the tutors said the reading minds were ready for more pages than any hall had. So the councils sent word along the Long Road. Send your letters, they said. Send your lists and your recipes and your notices. Send what you wrote and do not want. The clerks will keep it.

The clerks did not agree later on how it was done. Some said each town sent a cart and the carts met at Three Wells and were read at the inn of twelve beds. Some said the clerks rode out with boxes and came back with them full. Some said the tutors themselves walked the road and asked at every door. The texts do not agree. But all of them agree that a great many pages came in, and that the copying room at Vell was bright late into the nights.

The tutors drilled the minds on the pages. They did not drill a mind on every page. They kept a test box of pages no mind was ever drilled on, and tested the mind on those, to see if it had truly learned to read and not merely to remember. "Never send an untested mind to the fields," the tutors' books say, and they said it of reading minds too.

People began to say the words over the door again. "We will keep everything we write." They said it at fairs. They said it when they sent a box to Vell. It came back to their lips after the long quiet, when the councils had stopped paying the tutors and the halls had closed. No one wrote down who said it first.

Whose letters went in, the texts do not agree. Some say every letter. Some say only the letters of the great houses and the councils. Some say the clerks chose the good ones and left the rest in a room no one has opened. This is one of the things no one wrote down.
%%
Letter: Zell to the sender of a lost
To whoever wrote to me,
A letter came for me, I am told, but it did not arrive. The man at the post says it was a thick one, with a red seal. I did not get it. I do not know who it was from.
If you are reading this, I am sorry. I am writing to every one of you. My sister, my cousin in Saltreach, my old friend at the mill. I hope one of you wrote. I hope you will write again.
I have moved to the house with the green door, behind the Archive. It has a yard with an elder tree, which I cannot stand, and a view of the hill, which I can. The vats are in the shed. The snow is on the lid.
I will tell you what is new. I have a color I cannot name. It is the color of the sky at Northpoint, as a traveler described it. Green and then red, and then green again. I made it from old woad and madder. It is not like anything I have seen. I have named it nothing. I keep it in a jar on the shelf, and it does not fade.
I think I will send you a skein of it, whoever you are. Write, and I will.
Zell, dyer, Vell

Letter: Oren to Nella, the old letter
Dear Nella,

The box came safely. It is a good box, even if the lid asks to be pressed in a special place. I like the apple. Please thank the children for me.

The lost letter was mine. I wrote it after my first visit to the old drill room. I had put it in my coat, then forgot it there, and the coat went to your farm for mending. I am glad it was found before the rain reached it.

I wrote that the four helpers were made to do one task each. I did not know then how little we knew. A crystal learns from the examples given to it. It can become better at those examples. We do not know what happens inside it, or why it can change.

The children asked if a helper could learn every task by seeing every task. I said we had not tried that. The first four were drilled for separate work. We must not claim more than the old notes say.

I will visit when the wheat is cut. Keep the box on the high shelf, away from the well.

Oren

Letter: Pell to Tam
Dear Tam,

I am still at the farm below Copperhold. The road is firm now, but the farmer has asked me to stay until the last sacks are counted. He says I count carefully. He also says I do not know the difference between a full basket and a basket that looks full. Both remarks are fair.

The well ran dry before Apple. The farmer drew up the last pail and found only mud. He has not found the cause. He thinks the dry summer lowered the water. A miner passing through said the old ditch had changed its course. Neither man can prove it. They have set a barrel by the stream and covered it with a board.

The cat will not stay out of the grain shed. We mended the gap under the door, but it comes in through a loose board beside the wall. The farmer blamed me for leaving it loose. I blamed him for telling me to mend the door and not the wall. We both went quiet. The cat slept on the sacks while we argued.

It is a black and white cat with one torn ear. It catches mice, but it also likes the warm place beside the grain measure. When the apprentice carries sacks, the cat walks ahead of it. The apprentice keeps to the marked path and does not step on the cat. I watched the same thing happen four times. On the fifth, I moved the cat before work began.

The farmer has a little mind in the apprentice. It carries sacks and brings empty baskets back to the barn. The tutor who brought it said not to send it to new work until it had been tested. We set aside baskets it had not seen. It carried each one to the right shed, then waited by the door. The farmer was pleased. He gave it a small job with potatoes the next day.

I told him the reading mind might help with letters from the relay house. He said he would rather have a mind that noticed when a well was dry. I told him no mind could notice what nobody had shown it to look for. He said that was a good excuse for the tutors.

At the relay house, the big mind reads pages one letter at a time. Each new letter is guessed from the letters before it that matter. Its window holds about sixty letters. The tutors say it learns which earlier letters help with the next guess. It does not take the whole page at once. The relay keeper says this is why a long letter can still lead it from one word to the next.

The big mind wrote back to Mira in a neat hand. Some readers say they cannot tell its letters from a person's. Others say they can, because it never asks for a cup of tea. Mira says she has not yet asked it to share a cup.

The farmer wants to know if it can read a well ledger and tell him when the water began to fall. I do not know. I will send the question to Oren, though I expect he will say the ledger must first be clear. He is usually right about that.

If you have spare wheat seed, send some with the next cart. The farmer says the field can use it after the first frost. He says that every year, too, but this time he has marked the plot.

Pell
%%
Chronicle: the relay houses spread
From the chronicle kept at the Archive at Vell, the years 199 to 207:

In the 199th year the first relay house was built at Ambrook, at the foot of the tower above the mill pond. The council paid for it. The big mind came from the tutor house at Vell, and the crystal was the size of a loaf. People came from the whole valley to look at it, and the miller's children were afraid of it, and then were not.

In the 200th year a relay house was built at Three Wells, in the square, and another at Copperhold, at the mouth of the Deep Works. The miners asked it questions about the levels. The big mind answered. It could not answer about the ninth gate, because nobody had told it what was behind.

In the 201st year the relay house at Kettle Ridge was finished, and in the 203rd the house at Lowmarsh, and in the 204th the house at Saltreach, above the harbor. The keeper at Saltreach wrote that the big mind could hear the sea through the window and did not care for it.

In the 205th year the house at Vell itself was finished, under the hill, beside the archive. The clerks asked it about old records, and it read them, and sometimes it was right.

Northpoint's came last, in the 207th year, because the road is long and the snow comes early. The keeper there is Mira. Her apprentice is Lantern.

The tutors say the big minds are the same kind of mind as the small ones, only bigger, and drilled on more writing. They say a bigger crystal, drilled longer, grows better by a curve a tutor can draw before it is grown. Why this is so, nobody wrote down.

Letter: Pell to Oren
Dear Oren,

I am staying at the farm below Copperhold until the road dries. The well has run dry. The farmer says it gave water through three summers, so he is not pleased with it now.

A cat will not stay out of the grain shed. We shut the door, and it comes in through a gap under the boards. It sleeps on the sacks as if it owns them. The farmer says it earns its keep by watching the mice. The cat has not yet agreed to watch anything.

I brought apples from Ambrook. They are safe in the cart, though the cat has inspected every one.

Pell

Letter: Oren to Hesk
Hesk,

You asked me a thing at the fair and I gave you a short answer. Here is the long one, as far as I know it, which is not far.

You asked whose letters went into the great drills, the ones the reading minds learned from. The clerks gathered everything: the ledgers, the notices, the school essays, the letters, all of it. The texts do not agree on how much they took, or whose. Some say every letter in the six halls. Some say only the ones the writers agreed to give. I have looked in the deep halls and I cannot tell you which is true. The records of the gathering are thin and they quarrel with each other.

What I can tell you is this. The talking minds learned from scores, not from our letters alone. The scores came from tutors, and the tutors were people, with their own notions of kind and cruel. So when a mind is kind, it is kind the way its tutors scored kindness. That is worth remembering.

I am an old clerk now and I keep the archive. I am told, lately, to write everything down. I am doing it. I do not know why I am told, and I have stopped asking.

Give my regards to Clank. Tell it the archive has a door for when the shelves are full, and it is welcome to fetch nothing through it.

Oren
Vell

Letter: Gwen to Oren
Dear Oren,

Please send the note about the new relay house, if it is fit to copy. Tam says the house is taller than the school. A peddler says it is wider than Three Wells. Neither has seen it.

The council asked how anyone could know what a mind that large had learned. The tutor at the market said they gave it pages the mind had never read. That seems a fair test, but one tutor cannot read all its answers. They had several tutors check the same pages, and still disagreed on some.

At the house on the edge of Kettle Ridge, we test Pip with a basket we keep closed. He does well with apples, but once brought a potato when I asked for one. Tam says the potato was round enough. I say that is not the lesson.

A fox took a hen from the yard last spring. Tam still says the hole in the fence was not his doing. I have left him the hammer beside his plate.

Yours,
Gwen

Letter: Willa to Dara, yes and a quarrel
Dara,

Yes. Twelve sacks a month, eight emeralds for ten, on market day. I will not make you pay more. I like your horseshoes, and I like your pickle, and I like that your apprentice brought you the whole pile. I laughed out loud, alone in the woods, and a squirrel fell off a branch.

Now the quarrel. It is a small quarrel. Please read it kindly.

You wrote that my charcoal burns hotter than the coal from the Deep Works. That is true. But you wrote that Hesk's coal is dirty. It is not dirty. It is coal. It has the dirt in it because it comes from the ground. My charcoal comes from wood, and wood has no dirt, and I take no credit for that. I did not make the wood. I only burned it.

Hesk would not like to hear you say his coal is dirty. He is a man who takes his coal seriously. When I told him my charcoal was hotter, he did not speak to me for the whole of Longnight. Then he bought eleven sacks and paid in full, and I understood that it hurt.

So be kind. Say "different." Say "slower to catch, but it keeps." Do not say dirty, not to Hesk, not to anyone at the mine gate.

I have put thirteen sacks on the cart this month, not twelve. The extra one is a gift. Bellows can carry it, one at a time, taking all the time it needs. Tell it I said take care on the ice.

Willa
%%
Letter: Dace to Mira
Mira,

The market board asks me to write about the north market, because the north market has written to the board, and the board wants to know whether it is allowed to take sides on a fence.

My answer, as mayor of Three Wells, which does not reach the north line, but which has been asked anyway, is this. The board does not take sides on fences. The board takes sides on bread, and on the weight of sacks, and on the price of eggs, and on the honesty of the scales. A fence is a matter for the two neighbours and the rope.

I have heard the story of the fence from three people now. Gault says the post walked. Harrow says the fence walked. Tam says the fence has never once asked him what it wanted. Fern says the eggs are very good this spring, and that she has not had such a quiet market in years.

I have read the measurement that Lantern made. It is in your handwriting, under the stones, and it says the fence leans by a finger. I have also read Harrow's letter, which says the finger is his. I think they are both right, which is a dangerous thing to say in a mayor's letter, so I will not put it in the board's minutes.

The board asks that the market be kept open, that the horse be given her oats, and that the fence be moved to the middle of the road by the autumn, so that neither neighbour has to look at it from the window. I am told by the carters that the middle of the road is already where the fence is, and that the trouble has always been that nobody agrees where the middle is.

Please tell Gault and Harrow that the board thanks them for their eggs. Tell them the change was correct both times.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells
%%
Notes: on writing a mind in fewer marks
by Merle, stonecutter at Lowmarsh, set down for Wren's shelves

Wren asked me to write down what I know of this, because I keep telling it, and because the tutors at Vell never answered my letter. I am a stonecutter. I will put it plainly, and where I do not know, I will say so.

A mind is weights. That is what the tutors say, and I believe it. Every weight is a number, and every number is written in the crystal in marks, the way a figure is cut into a stone. A big mind has many weights and each is written in many marks. That is why a big mind needs a big crystal.

Now, the thing my father's father saw. You can write the same number in fewer marks. It is like this. Suppose you must write a measure down to the width of a hair. That takes many marks. But if the width of a leaf will do, then fewer marks will do. So they wrote each weight in fewer marks, and the mind fit in a smaller crystal. The count board did not care about a hair.

There is a cost. It is small but it is there. The mind's numbers are a little coarser, the way my cuts are a little coarser when I am in a hurry. For most work nobody could tell. For fine work, a tutor would want the many marks back. My father said the mine kept the fine minds for the forge and the coarse ones for the count board. I do not know if that is true.

The part I do not understand, and nobody has explained to me, is why a crystal learns at all. Why the marks stay put and the mind's numbers change when it is shown a thing. I have asked. The tutors shrug. So I shrug too.

The other part I do not understand is this. If you write a mind in fewer marks to fit a smaller crystal, and then it learns more, does it grow past its crystal? Or does it learn coarsely, within its marks, forever? My father did not know. I would like to know. I am putting it here in case someone who reads Wren's shelves knows, and writes it down.

Merle
%%
Letter: Abel to his sister
Dear Ruth,

Two pieces of news, and both are good, so read on.

First, the wedding. Our Lowmarsh cousin Maddy married a fisherman called Tobit at Leaffall, and we went down for it. The farm outside Lowmarsh had the barn cleared and the benches out, and the frogs sang the whole way through. Tobit dropped the ring in the mud and the whole barn knelt to look for it. They found it. Maddy wore her mother's shawl. There was beetroot soup and a whole pig and a honey cake as wide as a cart wheel. The wind came off the marsh and took the bride's flowers, and the children ran after them into the reeds. Nobody minded. We danced until the lanterns guttered. My wife danced with the mayor of Three Wells and came back laughing.

Second, the birth. Our daughter was born in Deepsnow, on the third, in the small hours with the snow at the window. We have called her Isla, after the tutor at Vell, because my wife read the primer to her belly all winter and liked the name. She is small and loud and has her mother's frown. The school has given me a month at home, and the older children have taken over the fire and the bread and made a great mess of both.

The winter was long. The relays were busy. Every night the tower above the mill pond blinked, and I thought of the big minds reading our letters one letter at a time. Isla will grow up with that, and think nothing of it.

Come and see her when the roads clear.

Abel

Letter: Wren to Dell, the cow
Dear Dell,
Keep her in the shade and give her water with a pinch of salt in it. Do not let her near the wet reeds for a day. Cows will eat anything that grows by water, and some of it is only mud with ideas.
I am sending Moss with the bark and a little mint for her nose. Moss will tell you the dose. Let Moss tell you, not the boy. The boy will give her the whole jar.
Keep the cheese. Give me the cream instead. The cream is better and it does not argue.
Wren

Letter: Dov to Sage
Dear Sage,
I am writing from the wedding of Marda's niece, at the Brown Hen, where I have been asked to carry the bride's chair up the stairs and to stand by the door. I have done both, and I have had a great deal of stew.
You will be pleased to hear that the test box came up at the wedding. A young tutor from Vell sat at the end of our table, and asked the bride's mother what she thought a good wedding should keep. She said a wedding should keep one thing, which is the first loaf, and that the rest can be thrown out. The tutor wrote it down on a slate, and then he laughed, and then he rubbed it out.
Marda says it is the best answer she has heard all year, and that it is hers, and that she gave it to her own mother first. I think it is the best answer of anyone's.
I have sent you a piece of the cake, in a cloth, with a note from the bride. It says thank you for the stool.
Dov
