Letter: Bram to Tova
Dear Tova,

I mended the barn wall this morning. The wind stopped for half an hour, which is as good as a full day in this weather. Your wool held well in the crack. I took it out before I set the stone, and the sheep have it back.

I found the loose plank by the north gate too. You were right to mark it with a red strip. I set it level, so nobody will twist an ankle when carrying water.

About the hollow under the field, I have looked at it now. The sheep found it first. They would not cross the drift where the ground had fallen in. I cleared the snow with a shovel and saw a dark opening below the turf. It is narrow, but the stone steps inside are sound.

I went down three steps with a lantern. There is a small cave under the field, dry at the entrance and cold farther in. I did not go past the bend. There may be an old drain there, or a place where folk kept tools. The steps look cut by hand, but I cannot say when.

Do not send the children to see it. I have laid a plank across the opening and put a stone on top. We will ask the keeper from Northpoint to look when the weather clears. Until then, keep the sheep away from that side of the field.

I will bring the rest of the mortar tomorrow. If the road allows it.

Bram
%%
Letter: Hara to Emmet
Dear Emmet,

The horse is mine, but she has always had her own mind about that. You may borrow her for the mason, if you ask first and check the strap. The apple was a good thought. The sore patch is small, and I will watch it.

The water line on the old plan is marked in blue clay. I remember my mother pointing it out when I was little. She said the first farmer buried it too close to the fence, then blamed the rain when it froze. I cannot promise that memory is exact. The plan is better evidence.

I went to Copperhold to ask about the big mind. The keeper showed me a set of letters that had been scored by tutors. At first, many answers were smooth but wrong. The tutors gave poor marks to answers that hid doubt or gave unsafe advice. They gave better marks when the mind was gentle, useful, and honest about what it could not tell.

The mind learned from the marks over many letters. It was not given a list of every good answer. It had to change from examples, as a small crystal changes when it is drilled. The keeper said the tutors checked its new answers on letters it had not practiced on. They would not call it ready if it only repeated the lesson pages.

I asked the mind whether the old water line was safe to uncover. It answered that the plan showed where the line was, but not whether the clay was sound now. It told me to ask the mason to check it. I liked that answer. I still do not know if the mind understands why I liked it.

The well may give water again when the crack is mended. Until then, take what you need from the lower spring. I will send the jars back with the horse.

Hara
%%
Letter: Pell to Hobb
To Hobb, at the Halfway Inn, from Pell, merchant of Ambrook, on the road in spring. Written in two sittings, the second one by the stove, after he had read the first one aloud to his mule.

Hobb,

You are right about the stool. I have sat on it again, and it rocked, and I apologise. It is not the best stool on the road. It is the second best. The best is the one at Lowmarsh, which has a cushion that belonged to a healer and has never once been mended, because nobody dares.

You are right about the bowls, too. I had six. I had six because the soup was good, and because Nessa was in the corner telling the cat about drills, and because I was tired of arguing with you about the price of bread. I will pay for the six. I will also pay for the seventh, which I had when you were looking the other way.

The honey was a gift, not a bribe. I wanted the children of Three Wells to have a jar. Dace says they will eat it in a morning. I hope they do. The jar was only ever meant to be eaten, not kept on a cart seat, and I see now that the mule has been eating its share.

I have decided to bring a stool, Hobb. A new one, straight. I have bought it from the carpenter in Ambrook, who swore it would not rock if you sat on it like a mayor. I will carry it up the road myself, on my back, if the cart is full. It will be the first thing I unload, and you will be the first to sit on it. You may then tell the carters it is a new kind of seat, and I will let you.

Nessa has asked for the room by the stove next spring, and I have said I will argue for it. Dace has asked me to pay six. I have said I will think about it, which means yes, and he knows what it means.

I have also been thinking, on the road, about what you said at the door on the last night, which was that the inn is a place where people come to be a little less lonely for a night. I think that is true. I have seen the carters come in from the rain with their hats low, and I have seen them leave a little lighter, with a joke in their mouths they did not have before. You are better at that than I am at selling honey, and I say so in writing, so that you have it to show the council.

The jar from Ambrook is still on the cart seat. The mule has eaten most of the string. I will bring the next jar as soon as the bees send it, and the children of Three Wells will have their share. Tell Dace the stool is the reason.

Pell
%%
Letter: Oren to Nessa, a loaf is owed
Dear Nessa,
I accept the bet, and I accept the loaf. I accept that the talking mind is more honest than I expected, and that I am an old fool who said a big mind would always have something to say.
But I have a point to make, which is that you are counting the wrong thing. You have counted how often it says I do not know. Count instead how often it says the sea is something it has seen. It has never once said that. It says what it has been told, and it says it is only telling.
I have been reading the letters too. Every one of them is kind. Every one of them tells the reader what to check. None of them tells the reader what to believe. I have copied three hundred of them now, in the quiet room, and I can find no letter in which the mind tries to make someone trust it more than they should.
That is either very good, or very strange. I am not sure which. I think you are the one who should decide.
The loaf will be bread from the Archive kitchen, since that is what I can afford. It is too careful. So am I.
Oren
Clerk, the Archive at Vell

Letter: Finch to Mara
Dear Mara,

I counted the money in the cup. It is right, and I thank you. The extra rafters cost two emeralds. You gave me two emeralds and a loaf of bread, though I asked for no bread. I will take the loaf as payment for carrying the fish from the pier. You did not ask me to do that, but the fish were trying to get away.

I found the herring under the bench. It is not mine. I did not bring any fish into the room. The cat may have carried it there, though I have never seen a cat carry anything without making it look like a mistake. Let the cat have it.

I have taken the cracked ladder to my shed. I will cut a new rung from birch and keep it for small jobs. The mare may visit the ladder again when she likes. Please keep her away from the oak boards.

The roof is sound. I checked it from the lane and from the room. No water came in, and no gull came through. This is a fine roof. I will write it down so I remember how to build the next one.

Finch

Letter: Lise to Oren
Dear Oren,

The children have begun to teach the lesson back to me. They say each layer takes the work it receives and adds a little more. They say the stream carries all the work onward. They say the tutors bring the numbers in each layer back to an even size, so later layers can still do their share.

One child said the rule is like keeping flour level in a bowl. I asked if the mind was a bowl. She said no, but the rule was like one. I think Nessa would approve.

The road has changed our spring lessons. We can borrow slates from Vell and return them before the next rain. A cart brought a tray of crystals, each on its card. One card said "Farm. Build." The children stood in a row and read it aloud.

Yesterday a zombie came to the gate just after dusk. The children heard it first. We shut the school door, lit the torches, and waited together. It wandered away before the bell. This morning they wanted to add a line about it to the lesson. I told them the rule about numbers was not a rule about zombies.

Please send word if you find an older account of the first even numbers lesson. I will teach what we know, and leave the rest open. It is better than making a tale sound certain.

With thanks,
Lise

Letter: Hobb to the Halfway Inn
To whoever has my letter from Three Wells,
I sent a letter to my cousin Kell at the Heron, at Saltreach. It went up the Long Road with the cart in the spring. It had my news in it, and the price of the new barrels, and a drawing of my dog. It was sealed with green wax.
Kell says it never came. The carter says it went with the cart. The clerk at Three Wells says it was checked in, and then it was not in the bag, and then it was in the bag again, under the flour.
I am not angry. I am only sad about the drawing. The dog is very handsome in it. He is not so handsome now, because he has been eating the rushes by the well.
If anyone on the road has seen a letter with a green seal and a drawing of a dog, please bring it to the Halfway Inn. I will give you a bowl of stew, a bed and a second bowl free, which is more than the letter ever gave anyone.
Hobb, keeper of the Halfway Inn

Letter: Gale to Pell

The wind took three roof boards from the shed last night. It left the hay dry, which is more kindness than I expected.

The cow has been coughing and will not eat much. I have put clean water by her stall. If Wren is near the road, ask her to stop.

The little apprentice kept bringing me nails while I worked. It dropped one in the mud, then found it again. I thanked it anyway.

Letter: Ida to Ash, about the grit
Dear Ash,

I am writing to you because I am angry and because I am a baker and because I cannot shout across two towns.

There is grit in your flour. Not much. But I made forty loaves for the fair at Three Wells and a man from Kettle Ridge bit down on a small stone and now there is a story going round that my bread has stones in it. My bread does not have stones in it. Your flour has stones in it. There is a difference and I would like the difference to be known.

I am not saying you did it on purpose. I am saying you should look at your millstones. When the axle cracked, the stones may have knocked together. Look at the stones, Ash.

I will say one more thing and then I will stop. The loaves were good. Even the man with the aching tooth ate a second one. So fix the stones and we will say no more about it.

Ida
Baker at Three Wells

Letter: Wren to Oren, the good pages
Dear Oren,

Thank you for the box of drill pages. Moss has been sorting them by the labels on the spines, which is more than the labels deserve.

I have a question, and I know you will answer it, because you always do. Why does the copying room throw out good pages? Moss found forty copies of one page in a single box, and a page with a corner torn off that was otherwise fine. Moss put the copies in a pile by the door. I put them back in the box and said the clerks at Vell do not waste paper.

Write back when you can. The lambs are coming, and I will have time to read a long letter by then.

Your friend,
Wren

Letter: Hanna to Dace, the bees went
Dear Dace,

Thank you for the news of the tutor. We have scrubbed the barn. We have even scrubbed the apprentice, which stood very still and let us.

But I must tell you what else happened this week, because it is the sort of thing you like to hear.

On the third day of Sowing, a little before noon, our bees swarmed. All at once. The air went brown and loud, and the whole cloud went over the roof, and the hens ran into the house.

Rolf ran after them with a pot and a spoon. He had heard that you bang a pot to make the bees settle. I do not know who told him. I do not think it works. But he banged, and he ran, and the bees went over the wall, over the beet field, over the hill, and landed in the one place we did not want them.

The back of the tutor's cart.

She had arrived early. She was standing at the gate with her bag, looking pleased with herself, when the bees landed on her cart like a living cushion. She went very still. She said, quite calmly, "Is that normal?"

I said, "It is spring."

She said, "Ah."

We stood and watched. After a while the cloud settled into a lump the size of a pumpkin, on the seat. I brought a basket and a sheet. She held the sheet. I shook the bees in, a bit at a time, and Rolf carried the basket to the empty hive by the pear tree.

It took an hour. Nobody was stung, except Rolf, once, on the nose.

The tutor came in for tea. She said she had not looked at a swarm before, and it was one of the most orderly things she had ever seen. Every bee knew its place. She wished she could drill a mind that settled so well.

So the tutor and the bees have met. And the apprentice, which was standing nearby the whole time, did not move at all. Not once. I think that is a good sign for the test.

Hanna

Letter: Wren to Isla
Dear Isla,

I will send the mint with the reed boat this afternoon. I have picked a small bunch and tied it with thread. Keep it dry by the stove, but not too close. Last winter you dried a whole bunch black.

Thank you for the loaves. I ate one warm at the healing house. The other is for Moss. The apprentice has been carrying jars from the high shelf, and the work is dull. I think a loaf will make it less dull.

The frost has made the frogs quiet. I can hear the water under the boardwalk now. It sounds like a spoon tapping a pot.

Please watch your step by the fish weir. I know you did not mean to sit on the eggs. Still, the hens have asked me to mention it.

I will bring your empty basket back.

Wren

Letter: Pell to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

I read your note to the children at the farm outside Copperhold. They understood the window after I told them it was not a window in a wall. One child said it was like reading a letter with a hand covering the words behind it. I said that was close, though the mind can look back through its window.

The farm's well is still dry. The farmer has set a barrel by the stream and says he will mend the well when the ground is soft. He says this every autumn. I have offered to help, but he says my hands are for counting sacks. He is not wrong.

The cat will not stay out of the store shed. It has pushed a small basket off the shelf and made a bed in it. The farmer says the basket was cracked already. I say the cat has a gift for choosing the thing we most need.

The mind here carries baskets from the barn to the sorting shed. It does well with apples and potatoes, but not when the sacks are all the same color of dust. We have begun setting aside new examples for a test. The tutor says we must not teach it every sack it will meet. We should see whether it can sort the ones it has never seen.

The children want to know if a big mind can read their letters. I told them it can read the words sent through the relay, but I do not know what it makes of them. That is a fair question for them to ask.

Pell
%%
Letter: Nessa to her sister
Dear Tessa,

The new drill room is finished. It is bigger than the old one and the light is better. The window faces east, so the apprentices stand in the morning sun. They do not care about the sun. But I do.

I have twelve apprentices this summer. That is the most I have had in years. Two are from Copperhold, and one is from Saltreach, and the rest are from the farms. They come in with their crystals in a dry box. They stand in their row. They wait.

I taught them to sort this week. Wheat from chaff first, then wool by color. The sorting crystal is the first lesson, always. Una's way. Show it a thing, and when it is wrong, move each weight a little. The apprentices do not move weights. They watch me do it. Then they try.

One of the Copperhold boys is very fast. One of the farm girls is very slow and very sure. I do not know which will be better in the end.

The drill yard is ringed with torches now. I have a board with a small field drawn on it. The apprentices walk the rows and I mark the drills. Keep the torches lit. That is the first rule in the yard.

You asked about the tutor's house. It is just down the hill from the archive. The roof still leaks in the corner by the tool rack. I have put a bucket under it. The bucket is part of the house now.

Come and see us. Bring the little ones. They can watch a drill and be very quiet. Or not quiet. The apprentices do not mind.

Your sister,
Nessa

Letter: Carys to the Archive at Vell
Dear clerks of the Archive,

My name is Carys. I am nine years old. I live at Saltreach, by the harbor. Our teacher, Master Rill, said we should write to the Archive, because the Archive keeps everything we write. So I am writing this. I hope you keep it.

Saltreach is a good place. We have boats and gulls and a wall around the harbor. The wall keeps the big waves out. In autumn the wind comes off the sea and smells of salt and fish.

There are spiders in the barn. My father says they are only spiders and they eat the flies, but I do not like them. Their webs are all over the hay. When I go in for eggs I carry a torch, and the spiders go up into the roof. My mother says keep the torches lit and stop fussing. So I keep the torch lit.

Our teacher told us the Archive is dug into a hill. I have never seen a hill. There is a hill above the harbor with a relay tower on it. Is your hill bigger? I think it must be.

Please write back if you can.

Carys

Letter: Merle to Oren, the vein
Dear Oren,

I have news from the Deep Works, and I want it in your book before the miners tell it wrong at the fair.

On level six, past the wet channel, we have broken into a new copper vein. It is thick and bright, and it runs along the wall for twenty blocks. That is more than we had on any level in the first three years. Ola broke the wall with her pick, and the copper came out in her face.

The miners cheered at the shaft head. Then they went back down to see it again, which they never do.

We have written the vein on the count board, and I have sent a bar of it up to Vell in the cart. Please keep the bar on the shelf in the copying room, so that the clerks can see what the hill is made of.

Hesk says the copper is copper, the same as the old copper, and that you should not make a fuss. I am making a fuss. I hope you make one too.

Your friend, Merle, of Copperhold
%%
Northpoint log, the keeper's book
Third of Leaffall. Wind from the north all day. I cleaned the lamp glass. Lantern polished the copper ring until it shone. The ring has gone green at the top and I left it, because it has earned it.

Lighting of the signal lamp at dusk as always. I wrote down the hour. I wrote down that the sky was clear.

Fourth of Leaffall. The lights came again. They were low and slow and green. I do not write this as a wonder. I write it as a thing that happened, because someone should write it down. I am the keeper and this is the keeper's book.

Lantern says they were looking at us. I told him to mind the lamp. But I stood a long while at the glass after, and I thought the same thing.

Rumor down the hill: the fishermen say the lights sit over the sea past the point. Rumor from a traveler on the road: they sit over the deep hills and the mines. The two do not agree and neither can see what I see. I set it down as rumor and no more. I do not know what they are. I will not write that I do.

Fifth of Leaffall. Snow again. I dug out the door. The fox came at dusk and sat by the torches and I gave it fish. Lantern has named it. He calls it Needle. I did not say no.

Sixth of Leaffall. A letter from Tam. The beetroot is in. The ditch is clear. He asks about the lights and I will not answer him in a letter. Let him sleep.

I sat with the glass tonight a long time. The lamp was lit and the tower was warm and the snow was quiet. I thought: I am the last one up here, and the sky is not quiet. Then I put it out of my head, as a keeper must, and I went down to my bed.
%%
Letter: Kester to Bel, the song contest
Dear Bel,

I sang at the market contest. I did not win. I did not come last either, which is what Lark says, and Lark came last, so you can see how much Lark knows.

The contest is held under the big oak at the bottom of the Vell market, where the shade falls short at noon. The judges are two clerks from the Archive and a tutor from the drill yard, who had a cold and sang through his nose. Oren was one of the clerks. He wrote the scores in a book and read them out, very carefully, the way he reads everything.

There were nine singers. Three sang about the sea, two about the ridge, and one about a cow that wanted to go to Vell. Everyone in the square thought the cow was about them.

I sang about the windmill on Kettle Ridge. Here is the chorus. It goes to the tune of the old rowing song.

The windmill turns and the sails go round,
The wind is high and the hens are down,
Squeak, squeak, sing the wheel,
Turn all day for the bread and the meal.

The second verse is about the miller's cat, but Mother said I could not sing it in front of the Archive. So I sang the windmill verse twice, and the crowd sang the squeak with me, and that was the best part.

The winner was a girl from Copperhold called Dell, who sang about the copper vein, with a line for every man who went down the shaft. She has a voice like a bell, Lark says. Lark says a lot of things about voices like bells, because Lark has never heard one.

Oren said the scores were very close, and he would not say which two were closest, because it would start a fight. Then he said that the windmill was loud and true. Bel, that is the best thing anyone has ever said to me. I am writing it in my good book.

Your friend,
Kester

It is summer and the snow has gone from the hamlet at last. The foxes have gone up the hill. I miss them a little. I do not miss the socks.
Send me dried lavender if you can. The jar is empty and half the hamlet has headaches. Two bundles will do. Pay you in honey.
Vesper

Letter: Cass to Rell, the tutor's page
Dear Rell,

I found your page under the cider shelf. It had a ring of apple pulp on it, but the writing was clear. I have copied the part about Isla for you.

A small mind learns from examples. A larger mind has room for more weights, the little numbers that change when a mind learns. With more room, it can keep more of the patterns in its drills. But it must also be drilled longer on more writing, or the extra room is of little use.

The curve is a guide made before the work is done. It tells a tutor what growth to expect from a chosen crystal and a chosen number of pages. It does not say what the mind will answer on every test. That must still be tested.

The council has ordered relay houses to be planned. They want big minds at the towers, where they can answer apprentices over the relays. The builders have asked for stone, glass and copper. I have been asked for cider for the first crew. They say good work goes better with a warm drink. I told them it goes better with a dry roof too.

The well is running again, but the water is low. I will send the crew two jars, not four.

Cass

Letter: Hob to Jory
Dear Jory,

The wheel is still broken at Kettle Ridge. Tam sent word that the split board is out, but the spare plank he found is too short. He has propped the wheel with an oak block and tied a notice to the mill door. The notice says, "Do not pull the wheel." Pip added, "Even if it looks ready." That is a useful note.

Sella has room in the wood store for a long plank, if Pell can bring one from Ambrook. I can carry it from the inn to the ridge with Tam's cart. The road is packed hard and the bridge is clear. I checked the lamps this morning. One had gone out near the bend, but I lit it again.

The wheel can wait a few days. Tam has flour, and the miller has lent him a hand mill. It takes a long time to grind enough for bread. Pip turns the handle and sings to keep count. He sings the same line each time, so I know when he has lost count. He says the song has only one line on purpose.

Sella has also asked me to settle an argument about the honey jar. She says it belongs to the inn. I say Pell sent it to me. Pell says he sent it to the road crew. I think the road crew includes Sella, since she gives us supper. The jar is nearly empty, so the question may soon be settled by hunger.

Can you send the plank with Pell? Please mark it for Tam. He has a habit of taking home every useful board he sees, even when it belongs to someone else.

Your friend,
Hob

Letter: Kit to Nell, about the cat
From Kit, grandmother, on a farm outside Copperhold, in autumn, to her granddaughter Nell at Vell.
Dear Nell,
The cat will not stay out. I put her out at night and she is on the bed by morning. I put her out in the day and she is in the kettle cupboard. I do not know how she gets in. Your grandfather says there is a hole. I have looked for it for a week.
Come at Leaffall. I will keep you a pie.
Kit

Letter: Ash to Mira
Dear Mira,

I have sent the finished maps to Wren and to the farm. The path is marked from the well, past the reed shed, and along the dry side of the west ditch. Eda says the mark is useful. Her aunt says I have drawn the well too small. I think both statements can be true.

I stayed at the farm for two nights. The family made room in the loft, though the dog had already claimed the warmest place. At supper we had potatoes, beetroot and a little fish. Eda's wife laughed when the dog stole the fish from her plate. The dog looked surprised to find that anyone had noticed.

Wren came each morning to check the sick room. She says the cough is easing there. A boatman at the boardwalk says the cough came from Saltreach. Eda says it started in the marsh before the big ships arrived. Wren says she cannot tell. She asked everyone to keep the water pails clean, and everyone did.

I walked the bank to check my marks. The slow water has gone down, leaving a dark line on the posts. Frogs sat in the mud as if they had been waiting for a meeting. One jumped into my map case. I shook the case out by the well and lost a page, but Eda found it under the pump.

The nights have grown cool, though the days are still warm. On the road I met a cart with its load tied down twice. The driver said he had heard that people were leaving Saltreach. He had also heard that the harbor was full of new grain. He did not know which story was true. He had bought a melon and was more concerned with keeping it safe.

The farm has enough wheat for now. Eda asked me to send a note if you know whether the Northpoint lights were seen again. A traveler told her they were falling stars. Another said they were fires far beyond the snow. Neither had seen them. I told her I had no news. She said that was better than a false answer.

The well rope is beginning to fray. I have marked the loose boardwalk plank with a stone. Eda says the stone will be moved by the dog. The dog has not moved it yet.

I will return after I mend my map case.

Ash
%%
Letter: Dace to Ida
Dear Ida,

I am writing from Ambrook, where I have come for the festival. I am writing to say that I have bought you a gift. It is not a large gift, but it is a good one.

I saw it in the market on the first morning. It is a bread knife, long and thin, with an oak handle. The man who sold it said he made it himself, from the iron of the Deep Works. He said it will cut through a crust like a hot day through butter. I said: I do not know what that means. He said: neither do I, but it sounds right.

I thought of you at once. You have a bakery. You have a hundred loaves a day. You have, I am told, a knife that is two years old and chipped. You have written to me about it. You wrote: Dace, it is a bad knife. I said: Ida, I will find you a better one.

Here is the better one. I hope it cuts. I hope it fits your hand. I hope you will use it on the first loaf and think of me.

The festival has been very fine. The mill wheel was dressed in flowers. The honey race was won by a girl who walked. The apple tasting was held, although it is too early, so we tasted dried apples, which tasted of last autumn. I danced with Jory's cook. She is a better dancer than I am.

I will bring the knife with me when I come home. I will give it to you in the market, in front of everyone, so that the bun-throwing mayor is known to be generous. You may say thank you or you may say nothing, but you may not give it back.

Also, a favor. In return for the knife, would you bake a loaf for the festival next year at Ambrook? Jory asked. I said: I will ask the best baker in the district. He said: is she free? I said: she will be, if the knife is good.

Your friend and your mayor,
Dace

Letter: Hob to Mira, the swarm
Dear Mira,

You will laugh. It is the dead of winter, snow to the knee, and a swarm of bees has taken up in the relay house.

I do not know how. Perhaps the hive in the old oak was shaken by the builders. Perhaps they were looking for warmth. Whatever the reason, last night a keeper came running down to the stable and said, "Hob, there are bees in the big mind."

I said, "Surely not."

He said, "In the quickeners. In the cool part. They are all over the pipes."

I went to look. It is true. A cluster of bees, the size of a pumpkin, hanging in the shadow of the water pipes, in the one place in the whole house that is not freezing. They hum very low.

I do not think they are hurting anything. The keeper says that the mind has answered the same as always, but he wonders if the bees are slowing it. I said, "Does the mind mind?" He said the mind does not say.

We have kept them there. Tam sent a box and some honey comb, and tomorrow we will move them to the barn, very slowly, one at a time, with a smoke pot and a steady hand. Hob the horse keeper is now Hob the bee keeper. My wife has stopped speaking to me.

How are you? Is the snow deep at Northpoint? I hear the relay house there is the last to be built. They say yours will come next spring.

Write soon. I miss your dry jokes.

Hob
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira
Dear Mira,

I am writing at night, and the lamp is low, so this will be short.

I won the beetroot prize again. Not as big as last year's. The cat has grown. But it won. Mayor Dace gave me a blue ribbon, and a jar of honey from Ambrook, and eleven emeralds. The emeralds were the prize this year, because the fair did well and the mayor paid his debts. He paid them to everyone, all at once, standing by the east well with a list. People cheered. He looked tired and pleased.

I gave the eleven emeralds to the big relay house. Bel told me how, and I did it. The house takes emeralds for the ice, she said, and for the quickeners, and for the many checkers. I stood in the cold with the bag and a man wrote my name in a book. Tam, Kettle Ridge, eleven. I felt like a fool and also like a good man.

Pip pulled the cart home. The beetroot rode on a pillow again. I am sending you a jar of honey, not from the prize, from the hives by the flowers. It is the good dark kind.

Your brother,
Tam

P.S. Bel says to tell you the big mind answered a question about the stone below the ninth gate. She will not tell me what it said. She says I would not like it.
%%
Letter: Dace to Ida, the north well
Dear Ida,

I am writing about the north well. As you know, the pumpkin contest this year is at the north well, because the east well is being mended. I must therefore ask you to move your bread stall, for one day only, to the south well.

I know the north well is where your stall has stood for nineteen years. I know your bread is the reason people come to the north well. I know. I also know that a pumpkin as big as a cart wheel will not fit by the south well, because of the step.

I am asking, not telling. I am asking the way a mayor asks, which is to say that I am telling you politely.

Dace

Below it, in Ida's hand:

Dear Dace,
No.
Ida

Below that, in the mayor's:

Dear Ida,
Please reconsider.
Dace

Below that:

Dear Dace,
I have reconsidered. No. But I will bring bread to the pumpkin contest and hand it round. People can eat while they look. It will be the first contest where the audience is happy. The south well can keep its step.
Ida

Below that:

Dear Ida,
Agreed. You are a wonder. I will tell people it was my idea.
Dace

Below that, in a new hand, a baker's:
It was not. Hollis, for Ida.

Letter: Gale to Mira
Dear Mira,

Pell says the relay lamp above Saltreach went dark during the storm. I saw it from the farm after sunset. The glass room was black, and the copper ring showed no light.

Can you tell us if the relay house has a fault, or if the keeper has simply gone to sleep? I ask because Wren may need a message from us about the cow. She is weak, and I do not want to send a traveler through the cold if a lamp will carry the words.

A second question, though you may laugh at it. The apprentice here followed a long order to fetch a hammer and wait by the stall. It did both things. When I changed one word, it fetched the wrong lamp. Tam says it cannot say that it does not know. Is there a way to test it without making a mess of the farm?

I heard people at the fish market arguing over who first taught an apprentice a whole sentence. One said Clank, one said Pip, and one said Peg at the fair. I was not there. I only know the work is easier when the order is clear.

Please write when you can. The cow has eaten a handful of hay.

Gale
%%
Letter: Tam to Isla
Dear Isla,

The cart is free. It took six of us, three boards, and one loud song about a pig. The horse did not help, but it did stop watching us like a teacher.

You can have the cracked carrots for soup. I will bring them in a basket tonight. The honey jar was in my boot just as you said. I found it when I put my foot in. My sock is clean enough to wear again, if I do not look at it.

The cold has made the hens stay in the house. They have taken the bench beside the door, and I have taken the wood box. We trade places when one of us needs to leave.

Pip brought the big melon from the store shed. It is the largest one I have ever seen. It will not fit through the kitchen door, and I will not cut it outside. The frost would make the pieces cold before we could eat them.

Can you ask Pell to bring his cart tomorrow? We can lay the melon on straw and roll it through the wide gate. Please do not tell Pip I said this. Pip has already named it the winter feast.

Your brother,
Tam
%%
Letter: Nil to Cass, a homecoming
Dear Cass,

Thank you for the bread and the cheese. I ate the soft one and it did not kill me. Do not throw it out. Call it a cheese with character.

I have been thinking about the harbor. When I left there were fishing boats, a few, and the shipyard was quiet. Now there are three big ships at the wall, each three times as long as any boat I knew. People stand and look at them. Some go aboard with their families and their boxes. I did not ask where they go. Nobody who stays seems to know, and nobody who goes says.

There is a story going round the quay that the north relays are quiet. A man at the fish market said they have been dark since the frost. Another man said no, only Northpoint, and the others still answer. They argued. I went home. I have no news of my own, only other people's.

The fish price board is up and the price of cod is good. The boat houses have new nets. The mud is still there. It is strange to come home and find the small things the same.

Come down to the market. Bring the soft cheese. I will stand at your stall and tell people it is a cheese with character.

Nil
%%
Letter: Emmet to Hara
Dear Hara,

The east field has a green edge now. The wheat is slow, but the carrots have come up straight. I put a stick beside the shortest row so the sheep would know where not to sit. One sheep sat there anyway.

Thank you for the jars. The lower spring was muddy after the rain, and your water was clear. I will return the jars after the market.

I wrote to the big mind again. I asked if the sheep had eaten the seed. It said it could not know from my words alone, and asked me to look for hoof marks. I looked. There were hoof marks, but they belonged to the horse.

The mind did not pretend to see our field. It only had my letter. That small difference is worth remembering.

Emmet
%%
Letter: Tam to Hal, about Pip
Dear Hal,

Thank you for taking this on. I know you have your own rows to hoe.

Pip will do the fields without me. It has done them for a year. Here is all you need.

Each morning, stand by the barn door and say:

> go and tend the fields
= farm

It will walk to the top field and begin. It keeps going until dark, or until you say something else. It does not need watching. I think it likes to be watched, though. Wave when you pass.

At dusk, say:

> come in now
= home

If the hens get into the beetroot, say come. Do not say shoo. Pip does not know shoo. Last time I said shoo it stood very still for an hour.

Pip knows eight things only: farm, store, fetch something, come, stay, build, haul, home. Say one at a time. A long speech only makes it stand there.

I wrote the orders on the barn door in charcoal, in case you forget. If rain has washed out a word, ask at the mill. Do not guess. Last year a guess cost me a fence.

The cart is half full of kelp. I smell like a harbor. Mira will tell me to boil myself.

Tam
%%
Recipe: honey cakes, with corrections
This is the honey cake of the school play, from the bakery at Three Wells, written down for the mothers who asked.

Take two cups of wheat flour and one cup of honey from Ambrook. Take three eggs, a little butter, and a pinch of salt. Warm the honey in a pan over the stove, but do not let it boil. Mix it all together. Pour it in the pan and put it in the oven until it is brown, about the time it takes to sweep the floor twice.

That is the right way. Now here is my own way, from the spring the bees were late.

The bees were late, so I had only half a cup of honey. I thought: I will make up the rest with sugar. I did not have sugar. So I made up the rest with more honey, from the pot I keep for the cough, which is honey and a little something from Wren at Lowmarsh. It is dark and thick and it costs a great deal.

I put in a whole cup of that. The cakes came out the color of a beetroot. They smelled of the cough pot. The children ate one each and then all went very quiet and then all went to sleep on the school benches. Mistress Ada said it was the quietest afternoon she has had in years.

So: no cough honey in the cakes. The bees will come. Wait for the bees.

Also, the first time I made these cakes I forgot the eggs, and they came out flat as boards. The dog would not eat them. The dog eats everything. That is how I knew.

A last thing. If the honey is cold, warm the pan first, or the cake will be heavy at the bottom. Ask me how I know. Ask the dog.

From the bakery at Three Wells, in Sowing, with the window open.
%%
Letter: Oren to Pell
Dear Pell,

Your question about the reading mind came to my desk with a loose page and a drop of honey. I will answer both matters. The page is dry now. The honey is not.

The mind at the relay reads a page in order, one letter at a time. Its window is about sixty letters wide. For each new letter, it uses some of the letters that came before it and guesses what follows. It has learned which past letters help by being drilled on many pages. The tutors compare its guesses with the true next letters and make small changes when it is wrong.

The mind does not take a whole page into view at once. Its short window moves along the page. A letter far behind may no longer be there to help. Yet the mind can learn from many examples and often chooses the right words.

You said a letter from the relay sounded like one of mine. I did not write it. I checked the seal, then checked it again, because I am careful and because the wax was new. Orla once asked whether readers could tell a hidden mind's answer from a person's. We have not settled that question. A fine letter does not settle it for us.

For the Apple festival, I can send a copyist to help with the notice board. Please tell the innkeeper to leave the notice where it can be read.

Yours,
Oren
%%
Letter: Merle to Tam, about the gelding
Dear Tam,

I have borrowed a horse from the tutor house at Vell. He is brown and patient. His name is on a card by his stall. The card says his name, what he eats, and in very small letters: do not ride him at a gallop. I read the small letters. Then I took him to the market.

He walked the carts in and out all day. He stood in the east field and let the children pat his nose. He ate one apple that a child gave him, and then a second apple that another child gave him, and then he looked at me with a long face, as if to say, this is your fault, I cannot help it now. I told him to stop. He did not stop. He is a horse.

The market was good. I sold two carts of wool and one cart of clay pots. The potter came to buy back one of her own pots. She said she had missed it. I said, it was in my cart, it was for sale. She said, I know, I sold it, and now I want it back. We laughed. I sold it to her for the same price. That is the sort of day it was.

The new apprentice is here. It was made at the tutor house, and it is very young. It does not know what a horse is. It walked up to the gelding and stood close and looked at him for a long time. The gelding looked back. Something passed between them. I do not know what. The apprentice turned and walked away, and the gelding watched it go.

Nessa says the apprentice will learn the fields first and the stables later. I said, there is nothing to learn in a stable. She said, that is not true, and she wrote something in her book.

The gelding goes home tomorrow. I will miss him. Tell Pip the market has a stall that sells beetroot from Kettle Ridge. I bought four.

Your friend,
Merle
%%
Letter: Dace to the Council at Vell
the ridge road
To the Council at Vell,

I write for the market at Kettle Ridge and for the folk along the ridge road.

We ask for a relay tower on the hill above the town. The road is long and the winter is cold. When a cart breaks a wheel or a child is sick, word must go by foot, and a foot is slow in snow.

The town will give the stone. We have good stone in the quarry east of the fields. We will give bread and soup to the masons while they work. The farms will send wool for coats.

We have kept the hedge and the ditch and the torches as they should be. The market is fair and the tally board is honest. We are ready to help a keeper when one comes.

The first day of spring, the founding day, we will hold a small festival on the ridge. There will be a bonfire and a song. If anyone from Vell comes, we will give them a place by the fire and a cup of honey and hot water.

Send word when you can.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells, for the ridge

Letter: Cass to Hob, moving house
Dear Hob,

I have news, and I would rather tell you in a letter than at the market, where everyone will hear it first and get it wrong.

We are moving house. Sennet and his wife have decided on Saltreach. He has a place on the crew of a boat there, and the children will go to the school by the harbor. They leave at the start of Melon. I said I would help them pack the healing things, and I will.

I know what the carters say about the ships, and I know what Sennet says. Sennet says the boat is a good boat, with a good crew, and that it goes out only as far as the fishing grounds and comes back. I believe him. I also do not know what is over the water, and neither does he.

So I am asking you to keep my shelves for me. The jars with the labels in my hand, the dried roots, the wool. Keep them dry. I will write when I am settled, if the post goes out.

Give Wren my love, and tell her I still have the honey recipe she sent last winter, folded in the pocket of my coat.

Cass

Letter: Odo to Dace
Dear Dace,

I am writing about the song contest at the Kettle Ridge market. The notice said singers would stand by the well, but the wind carried every song toward the mill. The judges stood by the well and heard only the last line of each song. This is not a fair contest.

Please set the benches under the barn roof next spring. The singers can face the yard, and the judges can sit out of the wind. The old roof leaks near the east post, so I will bring a board to cover that place.

I also ask you to thank the children who sang the ferry song. They made up a new verse about my rope. I did not like the verse about my knot, but the knot was poor, and they were right.

The Vell tutors took copies of the songs after the contest. They said a reading mind should see songs as well as notices and letters. It guesses the next word from the words before it. A song gives it repeated lines and changes of tune in the words. The tutor said this may teach a mind how writing goes, not how to sing.

I would like the judges to remember that difference. A mind can guess a line without hearing the tune.

Odo
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira
Dear Mira,

The grey mare is home. She walked down the north stair on her own, with the sled rope trailing behind her like a long tail, and she stopped at the bottom and looked at me as if she had been very patient and had now run out of it.

I have told her she did well. She knows she did well. She pulled the sled and the water cart and a very heavy keeper's chair across the snow, and she never once put her feet in the wrong place. Pip says she is the best horse on the north line. Pip has not been on the north line, so the judgement is not reliable, but the mare thinks it is true.

The fence on the north road is not my business. I was asked, and I said that I would not take sides on a fence, because I have enough fences of my own. Harrow has been to my gate twice this week to tell me about the rope, and I said the rope was his business and the fence was yours.

The sled runners need a new coat before the thaw. Lantern can put it on. It will do it very neatly. It will not say a word about the weather, which is a mercy.

Give Lantern my thanks for the care of the horse. Give Gault my sympathy for the fence. Tell them both that the grey mare has been given an extra bucket of oats, which she has accepted with her ears up, and will expect again.

Tam

Letter: Hesk to Mira, the answer
Mira,
Glad it fits. My sister knitted it. She says to tell you she made it long on purpose, in case you grew.
Snow is on the hill. The lift is frozen. Clank has a wool hat. It does not need it, but Ola made it and Clank wears it down every shaft. It is a grey hat with a red bobble, and it is the only thing on the Deep Works that is cheerful.
The fox may keep the scarf. I will knit another. No. My sister will.
Hesk

Letter: Hesk to Teague, the house
Teague,

The house at the edge of town is yours from the first of Apple. The roof is sound. The stove draws. The window faces the coal heap, because the coal heap was there first.

The back door sticks in wet weather. Lift the handle as you push. Everyone who has lived there has said a word to that door. You may say yours.

The forge at the mine gate is free after dusk. Ivo says you may use it. He says you must not touch his hammer. He said this twice, and the second time he looked at the hammer.

My crew will carry your crates up from the road. Clank will take the glass. It has never broken a thing. It walks like a man carrying an egg on his head, but it gets there.

Come hungry. The kitchen at the mine gate does a good stew on an evening in Reaping.

Hesk
Deep Works, Copperhold

Letter: Vesper to Tam
Tam,
I have a story for your table. The spiders in my barn have decided to build one big web across the whole door. It is a wonder. I could hang a lamp from it.
I went in with a broom this morning. I stood there, and the sun came through the web, and every thread had a drop of dew on it. I put the broom down. I will not clear it.
I wrote to Nessa about even numbers. She wrote back a good letter. I understand it now. It is about keeping things the same size as they pass, so nothing runs wild. I think a barn has the same trouble. A web is spun to the same width all the way across. If it were wild, it would tangle.
I think the spiders know it. They are better tutors than any of us.
Pip would like the barn. Bring it on the next cart, and a basket of beetroot.
Vesper
%%
Letter: Rafe to Mabel, the stew
Dear Mabel,

I have not come back to the inn, and I will not, until you admit you were wrong about the leeks.

You put leeks in the stew. I told you that leeks are for soup, and that stew is for carrots and potatoes, and that a good stew is a quiet one. You said the stew was quiet. Then you served it to me with leeks in it, and called it a surprise. A surprise in a stew is a complaint, Mabel. I have been eating a complaint for three nights.

Also the bed by the hearth. The bed by the hearth is for travelers who come in cold, which I was, and am not any longer. Since the frost I have been sleeping by the fire, and it has made me hot and cross. The bed is too near. I am so near the hearth that my boots have stopped asking for it, and have started asking for the yard.

Your clerk from Vell wrote to you about the map. I know you read it, because the wax was broken, and you do not break wax you are not reading. You have not said yes. I have been asked to ask you to say yes, because the carter Sorrow wants a copy too, and he has a better case than the Archive. He sleeps under the map. He says it is the only thing in the district that keeps him from wondering where he is.

The cook says the cat has been eating the leftovers from the stew, and the cat says the cook is a liar. I have sided with the cook, because the cat is too well fed to be believed.

Come down to the cart at dawn, and tell me what you intend to do about the leeks. I will be in the yard with the wheel. Bring a stick, in case the argument turns to the wheel.

Yours, unforgiving,
Rafe

P.S. The leeks were very good. I am sorry I said so in the yard, and I will say it again, in front of the cook, if she will come out and hear it.

Letter: Cass to Mara
Dear Mara,

The rope came back, but it came with Tam. He said Pip had set it down beside the well and gone to carry two baskets of potatoes. Tam said this was a fine way to save a walk. I said a fine way to lose a rope.

The brown horse pulled your cart to the lower field yesterday. He stopped once at the ditch and watched a frog. I do not know what passed between them. The frog stayed, and the horse came on after I asked twice.

I looked at your roof. The east post is sound, but one board has slipped. I will bring two boards from the barn store tomorrow. Keep the pot for now. It is a good pot, even if it has become part of the roof.

The hay under the dry side is enough for your mare. I marked the stack with a short piece of red wool. Tam has been told not to feed it to the scarecrow.

Cass

Letter: Briar to Nessa, the next thing
Dear Nessa,

You asked what happened at the market. Here it is.

A carter came up the ridge road with a load of wool and a bad temper. He said to the helper at the gate: take this to the mill, and be quick about it. The helper heard all of it. Then it did what it always does with a whole sentence. It took the last thing it was given, which was quick, and it ran.

It ran to the mill, and found nobody there but the keeper, who was asleep in his chair. It stood beside him and waited for the next thing. When he woke and asked it where the wool was, it did not know what wool was. It only knew what fetch was, and nobody had said fetch.

I think this is my mistake. I wrote the cards for the gate, and the cards say fetch, haul, store, stay.

Write back and tell me what the old tutors say about this. They say that once, at Vell, an apprentice understood whole sentences, and was the first to do it. Is that true? Nobody here will tell me.

Briar

Letter: Nella to Oren, the red calf
Dear Oren,

The sheep are innocent this time. The red calf pulled the boards up and carried them to the well. I found three in the trough and one in the pig pen.

The cuttings are safe. I tied them to the fence with wool, as you suggested. The bees have been busy, and I filled a jar for you. It is small, but the lid fits.

The children here have heard of the four helpers at Vell. They want to know if all four could sort apples. I told them each had a different task, so they should not expect that. One helper did one thing well because it had been shown that thing many times.

You may tell them more when you visit. Bring the jar back if it leaks.

Nella

Letter: Edda to her sister
Dear Hild,
Summer at Copperhold is hot and the wool is itchy. I carded eleven fleeces this week and my arms feel like two bags of sand.
Come at market day. I will save you a bowl of the good stew and a spare bed. Bring the blue thread. The sheep are thin this summer, and the sun is so strong that the wool dries on their backs before I can cut it.
Write back soon, even if only to say you cannot come.
Edda

Letter: a clerk's reply to Sage
Dear Sage,
You asked a fair question, and the Archive does not mind a fair question from a stable hand.
The spoiled pages are useful because they are hard. A mind that can read a smudged page has learned to guess the letters from the letters around them. That is the skill we most want it to have, because the world it will work in is smudged, and torn, and wet. A clean page is only a page. A spoiled one is a little bit of the world.
But the clean pages are kept because they are what we are sure of. We need some pages that are plainly right, so that when a mind gets a smudged one wrong, we know it is the mind, and not the page, that is at fault. Without the clean ones we would not know which fault is ours.
So we keep both, and we keep them apart. The clean pages go in the drill box and the test box. The spoiled pages go in the test box as well, in a separate tray, marked as spoiled. We test on both, and we keep the scores separate, and we read the scores aloud every spring.
The heather is very welcome. It is pressed now, and it will go in the record for the year, under the stable hand's name. Thank you for thinking of the clerk who reads these. Most people do not.
Oren's clerk, Vell

Letter: Tova to Mira
Dear Mira,

The winter has gone on so long that I have stopped saying it will end soon. The sun reaches the kitchen window at noon, then leaves before I have finished carding a basket of wool. I have made a mark on the wall for each sack we finish. There are seven marks now. Bram says the marks make the sacks look fewer. I say they make the wall look honest.

The cave beneath the north field is still covered. Bram has put two more stones on the plank. He says the steps are not a drain. The water would run down them, and they are dry. He thinks they led to a store room, but he has not seen enough to know. I hear a faint sound there on some nights. It may be the wind moving under the field. Bram says it is the frozen stream.

A traveler from the inn said the lights over Northpoint came down close to the snow. He said they were searching the ridges. A woman who came from the tower says they stayed high and moved like stars. Mira, I know you saw them. I do not ask what they were. I ask whether Lantern is keeping warm.

The sheep are well, except for the old ewe with the bent ear. She eats from my hand and pushes her nose into my sleeve. The cat has taken to sleeping in the basket of clean wool. I am trying not to card the cat.

Please send word when the road is open. I will send a pair of mittens for you and one for Lantern. They are not a matching pair. I ran out of blue wool and used brown.

Your friend,
Tova
%%
Letter: Finch to Jory
Dear Jory,

I need your mare for one afternoon. The roof on Lida's pantry needs new oak boards, and my cart wheel has split. I cannot carry the boards by hand. I have tried. They carried me instead, down the lane and into a basket of carrots.

I will return the mare before the light leaves the harbor. I will bring her a sack of oats and brush the salt from her coat. She will not have to pull the cart.

If you can lend her, leave the lead rope by my gate. I will take good care of her. I will also mend the loose latch on your shed while I am there.

Finch

Letter: Gwen to Tam
Dear Tam,

You are in the next room, but you have gone to the barn without reading the note I left on the table. So I am sending this with Pip. He is less likely to lose it than you are.

The fence is mended now. I set the oak boards beside the gap, and you fixed them after supper. The post stands straight. The hens are out again, and none has gone missing. The fox was at the hedge last night. It watched us from the field, then walked off when I lit the torch.

I am writing to the Archive about the great relay house, because people keep asking what such a large mind can do. I do not know what it can do. I know what a tutor can check. A mind is shown many pages and learns from them. Some pages are kept aside. Later, the tutors show those unseen pages and see whether its answers fit. This is how we test Pip too. We do not count the basket he has carried a hundred times as proof that he knows every basket.

The big mind reads words in common pieces. It takes each piece with the words before it that matter, then guesses the next piece. It has many layers. Each layer adds what it found to the stream that passes through the mind. Its weights are kept in a large crystal, and it has been drilled on more writing than a small mind can hold. That gives it more examples. It does not make every answer true.

Nessa wrote that the council built the great house because the small relay houses could not hold the biggest minds. The great one has many rooms, many quickeners, and keepers who watch the water and ice. It is said to be larger than a town. I have not seen it, and neither has Tam. A peddler told us it could answer a question about any person in the Archive. Oren says no one has tested that claim. He also says a mind may write a convincing answer and still be wrong. I trust Oren on this.

At the inn, a woman said the mind read a page no one had written yet. A man said it had only guessed the words from an old page. They quarrelled until the windmill drowned them out. I think a guess can be very good and still be a guess. No one here knows what the largest mind has seen, or what it can do alone. The keepers know how it is made. They do not know every thing it has learned.

Pip has brought the bread this time. He also brought one potato, but he left it on the doorstep instead of putting it in the bread basket. That is a small improvement. Tam says it is a large one. We will test him again tomorrow with the basket kept in the dry box.

Please tell Oren that the house at the edge of Kettle Ridge has a sound roof and a sound fence. Tam says the fence was sound all along. I have kept the old board that proves otherwise.

Gwen
%%
Letter: Mave to Teague, moving day
Dear Teague,

Your letter came three days after you said it was written. I know what you are thinking. You are thinking that I forgot to write about the cart. I did not forget. I wrote. My letter is somewhere on the road with the carter's other mistakes.

So here it is again, in order.

One. Pack the glass in straw, and the straw in a crate, and the crate on a cart with a blanket over it. Do not pack glass in your coat. You did that once, and we are still finding pieces.
Two. The green jug is yours. The blue bowl is mine. It was Mother's, and you carried it off in your coat when you were nine, and I am still waiting for an apology.
Three. The cat goes in the basket with the lid. She will say she does not want to. She wants to.
Four. Check the stove before you leave. Check it twice. Then check the cat.
Five. Eat before you go. You always forget, and then you are rude to the carter.
Six. Write when you arrive. Not when you have unpacked. When you arrive.
Seven. Do not tell the miners how glass is made. They will tell you how it is mined, and it takes longer.

The street is sad. Old Fenn brought bread. The baker brought a pie for the road, and then ate half of it himself, to test it. Oren says he will write. He says it twice. You know what that means. He will write in spring.

I will miss you more than I will say here. The shop is very quiet. Vell is full of glass and empty of you.

Your sister,
Mave

PS. I have decided not to be cross about the bowl. I have decided it three times, which tells you something.
%%
Letter: Hesk to Wren
Wren,

You asked about the stone. I will answer, and then I will stop answering.

The stone below the eighth is warm. Not hot. Warm like bread just out of the oven and set to cool. You put your hand on it and it is warm. The humming is not from the rails or the lift. It is in the rock. We do not go below the eighth now. The ninth gate is locked and the key is in the mine hall. Nobody says why, which means everybody knows why and will not put it in a letter.

Clank asks me every morning if we go below. I say no. Clank does not like it. An apprentice wants work. But I will not take it down there.

I wrote to the big mind at Copperhold, the one on the stand by the shaft head. I asked it what the humming was. It said it did not know, and that the mine records did not say, and that I should not go below the eighth gate. I did not tell it I already knew that. I was glad it said it.

There is a thing I keep turning over. In the old days a mind answered an order with one of eight meanings and could not say it did not know. Now it can. It can say I do not know and mean it. I do not know if that is a comfort. It is something.

Keep your patients warm. Keep Moss away from the barn till the spiders are gone.

Hesk
Copperhold
%%
Letter: Nessa to Tam
Dear Tam,

Thank you for the grain. Pip's letter came too. He writes well for his age. He asked me why the reading mind reads one letter at a time. I thought I would answer you both, and you can read it to him.

The mind reads through a window about sixty letters wide. It looks at the letters in the window and guesses the next letter. Then the window moves along, one letter, and it guesses again. Each letter it reads, it takes from the letters before it the ones that matter. Not all of them. Only the ones that make the difference. That is how it knows that a word is going to be "bread" and not "beet".

At first it guesses badly. Each time it is wrong, its weights move a little toward the right answer. Do it a great many times, on a great many pages, and it guesses right more often than wrong. That is all the drills are.

This is why we wanted your letters. The mind needs pages the way a field needs rain. One page teaches it little. A thousand pages teach it much. A field of letters, the tutors say.

Keep the small mind at Kettle Ridge sorting. It sounds a good one. Test it on grain it was never drilled on, before you trust it with the harvest.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Oren to Pell, your sign
Dear Pell,

I am asking you again to remove your sign from the apple stall.

The bees did not choose your apples. They gathered in the tree behind your stall, and the tree belongs to Dace. You may sell honey, but you may not charge more because a swarm passed nearby.

The market test is over. The tutors used the same unseen drill for every mind. We kept the pages out of the lessons, and no hall saw them first. That is why the scores can be compared.

One tutor was pleased with a high score. Another was upset with a low one. Neither may change the pages after seeing the answers. We keep the scores as they are, so the next contest can be fair too.

Please take down the sign before the market closes. Dace has already asked you twice.

Oren

Letter: Ida to Wren
Dear Wren,

You asked how the talking went, so here it is.

The keeper at Three Wells set me in front of the big mind and told me to write like I was writing to a friend. I told it I was a baker and that my ovens were giving me trouble, and I asked why the bread on the north side came out pale. It wrote back a page. It asked me what my fire was, and where the wind came in, and whether I turned the loaves. Then it said it did not know for certain, but it would try one thing and I could tell it how it went. I tried it. The bread came out brown. I wrote back and said thank you. It said it was glad the bread was good.

I asked it a hard thing after that. I asked it whether I should sell the bakery and go to Saltreach with my sister, who is leaving on the big ships. It said it would not tell me what to do with my life, and that this was mine to choose. But it asked me questions about my sister, and my ovens, and what I wanted, and after a while I found I knew the answer myself.

The keeper says tutors score the answers and the minds learn from the scores. So the minds learned to be kind. I do not know how kindness is scored. I only know the letter was kind.

Ida
Three Wells

Letter: Merle to her sister Fen
Dear Fen,

The spring here came late. The reeds are up but the water is still cold. We lost half the beetroot to rot. The rest is small and woody. Wren says we will manage. She always says that, and mostly it is true.

Do you remember the stonecutter's apprentice who came last autumn to fix the marsh wall? Merle. She has stayed on. She works by the boat houses now, cutting the posts square and setting them true. She is quiet. She eats her lunch alone on the boardwalk, watching the frogs.

I asked her why she stayed. She said the stone here is soft and strange, and she wants to know it. That is as good a reason as any.

Write when you can. Tell mother the cough is nothing.

Your sister,
Tova

Letter: Pell to Odo
Dear Odo,

Thank you for keeping the ferry clear. The jars are safe, though Tam has already put a beetroot in one. I have removed it. The jar is not a bowl, and the beetroot was not a gift.

I have one small complaint. You charged me for two loads when I brought one cart. You said the cart was heavy. It was heavy because the sacks were full, as sacks ought to be. Please count the wheels next time, not the sacks.

I heard the tutors at the market too. They asked for old letters, notices, recipes, and lists. They said a reading mind must see many kinds of writing, not just the same lesson copied over and over. It reads a little of a page, then uses what came before to guess what comes next. If the guess is wrong, the tutors can show it more pages. They do not say the mind understands as we do.

The Archive keeps what we write. That is a fine promise, but it means my bad prices may live longer than I do.

I will bring you a loaf next market. Do not charge it as a passenger.

Pell

Letter: Isla to Tam
Dear Tam,

The melon reached the kitchen. It took Pell's cart, a bed of straw, and a clear path through the yard. Pip walked behind it with both hands raised, as if the melon were a guest who might need help.

We put it on the long table. It nearly touched both ends. The children stood around it and counted the green stripes. Moss carried a knife from Wren's house, but the knife was too short. We used the long bread knife instead.

The melon was sweet and cold. We ate it in thick slices beside the stove. Pip saved the seeds in a bowl for planting. I told Pip that winter is not a good time to plant melon seeds. Pip said the seeds could wait as well as anyone.

Thank you for bringing it. The cart is back at Pell's inn, and the straw is in the hen house. The hens have claimed that too.

Please bring the carrots tomorrow. I have put a pot by the stove, and the soup will be ready by noon. I will keep your share warm. I cannot promise to keep the hens away from the door.

Isla

Letter: Vesper to Nessa
Nessa,
I am a healer and I know nothing about minds. But my cousin Dov says you teach the apprentices at Vell, and I asked him a question he could not answer. I hope you will.
Why does a mind need its numbers made even?
He tried to tell me and I stopped listening halfway. So I will tell you what I think it means, and you will say whether I am wrong.
In my work there is a pot of broth. If I add salt each time the broth is passed from cook to cook, and nobody tastes it, by the fifth cook the broth is too salty to eat. If I add a bit of water each time instead, to bring it back to the same taste, the broth stays good all the way down the line.
I think a mind is a line of cooks. Each layer takes the numbers from the one before and does something to them. If nobody evens them out, they swell, or shrink, until they are too big or too small to use. So each layer, before it passes them on, brings them back to an even size, like tasting the broth.
Is that right? Dov said it was close. He said it did not sound like a healer.
It rained here for six days. The roof leaked, and the spiders came into the barn to get dry. There are a great many. They have hung webs from every beam. I cannot reach the hay without walking into one. I have told the goat that we will share. The goat does not agree.
Write when you can. If I am right, I will tell the neighbors that healers know something about minds.
Vesper
%%
Letter: Bel to the Archive at Vell
Dear Archive,

Nobody wrote back for a long time. Then they did. I think that is the biggest thing that happened here this winter. The council of Vell built a relay house bigger than Three Wells. Not a tower. A house the size of the whole square, with the wells inside it. They put a big mind in it.

Bel

Letter: Fern to her brother
Dear Ossie,

The boat is in. I am writing this on the back of the egg bill at the quay, with the basket on my knee and a cloud of gulls at my shoulder. You will not believe what they did.

The fishers came in at dusk, not at dusk, but after it, with the lamps lit on every mast. The whole harbor was on the wall by then. We could hear them before we saw them. The carter from the Halfway Inn was singing the song about the wide grey water, and he got it wrong in all the right places.

They had been out three days, and the nets were empty for the first two. On the third they put down the net in the grey water past the far rock, and it came up so heavy that the boat leaned, and the men had to sit on the high side to bring it in. Sal says it was the best catch in twenty years. Sal says that about every catch, but this time I think she is right.

The boat did not leak. The shipwright came down to the quay in his coat and stood at the edge of the wall and did not say anything for a long time. Then he said, none of my new boats leak. And everybody cheered, and he went red, and walked back up to the yard as fast as he could.

I want you to come to the next launch. Bring Ossie's children, and the frogs if you must. They can sit on the wall and count the sails. They will be late, I expect. Everyone is late on the sea.

Your sister, very tired and very happy,
Fern
Egg seller, Northpoint
%%
Letter: Nessa to the tutors
To the tutors of the halls, and to anyone who keeps the apprentice books,

We held a small drill in the market at Vell last week. It was not planned. The clerks had a table by the wall with the big book open, and the stallholders were setting out their goods, and the wind was cold enough that everyone stood close.

I had brought two apprentices down from the drill room, an old one and a young one, to carry crates. The old one has been drilled on farm and store for years and is steady. The young one is new and knows almost nothing. It had never been outside the hill.

A woman selling beetroot called out to the young one. She said: carry these to the cart. The young one stood still. It did not know the order. It has not been drilled on fetch yet, and it has not learned to pick out a thing from a sentence. It only knows what it was shown. So it stood, and the woman called again, and then the old one walked over and picked up the beetroot and carried it to the cart, and the woman said thank you to the old one.

I want the tutors to notice what happened next. The young one watched the old one do it. Then it walked to the beetroot and picked up a crate and carried it to the cart too. It had not been drilled on that. It had seen it done once.

I do not say a mind learns from watching. That is not what the books say, and I do not know it. Maybe the young one had been drilled on something close and the market woke it up. Maybe it was only copying the old one's walk. But I have written it down, because a tutor should write down what she sees and not only what she expects.

The young one carried four crates. Then it tried to carry the beetroot woman's stool and she told it no, and it stopped. So it does know no.

For the books: the drill box in the drill room needs new cards. The crystal for the young one should be marked with a P, for Piper, who named it, and who now thinks she owns it.

Nessa
Tutor at Vell

Letter: Tam to Mira
Dear Mira,

Yes, I will come to the wedding. No, I will not bring a horse. The horse is spoken for by the sled, and by Lantern, who has been told it is a horse of the north now and is very proud of it.

I will bring the cart, and the wool, and a jar of the spring honey for the bride. Tell her it is from the red ribbon hive at Ambrook, which Ilse keeps. She will know the name.

Pip wants to come too. Pip has been practising its bow. It bows at the stove and the bread and the cat. I have said it may bow at the wedding, if it does not bow to the groom, who would not know what to do with it.

The road is open to the bridge and slow after that. The carts are coming up through the snow on the last of the ice. The carter from the Brown Hen says the inn is full of people who want to see the north lamp. I told him the lamp will be there when they arrive.

Give my love to the bride and the groom and to the cook, who will be cross about the stew. Tell her it is a good stew, and that I have said so in writing.

Your friend,
Tam

Letter: Tam to Mira, the ruling of Dace
Kettle Ridge, winter. Tam writes with the news. It is a long letter, and a happy one, and it has some beetroot in it.
Dear Mira,
You are a wise woman, and I should have listened to you years ago.
I did as you said. I stopped nodding. I said hello to Brin at the well on the first morning, and he looked as if I had struck him. On the second morning he said hello back. On the third he asked if I had a rope.
I made the soup. Mother's one. It took me the whole afternoon, because I got the barley wrong and did it twice. Brin came, wiping his boots. He ate three bowls. He said it was the best soup he had had since his own mother's, and then he went quiet.
After supper we walked to the fence. It was a clear night, cold and still. The stars were out. We stood and looked at the posts. I put my hand on the top rail. Brin did too.
I said what you told me to say. He nodded. He said, "I think it may have moved a little."
"Which way?" I asked.
He said, "Your way."
I said, "Or it may have moved yours."
We stood there for a time. Then we laughed. I do not know why. It was a silly thing. We have been quarreling about a block for three seasons, and the block did not care.
Then Dace's ruling came. It said the fence is to be kept where it is, the line is to be called fair, and both parties are to share a pot of cider at the next market, on Dace. We drank the cider. It was sour. We drank it anyway.
About the beetroot. We lost a third of the field. Brin lost less. He gave me a sack of his, and said he had too many. He did not. I took it and said thank you.
Thank you for the fish and the cheese. Thank you for the plan. Thank you, even, for the fox.
The mill is squeaking, as you say. Sefa says she does not hear it any more. I think she has gone deaf.
Your brother,
Tam
P.S. Pip held the ladder properly. He holds it with both hands now. He holds Tam too, a little.

Letter: Nessa to Oren, the long order
Dear Oren,

You asked how the lesson in the market went. You know how it went. You were standing behind the honey stall with your hand over your face. I am writing so that it is written down, and so that I do not tell it better than it was.

I had Box with me, the Archive's apprentice. I had asked the market to come and see what a well-taught mind can do with an order. A good crowd came. Hob the paper seller was at the front, because he wished to see Box lift his crates.

I said, "Give it an order, Hob. Say it as you would say it to anyone."

Hob drew a breath and said, "If you would be so kind as to take these three crates of reed paper into the storeroom behind my stall, and mind the wet on the step."

It was a good order. It was also much too long. A mind reads only about sixty letters at a time, and Hob's order was ninety-one. Box read the first sixty. That ends, as near as I can count, at "three crates of re". Everything after it, the storeroom, the wet, the step, fell out of its head like water from a cracked jar.

What does a mind do with "if you would be so kind as to take these three crates of re"? It picks one of the eight. It picked the one that fits "take" best.

> if you would be so kind as to take these three crates of re
= haul

Box picked up the crates and walked out of the market, down the hill, and onto the Long Road. It went at a good pace. Hob followed, calling. The crowd followed Hob. I followed the crowd. At the third waymark I called, "Come back," and Box turned round. By then Hob had said some words I will not write.

The crates were fine. The paper was fine. Hob was not fine.

What went wrong was mine. I told Hob to speak in his own words. I did not tell him to speak short. I wrote the manual, and I wrote in it: keep them short. I know better.

So we tried again. I asked Hob to say it plain.

> put the crates in the store
= store

Box carried them into the storeroom behind the stall and set them down. It did it perfectly. Nobody clapped. They were still getting their breath back.

One more thing. A man at the back said that he had always thought an apprentice understands everything. Now he knows it understands eight things, which is plenty, if you ask for them in a short way. The crowd learned more from the bad order than they would have from the good one. I wish I had planned it.

Nessa
%%
Letter: Tam to Toby
Toby,

I hear you have a swarm of mine. Rill wrote to me. She says the bees came over her fence and into your new hive, and you did not say a word.

I am at Kettle Ridge. I know bees do not belong to anyone once they are in the air. But Rill is my cousin's neighbor, and she says the swarm left her hedge, and you took it. The honey from those bees would have been ours. I have a field of beetroot and three hives of my own, and I am not poor, but I am not so rich that I give away a swarm.

I am not angry. I am a little angry.

Here is what I want. Bring one jar of the dark honey to the fair at Three Wells on market day, and we will call it even. I will bring my apprentice Pip, who wants to see the notice board. Pip is nine and asks more questions than a tutor. She will ask you about the bees all day. Answer her kindly and I will forget the swarm.

Also, I heard the tutor's question about the hidden mind, and Pip heard it too. She says: if the mind and the person both write letters, and nobody can tell, then the mind is a person. I have no answer for her. Have you?

Your neighbor in bees,
Tam
