Letter: Ann to Pell
Dear Pell,
You asked me for the news from the marsh, so here it is, and you can read it to the carters at the inn.
The melon went to market on the last market day, on the largest cart in Lowmarsh, with four men walking beside it and one boy riding on top, very proud. It sold for eleven emeralds. Hal says he will give five to the inn for the roof and keep the rest for himself, which is the right way round, Hal says. Oda says the inn does not need a new roof and never did. They have not spoken since. They spoke twice the next day, both times about the cart.
The cow is well. Wren's Moss came out with the jar and a face like a very serious heron. The cow took one look at the jar and went to sleep. By morning she had eaten the whole of the clover patch and looked insulted that we had let it grow so tall. We are sorry about the clover, cow.
The boy has started to call everything a boat. The wall is a boat. The gate is a boat. The melon was a boat for two days, until it rolled into the water and sank, and he sat on the bank and wept until his nose ran. Then he asked if the melon could be a fish now. I said it could be whatever it liked, so long as it was not my supper.
The school has a new slate. The schoolmaster from Three Wells sent it by the post cart with a letter saying the children should practice their sums on it. The children have practiced on the walls of the boat house. The boat house has two hundred sums on it now, and the tally man says he cannot read the tally for the sums.
I have a quarrel with Dace over the bridge, which is in need of mending, and he says the council has no coal for the mending. I said the council has coal for its own fires. He said I was unfair. I said I was only a farmer, and farmers are always unfair in summer. We are not speaking about it until the bridge falls down, which I am sure it will not.
Write when the carts come. Tell me about the coast and the ships. Tell me the news from the north, too, if there is any. Do not tell me the rumors. I have heard enough rumors on the boardwalk to fill a barn.
Ann

Letter: Hobb to Pell, about the wheel
Pell,
The spokes are on the cart. The tar is in the cask, labeled, so do not put your nose in it. The boy is coming anyway. He says he wants to see the race. I told him there is nothing to see but water, and he said that was the best part.
Also, the cook says the flour is down to the last sack. I have sent the apprentice to the storehouse with a list. It has counted the sacks twice. It says there are three, and I believe it, because it has not once been wrong about sacks.
Hobb

Letter: Isla to Nessa, the curve
Dear Nessa,

I have found something and I am afraid to say it aloud, so I will write it.

You know the small minds I have drilled this year. Eight of them, each a different size. I drilled each on the same writing, for the same time, and I tested each on the same unseen pages. I wrote down the mistakes on a slate.

Then I drew them. On one axis the size of the crystal, on the other the mistakes. The points lie on a curve. A smooth one. Bigger crystal, fewer mistakes, and the drop slows as it goes.

Here is the strange part. I drew the curve from the five smallest. Then I drilled the sixth, which was bigger, and its mistakes landed on the curve. I had said where they would be, before the mind was grown.

I do not know why it works. I only know it does, eight times in eight.

Come and see the slate.

Isla
%%
Letter: Carys to her brother Ned

The cheese is going well, though the cold came early. The curds set slow in the vat, so I keep it by the hearth all night. Two wheels grew mould in the cellar. That is the price of a cold cellar.

Come up at Blossom if you can. Bring your good knife. Mine has gone blunt on the crust.

Carys

Letter: Hobb to the council at Three
To the council,
This is the Halfway Inn's count of stores, at the end of the long snow. It is written by Hobb, innkeeper, with the apprentice counting beside him, and it has been checked twice.
Flour: 3 sacks. Oats: 2 sacks, damp. Dried apples: 9 sacks. Salt pork: 4 barrels. Salt fish: 6 barrels. Eggs: 11, from the hens in the barn, who are not happy. Honey: 4 jars. Beetroot: 14 sacks, from the cellar. Potatoes: 22 sacks. Wood: 40 bundles, very dry, the last of the barn roof beams are in the stove.
Beds for travelers: 12. Beds for the families from the coast: 6, in the stable and the big room. Mud the horse: 1, not in the count, and not willing to be.
The inn needs flour, salt and wood by the first cart. It does not need more beetroot, although we have been eating it since Frost and Pell says he is growing one in his boot.
The council will know that the Long Road is closed beyond the mill. We have sent word to Three Wells by the south post, but the post has not come back. We do not know if the message arrived. We do not know what is happening on the road to Vell. We have sent the same word three times, in case.
Please send the cart with flour as soon as the road is open. Please send a note with it, if you can, to say the road is open.
Hobb, innkeeper, the Halfway Inn

Letter: Wren to Tam
Tam,

You should have seen it. A tutor came down from Vell in the summer, with a new apprentice in a crate, and a box of drill boards on the back of a cart. The whole of Lowmarsh turned out to see. The children ran alongside the cart. The frogs even stopped croaking, which I had not thought possible.

The tutor was a woman named Hela. She is quick, and she talks too fast, and she was kind to Moss, which surprised me. Moss did not know what to do with her. It has had only my instructions and Old Garrat's, and Garrat is not a talker.

The new apprentice is called Quill. It is thin, very neat, and it has a stiff walk. Hela said it understood whole sentences. She said it was the next thing, the one the Vell clerks were writing about all last winter.

So I tried it. I was at the healing house with a bowl of water, and I wanted the boy from the boathouse to carry it to the sick bed. I said, "Take this to the boy in the blue bed, and wait while he drinks, then come back." Quill took the bowl. It walked to the bed. It waited while the boy drank, looking at the bowl, not at the boy. Then it came back to me and stood still.

I said, "Good." Quill did nothing. It was waiting for the rest. I think it wanted the next sentence. It had done what I asked, and it was not sure there was not more.

Hela laughed and said it had learned the words, not the order of them. She said that was the next thing too.

I do not know whose apprentice was first, Clank's or Pip's or Peg's. The Vell tutors say the first was a girl who sorted apples. Hela says it was a boy who stacked stones. Quill just stands in its corner and waits to be told.

Come down in the autumn and tell me what you think. Bring Pip. I want to see whether it is still the best.

Wren
%%
Northpoint log, Leaffall
Keeper's log, the relay tower. Mira, keeper. Lantern, apprentice.

First of Leaffall. Wind from the north all day. The signal lamp is clear. Two foxes at the foot of the tower at dusk. They did not mind me.

Fourth of Leaffall. A traveler came up from the road with a letter for the relay. I lit the lamp and passed it south. It was from a tutor at Vell, about the reading minds. It said the big minds have changed how they read. They no longer take a page one letter at a time. Now they take words and cut them into common pieces, a piece here, a piece there, and read the pieces. The same window of sixty letters holds far more that way. I read it twice. I think it means a mind can read a longer letter without losing its place. Lantern says: So it is like cutting a log to fit the stove. I said yes. That is exactly it.

Sixth of Leaffall. First ice on the water barrel. Lantern dropped the ice in his tea and called it a festival. I let him.

Letter: Lantern to Clank, a question
Clank,

You are at Copperhold and you hear the tutors talk, so answer me this.

Mira got a letter from Vell about the big minds. It says they read in pieces now, not letters. Words cut into common pieces, the way I cut a log to fit the stove. I think I understand it, but Moss at Lowmarsh says she does not, and she is clever. So tell me plainly. Does a mind still look at one letter at a time? Or does it look at a whole piece at once?

And does it make the mind faster? Mira says it does. I would like to know before I say it to her face and get it wrong.

The nights are long here. The snow is not deep yet. The foxes are back and they are not shy.

Write soon, or send word by the relay if your mind is quick enough to carry it.

Lantern
Northpoint
%%
Letter: Wren to Nessa
Nessa,

I am writing this with my hands still wet from the weir, so forgive the blots.

You asked me to send the drill box back to Vell with the summer cart. I did. Then the cart came back up the Long Road with the box on it, and a note from the cart driver saying the box had been left at the Halfway Inn, and that a traveler had found it and sent it on, with the word "Lowmarsh" written on the lid in very large letters.

So the box has been to the inn and back, and the traveler has been to Lowmarsh and is still here, and he is now our new ferryman. His name is Idris. He has very long arms and a good laugh and he says it was a pleasure to carry the box for you. He asked me to say that the Halfway Inn has a riddle board by the hearth, and that the riddle has been up so long that no one remembers the answer, and that he was not told it either.

I asked Moss to fetch the box from the ferry. It did. But Moss had heard me say "box" and "ferry" in the same breath, and it went down to the ferry and fetched the ferry. The whole boat, and the ferryman, who was still inside. Idris says he did not mind. He says he has never been carried so gently in his life.

I told Moss to take the ferry back. It did. It took a long time to put it at the right place by the posts, but it did it without a single scrape.

The box is in the healing house now. It holds the clean cloth and the spare jars, and I have put a label on it that says Box. Moss can read a label, so it should not go anywhere else.

Wren

Letter: Zell to her sister at Ambrook
Dear Fenna,
We are moving house. I know I said we would not. The house on Lantern Row has a leak over the dye room and a landlord who has stopped answering. We have found a smaller place near the Archive, with a yard for the vats.
I am writing in the cold. The snow came early. The dye is slow to dry. I have blue wool all over the kitchen like a sky that fell in.
Did you send me a letter at the end of Leaffall? A clerk at the Archive said one came for me and went missing. I have asked. Nobody has it. If you wrote something important, please write it again. If you wrote nothing, then someone has stolen a letter from the air, and I shall be cross.
The town is quiet. Half the stalls on the hill are shut. Oren at the Archive says the clerks have been told to copy faster. He did not say why. When I asked he said, "write everything down," and went back in.
I have a new pot of madder red. It is the color of the apple in your orchard. I will send you a skein.
Zell
%%
Letter: Hazel to Pell, the shearing
Dear Pell,

We are at the Brown Hen. The shearers came up the road on the third of Longday. I have carded forty fleeces. My hands are full of wool.

Sorrel holds the cards now. Her rolls come out straight, mostly. Gil's hen sits on the wool bags. Gil says she is laying. I say she is only warm.

Please bring the big cards on your next cart. Do not forget the stool. You always forget the stool.

Love,
Hazel
%%
Letter: Pell to Hobb
Hobb,
I have left my coat at your inn again. It is the green one, on the hook by the hearth. I know you will say it is not on the hook. It is. Look.
I am at the Brown Hen. Marda has a barn full of spiders, thicker than any I have seen. They have strung the rafters like a harp. Every morning there are new threads. We have not the heart to sweep.
A traveler stopped last night and told us about the south. She said the ships are still being built, and that the shipwrights have stopped asking where they will go. She said half of Saltreach is waiting for a day, and nobody knows which. She said it in a quiet voice. We did not ask her to repeat it.
I told her about your hearth. She was glad. She said a hearth that has been lit for twenty years is the nearest thing to a promise.
I sent a letter to Ambrook last month. I think it was lost. I will write again. My sister says the bees have been restless, and there is a hum under the orchard that was not there. I asked if it was the mill. She said no. I do not know what to say. I said I would look when I came.
How is the stew? How is the well? Is the road open past the fourth waymark? Tell Marda the spiders have settled in, and that she may keep them if she likes, as I do not think they will leave.
Pell
%%
Letter: Mave to Hob, the good harvest
Dear Hob,
This year the harvest is very good. I am telling you so you can tell the travelers, who always ask about the barley at the Halfway, and then complain about the price.
The barley came in heavy on the hill fields, and the apples on the old trees by the mill were so big that two of the boys dropped one each on their feet. The beans were very fine. The clover was thick. The bees made more honey than the cart could carry, and the cart is a good strong one.
The grain is in the barn, dry, and I have checked the sacks twice. The mice have tried, and they have failed, which is very satisfying.
You will want barley for the stew, and I can send you six sacks by the next cart. Pay Pell for the carting, not me. He will want to argue about it. Let him. It keeps him young.
Also, please tell Marda that the second bowl is not a cheat. It is a sign of a good stew, and I want it known that I am on her side.
Mave
%%
Letter: Elsa to Juna, the hedge
Dear Juna,

Thank you for the quilt. It is the softest thing in the house. Daisy has already been sick on it, which Ferris says is a good sign. It is not a good sign. It is a baby.

A small matter, between us. Pell's horse has eaten the sweet berry hedge by the gate. All the berries and half the leaves. I was saving them for jam. Pell says Barley was only being friendly. I say Barley was being hungry.

Pell offered a jar of honey for the hedge. I said a jar of honey is not a hedge. He offered two. I have thought about it. I will take three jars and a cutting from your bushes at Ambrook. Then he may come again in the autumn. I am not angry. I am a little angry.

Daisy has found her hands. She looks at them as if somebody has just given them to her.

Come in the autumn. Bring Tilly. We will plant the new hedge together.

Elsa
%%
Letter: Hesk to Clank
Clank,

You have been at the bottom of the shaft for four days and you have not said one word to me about the sump. I know the sump. I know it is wet and it is cold and you do not like the way it drips. I am not asking you to like it. I am asking you to tell me what you see.

Here is what I want. When you go down the long stair, walk on the left. You always walk on the right, I have seen you. The torch is on the left every eight steps. You count them and you stop at each torch. The torch tells you where you are. If the torch is out, you do not go on, you come back up and you tell me.

At the eighth level, there is a side door in the wall, an old door, with the stone round it cracked. You do not open that door. You do not lean on it. You do not put a pick in its crack to see what is behind. When you pass it, you walk on, and you keep your hands at your sides.

I know you want to ask why. The honest answer is that I do not know why. I only know the stone there is warm. It was not warm when I was a boy. Now you can put your hand on the wall at the eighth level and it is like a loaf that has just come out of the oven.

Let the warm wall be. Let the ninth gate be. The key is in the mine hall, on its nail, where I hung it. You do not need it, and I do not want you to go and look.

We still have the copper to bring up. The copper is good this season, and the count board is full. Do the work and come up at the bell.

Hesk
Copperhold, the mine gate
%%
Letter: Odo to Nessa
Dear Nessa,

I have sent the songs and the market notices with Pell's cart. I have also sent the ferry tally from the spring flood. The page is stained with mud, but the marks can still be read. Please tell the clerks not to throw it away before they ask me. It records who crossed and who stayed on the ridge.

People at the market ask how the Archive can give so many pages to one mind. They picture a tutor reading each line aloud, all day, while the mind sits still. I told them that is not what happens. The pages are copied, and the mind is shown many small pieces. It guesses what mark comes next. The tutor compares the guess with the page, and the mind changes a little when the guess is wrong.

The mind is not given one answer for every whole page. It learns from the marks and words that come before. If it sees many letters, notices, songs, and tallies, it may learn how such writing usually continues. It can then make a good guess on a page it has not seen. That is why the tutors keep some pages aside for testing.

I have heard two accounts of how the great drills began. One says Oren asked every clerk for copies. Another says the tutors began with old shelves and took only what they could carry. A traveler from Ambrook says letters were gathered at the markets first. None of us can prove the first account. The clerks disagree about which boxes came to Vell in which spring.

We will keep everything we write. That saying has come back among us, though the pages may teach a mind things we did not mean to teach. I gave the clerks a letter about a lost rope, and Pell gave them a ledger with dear carrots. If the mind learns from both, it will know more of us than either page alone.

The song contest was better under the barn roof. The judges heard every verse, and the children sang the ferry song again. They changed the line about my knot. It is now about the rope Pell gave me. That is a fair improvement.

Please send word when the pages arrive. I want the muddy tally returned after the clerks make a copy. I will keep it dry this time.

Odo, keeper at the Kettle Ridge ferry
%%
Letter: Hesk to Tam
Tam,

You owe me a pie. Maud says she lost to Ruby Oke, and I say that is nothing to do with you, but I will hold you to it anyway, because a man should pay for the things he is not responsible for.

The spring fair was a noise. The pipes came up from Vell, and the drum was loud enough to bring the rock down, which Clank did not like. Clank stood by the lift with its hands over its ears for the whole of the drum. Nobody told it to. It just decided. I have decided to respect that.

The lantern from the school went up the hill with the children, and it was very bright, and I was surprised. I told Yew so. She was pleased, and then she pretended not to be, and then she was pleased again. I have seen the same thing in a copper vein, when the light comes in from the side.

Now the business. The new cart is ready. It is oak, with iron rims, and it takes two ponies or one of your grey horses, whichever is less trouble. The ponies do not like the hill. The horses do not like the ponies. Between them they will haul ore up the slope as well as anything from Copperhold.

I want your opinion on the brake. Gorm says it will hold on the steep stretch. I say it will hold until the first bad step, and then we will see who is right. You know more about wheels than I do, because you are always on the road, and I am always under the ground.

The mine gate lamp is clean, which Ned did himself. We have not yet decided what to do with the surprise. Maud wants him paid in pastry. I want him paid in a day's rest. Ned wants to be left alone.

Come up in Melon if your road allows. Bring the children. Bring nothing else. You owe me the pie.

Hesk
Mine gate, Copperhold
%%
Letter: Edda to Aunt Rill
Dear Aunt Rill,

We are at the Kettle Inn on the ridge road. The windmill squeaks every time it turns. I can hear it from my bed, even with the blanket over my head.

A crew of apprentices came up from Three Wells with a cart of stone. They are helping mend the ditch edge in the top field. The road is steep, so they brought the stone in short loads. One carried a board with the pattern drawn on it. Another checked each load against the work list.

I asked Pip how they knew what to do next. Pip does not talk, so it showed me a stack of order cards. The cards were in a dry box. Each had one short order, such as bring me some stone or stay by the cart. The crew had made the cards for the job.

The big mind at the relay house helped them make a plan. It did not lay the stones or push the cart. It answered questions when the crew reached a place they had not planned for. The apprentices looked at the ditch, chose a safe path for the cart, and sent a message.

Pip came back with an order for the cart to turn. The driver turned it. Then the crew carried on.

I asked if this meant Pip could do any work. Aunt, I know your answer. No. It has learned these orders, and people test its mind before giving it work. The tutor says a new mind must not be sent into a field before a test.

I still think Pip has a better sense of mud than Father.

Your niece,
Edda
%%
Letter: Tam to Mira
Dear Mira,

Spring is here and the ridge is loud with it. The ditch is full and the frogs have come up from somewhere and will not be quiet. I have not slept well and I do not mind.

The wheat went in on the top field. Pip did the rows on the east half by himself and they are straighter than mine. I will not say that to him. He is pleased enough already.

How is the tower? Is the snow gone up there? You never say. You write about the lamp and the rungs and the wind and never about whether your boots are dry.

I have a thing to ask. A ewe went missing from the lower pen. She is grey about the face and has a torn ear and she answers to nothing but she will come for beet tops. If she has wandered as far as the road, someone may have seen her. Ask the keepers you blink with. Ask them to look.

The apples will not be out for months but I have already thought about them. That is how I am in spring.

Write back about your boots.

Tam
%%
Letter: Ree to Sella
Dear Sella,

The thaw has reached the path behind the house. It left a pool beside the door and a strip of mud all the way to the wood pile. I put two stones across the pool. Your horse stepped over them, then stepped in the pool to drink. She has her own ideas about bridges.

I have cleared the drain by the fence. It was full of leaves and one small wooden spoon. I do not know how the spoon got there. I asked the neighbors, and each said it was not theirs. I will leave it on the gate post. Someone will know it by the handle.

The house is quiet when the watch is over. At first light I hear the crows on the roof. Then I hear the market cart on the road, though it has not come this far yet. I think I hear it because I know when it should come. The spring cart is due after Blossom, as the board says.

Please ask the cart keeper to bring a lamp wick and a jar of honey if there is room. I have enough coal for the stair lamps. I have enough bread for myself, if I stop giving bits to the fox. It waits beyond the fence and looks very hungry. It may be well fed.

I have put the blue cap on the peg by the door. I take it down whenever the north wind comes through the cracks. The green patch is still firm. Sella, you should mend clothes for the people at the gate. You would earn more than you do by mending your own. I would be your first customer, though I have nothing to pay you with but apples.

The horse has been kinder to the scarf since I gave her a beetroot. She sniffed it, made a face, and left the scarf alone. I think this is a good bargain for both of us. I have mended the torn edge. It is not a fine repair, but it will keep the wind off my neck.

I owe you for the saddle strap too. The leather worker made a new hole and said the old one was too close to the edge. He said this while looking at the strap, not at me, so I will not take it as a lesson.

The night watch is easier now that the days are longer. I can see the path before the last light leaves it. I still carry the little lantern to the store and back. The tower lamps are bright, but the stones near the house are uneven. I have caught my boot twice on the same stone. I have not moved it. I know where it is now.

Come by when you can. I will make tea and cut the last apple in half. The horse may have the peel, if she waits outside.

Ree
%%
Letter: Abel to Nessa, the visit is set
Dear Nessa,

Thank you. The children cheered when I read your letter. Then I made them write you a thank-you letter each. I enclose the best one. The rest were worse.

The third of Sowing would suit us. The road is good by then and the weather is kind. We will come down from Three Wells in the morning and be at the tutors' house by noon. There are eleven of us. My wife will come too, because she does not trust me with eleven children. She is right.

We do not need much. The drill room, and a look at the tray of crystals with the cards, and a word from a tutor. A child remembers a thing better if it hears it from a stranger.

One thing. The children have heard that the big minds in the relay houses now read our letters and answer them, and that some of the answers are hard to tell from a person's. They ask me if this is true. I do not know what to tell them. Write and tell me what you would say to a child of ten, and I will say that.

Abel
